L'ÉCOLE DU JARDIN POTAGER. CHAPTER XXVII. Description of the CIBOULE; its different species, properties, cultivation, etc. THIS plant is an annual; there are three species, the common, the S. Jacques, and the perennial. The root of the first is an assembly of several bulbs joined together and aligned, forming a single bundle of hairy, white roots; its leaves are small, eight to ten inches long, cylindrical, sistulous and pointed, with a pungent taste; its stem is naked, straight, two to three feet high, M731699 A 3 1 swollen in the middle, bearing at the top a head shaped like that of the onion, but smaller, composed of white lily-like flowers, each with six small stamens and a pistil that turns into a rounded fruit divided into three chambers filled with rounded, angular, black seeds. The second differs in that its leaves are shorter, more swollen in the middle and more upturned on the ground; its flavor is stronger; but in all other respects the same. The third species, which is perennial, resembles the first in every way. All three species are used indiscriminately in most stews, both fatty and lean, eggs and vegetables of all kinds. It is also eaten in salads with lettuce when young, and is one of the most necessary plants for cooking. In medicine, it is used for the same purposes as onion, for which it is sometimes substituted, as it has much the same qualities and properties. The first species, which is the most widely cultivated, is sown from the end of February to August, and the newer it is, the more delicate it is; this is why it is sown in every month during the summer season: those sown in July and August are very prone to perish if not covered; on the other hand, once they have withstood the frosts, they have the advantage of keeping well into the spring much longer than the first seeds, which go to seed very quickly. The best way to preserve it and make it more profitable for the winter is to replant it in June from the first seed; when it is replanted later, the worm destroys a lot of it: When planted later, the worm destroys a lot of it, so we erect boards and trace small spokes eight inches apart; we join three or four feet of them together, and space the clumps half a foot apart, four good inches apart: these little clumps grow considerably, and provide abundantly until spring; they even make up for the lack of onions, when we run out. When frost approaches, we pull up a certain quantity and carry it to the greenhouse, or if there is no greenhouse, we make a trench seven or eight inches deep in the garden, and bury it close to the ground, covering it with dry litter in such quantities that frost cannot penetrate it, and we can always remove it if necessary. Market gardeners replant chicory in the same beds where they sow their spring onions during the months of June and July; and as the chicory is removed six weeks after being planted, it does almost no harm to the spring onions, and they thus make a double harvest in the same land; those for whom the land is precious can practice the same thing. Cultivation of this plant is quite straightforward, and any kind of soil is suitable, provided it is well prepared: If the soil is hard, cover the bed with an inch of potting soil, wet it if necessary and weed it. There is no need to take any other precaution, as it sometimes wilts or is eaten by worms. Replanted spring onions are the best for seed, because they have more body, and their heads are larger and better nourished; consequently, you need to keep a bed of them in place, which you let grow in spring; when the stem is fully formed, it is supported either with running laths, or with stilts to which several nipples are tied together, and in the absence of laths and stilts, they are tied with the month of August when the seed begins to uncover and emerge from its lodge, We leave it to dry in the sun for a few days on a sheet, then rub it with our hands, winnow it and lock it up, but it's better to leave it in its stuffing until it's needed, as it keeps better there and lasts four years, whereas winnowed it's only good for two years; It differs from onions only in size, which is slightly smaller. Once the seed has been harvested, if you don't need the space, cut off the stalk and all the foliage at ground level. The plant sprouts new shoots and produces seed again the following year, sometimes even three years later; that's what I've found. The S. Jacques has an advantage over the common one, in that the worm does not get into it, and that one foot is as effective in food as twice the size of the other; it is also not subject to winterkill, whatever the severity of the weather; I'm referring to the foot of the plant, as its leaves dry up and disappear in August, and it's only in the following spring that it comes back to life; then you can see it grow at a glance, forming extraordinarily large tufts that supply abundantly, and which are later to go to seed than the other species: It is not cultivated differently from the first, except that there is only one season to sow it, which is spring. Perennial chives bear no seeds, and multiply only from offshoots, which are detached from old clumps and replanted in the same way in spring and autumn. They keep well for ten years, and are planted 7 or 8 inches apart in all directions in prepared beds: A single plant is sufficient and produces 110 to 12 in the course of the year, which are detached little by little as needed, without destroying the whole clump; the few that are left reproduce again, and supply from year to year during its life; if, however, you prefer to replant new ones every year, it's all the same. In addition to this merit, it grows more rapidly than the other variety in spring, even though it is stripped in winter; during most of the summer, its hay dries out just the same if it is not wetted exactly; but in autumn, it greenens up again, and resists all the bad weather of the seasons, and will supply without interruption all year round if some care is taken to maintain its vigor; These advantages make it preferable to the other, and keep those who know it attached to it; our market gardeners, however, hardly cultivate it, and give as their reason that it is harder than the other; but the fact is that they want the same bed to yield them three or four different things every year, which the nature of this plant does not allow; its cultivation has nothing different from the other: We crimp it from time to time, and clean it of its bad leaves, particularly at the beginning of spring; those who have only this species, pull it up when frost approaches, and put it under cover either in the greenhouse or in a trench, as I explained for the other. There is a second species that is almost unknown in France, but is fairly common in Holland and Germany; it multiplies like the shallot, and forms an assembly of small bulbs numbering 60 or 80 very distinct from each other, which are used for the same purposes as ordinary onions: a single bulb forms this quantity from the month of November, when it is placed in the ground, until the month of August, when it is pulled out; it is left to dry like the shallot, and is locked up dry; it keeps all winter, and is no longer used as a spring onion; after the month of June, the stalk begins to turn into an onion. This species can be considered as much an onion as a spring onion. CHAPTER XXVIII. Description of CITROVILLE; its different species, properties, cultivation, etc. Of all plants, this is the one that takes up the most space; it crawls like a cucumber or a melon, and its stalks extend up to 3 or 4 toises from its base. Its roots are small, straight, fibrous and hairy; it grows large, hollow, fragile, hairy sarmens on the ground, lined with large, deeply-cut, rough, bristly leaves; from the axils of these leaves, tendrils and pedicles emerge, bearing yellow, bell-shaped, flared flowers, divided into five parts, some sterile and others fertile, resting on an embryo that turns into a rounded fruit; The flesh is white, firm and sweet-tasting; the seed is contained in a fungus-like substance in the middle of the fruit; it is oblong, broad, flattened, cleanly rimmed all around, and lined with a slightly rough rind, under which is a white almond, pleasant to the taste. This species, as I describe it, is the most remarkable of all those cultivated in France; but there are an infinite number of others that vary either in the shape and size of the fruit, or in its external color, which can be green, white, yellow, mottled or striped in several ways; Suffice it to say that the quality of the fruit is always more or less the same, and there is no marked difference except in the species grown around Paris, known as Potiron. The bulk of the plant is quite similar to the previous one, but the leaf is almost round, without a cut, preceded by an extremely long and concave tail, about the size of a thumb; the fruit, in shape, is also quite similar; but it surpasses the pumpkin in size, as I have seen some weighing around 170 lb. It is usually a little flattened, especially on the head side, but chance produces some that are more or less elongated, and in general, they have many inequalities in their circumference; some are even marked with ribs, the skin is smooth, orange-colored, and the flesh the previous yellow; it melts less than all the other pumpkin species, and its flesh has more taste, but it still has the blandness of the others: however, cooked and mixed with milk, it can be served as a soup, and has its fans; this is also the only way to eat it that is bearable for people of a certain kind: country folk and craftsmen fricassee it with onion and in several other ways; but this stew can be abandoned to them, for on the whole, it's a tasteless dish and bad food, especially for cold temperaments; nevertheless, as it is an economical resource for people whose stomachs suffer everything, and for the peasant in particular who cannot feed on expensive food, it is appropriate for those who have country possessions, or who are burdened with children and servants who must be supported at a fair price : it is advisable, I say, to raise a certain quantity that can be found useful during the winter. The flesh of this species, like all the others, is humectant, pectoral, refreshing, and suitable for tempering the heat of the entrails, when taken as a decoction. There are two kinds of pumpkin: an early one, which is good at the beginning of August, and which is of medium size; and a later one, which is good only at the end of September, and which comes from an extreme size, as I said above: This is what differentiates them, being similar in all respects; the early one can be recognized by another mark, which is that its tail is yellow, while the other is green. This species, both the early and the late, multiplies, like all the others, from its seed, which must be sown in this climate at the beginning of March; because if it is not brought forward a little early, the fruit is prone to ripen only imperfectly, and it is of guard only as much as it is well August. It is placed under a bell, and cared for in the same way as melons and cucumbers; but as the plant is stronger, six seeds are enough under each bell, and when the plant is strong enough, it is replanted in the open ground, and two feet are placed together; this is at the end of April or the beginning of May. We make holes two feet square and a foot deep, which we fill with manure and a few inches of potting soil above; we wet the plant as soon as it is in place, and cover it exactly for s to ó days, unless it rains, as the sun ruins it in this circumstance; when it is picked up again, we lift the covers, and leave it to grow for about six weeks, until the fruit begins to appear: But when the fruit has stopped, which is always 8 or 1o feet from its root, pinch the dragnasse a little above it, and throw a few spades of earth on the node above it, which takes root and gives more nourishment to the fruit: at the same time, rest it on a tile, on the side where it is curved, so that rainwater can run off to the right and left. When the fruit has reached its full size, it should be exposed to the air, i.e. cut off all the leaves that shade it, so that the sun will ripen it and aptly aptly aptly apt it; Once it has reached maturity in August or September, depending on the species, we cut it and leave it exposed to the sun for ten or twelve days before locking it up; we then put it in a dry place on boards where it can breathe and where the frost cannot penetrate. When you find yourself a few piles of sumier or useless compost, no situation suits it better; it grows quickly and prodigiously. It is claimed that there is a male and a female in this plant, and it is at least true that there is a mark that characterizes the two alleged sexes, and it is chance that has produced it, just as single and double seeds come from the wallflower; the female grows two equal leaves, opposite each other, above her two ears, with the stalk coming out of the middle; This is easy to notice, and by experience our market gardeners have recognized that the male bears much more fruit, and that the fruit is bigger and fuller, so they let two second branches grow on these male plants, which give them three and four fruits, while the female is quite busy with one; They are therefore careful, once the seed has sprouted and marked, to uproot all the female plants and replant only the males, which also give better and more abundant seed. I tried it this year, and the fact seemed to me to be true to a greater extent; but as one year does not decide, I establish the certainty of the fact only on the say of the market gardeners who assured me of it. I don't know if the other pumpkin species are in the same situation: as they are not grown in this country, I haven't had the opportunity to notice it: those who grow them should take care. The seed of this fruit, about which I have not yet said anything, is its most valuable part: it is one of the four major cold seeds used so usefully in emulsions; it softens and refreshes the blood; it tempers the ardor of urine, calculus, kidney or bladder ulceration, and even the pain of gout; it also yields an oil that softens the skin, makes it even and erases blemishes, and is commonly used in cosmetic ointments; Everyone knows that it is used in the composition of that refreshing drink we drink in summer, as much for pleasure as for health, which we call orgeat, because barley water used to be its base; but as it is always being perfected, the way it is composed is quite different today; I'll give the recipe for the benefit of those who find themselves remote and isolated in their countryside. Take half a pound of the 4 seeds, which are pumpkin, squash, cucumber and melon seeds; wash and dry them, then add six ounces of sweet almonds and two ounces of bitter almonds, soak them a little in warm water to remove the skin; put the whole in a stone mortar and crush it well, then throw in two pounds of sugar with the skin. of sugar with the skin of a lemon, which you will grind with the seeds, this will make a paste that you will put well pressed in an earthenware or earthenware vase, and that you can keep for a month or more in a dry place. To use it when needed, you will take some from each pint of water, the size of a hen's egg, which you will put in a serge cheesecloth and dilute with a spoon; you will then press the pomace with both hands, and you will throw a few drops of orange blossom on the liqueur; you will then decant it into some jug or bottle which you will cool before drinking it. This seed keeps well for seven to eight years, both for the uses I've just mentioned, and for the seed; it has one more small merit that I don't want to omit, which is to serve as bait to catch rats, mice, field mice, leaches and other insects of this species, which are very fond of it. Before concluding this article, I feel I must say a word about several small species of fruit that are very similar to pumpkins: priest's bonnets, German trumpets, false oranges, coloquintes and so on. All these species have flesh and skin quite similar in small to the pumpkin, but they serve almost only to amuse the eyes, and are of no use to life; their shape and color vary infinitely; some are made into snuffboxes, and this is the surest merit I know of them, some others have properties for certain remedies, but their virtues are not proven enough to make them public, especially since the authors I follow say nothing about them; the first species that I have named, the bonnet-de-prêtre, otherwise known as the Jerusalem artichoke, must nevertheless be distinguished from the others, insofar as it is eaten, and it is not a bad eater: but it is neither fine nor delicate, and its shape is singular, having around its head several horns arranged more or less like those of a priest's cap, from which it takes its name. We have recently acquired a new species, called Giromont, whose leaves are shaped more or less like those of the fig tree, hairy and of medium size, and whose sarmens crawl like all pumpkins, flowering in the same way; but the fruit is different, being very black-green, sometimes striped with yellow, loaded with a few warts, a little elongated in the shape of pears; its flesh is firm and yellow, the great merit of this species is that it bears much fruit and keeps longer than any other, without being subject to rotting; Each plant easily yields 12 to 15 fruits, but the taste is no better than that of the others; on the contrary, I found it bland, and the flesh stringy; perhaps it would appeal better to others, or be better in other climates. It is certain, at least, that there is no species that would be as profitable as this one, if it could become established among country folk. CIVE. CHAPTER XXIX. Description of CIVE, its different species, properties, cultivation, etc. There are three species of Cive, the Cive de Portugal, the grosse Cive d'Angleterre, and the petite, otherwise known as the Civette. They differ only in leaf size, so the same description will apply to all three, noting that the Portuguese species is the strongest. Its flower is also higher and more elongated. The root of this plant is an assembly of small bulbs much like shallots, but which are formed in greater quantity, and which are not enveloped in the same way, being linked together only by roots which are white and loose fibers like those of spring onions; The leaf is long, cylindrical and fistulous, extremely small, with an odor and flavor similar to that of the spring onion; it sometimes sprouts a small stem ending in a spherical bundle of small purplish flowers the size of a filbert; these flowers are followed by a small seed enclosed in the calyx of the flower. In spring, this plant is frequently used in salad dressings, and sometimes in omelettes; I know of no other use or property for it, and medical writers have not mentioned it. Cive can be propagated from seed if necessary; but it is easy to propagate from offshoots, i.e. you separate the old clumps that make up yo, and each tuber you replant forms a clump in the same year: but 2 or 3 are usually left together; it is in March that it is replanted: as soon as it begins to grow, it is placed in beds or borders 8 or 9 inches apart; it requires loose, well-prepared soil, and will support itself in the same place for 4 or 5 years. Weed it as needed, wet it in dry spells and crimp it from time to time, especially in spring. At the end of autumn, cut off all its leaves just above ground level, and cover it with an inch of potting soil to help it grow more vigorously the following spring; the more you cut off the leaf, the more it grows back; and the newer it is, the more tender it becomes. CHAPTER XXX. Description of the CUCUMBER, its different species, properties, cultivation, etc. Four species of cucumber are cultivated in this climate for tablé use, apart from the one that is grown for medicinal purposes, or only for the singularity I shall discuss next. These four species are the early and late cucumbers, the bouquet cucumber and the black cucumber. The first two are similar in all respects, except that the early cucumber is smaller and ripens earlier than the late cucumber. The roots of these two species are straight, fibrous, and covered with a lot of hair; the stems are sarmentose, thick, long, branching, and crawl on the ground; the leaves are born alternately borne on a tail six to eight inches long, thick and concave; they are shaped for outline like those of the vine, but larger, much less dissected, without serration at the edges, rough and covered with small prickles, especially below il cort de l'aisselle des vrilles, ou mains de fleurs d'une seule pièce, disposées en cloche, évasées et partagées LCHXPITRE XXX. 26 Prede in five parts, each about half an inch wide, of a pale yellow color, some of which are sterile and others slow; the latter are announced by the fruit which forms an inch long at the same time as the blossom, and even before the blossom opens, this fruit, which gradually expands, grows to a foot long by about three inches diametrically. It is rounded at both ends, sometimes straight, sometimes curved, most often dotted with small warts; white, green or yellow, depending on the species, as there are some of these three colors, its stalk is very thin, its flesh firm, white and transparent, without any odor. detidated, the seed is placed in the middle of the fruit, in three or four compartments lined with a watery pulp. This seed is white and oblong, pointed at both ends, a little larger than that of the melon, and covered with a hard rind under which lies a milky, sweet kernel. The third species, known as the bouquet cucumber or mignon cucumber, differs in several respects from the two preceding species. Its root is quite similar, but its stem, although equally shaped, is much smaller; its stems are shorter and less numerous, rising straight up to the base of the fruit. B rise straight up until the load of the fruit causes them to fall back; it hardly extends beyond eighteen inches, and often the bell is enough. Its fruit comes in bunches along its small stems, three or four of them together; it is green at first, and as it ripens, it takes on a yellow and golden color; its length is usually limited to four or five inches by two inches in diameter; its flesh is similar in all respects to that of the two preceding species, and its seed is the same, but a little smaller: its main merit is that it is very early. The fourth species is quite different from the other three: its root is very short, white and hairy, its leaves are born in crowds one on top of the other, placed alternately, and when they are in their full strength, they extend to fifteen inches and more, very similar in outline to those of the vine, but less deeply cut, rough, blistered and hairy, both above and below, spreading a bad odor; They are borne on a hollow tail the size of a finger, and from fifteen to eighteen inches long.Sometimes there is only one, sometimes there are two or three; it is very large, five-sided and partly hollow; when cut laterally, it represents a star; its length is about three feet. From the armpits of the leaves come the flowers, which are golden-yellow and velvety, bell-shaped, and resemble in every way those of the pumpkin; some are sterile, borne on pedicels three to four inches long; others are already formed before they bloom. At first, the fruit is very small and soft green, but as it grows larger and longer, it takes on a dark, almost black color. Its size and length are very similar to those of the late cucumber, but its skin is much rougher, forming a number of small ribs; its flesh is firm, yellowish in color, and much less watery than the other species. The seed is placed in several chambers separated by a pulp, as in the others, but twice as large, more flared and ruddier. One is called the Barbary cucumber, which is very large and oddly shaped, with a large number of warts; yellow in color, striped with green, the stem and leaf are nothing special, and I don't know of any properties. The other is called the snake cucumber or Luffa; its shape is elongated, but much larger at the head than at the tail; its skin is smooth, pale green, marked at the sides by small protruding fillets, arranged with symmetry; its leaf is cut, quite small, and its arms small; it is used neither in cooking nor in medicine of which the authors have spoken; it seems to me that it is a very new plant for us. A third, much better-known species, and very useful in medicine, is the wild cucumber, which is perennial; its root is long and thick, and it produces several thick stems that rise straight up to a height of fifteen to eighteen inches, and then fall to the ground when loaded with fruit; its leaves are rounded and pointed, quite large, and pillowed at the base; the flowers come from bell-shaped armpits, in one piece, pale yellow in color: its fruits, which come in large numbers. are the shape and size of a large Spanish olive, with a rough, uneven skin; they contain black, flat, almost oval, shiny seeds in three separate chambers. This plant grows naturally in our southern provinces, and is just as easy to find here in any garden where it is sown, both for utility and pleasure; in the latter respect, here is its small merit. When the fruit is ripe, which can be seen by its color, which is yellowing a little, whoever wants to enjoy it gently cuts it with the tail to present it to another, under some imagined pretext, and by presenting it on the side of the tail, and pressing it a little between the fingers opposite the one to whom it is wanted, the tail leaves, and the liquor, as well as the seed that is inside, come out with impetuosity, and rushes to the face up to four paces : we laugh for a moment, and the niece ends there. With regard to its properties, it is a plant of which all the parts are extremely purgative, especially the fruit; it is with its juice that one composes this famous elaterion of the ancients, of which the moderns also recognized the merit; it is employed particularly in old diseases, when there are inveterate obstructions to be removed, of which the ordinary purgatives cannot triumph, or verminous matters to be destroyed; the dose is from twelve to fifteen grains: one finds it at all the apothecaries. Honey in which this fruit has been boiled is given in an ounce or two at most as an enema for B 3 people prone to vapors, and for those who are not regulated. Let me continue with the species suitable for life. The first three do not differ in taste, qualities or properties: they are widely used for life in their season. The common man does not, however, pay the same attention to them as to those of a delicate nature, for the reason that they require a somewhat elaborate seasoning, which is not available in all conditions, and that they are perfectly good only with meats, whether fine or common; but, when they have passed through the hands of a good cook, they are well worthy of the attention of people of taste; and moreover, they are healthy and refreshing in themselves. Some modern authors, no doubt working on ancient and false traditions, have accused it of having several vicious, even poisonous qualities: it may well have had them in its origin; but this plant, like many others, having come to naturalize in this climate, which has proved favorable to it, has undoubtedly lost its bad qualities, assuming them to be true; and the fruit is currently recognized as good by everyone, except for cold stomachs, because of its cold quality. It is eaten raw and cooked in twenty different ways, both fatty and lean: We garnish soups with them, and when they're stuffed, the stuffing lifts them up a lot. We put them under chickens or poulardes, and it's a distinguished stew; we put them again under roasted meats, after having cooked and drained them. They are chopped with various meats, as well as with fish, and made into a very delicate stuffing; they are fried in a pan with butter and onion; they are also prepared in a saucepan with cream, parsley and cboule; finally, cooks take particular pleasure in varying the taste and seasoning of this vegetable, which is tasted and desired at the best tables. To eat it raw in a salad, there are two different ways of preparing it: some, after having mixed and sliced it, sprinkle it with coarse salt between two dishes, and stir it from time to time until the watery liquor has run off: they then season it with vinegar, oil and pepper: others cut the fruit into slices, and leave it overnight between two dishes; the next day they press it between their hands to draw out the juice; and, when they want to eat it, they pour oil and vinegar over it, with pepper and salt, and B 4 a few finely chopped herbs. Either way, they taste very good, and some authors claim that they are good for the stomach; but others claim, with more plausibility, that they are very indigestible, when eaten raw as I have just said. The use of cucumber is particularly favorable to hot temperaments whose viscera are overheated: it is its essential property to cool in kidney and bladder diseases, and especially in calculus, where its use is highly recommended; but, in these kinds of cases, it must be cooked. Its pulp, crushed and cooled in ice and applied to a freshly shaved head, is, according to two leading physicians, a specific remedy for frenzy. Its seed is one of the 4 major cold seeds so frequently used in emulsions, and is an ingredient in orgeat. The two early species are the ones that attract the attention of gardeners, and that everyone is eager to have early; their cultivation is difficult: but the other two, which I'll talk about next, are easy and inexpensive to grow. They are both easy to grow, and require great care, as no other plant is so delicate in its birth: they are sown on a bed in November or December; four inches of compost are enough for the seed, which can be mixed on the same bed with the other plants for which this dose is sufficient. Fifteen or twenty seeds can be placed under each bell; some people make holes with their finger and place two seeds in each hole, which they cover very lightly or not at all; others, after arranging their seed at equal distances, push it in with the tip of their finger, and leave the holes open to the air. Either way is arbitrary: I've tried them both, and have found no difference in effect; the only thing to note is that the seedbed must be warm, and the soil fine. As soon as the seed is sown, the bells are lowered, and even bounded if the weather requires it: they are covered overnight, and the same care is given as for melons; I will therefore omit this detail of conduct, which can be found in the melon chapter. Three weeks or a month after sowing, they should be ready to be planted out on a new bed, and only six should be placed under each bell, so that they can get the most out of it: they are strengthened on this second bed, and provided that it is warmed exactly, and that the plant is given as much air as the weather will allow, it is ready a month later to be planted out on a third bed, to remain there. This last layer should be loaded with seven to eight inches of topsoil, and the finest manure should be kept to form the last bed, which will do much to nourish the fruit. We space them two feet apart in a chessboard pattern, like melons, and choose the lightest bells, which helps to bring them forward. Five or six days later, if the feet are strong enough, we make the first pruning, i.e. we cut off the ears, and pinch the stem above the second leaf, or, to put it better, we cut it off: if they are still too weak, we wait to do it until they have strengthened a little. The heat of the layer is maintained by means of stoves, which are loaded as often as needed. We cover and uncover it according to the weather, and let the plant enjoy the sight of the sun as often as it appears; for air is as necessary to it as heat, and when it lacks one or the other, we soon see it melt. Excessive heat is equally pernicious to it, so too much care must be taken to keep it at the right temperature. It is not usual for this plant to need water until the fruit is well established, but this can sometimes happen when the soil or manure used is too dry. In this case, which can easily be seen from the length of the plants, they should be given a light wetting after the water has been warmed in the sun; or, if the sun is not available, a pint or two of boiling water can be thrown over a jug of natural water, to correct its coldness and crudeness. As soon as the stalks begin to make their arms, they should be stopped at two eyes; and when the second branches show fruit, they should be pinched at one eye above, and always re-pruned in the same way, as more grow. You must also avoid confusing the branches, especially as the leaves take up a lot of space, and you must cut off the gourmands exactly; you can also remove some of the leaves, when they cause too much embarrassment and shade the fruit, and consume too much sap, especially those called hard leaves. B 6 As soon as the first fruits appear, begin to give the bells a little air, and gradually accustom them to this; when the arms ask to be extended, let them come out freely; but, if you still fear some frost, be prepared to cover the bed with straw mats by means of a trellis that supports them. Once April arrives, the fruit should begin to strengthen, if it comes from the first seeds of November and December; and, as in this month we begin to have hot days, it is then advisable to wet it copiously as needed; for this plant likes water as much as melons like it little; it also needs to be pruned regularly, and when it is well managed in all respects, it gives fruit for two or three months. The first ones should be good to cut at the beginning of May; I have grown some myself under frames, which were good at the end of April. It's also true that, regardless of the care taken, if the years aren't a little favorable, the fruit is much delayed; by comparing, for example, the winter of 1740, whose rigors were felt until May, with the winter we've just had, it's easy to understand the difference the plants experience. Cucumbers are not like melons, which have a specific point of maturity; they are good at any age, and can be cut as soon as they are two inches in size. In England and Holland, this fruit is harvested much earlier than is common in France, and its duration is extended by three months more; as early as the beginning of April, they begin to enjoy them, and they grow them successively until Christmas. Their method for bringing them in so early, is to sow them in the first days of October in the open ground, and they are careful to cover them during the nights and on bad weather days: the following month they replant them, each plant separately, in a small pot which they place on a warm bed, covered by canopies or frames; they warm it exactly as needed, and give the plant all the care it requires: this plant is strong when the big frosts arrive; and M. Bradley, a famous author, assures us from his own experience that the plant, fortified in this way, is much more resistant to the rigors of winter, flowering in February and bearing fruit at the beginning of April. I haven't tried this method, but I think it's a good one, and there's no doubt that it would be easier to succeed in France than in England, where the climate is much colder, if one wanted to take the trouble. The reason for this is that the artist is not sufficiently rewarded for the fruits of long and assiduous labor; the genius of the nation in general, applied to other objects, is not turned to these small marvels of art, like that of the English, Dutch and Germans, who all surpass us in this genre: in two words, the same emulation is not to be found here; and this is not the only part of agriculture that is neglected. It is to be hoped that we will acquire a greater taste for it, and that the Great, who are the soul of a State and the motive of all the arts, will motivate the worker to make it flourish, since it is true to say (leaving aside other reasons of State policy) that the productions of nature, entrusted by the Creator to the industry of man for his use, are the most precious gift He has given him, and consequently the most worthy of his attachment. This little digression is not superfluous in the context of the subject I am dealing with, and it is better to place it here than elsewhere; for the small number of curious people and amateurs who are to be found in France, moan daily about the indifference of the bulk of the nation to all that is called the study and cultivation of plants; and it is a kind of shame for them that, with a marvellous soil and a favourable sun, we are deprived of a thousand small goods which our neighbors, in an ungrateful position, enjoy rather more abundantly than we do. However, it is not the worker who is guilty of this; if we know him at all, he will never be accused of incapacity or lack of activity; but what use is it to him to lavish his pains and his substance, if no one is willing to put a price on the fruits of his labor? It is up to those at the head of the arts to encourage it, and to inspire a taste for these novelties in the rich people who can consume them. Back to my subject. We have just seen what is done in England to accelerate the ripening of the fruit I am dealing with. All that remains for me to do is to explain how to extend its life until Christmas. Nothing could be simpler: we sow them at the beginning of July on a bed made of dry manure and fresh litter mixed together, which we load with ten inches of soil without any other precaution, because the season requires none. The following November, as the nights become cooler and the white frosts begin, the plant is covered with cloches or frames, and if necessary, straw mats are thrown over it: the plant begins to produce at the end of the same month, and being well cared for, continues to give fruit until the big frosts. I believe, although the English author doesn't mention it, that the bed may need some heating around All Saints' Day, to maintain the action of this plant, which is extremely afraid of the cold; that's what we'll be able to test, if we want to try the effect. I'll now move on to the cultivation of the two other species of cucumber I'd like to talk about. The ordinary cucumber, which we call the late variety, differs from the two early varieties only in size, which is such that we commonly see cucumbers a foot long and three inches across; its flesh is the same, but it fruits later. They can be raised on a bed to be enjoyed earlier, and when they are ready for replanting at the end of April or beginning of May, they are placed in the ground after preparing the site: We usually know how to use muted beds, on which we plant them in a chessboard pattern, like the others, two feet apart; others are content to make one-foot quarried holes, which they fill with consumed manure, with two joints of potting soil on top: they do almost equally well in either way. They are kept covered for a few days, until they are well established; they are then left in the open air. Those who have not taken the precaution of raising them on a bed, sow them in place in mid-April, in holes laid out as I have just said, and they come up in the same way, but later. When you have bells to cover them until the end of May, it is a safety against the frosts of that month, which often kill them; But after this month, they only need to be moistened abundantly, and often pruned, as I explained for the early ones, and in this situation they yield much more than on bedding. It is even claimed that yellow is nothing other than degenerated white: I'm not talking about a third species, green, which is not esteemed, even though it tastes the same. Particular care must be taken to cut the fruit as soon as it is good, as it not only hardens on the vine, but also prevents the young from enjoying it: when cut and closed in a cool place, it keeps even better and lasts longer. When a counterfeit one is spotted, it must be removed; a better-made one benefits from the food. When you want to grow them to make gherkins that can be eaten all winter as a salad, you should only sow them at the end of May in the open ground; they are good for cutting in September, when they are the size of a little finger. This plant is subject to a disease known as miller's disease or white rot, because its leaves flourish and, as they perish, abort the fruit. The only remedy is to cut off all infected leaves; and the best way to prevent the disease, which is better than the remedy, is to cover the beds with straw mats or heavy bedding in cold weather, especially at night. The black cucumber is cultivated like the previous one, but it is not sown in place: it must be raised worm-on-bed, and then replanted in well-smoked soil; its fruit is of a quality quite different from the others; its flesh is neither watery, nor so melting, and its taste approaches that of the chestnut: I find it in my particular bland, pasty, very-inferior to the others; however it has its amateurs. It produces a lot of fruit, and can be stored well into winter, as it is closed off from the air; these two qualities are what make it the most popular: the seed is removed as the fruit is eaten, and can be stored for a long time. The seed is removed as the fruit is consumed; it keeps for a long time. For the first three species, a certain quantity is marked in proportion to need, and left standing until it begins to rot; the seed is then removed, washed in several waters to remove the silt, and left to dry for a few days before being locked up: it keeps well for seven or eight years. Before concluding this article, I will explain how to make gherkins, for the benefit of those who like them and are sure of their digestibility. Choose the smallest or whitest ones; burst and cut off the tail, and put them in a white cloth, rubbing them against each other, to strip them of their down: then blanch them, i.e., throw them into boiling water, for the space of four minutes and immediately put them back into fresh water; remove them, and let them drain on a white cloth: then put them back into the vessel in which you want them to remain, either in a jug or a bottle, arrange them in it as best you can, intermingling a few bay leaves and a few long peppers, and pour white vinegar over them to the surface, after boiling it a little in a pan; then add about an ounce of salt to each pint of vinegar, and cover the vessel with double parchment tied around it. Eight days later they will be good to eat, and will keep from one year to the next; you can still add a handful of percepierre, tarragon or purslane chops, if that's your taste. CHAPTER XXXI. "Description of CORIANDE; its properties, cultivation, etc. Although JUOIQUON makes no use of this plant in food, as it is useful for the pleasures of the senses and the relief of the body in many illnesses, I believe it may deserve a place in a regularly stocked vegetable garden. Its root is small, white and lined with a few fibers; its stem is simple, cylindrical, smooth and soft, rising to eighteen or twenty inches; its lower leaves are rounded and toothed, while the upper ones are more deeply cut and divided into very narrow strips; Its flowers are parasol-shaped at the top of the branches, shaped like roses, with five megaloid, indented, purplish-white petals, and a calyx that changes into two seeds which, when joined together, form a whole sphere, first green, then pale yellow. The odor of the whole plant, but particularly of the fresh seeds, is strong and stenchy; however, it softens as it dries, and the seeds acquire a sweet, pleasant flavor; these seeds are gray, round and pea-sized. They are used for the same purposes as aniseed, with which they are mixed, or substituted when it is lacking; it is stomachic, carminative and antivenomous. What I've said about the virtues of aniseed should also be said about this seed, and I won't repeat it here. I'll just add that its green leaf, cooked with a mound of bread, is good for dissipating inflamed tumors. The seed is also used to make small sugared almonds, which provoke the winds as well as flattering the taste; it is also used as a base for a liqueur, called coriander water for this reason, which is very pleasant to drink and quite beneficial; brewers also add a few kernels to beer, and it gives it a good taste. This plant grows easily in all kinds of prepared soil, and the countryside is covered with it around Paris, near Aubervilliers. The seed is sown in March, and the plant is weeded six weeks later, if weeds take over; but this can usually be dispensed with: there is no other way to care for it, only that there should be no thunderstorms at flowering time, as lightning will ruin it. The seed is best harvested at the end of August: the whole plant is uprooted or cut off; and, as the seed detaches itself at the slightest shake, to avoid losing as much as you would take out, you should have four sheets sewn together in the field, on which it is placed as soon as it is cut, and immediately beaten. A second precaution, equally useful, is to harvest in the morning at dew. The seed remains good to sow for two years. CHAPTER XXXII. Description of CORN-DE-CERF; its properties, cultivation, etc. THIS plant is annual; its root is woody, single, with a few fibers; its leaves emerge from the heart at ground level, eight to ten inches long, narrow, deeply and unevenly cut as they approach their tips, a little hairy, quite dark green; around the heart there are also a few spikes, borne on a round, slender tail, similar to those of the plantain, which bloom imperceptibly : each flower is borne on a small calyx surmounted by a few stamens with no visible petals: these stamens are followed by the seed, which is extremely small, more oval than round, and brown in color. The leaf is used to make salad, and has no other known use or property for life: in fact, it is rarely used today. It is useful in medicine, being astringent through the belly, and aperitive through the urine, vulnerary, and suitable for stopping stomach courses and haemorrhages; it is also good for renal colic, for urine retention, for alleviating stone; for digging up and consolidating wounds. It is sown in March, as sparsely as possible; despite this, there is always too much: two months later, you can start using it, and as you cut its leaves, new ones grow: it should be weeded and watered when young, and placed in loose soil; it requires no further care: its seed ripens in August, and keeps well for two or three years. CHAPTER XXXIII. Description of the gourd, its different species, properties, cultivation, etc. 11 THE gourd is an annual plant of which there are three species: the long gourd, the large gourd and the calabash. The first has a sinewy, white root, divided into several fine fibers; its stems are sarmentose, as big as a finger, two or three toises long, and creeping when not shaken to make them rise; its leaves are round and large, cottony and crenellated at the edges, borne on cylindrical, oblong and concave tails; its flowers emerge from the leaf axils, bell-shaped, white in color, flared and so cut out that they appear to be composed of five distinct petals, hairy on the inside, and trimmed on the outside with a short down; some are sterile, being borne on no embryo; the others are fertile, and supported by embryos that develop into cylindrical fruits up to four feet long; the size of the fruit on the tail side approaches that of the leg, but increases greatly as it grows towards the tip: its bark is thin and whitish-green when young; as it dries, it takes on a yellow color, and hardens like wood. Its flesh is white, bland and a little spongy, usually divided into six quarters filled with compartments, which contain long, flattened seeds, broad at one end and narrowed at the germ end, enclosing a soft, pleasant-tasting white almond under a hard skin. Its flesh is refreshing, but rather insipid; however, in many proTome II. C vinces it is eaten with pleasure, cooked with soup stock; it does not melt like pumpkin, but is nevertheless very tender: it is also made into a highly prized jam: these are its two main properties. Its use is good for hot temperaments, but pernicious for the opposite: it is also used to quench thirst, by drinking the water in which it is infused. Its seed is its essential merit; it is one of the four major cold seeds used in emulsions, for all illnesses resulting from blood acrimony; it also enters into the composition of orgeat, the recipe for which I gave in the Pumpkin article. Its green leaf, applied to the udders of new mothers, causes them to lose milk, according to a great author. Water distilled from the fruit before it ripens is suitable for external inflammations of the eyes, ears and gout; and taken internally, it soothes the body's great heats: its juice, by expression, has the same effect. Its crushed flesh, when raw and applied to burns, heals them and soothes inflammation. Its cultivation is the same as that of pumpkins, with the difference that it must be transplanted to the foot of a south-facing wall, or to the foot of a few strong, high poles, to which it can cling by means of its maidens; for this fruit wants to have air all around it: it comes out longer and more united, and is not subject to spoilage, nor to being pierced by insects, as when it lies on the ground. This species differs from all pumpkins in that it is good to eat only when young, whereas the others need to be well matured and even kept for some time. The second species differs from the first in that its fruit is round and pot-bellied, and grows considerably; it is used for the same purposes. The third species, called calabash or flask, differs from the other two by the shape of its fruit, which is bottle-shaped, with a narrow neck and a large belly; its seed is also browner. This species is particularly used to make flasks for holding wine or water, or whatever else is deemed appropriate; gardeners use them in various places to hold their seeds. The fruit is left to dry well, and then emptied, raking it as well as possible. A small tool like a rake would be very useful for this. I know of a fourth species, whose skin is a coarse green-black, striped with a yellowish-white C 2; but it is considered more of a coloquita than a squash, and I have not heard of any use being made of it. CHAPTER XXXIV. Description of CRAN; its properties, cultivation, etc. THIS plant, also known as horseradish or wild horseradish, is perennial. Its root is long and thick, creeping, white, with a pungent, burning taste; its leaves are fifteen to eighteen inches long, broad in proportion, pointed, a beautiful glossy green, a little blistered, rough and toothed at the edges; sometimes it bears a stem, and often it bears none: This stem rises to a foot and a half or two feet; it is straight, firm, fluted, hollow, and bears small white flowers with four petals, arranged crosswise, followed by small, almost round fruits, which contain the seed. The root, which is the only part of the plant used, stimulates the appetite, and is eaten with meat instead of mustard: when freshly grated, it tastes almost the same, which is why it's called German mustard. It's also eaten with fresh butter, which is made into toast; it's the usual Flemish breakfast. It's a useful resource in remote rural areas, where mustard isn't always on hand; I say useful for those whose taste isn't sufficiently piqued by natural meats. Its medicinal virtues are stomatal, pecteral and anti-scorbutic: it is cut into wheels and infused for twelve hours in a decoction of barley, or boiled to make a herbal tea; the dose is one ounce in a pint of water: scorbutics are greatly relieved by it, and it is one of the greatest remedies for this ailment. Phthisics are also made to drink the milk in which this root has been boiled, and hydropics the white wine in which it has been infused; asthmatics also receive relief from it, and it also has the virtue of stimulating urination. This plant grows naturally along streams, rivers, ponds and damp meadows. It is also cultivated in gardens, and its virtues make it well deserving of a place there. Some claim that there are two species, one larger than the other; but, after 2 giving it enough attention, I attribute the alleged difference only to the quality of the soil, more or less oily, which makes the plant stronger or weaker. It multiplies in three ways: either from seeds sown in spring, or from offshoots separated from old plants, or from the plant itself, which is cut three rows thick and placed back in the ground; but this must be done at the same time as the plant is uprooted, and in a season when the sap is in action: each root forms a root which forms a plant just like the offshoots. Most roots cut in the same way produce the same effect; it's an experiment that's easy to do, and proves that the same plant contains many germs in its substance, as well as in its seed. It must be renewed every year, so as to always have one that follows the other in the degree of goodness it should have; for its root becomes hard and cordate from the third year onwards; so that it is only perfectly good during the second, (I mean for the use of life), needing the first to form. There's nothing special about growing it; just make sure you place it in the freshest soil you can find, and in a slightly shady spot. Description of CRESSON; its different species, properties, cultivation, etc. Cress is an annual plant used in salad supplies; it has no other use in life, but it has great health benefits. There are three very different species, each with its own foliage, but all tasting more or less the same: common, curly and golden. The common cress, better known as cresson Alenois, has a fairly small leaf, cut deeply like parsley, of a tender green color and a pleasant taste; When it goes to seed, it sprouts a stalk eighteen inches or two feet high, all the branches of which are covered with small white flowers, arranged in a cross, which bloom one after the other, followed by a small, round, flattened capsule enclosing the seed, from which it easily emerges; this seed is dark yellow-orange in color, flat, long, small and hot-tasting. Curly cress differs in its leaf, which C4 is curly and curled like brown cabbage, quite large and dark green; it resembles the previous variety in all other respects. The golden cress has an oblong leaf, about the width of a finger, unevenly cut at the edges, and of a golden yellow, from which it takes its name: its stem is stalked, and its flower white as in the others; but its seed is half as small, and of a lighter color. The leaves and seeds of these three species are used medicinally: both are hot and desiccating, attenuating, aperitive and abstersive; the main use is for swollen spleen, scurvy and mucilaginous tartar of the lungs; the seed brings out measles: it is ground with sain-doux, to rub and heal the head and other scabby parts. The whole plant is specific against worms, especially those of the pericardium. These three species of cress multiply only from seed, which can be sown all year round, either on the bed or on the ground; it is mixed on the bed with all the small seeds, and sprouts after three days: eight days later you begin to cut its leaf, and it gives all the more pleasure, the smaller it is: you must therefore sow a little every two weeks. It requires no special care: four inches of potting soil are enough for this plant. After the months of rigor, it no longer needs the bed, and can be sown in the open ground by the rays; but during hot spells, it should be sown in the shade, and moistened often; it requires loose, slightly loamy soil. If sown in March, it will be ripe in June, and the plants are removed as soon as the first capsules begin to open. It is left to dry for a few days, then threshed and locked up immediately. It keeps well for two years. There is a fourth species of cress that grows naturally in fountains, wherever there is flowing water; I will describe its properties, although it is not one of the cultivated plants: the resemblance it has with the other species authorizes me to do so, all the more so as in large parks, it is quite common to see it around canals and springs. This plant forms several low, creeping stems, which feed in the water and swim on the surface. Its roots are white, numerous and very slender; its stems grow to about a foot; they are curved, fairly large, hollow, twiggy and green, sometimes turning a little red; its leaves are almost round, arranged in rows with several on a rib ending in an odd one, always green, fragrant, with a pleasantly pungent taste; its flowers are born at the ends of the stems and branches, small, white and arranged crosswise. The flower is followed by a silique which divides into two chambers filled with almost round, small, reddish seeds that are pungent to the taste. This species, known as watercress, not only has all the virtues of the others, but also has them in a higher degree, and has been perfectly tested; it is aperitive, diuretic, anti-scorbutic, stomatal and hysteric. A handful of its leaves and stems are added to aperitif broths of crayfish and hepatic herbs, which purify the blood and make it fluid; it also relieves hydropics and hypochondriacs, and diverts soporous affections. Its leaf, boiled in milk, is excellent for ailing breasts (I mean the beverage): taken as a decoction, it is used to purify the blood; at the same time, it is detersive and incisive; it restores menstruation and expectoration; it is the specific remedy for scurvy; it cures grátelle, by rubbing with the leaf: its juice wilts polyps in the nose and makes them fall off, provided they are washed off often; the same juice held in the mouth relieves tongue paralysis, awakens the mind and senses, and makes you sneeze; put in the ear, it soothes tooth pain when it comes from a cold cause. The leaf applied as a poultice, and mixed with sourdough, resolves boils and all kinds of apostumes; it likewise relieves sciatica drops, applied in the same way to the painful part. With so many health-giving properties, this plant also has the advantage of serving as a food: it is eaten with pleasure in salads; it is served under roast poultry, and is no less flattering to the taste: finally, it is well worth the effort of those who have it at home or in the vicinity, to see to its preservation, by ridding it of the weeds that choke it in the places assigned to it by nature, and by preventing cattle from trampling it underfoot when they go out to graze. It should be noted that there is another species of grass around fountains which is quite similar to watercress, and no less so; the leaf, although shaped in much the same way, is pointed and larger. The cress that grows at Cailli in Normandy is particularly prized: the species is smaller, more tender, and has a finer taste than that found near Paris. Those who find themselves in dry, arid countries, far from any stream or fonC6 taine where this plant grows, and who are in need of it at the moment for some illness, can grow a few plants at home, and here's how. Make one or more tubs about two feet in diameter and five to six inches high, with holes drilled in the bottom flush with the bottom on one side; fill them halfway with soil, and plant in them two or three feet of watercress, which you send to be dug up in the nearest place: as soon as it is planted, we flood it with water, so that it exceeds the surface of the earth by a good inch, and leave it in this state: it takes root again and grows as in a fountain, if we observe two things: 1°. always maintain this inch of water on the surface; 2°, change the water from time to time, as it could become corrupted by too long a stay in the heat: simply unplug the hole, and it will run out; and, when it has run out, replace the plug, then pour in new water. When frost approaches, these tubs are locked up, and given as much air as the weather will allow. Description of the ECHALOTE; its different species, properties, cultivation, etc... The ECHALOTE is a species of onion; or, to put it another way, it is an assembly of several bulbs joined together, a little larger than a filbert, and carried on a bundle of fibrous roots; these bulbs are aligned, pale red on the outside, and white on the inside, having a taste and odor approaching that of garlic, but less strong and more pleasant. Its leaves are numerous, long, small, cylindrical and fistulous, with the same taste and odor as the bulb: it has neither stems nor flowers, at least I've never seen any; and I think Mr. Lémery was quite mistaken in this respect in the description he gave. There are two species, the common and the large, which resemble each other in all the points I have just mentioned, except for their size. The bulb of the latter is once as large as that of the former, and its leaves are almost as strong as those of the chive: we can see from this that it is of better value than the other; but it is still only in the hands of a few curious people, and I cannot say where it came from; we must be eager to know and multiply it. This plant, by which I mean the bulb, is of great use in all types of cooking; it stimulates the appetite and pleasantly piques the taste; it is mixed into most sauces, both fatty and lean, and goes particularly well with oil and vinegar: it leaves no taste after it like onion and garlic, when it has been eaten; and this is why it appeals better to many people. The leaf is not used. Its bulb is highly aperitive; it is sometimes used medicinally for esquinancies, and its use is good for those with pits and who are subject to urine retention: it is also claimed that when crushed and applied to the bites of rabid dogs, it prevents the unfortunate consequences. It is also good for keeping out bad air, and is used in place of garlic. This plant multiplies from its tubers, which are separated like those of garlic, and are planted at the beginning of March in borders or beds, four inches apart in all directions, taking care not to bury them more than level with the surface of the soil. Four days later, they sprout, and from May onwards, you start using them; but to keep them, you have to wait to pull them up until the tops are completely dry, which is usually at the end of June; they keep perfectly well throughout the winter, provided you let them dry out well before storing them, and keep them in a dry place. Cultivation requires no special care; many gardeners plant them around onion beds, and they do very well in this arrangement, provided the soil is suitable; for I would observe in this respect that it does not succeed in any kind of soil, being very often prone to clouding and rotting in the foot; and I don't know precisely where this depends, or which type of soil is more favorable to it; for I have more than once seen it succeed and fail alternately in light soils, and have the same fate in other, fatter soils. I believe its success depends to a greater or lesser extent on the weather. Everyone will have to find the best place for it in their own soil, and to be on the safe side, you can put it in different places: if it doesn't do well in one, it may do better in another. Moreover, when planting them, care should be taken to choose the most rounded and elongated tubers; the bulb will be more beautiful as a result. CHAPTER XXXVII. Description of the EPINARD; its different species, properties, s4 culture, etc. This plant is an annual; there are two species: the common one, otherwise known as male spinach, and the one known as the large species. Some would like to count a third, which they call female spinach; but they are wrong to consider it a species, since all its flowers are sterile, and it is a degeneration of the other two species, among which this one occurs in all seeds. The root of the common spinach is small, white, simple, with few fibers; its stem is two feet or more high, hollow, cylindrical, fluted, and divided into several branches. Its ferns are almost similar to those of the arroche, borne on long tails; those at the bottom of the stems are sometimes cut on both sides at their base, ending in a point; and those at the top of the stem have only two extensions and are like ears at their base. This plant bears no flowers, but only green fruits, loaded with four whitish filaments; the embryos are born from the leaf axils at the ends of the branches, in clusters piled one on top of the other, which change into a fruit the size of a vetch seed, round, flattened and smooth, ash-colored as it ripens, and filled with a pear-shaped seed; others have it much more flattened, almost triangular and prickly. This difference between the seeds of one species and those of another authorizes some authors to make two different species; but it should be noted that this is the only difference in the plant, and that it is not worth dividing it into two species: moreover, I've found that when I sow prickly seed, I sometimes get plants that make the seed round; and similarly, when I sow round seed, I get plants that make it prickly: so it seems to me that this must be considered simply as a variety and a play of nature. The spinach of the large species differs from the first, in that the plant is much stronger and higher, and the leaves larger, rounder and thicker. Its seed is round like the other, of the same size and color. The first species is more delicate to the taste of some; the second provides more, and according to others, has a more decisive taste; it also withstands winter better, and melts less in the fire: everyone can be satisfied. It can be used for both fatty and lean varieties, but the method of preparation is always the same: first, all the leaves are peeled and washed thoroughly; then they are thrown into boiling water, where they are cushioned, and removed after half an hour to drain. The most common practice is to chop spinach when it is cooked; but good cooks do not do this, and apprehend it as it comes out of the water, having recognized that in this operation it loses most of its juice and taste, so that all that remains is the pomace. The method used by some others is to cook them without water, as they yield enough of their own, and it's true that they taste much better; but you don't want to start with too much fire, which would burn them. In some countries, they are eaten raw in salads, when they are young and tender. Finally, authors unanimously agree that, the way they are used, they are a healthy food, albeit not very nourishing. This dish is used for entremets at the most delicate tables, either with juice or cream, and is always the first dish eaten: simply prepared with butter, and seasoned with onion and spices, it is generally appreciated: others add cream, with a lemon peel and a few drops of orange blossom: under sirloin and leg of lamb, there's no grass that's so delicate, and that takes the juices of meats better; we still make excellent pies with it: it's also used in stuffings, with sorrel, to soften its acidity, as well as in soup. The water in which they are cooked is particularly good for degreasing and cleaning iron, especially roasting spits. Its medicinal properties include softening and loosening the belly, and easing coughs and acridity of the chest. The water distilled from its leaves is also sovereign in soothing the heat of the bowels, and the ardors of a stomach irritated by inflamed bile; its leaves are also used in emollient decocttons and poultices. Asthmatics receive great relief, and are sometimes cured radically, by the frequent use of spinach boiled with veal; and this was the only remedy used by M. Fagon, first physician to Louis XIV, who was greatly troubled by this ailment. Applied externally to the stomach and liver, they dispel inflammation and pain. According to a modern author, too frequent use of this plant makes the blood melancholy; but M. Fagon's experience seems to destroy this opinion. The two species I have mentioned can be cultivated in exactly the same way, and can be sown for a full eight months, from the end of February to October: you need to choose the best, most well-amended soil you have, and top-dress the seed when you can. Those to be sown over winter are broadcast, but those to be eaten in summer are best sown by rays, as they are easier to crimp and cut. The first crop, sown to overwinter, is sown in mid-August, and begins to be used in the first days of October: the second is sown in mid-September, and the spinach is hardly cut before winter: the third is sown in the first days of October; and these, which remain small, are used to succeed the first after winter. We start again at the end of February or in March, and these new seedlings are eaten after the last ones have overwintered. We cut them as soon as they are strong enough; they only need this cut, because afterwards they go to seed, as well as those from the autumn seeds: a fortnight later, we sow a few more which have the same fate: we continue from fortnight to fortnight throughout the summer, when we like this vegetable so much that we can't get enough of it, as is the taste of many people, so that we can eat them all year round, except in times of frost; still, with the precaution of covering them, or cutting some to lock up in the greenhouse when bad weather approaches, where they keep for three weeks, stirring them from time to time, we can have them without interruption. It should be noted that the seed of common spinach is much quicker to set than that of the larger species, which sometimes remains in the ground for two or three weeks. The only cultivation this plant requires after being sown, harrowed and walked on, is to be weeded exactly and wetted if necessary during the summer, otherwise it yellows and rises out of the ground; if it can be put in the shade in very hot weather, the leaf comes out greener and more beautiful. When sowing by rays, you can do as I said for carrots, and even wet the seed as soon as it is sown, and cover the rays the next day; this is the method used by our market gardeners. At the start of spring, those that have spent the winter should be cleaned of all the leaves that winter has spoiled; the heart grows back instead, and the earth, freed of all the garbage that covers it, is more easily put in the right mood; weed them at the same time. In winter and spring, no tools should be used to pick them: they are plucked by hand, flush to the ground with the tail; but in summer this subjection would be unnecessary, since they are only cut once. In some provinces, the whole stalk is cut off: but this method is not a good one, because, in addition to the fact that they cannot grow back, the heart, which is harder than the leaf, is always felt when they are eaten, and is not clean on a well-served table. We keep as many of the first ones sown during the winter as we see fit for seed; they yield more than the last ones, and the seed is rather ripe. As the stem rises very high, by which I mean the larger species, it's a good idea to support it with a few laths, and to defend it as best you can from birds, which are very greedy for it, as well as field mice. The seed of those we sow in spring, although less well nourished, will also serve to make up for the lack of those whose feet have spent the winter, but they are unlikely to provide as much. Finally, the stems are pulled out or cut when they start to turn yellow, without waiting for them to dry out completely; the seed finishes ripening in the sun, spread out on a sheet, and keeps well for three years. Sterile plants, as I said, can be found mixed in with fertile ones; these so-called female plants can be easily distinguished when the plant is raised, in that it is weaker and more yellow, and instead of seeds, it bears groups of small petalless flowers all along its branches, which are, strictly speaking, nothing more than stamens that dissipate and turn to dust as soon as they are touched. Care must be taken to uproot all these false feet as they become known, especially as they are unfruitful and harmful to the good ones. As soon as the seed is dry, it should be threshed and sealed. The common species is of two kinds as regards the seed, as I have said; one makes it round and smooth, the other triangular and prickly; and as the latter is very awkward to handle, both to clean and to sow, the former is rightly preferred. A new species of spinach has recently arrived from America, under the name of basella, which the Americans are making great use of; but it will still be some time before it can be widely grown. I saw it in the King's Garden, and perhaps it is only there: but, as far as I can judge, it will not be very difficult to multiply it with a little effort; and I can say that it is well worth the effort. There are two kinds, differing from each other only in the color of the leaves, one green and the other reddish like beet, with thick purple-red veins, and the stem the same. The stems are heart-shaped, seven to eight inches long at the bottom of the & plant, by four wide, united at the edges, smooth and glossy, very thick: the stem is very thick towards the base, and grows a number of rather loose threads, almost similar to those of beans, which rise up to six feet, and turn around the oars that are placed on them, with a little help: these threads or branches are covered with leaves throughout, and together make a gap of two feet or more in all directions. Flowers appear in great numbers all along the nets, arranged in clusters and borne on short, thick pedicels; but the term "flower" is a misnomer, for they are, strictly speaking, nothing more than small buds which are rose-colored on the surface, without any noticeable petals or stamens, and which change into a soft fruit or berry, the size of a pea, split at the head which is green at first, and which, as it ripens, becomes a black violet. These berries are placed unevenly along the tails where they are born, and each contains a hard seed the size of a peppercorn, which is the seed of the plant. Up to this point, we at the Jardin du Roi had been content to give it the necessary care to preserve it, observing the degree of heat and humidity it could require; we had succeeded perfectly, not only in preserving it, but also in raising it from its own seed: we Tome II. D apparently did not presume that this plant contained so many good qualities, nor that it could multiply in all hands, and we had not pushed our research any further; we had not even had the curiosity to taste it. Knowing it at first sight, I asked for a handful of its leaves, which they were kind enough to give me for testing, and the same day it was done. I had them cooked and prepared in the same way as our ordinary spinach, with the difference that I had the sides of the leaves removed, just as they are removed from perry: I then tasted them, along with three or four other people capable of judging them, and we unanimously agreed that their taste was superior to ours, with none of the pungency we are obliged to correct with sugar or cream; on the contrary, they are sweet on their own, with the advantage of being fleshier and melting much less. All this has given me a strong desire to multiply the species, and I hope to succeed; the difficulty is that it cannot spend the winter in the ground, and that it can only give its seed in the second year, from which it follows that it must be preserved for a winter. Experience has shown that it perishes in an ordinary greenhouse, and that it necessarily needs a warm greenhouse; but I think that, although few people have a warm greenhouse, if a few apply themselves to preserving a certain quantity of seeds, they will easily suffice to supply other curious people: besides, it's quite possible to get some from our American islands; a small quantity will serve for a long time, since a single seed, according to the calculation I made on the small quantity of leaves I picked from a single plant, should provide at different times during the year enough to make two or three good dishes; and provided you manage to get some seed, you'll be able to console yourself with seeing the plant perish at the first frosts, after having enjoyed it for six months or more. This is a particular merit that ours don't have, as they go up in the summer almost as soon as they're up. Moreover, nothing is easier than to preserve it in winter when you have the space; all you have to do is take it with you in May when you leave the bed where you sow it, in the quantity you see fit, and take care to water them as needed: the plants are not nearly as strong there as in the open ground, but they do not fail to give a large quantity of seeds. The one I'm talking about has the advantage of not being difficult to raise, and of growing surprisingly fast. I don't know whether the seed would grow in the ground, it hasn't been tested; but it's D2 certain at least that, replanted in the ground as soon as it comes out of the bed, it grows perfectly there; it's in this situation that I've seen and admired it. Everyone will be able to take advantage of this little discovery as they see fit, and in relation to the explanations I have given. CHAPTER XXXVIII. Description of the ESTRAGON; its qualities and properties, its cultivation, etc. ESTRAGON is a fragrant plant, commonly grown in all gardens, and used in salad dressings with chervil, burnet and others. It is also used in omelettes, chopped finely: a special vinegar is made simply by infusing the leaf in it; mixed with gherkins, it enhances the taste. This plant is cordial, stomatal, incisive, detersive, aperitive and sudorific; it excites urine; it drives away winds; it provokes appetite; it resists venom, and makes you spit when chewed; it is also good for scurvy: infused in boiling water like tea, with a little sugar, it is very good against stomach weakness, indigestion and the desire to vomit; but some people find it difficult to digest, and are prone to have indigestion after eating it. It multiplies equally well from seed, from rooted plants, and from cuttings; but usually a few old clumps are pulled up in spring, separated into several parts, and replanted in well-prepared beds, a foot apart: this planting is done as soon as the spikes begin to emerge from the ground, as the more advanced the spikes, the more difficult it is to regrow. This plant rises to around two feet, and produces several branches where the seed is formed, which is extremely small and preceded by a small, imperceptible flower. At this height, the leaf is too hard, and only those of the young shoots should be used. This leaf is long and narrow, light green, smooth and plain at the edges: we always tend it, taking care to cut off a certain quantity every two weeks, by the stem, which always grows back until the frosts. It should be crimped and weeded if necessary in the heat of the year: it is then cut to D3 level after All Saints' Day, and covered with an inch of compost, if available. Care must be taken not to pull out the root when cutting it, as it holds very little to the soil. It should be renewed every three years.
