There is no need to take any precautions to preserve it during the winter; it easily withstands bad weather, but its young shoots are often subject to frost at the start of spring. You need mats or dry straw to cover them when you're threatened by frost. If you want to enjoy it during the winter, replant a few feet of it on the bed at All Saints' Day, and give it the same care as the other plants that are replanted at the same time. It can be combined on the same bed with all those that are content with 6 inches of potting soil. CHAPTER XXXIX: "Description of FENNEL; its different species, properties, culling, etc. FENNEL is a species of anise, from which it differs in many ways that make it preferable. Everything about it is useful: the root, the leaf, the seed, both in food and in remedies: advantages that aniseed does not have, and whose merit lies in the seed alone. There are two species, the common and the sweet: I could name a third, of which I have recently received some seed, which I have been assured is superior to the others in fineness; but I have not yet tested it to seed. The threshold is extremely fine and delicate, and the plant is not nearly as strong as the others: I have been given the Italian name fenochio di zuecarro, sweet fennel. Common fennel is perennial, and will last for several years. Its root is straight, white, fairly coarse, with an aromatic flavor mingled with some sweetness; its stem is four to five feet high, straight, cylindrical, fluted, gnarled, smooth, covered with a thin, green bark, filled with a white, fungous pith, divided into several branches towards the top: your leaves are broad, branched, divided into narrow, cylindrical lobes, dark green, sweet-tasting, sweet-smelling; those at the tip are like hairs. These leaves are borne on tails that embrace the stems and branches in a gauntlet-like fashion: at the top of the stems and branches are large, rounded umbrellas, whose D4 FENOUIL. are pink with five fragrant yellow petals, supported by a calyx that turns into a fruit consisting of two oblong seeds, quite large, convex, fluted on one side, flattened on the other, brown in color, with a pungent, slightly strong flavor. This plant grows naturally on rocks and stones in Africa and other countries close to the Line: cultivation softens and changes it a little in more temperate climates, such as ours, and particularly in Italy and Spain, where it is much more widely cultivated than here. Sweet fennel, otherwise known as Florence fennel, has much more perfect qualities than the one I've just mentioned, and also differs in that its stem is lower and its leaves smaller; its seed, on the other hand, is three times larger, whiter and more regularly fluted: The seed, on the other hand, is three times larger, whiter and more regularly fluted; it has a whitened leaf and the stem is also more tender and sweeter; but, for the plant to reach its full perfection, it is claimed that the seed must be taken from Syria or the Azores Islands, and that the seed collected in our climates is very degenerate, which seems to be proven by all the varieties found in this species, which rarely conforms in all respects to the description I have just given: However, in Italy, where it is very successful, only the seed collected there is used, as well as in Languedoc, where it is widely cultivated; but it is true that it is not all equally good, and to get it right, one is rather embarrassed: I have tested it several times; and as a rule, one must observe that it must be of a greenish white, tending towards cinnamon; that which is whiter is degenerated. Both species are cultivated in the same way. The seed is sown in May or June, when the plant is to be blanched and eaten as a salad, and in March when the seed is to be collected. Sowing is done in rows, either in beds or in borders. The soil must be loose and well-prepared: water lightly to encourage emergence, if the weather is dry; when the plant is six weeks or two months old, thin it out and weed it. It requires little water, unless it is intended to be eaten as a stalk, in which case it is transplanted to a bed, like celery, when it is strong enough, and spaced at one foot in all directions. It is cared for in the same way, wetting it frequently and mounding it when it is as D5 FENOUIL. large as it can be; it whitens and forms a stalk much larger than celery, and much superior in quality. Its flavor, delicacy and fragrance appeal to both taste and smell. It is tender at the same time and much less indigestible than celery; in addition to being good for the stomach, it is good for many ailments, as we shall see below in detailing its virtues. The Italians make great use of it, and rightly so: they eat it in salads, or more simply with salt: they also put it in soup. The tips of young leaves are still used in their salad supplies, where they give off a pleasant smell and taste: they even eat the tips of young branches without any seasoning. It's true that, particularly in Rome, it has qualities that you wouldn't believe unless you'd eaten it on the spot. It's a great loss to us that this vegetable is almost unknown here. I agree that the climate not being so favorable to it, it would not have as many good qualities; but from the most to the least, it would always be of great merit: perhaps even that if one studied its culture with care, one would find veins of ground where it would succeed perfectly: I raised some myself which was found to be passably good, in spite of the cold and wet quality of my ground; from which I presume that in a more favorable ground it would be much better. The greatest difficulty I've found is to bleach it without it rusting or rotting, and another is to prevent it from rising. The remedy to these two inconveniences lies, I believe, in the choice of the soil and the amount of water to be given to it.

The seed ripens very well, and is used for many purposes. The seed ripens very well, and is used for many purposes; but I take no comfort in the fact that the merit of this plant for us is limited to the use of the screw; for it has many more beneficial qualities. It is aperitive, diuretic, sudorific, stomatal, pectoral and febrifuge. Its root ranks first among the five great aperitive roots. Its seed is one of the four major hot seeds, and its leaf, though less used in particular, when distilled with the whole plant, produces a water that is sovereign in certain situations. The juice of its roots, taken from three ounces up to six, is an excellent remedy for intermittent fever-quarts, being taken at the beginning of the attack: it excites sweating, and the patient must be careful to cover himself well Dó FENOUIL. after taking it. The decoction of its roots and seeds is specific against smallpox and measles, as well as in malignant and putrid fevers. No other plant, in the opinion of many great physicians, comes close to this one's virtue for opening and resolving. The seed has a particular virtue for dissipating winds and soothing colicky pains: it is boiled with herbs in decoctions to be taken as enemas; and simply chewed or powdered, it produces much the same effect. It is also a sovereign remedy for eye diseases of all kinds, including weakness, obscuration, colyria and humours. This infusion alone, according to a leading physician, radically cured a patient with a well-formed cataract before his very eyes. An essential oil is extracted from its seeds, and is available from all apothecaries. It provides great relief for asthmatics and calms the most obstinate coughs; twelve to fifteen drops are taken in a glass of chopped milk or pectoral tea; six or eight drops are also very useful for colic. Good fennel, a fairly common liqueur, which is nothing other than wine spirit soaked in this oil, has the same effect with one or two spoonfuls, especially in windy colic and indigestion. Thresholds and roots boiled in barley and rice water bring milk to nursing mothers. Some claim that use of this plant makes you slimmer; it is therefore favorable to those with a marked tendency to become overweight. Finally, it has the well-recognized virtue of fortifying the stomach, aiding digestion and dissolving glutens, whether used in food or taken as an infusion. Shouldn't a garden always be stocked with such a useful plant? It's because of its great virtues that the Italian says: Fenochio è pane mi bastua, It's enough for me to have fennel and bread. CHAPTERXL. Description- of the FEVE-DE-MARAIS; its qualities and properties, cultivation, etc. This is one of the hardiest plants, and one of the easiest to raise: it grows in fields as well as gardens, and succeeds in all kinds of climates, provided the soil is a little cool. Its root is partly straight and partly creeping, and furnished with many sibers. Its stem commonly rises to two feet, and more when in good soil: it is quadrangular, fluted and hollow, with leaves along its entire length, placed without symmetry, and divided into three, five or seven parts, separated by a rib to which they are attached: its leaves are oblong, rounded, thick, bluish and smooth: its flowers are born several together from the axils of the ribs on the same pedicel, arranged in order and on the same side; they are leguminous and white, variegated with purplish and purple veins at the base : the lateral leaves are black in the middle and white at the edges; the inner leaf is greenish: the calyx is green, divided into five quarters, from which emerges a pistil which then changes by degree into a long, thick, almost round, fleshy and hairy pod, filled with beans, three, four or five in number; these seeds, being green, are greenish-white, oval in shape, a little quartered, large, flattened, and enveloped in a hard, thick bark called the robe, with a long, black mark where they attach to the pod. As they dry, they turn red, and the inner substance becomes hard and solid. As the seed dries, the pod and plant also dry out, and everything turns black. Each plant produces up to fifteen pods, when the soil and the year are good, especially as the same plant often produces two or three stems. The ways in which it is prepared are fairly well known: it is eaten with butter, bacon or cream; when it's new, it makes an entremets dish, which people who are fond of and delicate are very keen on. For this preparation, we take the marsh bean when it is very small, i.e., the size of a large bean, a little larger: we only remove the tip of the germ with the fingernail to accommodate it. People are more accustomed to bog beans when they are larger: they are then stolen, to use the usual term; they are cooked with a little water, after having been lightly browned in butter; then pepper and salt are added, along with a pinch of savory, whose taste enhances that of the bog bean, which is a little bland by nature. It also has the disadvantage of being heavy and windy, and of tightening up a lot: you mustn't eat too much of it if you don't have a strong stomach. This vegetable has medicinal properties; its flour is one of the four resolvatives commonly used in poultices to soften, resolve and dispose tumors to suppurate: in tummy courses, when it is permissible to stop them, the porridge made with this flour and milk is a good remedy. The ash from the stems and pods of this burnt plant is an aperitif: an ounce is boiled in a pint of water, then filtered and drunk by hydropics. The bark and pod of this grain, infused from evening to morning in a glass of white wine weighing three large, is an almost infallible remedy for urine retention. Water distilled from the flowers is an excellent cosmetic for removing spots and freckles from the face. Five different types of broad bean are grown in this country: the large variety, the julienne, the picarde, and two small varieties called féverolles: the latter two are only suitable for horses, and are only sown in fields. I won't say any more about them. Of the other three species, the large one is the best, and the one you should stick to for your own particular use: the other two are much smaller, and have a wilder taste: they are only used when the first one is lacking, and are only eaten by people who can put up with anything. Few are grown around Paris, although they are consumed as much as the other species: they are brought from outlying countries, in preference to the large species, because being harder, they are more resistant to transport, and the seed is much cheaper for those who traffic in it. The large species is sown in December and February; we risk a little in this first season, when they can be preserved: they are a fortnight earlier than those of the second seed; but they often perish: field mice, magpies and crows eat them, or frosts destroy them, unless they are well sheltered, and carefully covered in times of snow and frost. It is therefore advisable to put off the strongest seed until February. The latter is the best, because it is easier to crimp them; three are enough in each clump, spaced a foot or fifteen inches apart. They are crimped in March or April, depending on their strength, and limed a little at the same time. The flower stops better, the pods come out more beautiful, and the aphid, which is their enemy, attaches itself much less when this part, which is the most tender, is removed. If the aphid has already reached the pods, collect the infected stem ends in baskets and throw them away, or bury them. Before sowing them, many people, slaves to old practices, soak them either in natural water, or in manure water, in the belief that this will make them germinate and sprout; for me, who experimented with this for my rule, the only effect I noticed was that after soaking them in this way, many rotted in the ground and didn't sprout, and the others didn't advance any further: everyone can experience this. You must choose the best soil you have to plant this vegetable, which does not do well in light soils; and the better the soil has been smoked and ploughed, the more it yields. At the end of May, the first seedlings begin to bear their fruit, which is desired by delicate people only as long as it is small: so it should be picked at this time, and the pods, which follow one another, provide three weeks' supply. You can continue to sow them until the end of April, but after this month, the flowers, which come into bloom in the hot weather, are prone to dropping; aphids join in and ruin them. What can be done at no cost and at the risk of the event, is to cut off the first few plants that have borne fruit in May or June, just above ground level. Only as much as you need is set aside for seed, and the stalks are pulled out when the pod and cossate are black and dry; they are then threshed, and the grain is locked up dry, taking care to protect it from the rats and mice that are fond of it. This grain keeps well for two years on land; by the third, it hardly ever rises at all. Some individuals dry it green to eat during Lent, and here's how. They are taken to a good size; their coats are removed; they are threaded into strings, and first placed in the shade for a few days; then they are exposed to the sun until they are quite dry, and they are locked in a dry place; but when they are well appreciated, they are a bad food that turns to mush, and is more disgusting than appetizing. One author has suggested that to fertilize land properly, it should be sown with seeds and ploughed with the whole plant, which should be buried well when it begins to flower, and that this amendment is preferable to all manures: I don't know of anyone who has tried it out, so I can neither approve nor reject it; but I do know, having witnessed it many times, that in the Kingdom of Naples, many private individuals fertilize their land with a similar plant, whose name I haven't remembered, which they let grow very high, and which they bury in the same way: hence I presume that this kind of fertilizer could be good; nr.However, in countries where manure cannot be found, this practice might be good and useful. CHAPTTRE XLI. Description of the GARDEROBE; its different species, properties, cultivation, etc. ETTE plante n'est d'aucuneut usage dans les alimentations; mais, comme elle sert à la conservation de nos vétemens, qui, dans l'ordre de nos besoins, tiennent le sccond rang, elle mérite bien qu'on lui donne une place dans un Traité de la culture des plantes utiles à l'humanité. There are two types of garderobe, distinguished by male and female. The first, also known as aurone, has a woody root with a few fibers: it grows several sarmentose stems, two or three feet high, hard, brittle, filled with a white, reddish pith, grooved and branched. Its leaves, which are difficult to describe accurately, are endlessly multiplied, cut into lobes slightly wider than those of fennel, and borne on broad tails. They are whitish, with a strong odor and a bitter, acrid taste. The flowers are numerous along the branches, yellowish in color, and similar to those of wormwood; namely, with florets, but composed of a large number of very short, pipe-shaped florets, divided into five parts at their apex, each borne on a seed, and enclosed in a scaly calyx: the flowers are followed by small, oblong, naked seeds, without aigrette, similar to those of wormwood, but smaller. This plant is more or less bitter, depending on the season, climate and terrain. The second species, called by several names, the female aurone, the santoline and the small cypress, has a thick, hard, woody, branching root: it grows stems a cubit or more high, woody, spindly, covered with a whitish down, and divided into several branches, which are surrounded by small, finely serrated leaves, or rather loaded with small tubers that surround it four by four along its entire length: they are whitish, with a bad odor, and a flavor that is at once pungent, bitter and aromatic: each small branch bears a yellow flower with florets, composed of several other pipe-shaped florets, divided into five parts at their apex, separated by gutter-folded leaves, and enclosed in a common, scaly, almost hemispherical calyx; each floret is borne on an embryo that changes into an oblong, striped, brown seed without a tassel. This plant, both male and female, grows naturally in Italy and Provence, in mountains and uncultivated areas, from where it has spread to gardens, where it is very easy to grow: it can be propagated from seed or from rooted plants, and is used to make borders around quarries. The female species has a particular virtue: its pungent odor keeps worms and ringworms away from any place where it is planted, thus preserving clothes and other woollen furniture from the ravages of these insects. Both also have medicinal properties. The first species is very good for killing worms, dissipating winds, and stimulating urination and menstruation, insofar as it removes obstructions of the kidneys and womb: it is substituted for wormwood, its virtues being more or less the same. It is claimed that a decoction of its buds is a specific way of bringing in hair and preventing it from falling out. It forms the basis of a reputed and well-tested ointment for hair, the recipe for which is given below. Take a handful of leaves of male Garderobe, and as much of Capillaire; join to it 1 ounce of honey of Laudanum, 1 large of wild Myrthe; 3 large of seed of Linen, 3 large of Fenugreek, 3 ounces of oil of sweet Almonds, 3 ounces of good white Wine; make infuse the whole together during twenty-four hours, then make it boil until there does not remain any more moisture; pass it by expressing it, and make onctions with this ointment during the new moon. A great physician assures us that the leaves of the second species, dried and reduced to powder, diluted in matricaria water in a dose of one and a half large, taken on an empty stomach for ten days, and then from day to day, stops women's white flowers. Another similarly assures, on the basis of his own experience, that one lump of the same powder is very usefully employed in pleurisy and fluxion of the chest. CHAPTER XLII. Description of the BEAN: its different species, cultivation, etc. The name bean is common to both the plant and the fruit it produces: however, to distinguish the pod, which is eaten green, from the bean when it is separated from its pod, we say green bean and white bean; and when the bean is dry, we say dry bean or bean feve. This plant is universally known, and is widely consumed in all countries: some species spin, i.e. grow upwards; others remain low, hence the name dwarf bean. The leaf is uniform in all species from this country: it is arranged like that of ivy, i.e., divided into three almost equal parts which grow together on the same stem, all three ending in a point, of a beautiful meadow green, smooth, thin and united at the edges; its flowers are odorless, small, irregularly shaped, white or purplish, depending on the species, and emerge from the leaf axils in bunches of four, six, eight or ten, placed two by two in echelon along the branch where they are held: the stem is extremely loose, and only supports itself by clinging to neighboring stems. for lack of any other support. The flower is followed by the pod, which is more or less long, depending on the species, and a finger's breadth wide; it contains five or six seeds, more or less, which detach easily when dry. This vegetable is used so frequently in the kitchen, that it's arguably the most common household staple. It can be eaten in all seasons, in all conditions, and with the most delicate of tastes: it can be eaten fat or lean, and in many different ways. Green with its pod, it can be eaten with juice, butter or cream; but it must be removed exactly from the pod. E it is necessary to remove exactly its fillets and both ends: in tender grains, we give him the same appetizers: under roasted meats it is particularly good. Dry, it can be fried in a variety of ways; it can be eaten in salads and purées. When candied, it holds its place perfectly in entremets, either in juice or in cream; and, although it is very difficult to resist the temptation to eat it, in whatever way it is presented on the table, it is nevertheless advisable to warn that in grain form it is windy and heavy on the stomach, and for this reason, certain temperaments should eat it with moderation. Its medicinal properties are softening, aperitive and resolving: its flour is very commonly used in poultices to soften, resolve and dispose tumors to suppurate. Distilled green bean water, taken in the morning on an empty stomach in a bain-marie, in a quantity of three or four ounces, is good against gravel. Chewed beans applied to horse bites are said to heal the wound. There are an infinite number of bean species. An individual of my acquaintance has collected sixty-three of them out of curiosity, all very distinct in shape and color, but with very little difference in taste and quality: I shall not undertake to detail them, so as not to deviate from my object, which is to make my work less curious than useful, and I shall confine myself to fifteen or sixteen recognized as the best, and very-sufficient for our needs. Those whose climate and terrain are more suited to a few other species will be right to cultivate them; and I have no doubt that some which are only of mediocre quality here, and which, for this reason, are abandoned, could be much better in other countries: I myself remember having eaten with pleasure, in the Levant, several kinds that I have never seen in France. The sixteen species I have chosen are: 1 the jack bean. 2 the grivelé. 3 the early dwarf white bean. 4 the early white flat. the parchmentless. ó the common white. 9 small round white. 10 white Swiss. 11 grey Swiss. 12 red Swiss. 13 de Prague. 14 le gros à confire, de Hollande. 7 Caux kidney. 15 cardinal. 8 from Soissons. 16 from Spain. All these species multiply from seed or grain, which is planted at the end of April in warm, light soils, and in mid-May in later soils. No plant is so delicate, and requires soil so well prepared and smoked at the same time: it must have had a good ploughing before winter, a second one at the end of February, and a hoeing before sowing: these three ways are amply paid by the product, and I have the experience that those who spare the one of February, lose instead of saving. The soil must also be loose by nature, and well amended. If the soil is greasy and damp, the grain is prone to rot, and if it rises, the plant is always languid, turns yellow and produces very little. This plant has one advantage over all the others, which is that it has to be changed every year; but it does better the second year in the same soil, provided it is given a little manure; the grain comes out clearer and more even. This tool is little known in many countries, but is nevertheless the most convenient I know of for most grain seeds, peas, broad beans, Turkey wheat, etc., so I'll explain here how to use it for those who don't know it, and who would like to try it out. I will also explain its shape. The iron is thirteen to fourteen inches long, by eight on the bushing side, and is used at the other end, which is thus square. It should be slightly curved in the middle, and the beans should be sown either in the open field, in beds or in borders. First, two hoes of earth are removed from their intended place, and thrown to one side, one to the right and the other to the left: We then give several blows with this sharp tool to loosen the earth; we pull out another good hoe which we hold in the air with one hand, while with the other we spread the seed at the bottom of the little pit we're making, and we throw this hoe back on top, which we level out a little with a turn of the hand, from two feet to two feet: we continue as long as the ground is long, and according to the species we put in more or less grain. A second row is started when the first is finished, and the clumps are arranged in a chessboard pattern, at the same two-foot distance. Be sure to crush the clumps and remove any stones. After a fortnight, the seed should sprout: but if, in this interval, the tool is placed on the end, which must be of well-beaten steel, one line or one and a half lines thick at most; at the other end is the socket, which must be bent so that the handle returns to the tool and takes the same turn. This handle should be a little curved like the iron, and should only be 5 to 6 inches away from its cutting edge, and three inches away from the socket; its length should be eighteen inches. E 3 times there are stormy rains which beat the earth, the grain which cannot pierce the crust which forms above, especially when there are a few days of heat, needs to be helped; and it is necessary, with some light instrument, to break this crust to give him facility to leave, without which it perishes between two grounds. A month later, when the feet have gained a little strength, they are put back on their shoes, i.e., the earth that was thrown to one side when sowing is put back around the clump, and the earth is broken at the same time. If you want to row them, this is also the time to prick the oars, and they produce once more. Three weeks later, if they are rowed, we go through the rows to help the stems, called nets, to hang onto the oars. In some countries, such as around Chartres, we cut the nets as they form, and they never fail to have an abundance of grain: I think this method is very good when they are not rowed, as all the sap remains in the stalk, the first flowers all hold, the grain is better nourished, and it costs less in expenses and trouble: at the very least, when the nets have reached the end of the oars, they must be stopped; for they cause confusion, and uselessly consume much sap from which the bottom would benefit. If there is a lot of weed growth, as happens in rainy years, it must be weeded by hand and with skill, so as not to harm the planters given the last shoe. They are finally picked green in July, if they are needed. Both hands must be used, one to hold the pod's tail, the other to pull it so as not to break the nets; but, for the best, it is advisable to designate a special area for those to be eaten in this way, without touching those to be left to dry; If they are dwarf beans, wait until the plants have been stripped of all their leaves, and the pods have dried thoroughly; make bunches of them, and remove them in dry weather. But if they are bush beans, as the pods only dry one after the other, they must be picked as you go along; otherwise, the bottom pods, which are the first to dry out, may partly open, and the bean may fall out; or it may wilt and rust in its pod, if the weather is rainy; and it's all wasted. If you have room to sow them dry, you'd better not thresh them until you need them, because they keep for two years longer, and the whites keep their whiteness and glaze better too. This vegetable is privileged in that no insect damages it like the others, so there's nothing to fear when they're threshed in the field. They're only good for sowing for two years at most, because by the second year some of them are missing. To advance this fruit and enjoy it from the first days of June, we sow it very thickly on a bed at the beginning of March. It springs up quickly, and great care is taken to cover it in bad weather; it is then transplanted to the foot of the southern walls at the sin of April, where it picks up again easily with a little wetting; but this is only good for early dwarfs. The bed should be filled with six inches of compost. If you want to preserve green beans for the winter, choose the most tender ones and throw them into boiling water for a quarter of an hour; then pass them into fresh water, thread them with string, and put them in the air for two or three days in the shade, and the same number in the sun; or, if there is no sun, let them dry out on racks in the oven, making sure it is only lukewarm: When sufficiently dried, remove and seal in paper boxes or bags. To use them, fry them for twenty-four hours in lukewarm water, into which you have thrown a piece of beurre manié, and cook them in the same water; then season them as you please, and you will find them as tender as in their season; but, to tell the truth, the taste is quite different. Now to the description of the species. The jack bean is one of the first to be sown in early fields, and the first to supply Paris. Its flower is purplish, its pod very long and tender, and its grain of a good size, very elongated and rounded, of a black color, speckled with white. It is usually used only in green, but its grain is one of the softest and best there is. The only drawback is its color, which makes the sauce blackish; however, if you are careful to discard the water when it is half-cooked, and to replace it with boiling water, you can partly remedy this defect. It yields a lot. The kidney bean does not have such a soft grain; it is a similar species in terms of earliness; its flower is purplish, and its pod of mediocre size, rather elongated, tender and striped with red. Its grain is lime-gray speckled with black; it yields a lot when paddled. It is commonly used, like the previous one, only in green, for the same reason that its grain makes the color of the sauce black; it can however be corrected in the same way as we said above. The early dwarf white bean produces its fruit at the same time as the two preceding varieties: it does not spin, as its name suggests, and yet it is extraordinarily profitable, especially if picked often. Its flower is white, and its pod long and plain; its seed is also white, but of a perfect white, smooth and glossy: it is small, elongated and a little rounded. It is eaten both green and dry, and is very tasty either way: of all the species, it is the one that gives the most pleasure and profit in a middle-class garden; except that the dry bean does not lead the way as much as the larger species, which I'll talk about next. E The white flat bean is still in the same class as the previous ones in terms of haste: it spins and loads a lot when rowed. It has white flowers and medium-length pods, with medium-sized, short, flattened and fairly white beans. It is used both green and dry, but when eaten dry, it lacks the perfection of some others. The bean without parchment is the most esteemed to be eaten green, by the reason that its pod does not have inside this small film, called parchment, common to all the other species, and which makes it more tender and more pleasant to eat: it also has the advantage of giving its grain much more than any other; it is notoriously tender and soft, and at the same time as early as the preceding ones; it provides a lot when rowed. Its qualities justly earn it the eagerness of those who know it: it has a white flower, a very long pod, and a short, flat, white, medium-sized grain. It is used for both green and tender beans with the same pleasure: when dry, it has nothing to distinguish it. The common white bean is found everywhere, and is most widely used in provinces where the more distinguished species are not known, and where perhaps they would not be as successful as around Paris. Its flower is white, its pod mediocre, and its grain short, flattened, of a slightly dirty white; it spins and loads a lot when rowed: it is eaten equally green, as a tender grain, and dry. The Caux kidney is so named because its germ is sunken, and its overall shape resembles that of a sheep's kidney; it is a very good and highly esteemed species, and loads a lot when rowed. Its flower is white, and its pod highly elongated, but less full than some others; its grain is a little small, elongated, and quite perfectly white, smooth and glossy. It can be eaten in all ways, but when dried it is one of the most esteemed, being soft and tender; it is the successor to the first species. The Soissons bean ranks first for eating dry or as a tender grain: it is the largest of all, the most tender, and cooks best: it is very heavy when rowed, but is prone to staining in rainy years. Care must be taken to pick the pods as they dry. Its flower is white; its pod very long, but not very full; its grain very flattened, of a white and a glaze superior to all the others: it is the latest species. It is as good in green as the others, when eaten young, but is always reserved for eating as a soft or dry bean. The small, round, white bean is the smallest of all the ordinary species, and the best, to many people's taste, to be eaten dry: it is tender, soft and cooked perfectly, even having something distinguished in the taste. It sails and loads prodigiously when paddled. Its blossom is white, its pod small, but well filled; its grain almost round in all directions, and of a slightly russet white. It is only used dry, and is very good value. The white Swiss bean is a species designed to be eaten green; it carries a lot of weight, even though it is dwarfed. Its flower is white, its pod very tender and long; its bean is medium-sized, rounded, russet-white and of very mediocre quality: it is quite early, so it is hardly used for anything other than green. Grey Swiss differs from the first only in the color of its grain, which is a blackish red with black inlays: it is dwarfed like white, and is intended for green use only. The red bean is of the same class, except that its color is a beautiful red, mottled with several colors that vary according to the terrain. The Prague bean, otherwise known as the Queen's bean, because, it is said, Her Majesty was the first to whom it was presented a few years ago, is a singular species grown only in a few private gardens, and which should be more widespread; its flower is purplish, and its pod extremely small, but very full. Its seeds have no definite shape; some are quartered and round, others pendulous, some smaller, others larger, but all smaller than the smallest peas: its color is isabella, speckled with cinnamon. It is not eaten green, nor as a tender grain, but dry: it has a fine, distinguished taste that makes it invaluable to its fans, especially when served in salads with oil and vinegar. It is sown and cultivated like the other species; it is neither more delicate to raise, nor later to bear fruit: it should only be noted that it must necessarily be rowed, if it is to be truly profitable; and in this arrangement it yields an incredible quantity of pods and grains, which compensates well for its smallness. The large confit bean from Holland, known in German as schewvert, which means saber, because it imitates the shape of a saber, is an extraordinary species for the volume of its pod, which is seven to eight inches long and a good inch wide. Its flower is white and fairly large, and its rounded grain is white and wormy, which is almost the only way it can be used. It is widely consumed in Holland and neighboring countries, but hardly known in France. The cardinal bean is a new species, whose country of origin I do not know. Its flower is white, its pod quite long, but not very full, and its bean flattened and very large; its color is white all around, and purple all around the germ: this is what distinguishes it the most, because it has nothing special about it, either for taste or produce; on the contrary, it has the defect of ripening very late and with great difficulty, which means that it yields little, and sometimes nothing at all. The runner bean is a singular species, cultivated more for its flower than for its fruit; this flower is similar to that of the others, but of a perfect fiery color, and it produces abundantly for three months, provided it is rowed: The flower is followed by the pod, which is very long, coarse green and rough under the finger; the grain it contains is twice the size of all the other species; its color is grayish-delicate, mottled black for the most part. It is not usually eaten; however, people are beginning to develop a taste for it, and having tasted it myself, I found it no different from the others; but it sins by color like the grey and Swiss bean. I mention the four species that follow only to give a few examples of my own. The Chinese bean spins like our own; it has a smaller leaf; its flower is flax-grey in some, and white in others: it emerges from the leaf axils in bunches of ten to twelve, arranged much like a wallflower sprig, and can form as many as twenty-five or thirty on each plant. Most of the flowers bear fruit, and there are usually eight or ten on each cluster, shaped like ordinary pods, but shorter, wider and more inverted, with the added difference that both sides are bristling with small saw-like teeth, and the skin is rough and rough. The mature bean is round and flattened like a lentil, but twice as big, and perfectly black, with a white border around half its circumference. It's because of this resemblance that we call it a Chinese pea; but the leaf, stem and pod are characteristic of the bean. It multiplies extraordinarily; I counted on a single pred that I had raised on a bed, up to two hundred and some pods: in the open ground it would not provide so much; but it always provides a lot, provided that it has been advanced on a bed, and replanted at the foot of some well exposed wall. I cooked a handful to see what it tasted like, and found it peculiar: perhaps it would appeal to connoisseurs; but I must confess that, although there's nothing wrong with it, it didn't flatter me. Perhaps in its natural country it has other qualities that we don't find here, or virtues that we don't know. The first species of Mississippi bean multiplies from seed like the others. It should be sown here early on a bed, and then replanted in a good exposure: The leaves are distributed partly to the right and partly to the left, diametrically opposed, an inch apart, set in a parrot-like echelon, and arranged in three equal parts like those of ordinary beans, but of a completely different shape, only about an inch long, forming a perfect oval, smooth and united at the edges. The fruit-bearing stalks arise from the leaf axils at the bottom of the plant, six or eight in number, and bear twelve to fifteen inches from their birth to the first siliques, which are gathered in the shape of an aigrette at the tip. The flowers are purplish, shaped much like all bean flowers, but so small that their shape is barely noticeable. The flowers are followed by siliques or pods which contain the seed; these siliques are finger-length, rounded and small, like small string: each contains fifteen to eighteen brown seeds, shaped much like the small round white bean, and no bigger than a grain of millet. From this detail, it will be seen that this grain is not suitable for use as food. In fact, only the green silique is used; small as it is, a large quantity is needed to make a dish: from which we must conclude that it apparently has a merit that we do not know. I thought at first that the plant was stronger in Mississippi than it is here; but I was assured that there was very little difference, and that it was an exquisite food for these inhabitants. As far as we are concerned, we can only consider this plant as a curiosity. The second species of Vississipi is completely opposite to the previous one; the stem is weak like that of ordinary beans, and also spins; the leaf is arranged in the same way, but it is larger, and the three parts that make it up are regularly oval, ending in a point: The succeeding pod is extraordinarily long and large, and contains several large, short, almost quartered, russet-white beans with a black germ. I don't know what the pod is; I've only seen it in the Jardin du Roi, where it has been brought recently: I can only assure you that if it were to become naturalized in our climate, it would be of great value; that's what we'll certainly know in a few years' time. The American bean is a spinning species like our own, but with a very slender stem and an extremely small leaf. The flower is proportionate, and the silique that follows it is only about an inch long, and contains only one or two almost round, coffee-colored seeds the size of a grain of hare's lead. From this description, I don't think anyone who grows beans for profit or propagation will be tempted to try this one. There are several other similar species of bean, which I will pass over in silence, as they are of interest only to the curious, as I have already remarked: so I feel I must end this article here. "CHAPTER XLIII. Description of the HYSOPE; its different species, properties, cultivation, etc. THERE are three species of hyssop; but, as they differ only in the color of the flower, and are perfectly similar in everything else, the description of one dispenses me from describing the others. Its root is thick, woody and hard; it sprouts several stems that rise to a foot or eighteen inches, hard, knotted, twiggy, topped, from the bottom to the top, with long, narrow leaves, a little larger than those of savory : its flowers are spike-shaped, but all turn to one side, and are white, blue or pink, depending on the species; The upper lip is raised, rounded and indented, while the lower is divided into three parts, the middle of which is spoon-shaped, indented and terminated by two points. Each flower is followed by four oblong, brown seeds, enclosed in a capsule that serves as the flower's calyx: these seeds are fragrant, sometimes approaching the smell of musk. This plant is of no use for life, but in gardens it is used to form borders around vegetable patches, where it spreads an aromatic and very pleasant scent, especially before it comes into flower. Its greatest properties are medical: it is incisive, aperitive, digestive, detersive, vulcerative and fortifying: it is mainly used for tartarous diseases of the lung. Its leaves and flowers are used in cephalic decoctions, and in the aromatic wine so frequently used by surgeons to dispel swollen wounds. Distillation yields a water and an essential oil with many properties. Its flowers are used to make a simple syrup, and another compound in which several other bechic and aperitive plants are used, and which is highly esteemed for chest illnesses, especially asthma and obstinate coughs. The minced leaf, mixed with fresh butter and applied externally, is good for bruises. Fresh or dried hyssop buds, soaked in mulled wine and placed in a knot and applied in fomentation to the eyes before going to bed, cure echymosis; and the same fomentation is sovereign for dissolving rumpled blood and resolving it when extravasated. Water or a decoction of the whole plant also cures eye inflammation. It is also used to make an excellent preserve for lung diseases. Finally, it is good for strengthening the brain, making the blood fluid, stimulating the months and urine, and removing obstructions. This plant can be propagated by seed and offspring, but more commonly by the latter, which is safer and easier. It is planted in borders around quarries in March and September, in the same way as lavender. The method for one is the same as for the other. It must be destroyed and renewed every two years, for cleanliness's sake, because when it stays in place longer, it creates a gap that is unpleasant to the eye. It should be placed as far as possible from the sun, in soil that is neither too oily nor too light. It requires no other special care. CHAPTER XLIV. Description of lettuce; its different species, properties, cultivation, etc. Lettuce is one of the most universal and common of garden plants. It can be found in all four parts of the world, and adapts easily to all climates and soils, provided the soil is well prepared, and the species chosen are suited to the vein of the soil. There are three main species, which vary considerably from one another, and form an infinite number of individual species. The first is that which only produces a large number of large, smooth, elongated leaves, without forming an apple. The second is that with a round leaf, which apples on its own and is called head lettuce. The third is a leaf that is curved and almost smooth, called chicon or romaine. These three species, with the exception of the leaves, which are round or curved, smooth or curly, and of different colors, grow their stems more or less equally; this stem, which rises to two or three feet, is firm, thick, cylindrical and branched: the branches are further divided into smaller ones, loaded with flowers, and spread out like sheaves. These flowers are composed of several yellowish half-florets, borne on embryos, and enclosed in a small, oblong, scaly calyx. These flowers are followed by the seed, which is oblong, flattened and pointed at both ends, white, black or yellow, depending on the species. This plant is so familiar and well-known that it needs no introduction. It is eaten raw in salads with oil, vinegar and salt; and provided it is eaten on its own without any supplies, it is very healthy, and can only upset extremely weak and cold stomachs: digestion, against general prejudice, is easy, and particularly so for those who drink little or no wine. Cooked, it is eaten in soup; it is also mixed with various vegetables, especially peas. It is refreshing and humectant: however, some doctors claim that it is not suitable for asthmatics. It is used almost as much in medicine as in food. A distilled water is extracted from its leaves, and is commonly used as a base for refreshing juleps and sleeping pills. Its leaves are also used internally in refreshing broths and enemas, to combat burning fevers and diseases that threaten internal parts with inflammation. Its seed, which is one of the four small minors, has much the same virtues, and is used in refreshing potions in a dose of two to three large doses. Externally, the leaf is applied to the forehead as a bandage, either alone or fricasseed with vinegar, chervil and purslane, and is very useful and effective against migraine. It is also claimed that frequent use of this plant, which is milky by nature, is very beneficial for nursing mothers to maintain and increase their milk. Its juice is used in all refreshing syrups, and is the main virtue of populeum, so sovereign for inflamed hemorrhoids. In addition to the three species of lettuce distinguished above, there are a few wild species commonly found in hedgerows, which have the same virtues as the cultivated species. Some authors even claim that, being milkier, they are more aperitive and more detersive; but they are not used for life, being hard and bitter; their leaves are usually long, narrow, sinuous, deeply cut on both sides, and armed with fairly rough thorns all along the coast; they flower and bear their seed, which is black, and quite similar to the seed of those grown in gardens. I'll now describe the three species in question. The first is of such little use for the table, and so mediocre, that it should rather be banished from gardens than cultivated: however, if, against my opinion, there are climates and land situations where it succeeds better than the others, as I have sometimes been persuaded, the only way to take advantage of it is to bind it like chicory; when it is in full strength, the heart whitens, but rots easily. The second species, head lettuce, is the most widely cultivated: it seems that nature has amused itself by varying it with perfectly distinct qualities, which have made it all the more varied in its names. The curious and the amateurs should be pleased that I have brought back twenty-five species, with the necessary details, by means of which they can obtain in all kinds of soils, and successively in all seasons, the enjoyment of a present that nature has been so kind as to make us so pleasant and so multiplied: I say in all kinds of soil, because there is no land where it cannot be cultivated; but at the same time, I make the observation that not all subdivisions of lettuce are suitable for all soils, that on the contrary, some are successful only in certain soils, and that it is only after testing one's own soil that one should stick to cultivating those that are the most successful. I therefore subdivide head lettuce into twenty-five varieties: petite Crêpe, grosse Crêpe, Bagnolet, George. l'Aubervilliers, la grosse Blonde, la Dauphine, la Perpignane, la Bapaume, la Batavia, la Laitue brune, la Sanguine, la Coquille, la Passion, la Gènes, la jeune Rouge, la grosse Rouge, l'Italie, la Royale, la Paresseuse, la Versailles, la Cocasse, la grosse Allemande, la Pomme de Berlin, et la longue Vissée. F 2 The third type of lettuce, known as chicon or romaine, is not much less cultivated than the previous one; it is not subject to the bitterness commonly found in head lettuces, which is why it is preferred to be eaten raw. I know twelve to fifteen species; however, only seven are generally known and cultivated, and are more or less equal in quality, the others being degenerate species of little merit, even the one with yellow seeds. These seven species are Romaine hâtive, Chicon vert, Gris, Blond, Rouge, Panaché and Alfange. I now return to head lettuces. I. Petite crêpe, otherwise known as petite noire, because its seed is black, is the smallest of all lettuces: it is yellowish-green, extremely curly and toothed; its head is small in proportion, especially when grown under a bell: it is very tender in this situation; but, to tell the truth, it has little taste, and its main merit is that it succeeds better than any other lettuce on a bed throughout the winter, a season whose harshness makes it find everything good. In the open ground, it is much better, and apples better; but it is only suited to spring: in the other seasons, it rises as soon as it apples; and, in strong soils, it even melts rather than peels: it therefore requires a soft, well-soiled soil, with a southern or eastern exposure, along some wall, or at the foot of some windbreak. It is the first species to give seed, but it gives little, and even that which it does give is very prone to clouding. There is a second, much larger species, whose seed is similarly black, but which does not grow under a bell jar, and which is hardly cultivated today. To grow it in all kinds of weather, intelligent gardeners, and particularly market gardeners, sow it from the end of August on small ados of potting soil with a southern exposure, and then transplant it to small, low beds where it bolts; this first sowing is eaten in December. They sow it on a bed, for the second time, around All Saints' Day; and the plant that comes from there, they replant it in place on another bed, when it is strong enough: it must have been transplanted beforehand: this one is eaten in January. The third seed is sown in December, and they continue to sow it every month until March, so that they always have some in succession; but this third seed requires much more care than the first. For this lettuce, as for all the others that are sown or transplanted, the first thing to do is to fill the bed with only four inches of potting soil: when it is ready to be sown, i.e. when the great fire has passed, the seed is sown under a bell, very clear, and very lightly covered with potting soil: many market gardeners are even content to beat it with their hand against the potting soil without covering it. The seed sprouts a few days later; and, when its two ears are well formed, and the first leaf begins to emerge from the heart, without waiting any longer, it is transplanted two by two, an inch apart, under other cloches. If the layer is still good, we transplant it on top; if not, we put it back on top of another one: sixty or eighty leaves enter under each bell. We leave it to fortify itself in this state until it has four or five leaves, and we take care to maintain its bed in a good warmth: I don't mean heat, because this plant does better with a moderate fire; and it's enough, so to speak, for it to find the bed warm when we sow it, and transplant it, or plant it in place; the two extremes of cold or heat melt it. We are also careful to keep the bells stuffed and covered with bedding, mainly during the nights, and to increase the blankets in proportion to the severity of the weather. When you have straw mats, it's even better to cover the whole layer over the bedding, not only to keep the heat in more securely, but also to more easily shake off any snow that may fall, and which, without this precaution, would mingle with the bedding, which would then have to be changed often, without preventing the cooling of the layer. From time to time, it is important to check that the plant is not overheated. This can be seen when you lift the bells and see hot steam coming out: you should then give them a little air for a few hours. When the weather is mild, or when the sun comes out during frosts, the bells should be lifted during the height of the day, and put back on as soon as the sun goes down. When it has finally reached four or five leaves, it is transplanted again under 4 cloches, on a new layer to remain there and to prune; and this last layer must be loaded with six inches of compost, so that the root does not touch the manure: we put twelve or fifteen, which we then reduce to four or five at most, as soon as they begin to be pressed. Those that are removed are also replanted in fours, under other cloches. Care must be taken not to use new cloches for transplanting at any time; they must have been used for other plants for two weeks, or exposed to the sun for a similar length of time along a wall, to allow them to dry out: experience shows that without this, the plants they cover melt and perish. The same care and heating should then be continued, according to the greater or lesser need, and care should be taken to give the bells a little air from time to time during the best hours of the day, whether the sun is shining or not; because without air, the plant withers and doesn't grow: It's enough to raise the bell by one finger, on the south side, if there's no wind, and you support it in this state with a bit of rolled straw; but if the wind is too strong, whatever it may be, you must always give the air on the opposite side. As they fill the bells, you need to raise them higher with forks, depending on the weather, and then cut them when you've reached the point where you want them. When the apple starts to peel, remove all the low yellowing leaves and press the soil against the base; the apple will be bigger and more beautiful. You must also change the forks every 3 or 4 days, in other words, give air in turn to the four sides of the bell, so that they all benefit equally; otherwise, the one that enjoys the benefit of air to the exclusion of the others, will strengthen itself at their expense, and do them a great deal of harm: try, however, to match this with the wind. II. The large crepe differs from the small one only in size, which is almost twice as large: it also has the same qualities, shape and color. Its main merit is that it is extremely soft and tender; it also requires the same quality of soil and the same exposure. It can be sown and brought forward to be enjoyed in the spring, which is its season: it can even be planted for peeling, but it does not tolerate the bell. It should be left in the open air as soon as it has been replanted, either on a bed or on the ground. Its 5 seeds are white and ripen early: it yields very little. There is another type of crêpe that market gardeners call "la ronde": it is very lightly curled, and comes a little larger than the others; but I don't know if it's the same one I'll talk about below, known more generally as "Bagnolet" or "petite-courte". The seed of both species of crepe is the one taken in preference to any other to make lettuce for cutting, otherwise known as nasturtium, as it is blonder. The way to sow it is to make small rays two inches wide by one inch deep. Others sow it broadcast, or in the field, to use the gardener's term, under cloches, and beat the soil well with the hand without burying it. Twelve to fifteen days after sowing, it can be cut; but to nourish it better, and give it more taste, it should be sown fairly lightly, and left to enjoy until it has two good leaves, in addition to its ears; it takes longer to come, but it gives more profit and pleasure. We sow a little every two weeks from the end of September: this way, we can have them for as long as we want, but we're not so keen on them as soon as the head lettuces start giving. The same care and precautions as I explained above should be observed for those intended for head lettuce. III. The Bagnolet, which others call the petite courte, the printanière, or the dégrebé, grows like the petite crêpe, but beats it in that it is earlier, and the head is larger, yellower and firmer; it also has the advantage of peeling under a bell without needing air, which is a merit. Its leaf is blond and smooth, much less curly than the crêpe, and less prone to melting. Market gardeners don't grow it as much; I don't know why, because it's better in every way; and I'm devoted to it in my own right, for winter and spring, to the exclusion of all others. It succeeds equally well on land and on beds; but it climbs easily as soon as the heat begins. Its seed is white; and for this reason, as much as for its resemblance to the crepe, some call it the white crepe, or the round crepe, as I said in the previous article. Only four should be placed under each bell, and it is 0 a species that everyone should be eager to have: it seeds little, given its smallness, and is very prone to clouding; more should be reserved than less. IV. There are two types of George, one called grosse George, the other white George. The first is grown on a bed, and the apple under a bell like the previous ones, where it succeeds perfectly, provided it is in the air and amply moistened: three are enough under each bell; and if you want to put four, you must space the bells four inches apart. Its leaf is blond and fairly smooth, and its apple tight: it is soft and tender, and can be considered an excellent lettuce, which in its time provides abundantly for all needs. The best time to plant it is at the beginning of April; take care to give it the right amount of air, and cut it as soon as the head is formed, as it rises almost immediately. The seed is white, yields many and ripens from the first. The second type can be sown either on the ground or on the bed; but it only apples on the ground, and particularly at the foot of walls: it comes very large and well-headed, sweet and tender. Its leaves are blond, brittle and not very curly: it requires light, well-soiled soil. It is the first to come on the ground when it has been a little advanced on the bed: it must also be cut as soon as it is heady, having the same defect of rising quickly. Its seed is white, abundant, and mú̂rit of the first. The majority of market gardeners choose it in preference to all the others, to make lettuce for cutting, both because it supplies abundantly, and because its leaf at birth is blonder and wider than the others. V. Aubervilliers is a small spring lettuce that also does well in summer, but its small size has disgraced it from most gardens. Its outer leaves are smooth and coarse green, its head very yellow and very tender: it hardly melts, and can be kept for quite a long time on the head; these advantages would deserve it a happier fate. The seed is white, yields little and ripens early. VI. The grosse blonde is aptly named, being large and very blond: it is tender and sweet, and apples perfectly; but it rises almost as soon as it is plucked, especially in summer: it is in spring and autumn that it is most successful. The seed is white, yields a lot and usually ripens well. VII. The dauphine is a spring lettuce, which is only good in this first season: it is very early and very large, with a flat shape; its head is quite tight: it is sweet and tender, and may be considered one of the best lettuces. It grows well in any kind of soil, provided it is moistened often and generously; it grows a lot of suckers around its first leaves, which must be carefully removed. Its seed is black, yields a lot and ripens early. VIII. Perpignan comes in two varieties: one green, the other speckled, i.e. with yellow spots. The green variety has plain, smooth leaves with a large rib; its apple is very large and very yellow, quite tender; without any bitterness: it holds up well in dry soils, and resists heat: it also climbs only with difficulty; but in damp soils, it rots easily; it must therefore be given its proper soil. Its seed is white: it supplies a lot of seed, but it needs to be sown early for the seed to ripen. The second differs from the other only in its external color, and in its ribs, which are a little smaller; in addition, its qualities are the same, and its seed is also white: it yields a fair amount. IX. Bapaume is a large, very blond lettuce, which stands up very well: it can be grown in all seasons and in all types of soil; this is its main merit. Its head is not tight at the top, but is very full at the bottom, which deceives those who look at it without knowing it. Its seed is black and yields very little. X. Batavia is a species totally different from all the others, both in volume, configuration and size: it is the size of a small cabbage. Its leaves are extremely curly and folded, light green in color, with a hint of red sown around the edges; its head is a little concave, whitening only imperfectly, yet tender and delicate, and at the same time so brittle that it hardly suffers transport. In strong soils, it has a slight bitterness, which is its only defect; but, despite this, it is highly esteemed by people of taste, and preferred to many others, especially as it takes seasoning better than any other. It does not do equally well everywhere: those who find themselves on its own ground can congratulate themselves. It needs to be watered frequently and generously, but only in the morning or evening, as midday watering will cause it to speckle and rot, and it is very sensitive to cool temperatures. After August, therefore, it is no longer to be relied upon, and this must be taken into account when planting it. The seed is white, yields a fair amount and ripens fairly early. XI. Brown lettuce, otherwise known as Dutch lettuce, is a fairly good lettuce that apples very well, and holds up better than many others in droughts; it is of a nice size; its outer leaves are dull green, very smooth, and very plain at the edges; its heart is yellow and full: if it were more tender and delicate, we would have nothing to reproach it with. It's always a good idea to have some in summer, as it doesn't melt and doesn't go up as easily as most of the others: it's at least excellent for cooking, when you find it too hard to eat raw. Its seed is black, and it yields a lot; but it ripens a little late, and in tender years it rots rather than ripens. XII. Sanguine is a medium-sized lettuce, covered with red veins like variegated chicory, and very pleasing to the eye. It apples fairly well in spring, but only in that season; in other seasons, it rises rather than peels. It requires soft soil. Its seed is black: it yields enough, and ripens quickly, XIII. The shell has a leaf that is more green than yellow, round, fairly smooth and fairly large: it is usually hard and bitter, and is good only for soups. Its merit is that it withstands winters better than any other, and is therefore the first on earth to turn pomaceous. When there has been no bedding to raise other species, the seed is sown in mid-August; the plant is strong enough by October to be replanted, and is then planted on the south or east coasts, in two, three or four rows, depending on the width of the beds. During heavy frosts, it is covered with well-shaken bedding, or playon of varying thickness, depending on the severity of the weather: it is carefully removed when the thaws arrive. Cocasse, Italie and chicory, which also survive the winter, can be covered in the same way, provided they are neither too long nor too tough. If you haven't grown crepe and George on a bed during the winter, you can put it on a bed at the end of February, to get it earlier. It is only good on the ground in mid-May, a little earlier or a little later, depending on the year and the soil. The seed is white; but there is another species whose seed is black, and which is valued more highly. However, having examined them both closely, I found little or no difference between them; both grow well and set seed very early. XIV. Passion is a species much like the previous one. Its leaf is green and blistered, of medium size, which also spends the winter; and this is all its merit, being otherwise hard and bitter. In spring, it is used for soups and refreshing broths: it is very useful for those who live in cold, snowy countries; it withstands the rigors of winter better than any other. Its seed is white: it yields a fair amount. XV. Genes come in three varieties, distinguished as blonde, green and red. The first has blond, fairly smooth leaves. Its apple is a little pointed and very white, without bitterness: it is good in spring and autumn; in summer it is cloudy. Its seed is white: it yields a lot, which ripens fairly early. The second has green, curly leaves; its apple is hard and yellow, larger than the first: it does not leaf out. Its season is spring and autumn, and in dry years: it also does well in summer. It should be watered only when badly needed, but needs to be crimped often. Its seed is white: it is very prone to clouding. Reserve more rather than less. The third has curly red leaves and a tender yellow heart. It survives the winter, but should be placed at the foot of the sunset walls, as the sun is against it at this time of year. It should be planted in spring and autumn; it does not do well in summer. Its seed is black and takes a long time to ripen. Medium-sized, it apples very well: soft, tender and very yellow at the core; its outer leaves are soft green, whipped with red, round and almost plain. Its favorite season is spring and autumn; in summer it nuils and melts easily: it requires soft soil. The seed is black, and often fails to ripen, being very late: it must be planted early on the bed, to be sure of harvesting it. XVII. The grosse rouge has all the good qualities of the preceding variety, and surpasses it in size, which is at least twice as large: it also has the advantage of remaining well-appointed for as long and more than any other, and of succeeding in all seasons, when the soil is suitable. It requires a rich soil; in the sands, it comes up thin and hard. It has the added merit of never wilting, at least in fatty soils such as mine: these advantages make me consider it a first-rate lettuce, a must-have in any well-stocked kitchen garden. Its outer leaves are round, almost plain, black-green, shaded with a large, dull red. The head is as yellow as an orange; the seed is black, ripens fairly easily, and yields a lot. XVIII. Italian lettuce has long been the most cultivated of all lettuces, having the advantage of succeeding in all seasons, and of lasting quite a long time on the head: market gardeners have abandoned it in favor of cocasse, which lasts even longer, but is not as good in all other respects. Its leaves are fine and tender green, edged with red; its head is tight and perfectly tasty: its only fault is that it speckles a little in summer; it requires light, lean soil and little water. Its seed is black and ripens well, but it does not yield much. XIX. Royale is one of the best lettuces for summer, provided it is moistened abundantly: it has a good size, apples well, and lasts a long time: it is quite soft and quite tender; its outer leaves are a beautiful green, a little blistered and shiny. It's a pity that it's a little prone to shading and melting; otherwise, it's perfect and of good quality. The seed is white and provides a fair amount of yield, but ripens with difficulty in tender years. It's a good idea to bring forward what you intend to sow. XX. Before the Cocasse and the Versailles were known, the lazy variety was the most cultivated for summer; it really does not climb as much as any other, and is more resistant to drought; but it is always a little hard and bitter. The best way to eat it is cooked. Its apple is very hard and quite large; its outer leaves are a coarse green, very blotchy, and packed one on top of the other. Its seed is white: it yields a lot, but it needs to be brought forward a little for it to ripen more surely. XXI. For the past ten years, cocasse has been the favorite lettuce of all gardeners, and especially of market gardeners, who almost exclusively grow cocasse throughout the summer. This preference is due to its excellent eating qualities, and to the fact that it can be kept on the vine for a very long time, despite heat and drought. Its outer leaves are a large, shiny green, heavily blistered: it is very large. Sown in August and transplanted at All Saints' Day, it survives the winter. These are the advantages that have made it so popular; but it is prone, in many soils, to rot between two hearts, or to spotting, especially in rainy weather. It requires a soil that is neither too light nor too strong; or, if it is light as in some of our marshes, it must be helped with a lot of manure and compost, and watered amply, in which case it succeeds perfectly well. These types of soil are particularly suitable for summer; in strong soils, it only grows leaves and does not close: in this season, it should still be sunk a little when replanting, as the apple forms better. In addition to the above-mentioned defect, it is also hard and a little bitter, and its exterior is not very attractive; In view of these faults, which greatly outweigh its good qualities, I am obliged to say that it deserves the preference it is given, only insofar as the land does not allow others to be raised during the summer, in which case it is better to have it as it is than to have none at all; and market gardeners in particular, who have a great interest in making the most of their plants, are right to be attached to them. To collect the seed, it must be raised on a bed; if the winter seed has perished, replant it on a hillside, and help it to rise by making a cross-shaped incision on the apple, without which the heart often rots because it cannot emerge. Its seed is white, and yields a lot when the season is favorable, but sometimes it doesn't ripen. which many people confuse with the cocasse, and which I had judged at first myself to be one and the same species, under two different names, better examined is certainly a different species. The leaf of the latter is a lighter green, without any freckles, like those of the cocasse; it is also less curly, and has a flatter head; its leaves are less crowded together, and it is less prone to rust. I have often compared them together, so as to be able to argue, against all opposing opinions, that they are two very different species, and that they are similar only in that they are almost equally successful in heat, and that their apples are equally supportive; nevertheless, I place this one above the other, especially as it is not so hard, nor so bitter. It may be, as some claim, that this one was formed from the other; but the origin makes no difference to the present state of this plant. Its seed is white, ripens late and yields more or less, depending on the year: it requires the same precautions as cocasse when harvested. Many market gardeners use this species to make lettuce for cutting, because it is more resistant to frost. XXIII. grosse Allemande, known in Holland and Germany as Impériale, is a lettuce which, although very old, was either lost in France, or never became well established: it is still only known to a small number of people; but its great qualities will soon spread it to all gardens: it can be called the queen of lettuces. There are no good qualities that it does not have, and I know of only slight defects. It is monstrously large, with the same volume as Batavia. Its outer leaves are extraordinarily large and smooth, ending a little pointed when young; its color is a pale, dull green; its apple is very tight, yellow and tender; its taste is sweet when the soil suits it. It can be kept for as long on the head as the cocasse, and does not spit in the same way: it comes in well in all seasons, except for its size, which is not the same in summer as in spring; but I have noticed that in our light soils, such as our marshes in the Faubourg Saint-Germain, it is prone to melting in the warmer months, especially when wet too often; this is one of its faults: another is that in certain areas, it is a little bitter, but this does not prevent it from being eaten with pleasure. It is prone to growing suckers between its lower leaves, which must be carefully removed, as they are harmful to the heart. It must be raised early on the bed so that it can set seed, because, in addition to being difficult to raise, it takes a long time to set apple: it must be planted at a distance of about fifteen inches in all directions. G ron apart in all directions, and whenever it is in good condition, it is tightly packed. This detail will show that it is worthy of preference over all the others; and I have no doubt that, once our market gardeners are familiar with it, they will substitute it for cocasse, if they can prevent it from melting through cultivation. It is advisable not to replant after July, as the apple does not have time to form after this time, being very late, as I have already observed: it has a white seed, and yields a lot when the year is dry. XXIV. The Berlin apple still surpasses the previous one in size, but differs in many respects. I have only had it for a year; the man who gave it to me, a Hanoverian, spoke unfavorably of it, and only announced it to me as a plant remarkable for its size, and in no way superior to the others in quality. But either this foreigner didn't know it, or our climate suits it better than Germany's, I conceived a very different idea of it after the trial: it came to me with all the qualities of a perfect lettuce, sweet, tender, fine, brittle. Its heart whitens very well; its color is a tender green, slightly tinted on the edges with a few red shades. It apples fairly quickly: it succeeds in both spring and autumn seasons, and a single plant is enough to make a reasonable salad. It should be planted eighteen or twenty inches apart in all directions: it apples fairly quickly, but its apple is not perfectly tight, and does not hold together for long. Its seed is black, abundant and ripens early. XXV. La longue vissée is a new lettuce that was sent to me from Italy last year, and which I have not seen anywhere else: it is singular in its shape, which is elongated like a sugar loaf, and ends in a point, with indentations and elevations that turn from bottom to top, and have the same effect as a press screw, which gives it its Italian name that I have francized. The outer leaf is yellowish-green, brittle and curly, much like Batavia: it is soft and tender; all in all, it's a very good species, regardless of its singularity. I wasn't able to test it in all seasons, as I didn't have enough seed. Its seed is black, ripens fairly well, but yields little. Some soils may be suitable for the twenty-five species of G 2 lettuce I have just mentioned; but at least there is no soil that is not suitable for some of them. So everyone, after having tried them all, if he sees fit, will stick to those that will be most successful for him. I don't approve of stubbornly bringing in those for which the stock is not suitable: as the multiplicity of species is only a matter of curiosity, we should be happy when we are provided with a few good ones, which, succeeding one another, can supply our needs throughout the year. I had heard of two new species, one called spinach lettuce, the other long apple. The first is unusual for its leaf, which resembles that of spinach; but it hardly makes an apple, and has no qualities to recommend it. The second makes, like chicory, a very elongated apple: it is always hollow and not very tender. Among the others that I have not mentioned, there may be some that do well in other climates; everyone will do well to keep the species they are happy with. I'll move on to chicory: the first is early romaine, which has the advantage of peeling, or, to put it more accurately, coëffer under a bell; it gives great pleasure in novelty, although it remains a little thin under the bell: its shape is quite similar to blond chicory: its outer color is a little less light, and its leaves more pointed, yet it is hard to tell them apart when they are both raised in the ground, both being cambered and of roughly equal size. They should be sown in beds as early as October, so that they can be enjoyed in April; the method used by some gardeners is to place one foot between four pancakes on their beds. These pancakes, which come much earlier, are removed, leaving the romaine the master of the place; but I find a drawback in this, which is that they occupy the bed for too long: it's better to set it aside, and put only three lettuces, so that they are comfortable, under each bell, which must then be raised on three forks, when the heads of the plants begin to touch the top: its seed is white, abundant, and ripens well. II.
