The green chicon, of the seven we know, is the most widely cultivated, for the reason that it is the largest and the least delicate in relation to the weather, and that it succeeds well in all seasons. It also has the advantage of being self-sealing; or3 at most, a tie at the end is enough. Its leaf is long, rounded at the tip, rough and a little blistered; its rib is blanshe, and its color a coarse green. The good species must be flattened at the top, which market gardeners call camus; when it ends in a point, it's a sign that it's degenerate: consequently, only those with a flattened shape should be marked for seed. It spends the winter being sown in August and replanted on a coast in October: it is the most profitable crop for those who raise it for sale; but to be eaten raw, it is the least tender and the least delicate of all. When replanting in the open ground, after having overwintered in layers or ados of potting soil, make sure to bury it a little when planting, and butter it: it yields a lot of seed, which is black. III. Gray chicon is not very different from the previous one; its color is a darker green; it has the advantage of being milder and more spring-like, but it also melts easily during the summer, and should only be used for spring. It overwinters well and adapts well to all types of soil: its seed is white and very abundant, but ripens a little late. IV. Blond is much more delicate to eat than the previous varieties: it makes "an equally large, flat and full apple, but melts more easily. It also rises very quickly. Its leaf is yellowish-green, a little pointed at the tip, with a white rib; it is less thick and less blistered than that of the other two varieties. It thrives best in soil that is somewhere between light and heavy, but its success also depends on the season: in tender years, it thrives best in light soil; in dry years, it thrives best in heavy soil. Its seed is white, but it yields less than the green variety, so you need to keep more of it than of other species. V. Red bears this name with justice, its outer leaves being truly red, but its heart is a perfect yellow and tender: its defect is that it is very prone to melting as soon as it is bound, and there are few soils where it succeeds well; strong soil is the one that suits it best, especially in dry years. When absolutely necessary, water should be poured from the mouths of watering cans right up to the ground, so that the plant does not get wet, and it should be cut as soon as its heart is white: this shows that only a very few G.4 should be grown at a time. It has the advantage of being sown very lightly in the months of July and August, along walls facing east and south, and then thinned out to a suitable distance, so that it can be kept until the big frosts, and whitens without needing to be tied. Its seed is black, and it yields a lot. VI. The variegated variety, which others call flagellt or Silesian lettuce, so named because its leaves are whipped with red veins similar to certain yellow tulips, is preferred by connoisseurs over all others, because it is both pleasing to the eye and to the taste: it is sweet and tender, and combines all the good qualities one could wish for. There are two species, one much more prone to melting than the other: the latter, which comes from England, has its outer leaves less variegated than the other; but those in the heart are more so, and it coëffices almost by itself like green chicory. In some soils, both are subject to melting as they whiten. Spring is its season, because in summer it melts and rises, and in autumn it rots. The seed of the first species is black, and that of the second is white: both yield poorly. The variegated variety ripens between the first and last lettuces. VII. Alfangium is a particular species, which is similar to the others in shape only; its leaves are pointed, snake-like, thin and smooth; the color is pale green, with a few red shades at the top. It is very tender and delicate: but it has the double defect of rotting and climbing easily; however, it succeeds very well in some soils, and it is advisable to test it before rejecting it: its seed is white, abundant, and ripens fairly early. All these species need to be bound to whiten. Depending on their strength, one or two straw or rush ties are placed on the bottom of the plant, not too tightly, and a few days later, a third tie is placed on the top, which is tightened even more: Bind them on a fine day, and wait until there is no more dew or humidity, taking care to bind only as much as you need, as they will soon rot after they are white, or go to seed; from eight to eight days, bind as much as you can use. The two types of chicory, red and variegated, which I have just mentioned, do not appear in public markets, nor does alfange: Their natural tendency to melt, as I have shown, is one of the reasons why our market gardeners do not grow them; to which must be added the difficulty they would have in selling them to fruit growers, who would be afraid to take them on, because they cannot endure, like the others, being closed for several days at a time in cellars while waiting for the buyer. The public loses out, because they are incomparably better; and all those who have gardens should endeavor to grow some for themselves in preference to any other, if the land suits them. You won't get much profit out of it, but you'll be compensated by taste. With regard to all chicories, I must point out that when they go to seed, we still make useful use of the stalk, which we call côton; we eat it cooked in white sauce, and it's a pretty good meal; we also pickle it in vinegar, and it's used in winter salads. It now remains for me to tell you what you need to do to grow all the different types of lettuce and chicory I've just mentioned, early in the season. You enjoy them late when you wait to sow them in spring, as is the custom in the provinces, and with most of the middle-class gardeners around here. The practice of our market gardeners is to sow their seeds under a bell as early as September or October, on ados of potting soil exposed to the south. Three weeks later, they transplant them onto other teens, as I explained for pancakes, and give the cloches a little air every day, as weather permits. They leave them to fortify themselves in this state until February, taking care to cover their bells exactly with litter during frosts, and to uncover them at daybreak: they are then strong enough to be replanted in place, if the season is right. For this, a good exposure is chosen; but beforehand, the plant must be accustomed to the open air, by gradually weaning it from the cloches, as the weather permits: for they would melt if they were put in the open ground as soon as they came out of the cloche. Some market gardeners sow them later, and raise them on a bed; but, in addition to saving manure by the above method, it is constant that the plant resists better in the open ground, when it has been raised without so much artifice. In the latter case, care should be taken to wean the plant from the meme des cloches eight or ten days before replanting. In the provinces, where C6́ one hardly knows the compost neither the layers, one can prepare light grounds mixed with horse dung, or manure of rotten sheets, which form a true compost, very good with this use as with several others. Ados can be formed from them, as I have explained; and if you don't have blown bells, you can have assembly pieces, such as those I have described in the chapter on layers. When replanting these lettuces in February or March in the open ground, if it's sunny or sunny, they should be lightly watered the next day at high noon. I have nothing to add to the description I have given of the various lettuces I have chosen and indicated as the best; now we come to the cultivation they require. First, choose the softest, smokiest soil you can find in your garden. When the beds are erected, and earthworked if possible, they are marked out to form five rows in each five-foot-wide bed, seven to eight inches apart; they are marked out very lightly with a chalk line and a pointed stick, so that they can simply be planted in a straight line: for this plant must not be buried; its heart must be flush with the surface of the soil, which must be pressed only slightly against its root with the planter. Water them as soon as they are planted, and continue watering them as the weather requires, observing during the first few days that they should only be watered in the morning, after the cool of the night has straightened out their wilted leaves from the previous day, otherwise the water will mix with the soil, suffocating their heart and delaying their growth. A distinction needs to be made for Batavia, grosse Allemande and Pomme de Berlin; it's to space them at the distance I've set in their article. After they have been well taken up, they are given a little way with the serfouette, and as many times as the soil needs to be weeded, they are serfouised; you must be exact until they are at the point of being eaten. When a lot of rain has fallen, causing them to rust and rot, if the weather is fine afterwards, they should be left to fast on water for several days at a time, so that the sun can revive and restore them. Beetle worms, and another gray worm, often make war on them by cutting their roots; care must be taken, as I said with chicory, to look for them at the foot of those you see wilted. In wet soils, limas, snails and, above all, loaches, destroy them as soon as they are born, to the point where it is sometimes impossible to keep a plant over winter, or to replant it in spring before the end of April. The latter insect devours as many as can be planted: it must then be sought with great patience at the foot of each lettuce that is seen to be gnawed, when the damage is perceptible; for there are some so small that sight can only distinguish them with great difficulty. But, as this operation costs more than it's worth, the shortest way, when you're reduced to this, is to stick to growing lettuce on a bed during these two seasons, and to wait for the onset of heat before replanting in the open ground. Care should be taken to remove as useless those that are beginning to grow; the others have more air and are more profitable. Lettuce that comes up without being replanted is usually more beautiful than that which is replanted, and is more successful as a seed. As it is customary in spring to sow the seed in beds or ribs, from which it is removed to be put back elsewhere, it is only necessary to leave a few for seed, suitably spaced. Don't forget to sow a little seed every two weeks, so you'll always have good seedlings to replant. For plants that are sown or replanted in March and April, and which sometimes need watering because of the fairly ordinary sunshine in these two months, they should only be wet in the morning or at midday. If they are wet in the evening, and there is a slight frost the next morning, they will perish, or if there is no frost, the moisture will attract loaches and limas of all kinds, which will destroy them. There are many soils where the seed, when the great heat begins, heats up in the ground and does not germinate; care must be taken to wet it every day to make it germinate; and when experience shows that this expedient is not sufficient, as is usual in dry and scorching soils, as soon as the seed has been sown and harrowed, the place must be covered with some straw mat, or large straw spread promptly, and wet over it: a few days later, when the seed begins to sprout, remove this cover at sunset, so that the night air hardens it a little. The seed of this plant is harvested in August and September, from plants that have spent the winter, as in spring. Care should be taken to mark the most beautiful apples for seed, and to prick a few sticks at the base to tie up the mounts when they begin to tire of the winds. At the same time

clean plants of all useless leaves which retain sap in the stem, and which then rot, sometimes communicating their rottenness to the stem, especially in rainy seasons: large lettuces are particularly in need of this attention. At flowering time, a small white worm is often formed in the bud as a result of some nuile: an ailment for which there is no remedy; you must therefore keep more than less at different exposures: some sometimes perish, and others escape. But the best precaution is to always have a supply from two previous years; and, if you don't have any, you can resort to another precaution, which is to reserve some for seed from two plantations, i.e., that they be planted a month apart from each other, because since these kinds of nuiles are only ruinous at flowering time, some may not yet have reached or passed this critical moment, if the others are caught. To be picked, when most of the buds are crowned with their aigrettes, and the plant is wilting and yellowing; it is then necessary to cut or uproot the plant, which is exposed upright to the sun for a few days, and as much as possible: it is then beaten, winnowed, and dryly closed by labeling. The German method is better than ours in this respect. After the plants have been exposed to the sun for two days, we take each one separately, and beat them lightly on the side of a broken barrel, at the bottom of which the seed falls as it falls: only the best detaches itself, the other, which is neither dry nor ripe, remains in its pod: we expose it again to the sun to ripen. But this second quality of seed is not nearly as good as the first, so it is only used to make lettuce for cutting: the first makes the plant much stronger and more straightforward. This is a very good practice to follow. This seed keeps well for three years; in the fourth, some of it still comes up, when it has been well-aged; but the safest thing is not to count on it. It is a little prone to bolting when sown in the same year as it is harvested; it is better not to sow it until the second year. When you're in a hurry to get it to rise, put it in a small canvas bag, and soak it in water for twenty-four hours: then tie it to the side of your chimney, or put it in a pile of very hot manure, so that it heats up for two or three days, until you see that it has sprouted; then sow it either on the ground or on the bed: however, this is only a good practice for those you want to sow on the ground; otherwise, on a bed, it always sprouts fairly quickly. It should be noted, moreover, that germs largely perish when handled and sown. CHAPTER XLV. Description of LAVENDER; its different species, properties, cultivation, etc. This plant is aromatic. There are eight species: male lavender, female lavender, English lavender, large-leaf lavender, Spanish lavender, Indian lavender, and two species of annual lavender. The male lavender, like the aspic, has a woody root, furnished with several fibers, and grows woody sprays from a foot or eighteen inches to two feet high, furnished with several knotty branches. Its leaves are green, placed in twos, and arranged in saltire, with a strong, pleasant odor and bitter flavor. Its branches all end in spikes, preceded by a long tail, garnished throughout with small bunches of one-piece blue flowers, whose upper lip is upright, rounded, cut into two parts, and the lower divided into three: they are borne on a narrow, oblong calyx, from which a pistil emerges, attached like a nail between four embryos, which change into as many seeds enclosed in a small, oblong, blackish, shiny capsule. The female lavender is similar to the previous one in root, stem and spike arrangement; but its leaf is smaller, narrower and whiter, and its tuft much higher; the odor is also less strong: the flower is of the same shape and color, but larger: the seed is round, small and brown. English lavender is shaped like the preceding varieties, but is not so bushy: its leaves are also thinner and whiter; the flower is white, and its scent sweeter. Large-leaf lavender grows as a shrub and throws out many branches: its leaves are smooth, long and pointed, not very wide, a beautiful green, flowering little or not at all, and with a scent more or less similar to the preceding ones. Spanish lavender, otherwise known as Stæchas by botanists, has extremely fine, dissected leaves of an incipient green, forming a small shrub that grows to twelve or fifteen inches. Its flowers are blue, arranged in spikes like the preceding ones, with a sweeter and slightly different scent. Indian lavender has an oblong, fairly large, thick, dark green leaf, with a scent quite similar to that of the first two species; it grows into a shrub and throws out many branches; its spikes are shorter, its flowers smaller and its color paler. Annual lavender comes in two varieties: one has a dissected, meadow-green leaf, forming a small shrub fifteen to eighteen inches high; its flower is blue, arranged in spikes like the previous two; but it is not nearly as fragrant: its seed is small and rounded, a little elongated, brown in color, tending to cinnamon. The second species grows from meadow to shrub, but its leaves are more regularly cut; it flowers in the same way, and has little odor; its seed is extremely small, longer than round, and its brown color a little reddish. The first three species are used indifferently, and have more or less the same virtues; however, in this climate, only the second is used; the others are more curious than useful. It grows naturally in Provence and part of Languedoc, and in such abundance that it is used to heat ovens, as is the case with rosemary and sage. It has more virtues in its natural climate than it has here, but it has enough everywhere to make it recommendable. Lavender leaf and flower are used interchangeably, although it is agreed that there are more virtues in the flower together with the spike than in the leaf alone. It is even claimed that lavender water, which is nothing other than the flower separated from the spike, and distilled with brandy, or simplemeat infused in a bottle of water exposed to the sun, produces even more sensitive effects: it is sovereign in wounds, in strains, and in all kinds of pain; we rub it repeatedly on the sick part, then leave a cloth or fog paper soaked in it: we mix it in all cephalic and nervous decoctions. But lavender water is not always used in accidents and unpleasant encounters; it is also used to awaken and revive the senses: we rub it on our heads after shaving, and it prevents the freshness from penetrating through the pores: it is used in cleanliness baths for both sexes; and it is as pleasant to the senses as it is useful to the body, from which it removes bad exhalations when we are subject to them. An essential oil is extracted from the whole plant, and is highly prized for diseases of the brain, hysterical vapors and epilepsy; the dose is eight or ten drops taken in a suitable liqueur. It can also be sprinkled on the volatile urine salts so often used by people prone to vapors. Four or five drops of the same oil, taken on an empty stomach, dissipate migraines and fortify the stomach. Mixed with St John's wort and chamomile oil, it makes an excellent liniment for rheumatism, paralysis and other convulsive movements. Lavender oil, also known as aspic oil, is infused into olive oil, and is equally suitable for the arts and medicine. It is used in the composition of the beautiful varnishes used on all precious paintings, and kills worms, bugs and other vermin that attach themselves to the human body. The spikes, loaded with their flowers and dried properly, are excellent, taken as an infusion like tea, for dizziness, trembling hands, convulsive movements, soporous affections, paralysis, asthma, stammering and other diseases of the nerves. Its freshly cut ears are used to make bundles to ward off bad odors; they are put in places where the air is foul, and among linen to give it a good smell. We use the flower in particular, which we detach from the spikes, to make scent sachets; and for this, after we have drawn the quantity of flowers we need, we mix in a handful of cloves and as much fine pepper, more or less, depending on whether we want the scent of lavender or spices to dominate in the sachet: the whole is put in paper bags, the opening of which is closed with glue, to. These bags are then covered with a silk cloth, which envelops them tightly. This scent mixture is far superior, according to the tests we've made of it, to all the perfume jars, which are common in many homes. I don't want to omit an important observation made by several authors on the use of lavender water or oil; it is that as much as it is sovereign when the weakness of the parts comes from cold weather or cold humors, it is just as pernicious when there is heat, because then it increases the illness by affecting the head. The first six species of lavender, which are perennials, can be multiplied by seed, cuttings or rooted plants: the first method is hardly ever used; it is easier to replant them fully formed, especially the second species, which is common in all gardens. I'll speak separately of the others, which require special care: for this one, by which I mean the female, we separate the old plants into several parts, which provide more, and which suddenly form their border. This is usually done in spring or autumn; it grows again easily without any particular care, and any soil is more or less equal to it; it resists all weathers, and stays in the same place for two or three years; but then it must be renewed, otherwise it will spread too far. Its spikes are cut when in full flower, for the use for which they are intended; they are left to dry, if seed is to be obtained, and are harvested in August or September. The first species, by which I mean male lavender, is rarely cultivated in this climate, even though it is no more delicate than the female; the reason, as far as I can judge, is that it supplies less than the other. English lavender is no more so, because it also yields little, and being very delicate, it is prone to perish in winter in the open ground: those who want to keep it safely must put it in pots and lock it up in the greenhouse. Large-leaf lavender is little known; it would, however, seem to have the same virtues, with the same odor and flavor; but, as it necessarily requires to be locked up in the greenhouse, Tome II. H this difficulty, no doubt, will always prevent it from being commonly cultivated. Spanish lavender is in the same class, and serves little purpose other than to satisfy the curiosity of the general public. The two annual species are useless, having far fewer virtues than the others. They are sown in the ground in March, then replanted: the seed ripens in September. They should be watered as needed, as should the other species, which are taken to the greenhouse for storage. With regard to the first species that can be planted from cuttings, I'd like to point out that it's much safer to start them on a warm bed than in the ground. Once they have recovered, they can be transplanted wherever you wish. CHAPTER XLVI. Description of LAUREL; its different species, properties, cultivation, etc. There are three main species of laurel; I will not mention others which have no use in cooking, and whose merit is only in the flower, to which some purely medicinal virtues are attributed, such as the two species of Alexandrian laurel, and the two species of laurel: the three species to which I will refer are the frank laurel, the royal laurel, and the cherry laurel. In warm countries, such as Spain, Sicily and the Kingdom of Naples, the bay laurel is more of a tree than a shrub: I've seen some as big as an average oak; but in our climate, which is less favorable to it, it doesn't grow nearly so strong, although it is quite common to find some that grow to ten or twelve feet, and sometimes more. Its roots are large and uneven, with many fibers; its stem is smooth and knotless; its bark is thin and grayish or brown; its wood is porous and fragile; it grows a number of branches that form a beautiful head when the lower part is pruned. Its leaves are oblong, pointed, hard and evergreen, sinewy, smooth, fragrant, with a pungent, aromatic and slightly bitter taste, attached by short tails. Its flowers are one-piece, cut into four or five parts, white or yellowish in color; the flowers are followed by fruits or berries as large as small cherries, oblong, green when born and black when ripe, H 2 under whose skin is a shell containing an oblong seed: these berries are fragrant, aromatic, oily and bitter. The royal laurel differs from the former in the color of its leaves, which are a cheerful green and have neither odor nor aromatic taste; but it resembles it in flower and fruit, with the difference, however, that the flowers of the royal laurel are born in clusters, whereas those of the frank laurel are born from the armpits of the leavesThe cherry laurel is neither a regular tree nor a shrub; It has no stem that forms a true tree body, and its branches are too extensive to be considered a shrub. Its stems are straight and twiggy; its bark is plain brown; its wood is white and very brittle; its leaf is similar to that of the lemon tree, but larger, wider and slightly serrated at the edges: it is very smooth, a beautiful shiny green, with a slightly bitter astringent taste. The flower is small, rose-shaped, with five white, odorless petals, and is followed by a fleshy, almost oval fruit, about the size of a sloe, green when it is born and reddish-black when it ripens, beneath which is a thin shell containing an oblong, bitter seed. This species has no other known cooking qualities than to give boiled milk an almond-like taste: a leaf or 2 is added to soups, and to certain soft cheeses eaten for dessert. The leaf is sometimes used medicinally: it is astringent and fortifies the stomach. This plant keeps its greenery all year round, and in winter is a delight to the eye. In warm countries, it is used to form paths and fences in the same way as we plant bower bushes: pruned in straight lines, it produces a very beautiful effect. Here, where the climate is different, we keep to a few feet, more for pleasure than for real need. We are obliged to place them at the foot of the south-facing walls, to protect them from the severe frosts that often kill them; but the plant usually grows back the following spring. As this plant is very vigorous, it is soon resuscitated: it requires no particular care, other than to be ploughed in the normal seasons, and to be supported by a few trellises or small poles attached to the wall; those who are very curious about it stuff it during the winter, but this care can be dispensed with. It is commonly propagated from offshoots that are detached from old plants, or from cuttings that are left to wither on the ground before being put back in place. 3. It can also be propagated from seed, but it will take longer to form. The first two species of laurel are the really useful ones, and are used indifferently; but the first is as common in this country as the second is uncommon. Its dried leaves are used in most stews and short broths, both fatty and lean; they are also used to crown hams; and they are also used, interwoven with fruit, to brighten up winter desserts. Its medicinal properties are far more numerous. Its leaves are astringent, resolving and warm; they fortify the stomach, aid digestion and dispel wind: they are taken in infusions of five to six like tea, or in powder, at a dose of two large ones, to restore the stomach, prevent nausea and dispel the pain of windy colic. Externally, they are used in fomentations with aromatic herbs, to fortify numb parts in cases of paralysis, rheumatism, etc. They are also good externally, for softening tumors caused by wasp stings; taken as a gargle, they soothe toothache. Its berries have even more virtue and warmth than its leaves, especially those brought from hot countries: they are used for diseases of the stomach, liver, rat and bladder; they digest raw humours; they divide and resolve thickened and viscous juices; they awaken appetite, dispel disgust, lift obstructions of the liver and spleen, excite urination, induce menstruation, and push out backwardness. An excellent oil is obtained for nerve diseases, paralysis, convulsions, colic and stomach weakness: this oil is obtained by expresion, by coction in boiling water, or by distillation; and it is given both internally, in small doses of ten to twelve drops, and externally as a liniment. A spirit with the same virtues can also be obtained by fermenting its fruits. For windy colic in particular, and for stomach weakness, one infuses four ounces of good wine with one large berry and two large dried orange peel, and drinks it with a little sugar; I have tried this remedy on myself with perfect success. These berries have virtues that extend to almost everything, and they are used in the composition of many unguens and electuaries, in orvietan, in theriac and in dressings. Laurel grows naturally in all H 4 warm countries, and particularly on sunny sea coasts; it grows straight up to a height of thirty or forty feet, forming a well-stocked, evergreen head that provides pleasant shade. It can be propagated from seed or from marcottes. In the first case, the seed is sown in March in crates or tubs filled with soft earth, mixed with potting soil, at a distance of three inches from one seed to the next, sunk a finger deep, and covered with a little potting soil or chopped dung; beforehand, it's a good idea to soak the seed in manure water for a few days, as it sprouts more quickly and reliably. These boxes are placed in full sun at the foot of a wall, and watered often: sometimes the plant is strong enough to be replanted the following year, either in boxes or in the ground; but the safest thing is to wait until the second year. In the second case, at the end of March, branches from old plants are placed in dummies, where they are held in place with hooks: they take root immediately, and can be weaned the following September; this is the best way to have fairly well-formed plants; but this plant usually grows a lot of offshoots from the foot, which are separated and replanted to form others, with only the care of watering them often in the first year. As for the old plants, they require no cultivation other than to be ploughed during the seasons, and brought closer together from time to time when they grow too far apart: the branches, which are removed and left to dry, are used for the various purposes for which this plant is suited. CHAPTER XLVII. Description of the EPICERIA; its various species, properties, cultivation, etc. THIS plant, although little known to gardeners, is useful for food and health; it is an annual. There are five species, but I will describe only one, as the others have no properties. Botanists call it nigella semine aromatico, to distinguish it from other nielles, a name which is really its own; but the one I give it is the one it bears in Italy. The plant bears a close resemblance to the anemone; its foliage is cut in the same way, but H 5 more delicate, finer and lighter green; its root is yellowish, with many fibers: Its stem is small, fluted and branched; its branches all end in a white, slightly bluish, odorless flower, arranged in a five-petal star shape, with several stamens in the middle, surrounded by a crown of small oblong bodies; the flowers are followed by small, fairly large fruits, ending in five or six horns, and divided into several chambers containing angular, hazelnut-colored seeds with an aromatic odor and a very pungent taste. This seed is sometimes used in foods in place of the four spices; it is true that it has the taste of nutmeg, clove, cinnamon and pepper all at once: however, it is not commonly used in France; but it is used in various parts of Italy. This seed is useful in medicine, because it is incisive, aperitive, vulnerative and resolutive; when chewed, it stimulates salivation, and when taken as a decoction or mixed in food, it increases the milk of nursing mothers; it induces months in women, resists venom and fights quarte fever; it is also good for killing worms and driving away winds. It is prepared according to the type of illness. This plant is sown on a bed in March, and the seedling is good for transplanting in May: it is very delicate, and requires loose, earthy soil. Plants should be spaced six to eight inches apart, and no wider or taller than an ordinary anemone. It thrives best in the hottest climate, which is why it is much stronger in Italy, where its seed is much wittier than here. It should be placed in a good spot, and watered often in dry spells. The seed is harvested in August, and keeps well for several years, both for sowing and for various uses, provided it is kept in well-stoppered bottles. Among the four species I have not mentioned, there is one called nigella cretica, whose seed is even stronger than the previous one, but I do not know its virtues. H6 CHAPTER XLVIII. Description of lamb's lettuce; its different species, properties, cultivation, etc. Lamb's lettuce is a small winter salad, naturally produced in the countryside in all temperate countries, but which becomes stronger and more abundant when cultivated. It goes by many names, depending on the country where it grows: blanchette, poule grasse, doucette, and salade de chanoine; all these names are its own. It's a small plant, not as wide as your hand; its leaves are long and narrow, and spill to the ground in a smooth, light green. It is cut from the root and eaten whole, with only the bad leaves removed. Some people eat it on its own, while others mix it with celery or chicory, and others with beet and anchovy. It is generally well liked, and much of it is consumed, especially as it is healthy, refreshing, soothing and laxative. It is used in veal and chicken broths to soothe the ardor of fever, and to ease the pain of nephritis. It is also used successfully in rheumatism, gout, scurvy and hypochondriac: lastly, it is very good for correcting the acridity of humors and the excessive saltiness of the blood. The latter differs from the former in that its leaf is much broader, and the plant a little stronger, which makes it more profitable: it is also much later to come up in spring, and consequently serves longer than the other; but it is not so sweet to the mouth, being covered with a kind of down which displeases most people, and which makes it less marketable than the other: our market gardeners grow very little of it. This plant requires very loose, well-amended soil: it can be sown very thickly, because it is always thinned out by pulling out the strongest. The weakest, which is then left to grow, is used to produce seed: it essentially needs to be loosened and moistened often until it has sprouted. The first sowing is done in mid-August, and is eaten at the end of autumn. We continue to sow from fortnight to fortnight until mid-October, to have some without interruption: the one sown in October is the best for Lent, when it has risen well before the frosts. It is weeded when it has emerged, and this is the only way it can be harvested. It can be sown in spring and in the following months, if you have a taste for this plant; it succeeds in all seasons, provided it is well watered, and placed in the shade in the warmer months; but in summer, lettuces are rightly preserved, and all this time lamb's lettuce is forgotten. To collect the seed, at the end of winter a few beds are thinned to three-quarters, and the rest left to grow: each plant, if it is at ease, grows to a spread of a foot in diameter, similar to a flowering talaspicum; and all its little branches are first loaded with small bunches of white flowers, tending to blue, to which the seed follows, which is small, flat, and yellowish in color, I mean common. Italian lamb's lettuce is quite different, being small and long, ashen in color, with a small black dot at one end: its flower is also different, being an obscure white tending to red. As soon as it approaches maturity, it detaches itself at the slightest breath of wind; consequently, you must prevent it from falling, by plucking the stalks in the morning with the dew, as soon as the stems begin to yellow; but far from exposing it to the sun, like other seeds, you must place it in a heap with its cossate in some cool, poorly ventilated place, and let this mass warm up and partially rot; the seed feeds and perfects itself in this humidity. After a fortnight, shake the cossate with a fork, the seed falls out, and is then exposed to the air for a few days; it is then winnowed and enclosed. This seed is good for seven to eight years, I mean the common one; but you mustn't sow it the first year, as it sprouts too late and with too much difficulty. The seed of the other species will only keep for four or five years. Our market gardeners, sometimes lacking clean places or leisure, collect it quite differently; they let it ripen on the ground and fall, then they tear off the cossate, and pick up with a broom all this seed spread on the ground; and as they pick up as much earth as seed, they put the whole in tubs with half-fulls of water, and stir it well: The earth sinks to the bottom, and the seed floats to the top; they remove it cleanly with a skimmer, or in any other way they like, and dry it, after which they lock it up. In light soils with a lot of earth, like theirs, this method is quite practicable; but in fatter and wetter soils, it would be very difficult to follow it, besides the seed would be prone to germinate on the ground, if it stayed there too long, and if it suffered some rain. CHAPTER XLIX. Description of MARIOLAINE; its different species, properties, calture, etc. DA marjoram is an aromatic plant of which there are many species, some perennial, others annual; the main ones cultivated in gardens, both for pleasure and utility, are wild marjoram, common marjoram, shell marjoram, variegated marjoram, small-leaved marjoram and hairy marjoram. Wild marjoram, otherwise known as oregano, has many small, woody, filamentous roots; it grows a number of small, hard, quarrelsome, hairy stems; its leaves are almost heart-shaped, brown-green, hairy, fragrant, and about the size of a child's fingernail. Its flowers are born at the top of its stems in spindly, scaly spikes that form large bunches. These flowers are small, arranged in the shape of a mouth, or a pipe cut into two whitish-red lips at the top: the flowers are followed by very small, almost round seeds, enclosed in an oblong capsule that served as the flower's calyx. Common marjoram is no less abundant in roots, which are also small and fibrous; it grows many small stems, rising to eight or ten inches, which together form a rounded tuft: These stems are woody, twiggy, small, a little hairy and reddish, lined with leaves arranged opposite each other, almost round in shape, soft, whitish, covered with a white down, half the size of oregano, with a strong, penetrating and very pleasant smell, and a slightly acrid and bitter taste. Its flowers are born at the ends of its branches, gathered in the form of a spike or head, rounder and shorter than those of oregano, composed of four rows of leaves laid in scales: these flowers are very small, whitish in color, one-piece in mouth, whose upper lip is upright, rounded, indented, and the lower divided into three parts; the flowers are followed by small, almost round, russet-colored, fragrant and bitter seeds. Shell marjoram, also known as gentile marjoram, differs from this variety in that its leaves are rounder, closer together and hollowed out in the shape of a shell; they are also whiter in color and smell sweeter. The flower is arranged in a spike like its predecessors. variegated marjoram is similar in all respects to the previous variety, except for the color of its leaves, which are mottled pale yellow; some are even completely yellow, making it a pleasing variety to the eye. Small-leaf marjoram differs from the above only in that its leaf is extremely small, similar to that of ordinary thyme, but more rounded: it is also the most fragrant of all. Hairy thyme is a species similar to the previous one, but its leaf is slightly larger and more hairy, which gives it its name: its spikes are also shorter. This plant is not very useful with foodstuffs; however, it is often mixed in, not only to make them more pleasant, but also to correct their flattery and make them easier to digest, especially with peas, beans and fish. It is also very pleasant to the sense of smell and, as much for these reasons as for its particular virtues, there is no garden. where one is not eager to have a few borders of it, or at least a few feet. It is cephalic, pectoral, stomachic, hysteric and sternutatory; all the species have more or less the same virtues. The powder of its leaves and flowers, dried in the shade, is very suitable for making serosities flow through the nose. The infusion of its flowers is also used successfully to suppress urination and menstruation, and is equally effective in making asthmatics and those with stubborn coughs cough up; the former is used in preference to the other species for these illnesses. Its distilled water is a marvellous help in indigestion, sour reports and winds: in these cases, the essential oil of this plant, the syrup or the preserve, can also be used. The oil in particular delights the senses, and soothes tooth pain, by soaking a cotton ball in it and placing it in the hollow of the affected tooth. For head colds and torticollis, oregano is dried over a fire, wrapped warm in a cloth and applied to the head. Marjoram powder, mixed with apricot marmalade or preserved orange blossom, is good for epilepsy, vertigo and trembling. Finally, this plant, however it is used, is recognized as sovereign for all cold diseases of the brain and head, for those of the chest and stomach, and for drawing out all aqueous humors. Marjoram can be propagated from seed, cuttings or offshoots, which are seeded or replanted in the same way as all other aromatic plants. The first two species are usually planted in borders around vegetable garden quarters, and withstand the winter without any particular care; but the others require a greenhouse in this season, and must therefore be raised in pots to enclose them more easily, and give them as much air as possible in their prison, moistening them a little if necessary. It is advisable to renew it from time to time, when you notice that it is being stripped, and that it is growing only weakly. Description of MELISSA: its different species, properties, cultivation, etc. MELISSA, otherwise known as lemon grass, is a perennial, fragrant plant. There are four species: garden lemon balm, wild lemon balm, Roman lemon balm and Pyrenean lemon balm, but the latter three are not cultivated in gardens. Its root is woody, long, round and fibrous; its stems are numerous, rising to a height of about two feet, almost square in shape, firm and stubby; its leaves are oblong and pointed, similar to those of common balsam, covered with short hairs, toothed at the edges, light green, with a pleasant lemon scent and a slightly pungent taste. Its flowers are born in the leaf axils, forming rings that are not complete around the stem, nor completely whorled; they are small, mouth-shaped, reddish or yellowish when born, forming a kind of pipe cut into two lips, supported by a calyx made into a hairy cone: each flower is followed by four slightly oblong seeds, stuck together, enclosed in the calyx where the flower is held. This plant is of little use in food: its young leaves are sometimes used in salad dressings, and some others put them in omelettes, as parsley is used, but this is not common. It delights the sense of smell; that's what's most flattering about it. Its greatest properties are in medicine, where it is often and very usefully employed; it is hysterical, cephalic and stomachic. Its dried or even fresh leaves, infused as a tea in half a pint of water, are sovereign for all diseases of the brain and those of women, for palpitations of the heart, for fainting, for vertigo, even for paralysis and for deciduous disease: a handful can also be lightly boiled in veal stock. A water made from it is sovereign for apoplexy, lethargy and epilepsy, as well as for vapors, colic, menstrual and urinary suppression; a spoonful is taken neat, or mixed in a glass of water, depending on the type of illness, more or less violent. This water, as well known as Eau des Carmes as Eau de Mélisse, is not a special secret, as many people imagine: M. Lémery has given a dispensation of it, which is recognized as the best: any private individual with a still can easily test it; and a pint, which is sold publicly for 20 livres, costs only 6 or 7 livres at most if you make it yourself. Here's how to make it. Take six handfuls of fresh lemon balm leaves, and an ounce of dried lemon peel, as much nutmeg and coriander; add half an ounce of clove and cinnamon; crush these drugs well, crush the leaves, and put the whole into a vessel suitable for distilling them, with two pounds of white wine and half a pound of brandy; Leave this mixture to digest for three days, after covering the vessel with its lid, to which you will add the container, the openings of which you will plug exactly; and then distill this material over a moderate sand fire, or in a bain-marie. A simpler lemon balm water is also made, without mixing the above drugs, to be used in cordial and hysterical potions, in a dose of six or eight ounces, like the others. It is usually only made from rooted plants, although it also comes from seed. We separate the offspring from old plants that produce a lot of it, and plant them in March in good, well-smoked soil, in a slightly shady spot: it does very well around fruit trees. It is sometimes prone to degeneration; when this is noticed, it should be destroyed and new ones planted. If it is not easy to find it in the vicinity, it grows naturally in the woods in certain countries, which must be distinguished from wild lemon balm, which also grows there, and which is very different, having a very unpleasant odor. The one I'm talking about is cut every year in autumn, just above ground level, and grows back in spring: it stays in place for a very long time, and withstands all bad weather without the need for any precautions. Its leaves are used for salad as soon as they begin to form, but are not used for distillation until July and August, when they are more perfected by the sun. CHAPTER CHAPTER LI. Description of the Melon - its different species, properties, cultivation, etc. The melon is a fruit cherished by all nations. The plant that produces it resembles the cucumber in many respects; its roots are extensive, straight, very hairy and yellowish in color; its stems, called arms by all gardeners, are sarmentose, hairy, untied, long, branchy and crawl along the ground. Its leaves alternate in appearance, borne on a three- to four-inch, medium-sized, hollow tail: they are almost round in shape, heart-shaped, light green and a little hairy; from the base and axils of the leaves come clusters of flowers, one-piece, bell-shaped, flared and split into five parts, star-shaped, very pale yellow; some are sterile, others fertile, where the fruit is uncovered even before they bloom: the fruit is smooth, shaped like an olive, green in color, Tome II. I but as it grows it takes on the shape, color and size of its species, which varies greatly, and also ripens sooner or later. its point of ripeness. It is a pleasant, refreshing food, and easy to digest when eaten in moderation; but excess produces wind and colic, sometimes followed by dyssentery and stomach upsets that are difficult to cure: We have also seen very severe quart fevers resulting from the immoderate use of melon, especially among people of a certain age, who should abstain from it, as should those of a pituiteux or melancholic temperament; each person must be his own doctor in this respect, and know how to deprive himself of a food that is harmful to his health. The Italians make an excellent jam from the rib of this fruit, which in their country is very thick, Provence melons; but I don't know if the rib of those we grow here, being much thinner, would be equally suitable for jamming. Here, we only pickle in vinegar, as we do with gherkins, those that are picked and thinned in May, when there is too much knotting, and which are then the size of a Spanish ôlive. Connoisseurs find them very delicate: see the chapter on cucumbers for instructions on how to preserve them. Its seed is one of the four cold seeds; it is used in all emulsions to cool overheated entrails, to temper the ardor of fever, and to provoke urination; it also enters into the composition of orgeat, the recipe for which I have given in the Pumpkin article. The southern countries are the most savory for this fruit; its taste is also much stronger there than elsewhere, and we are not obliged to give it the infinite care that we take here. We sow it in the ground like all vegetables, and let it grow freely without any cultivation other than crimping it once or twice: the sun does the rest, and gives it a quality that ours never have to the same degree; however, there are years when ours come very close to it; I have even seen Italians and Provençals be very pleased with it on more than one occasion. There are an infinite number of melon species, but I'll limit myself to a dozen or so that are the most successful in our climate, without claiming that they should be preferred everywhere to other species; on the contrary, I urge those who live in different climates to stick to species other than those I've adopted, once they've seen them succeed; and I'm happy to repeat I 2 here what I've already said more than once: such and such a soil will not be suitable for such and such a species of plant, which will be perfectly suited to such and such another; el vice versâ; and on this occasion I won't say precisely Tot capita, tot sensus; but rather, more or less and in an equally true sense, Tot littora, tot fructus. The species I want to talk about are: Le Melon Français, De Langeais. otherwise known as- A graine blanche. mé le maraîcher. A graine rouge. Des Carmes, which is Le Morin. of three kinds; the long De Florence, the round, and the white Sucrin de Tours. S. Nicolas à côte. large and medium. In the French melon class, which is the most widely cultivated, there are regularly round ones, flattened ones, and others with an elongated shape; some are more embroidered, others less so; some have more cut foliage, others less so; some are earlier, others later: should we conclude from these differences that the French melon is divided into as many species, or simply believe, as some do!Are we to conclude from these differences that the French melon is divided into so many species, or are we to believe, as some do, that they are simply a game of nature? For my part, without undertaking to treat the matter as a philosopher, I will declare myself in favor of the latter opinion; and I will say that these differences cannot in fact be regarded as characterizing species. In order for a production to claim the dignity of a species, it must be fixed and determined; in order for it to be believed fixed and determined, it must be followed without variation in its productions and multiplications, in much the same way as matter, which, although thrown repeatedly by the caster into the matrix, always renders bodies perfectly equal in form. Now, the productions we are talking about vary in their reproductions: for example, the seed of a round melon sometimes produces elongated ones, sometimes flattened ones; they are therefore without sequence, without determination; they are therefore only a game of nature which has no law for them, and only the effect of a passing meeting of the elements. But whether they are species or accidents, that's what I don't set out to describe; and I'm only concerned with the genus of the simple melon, I mean the market garden melon. Its shape varies, as I have just said, but in general its skin is extremely embroidered around the circumference, with no marked ribs and very full. It differs in these respects from melons from hot countries, which all have marked and very thick ribs, and which are as empty as they are full, with conse3 quently little flesh, albeit delicious. The color is very red, and it has a lot of water; but the taste, an essential ingredient, is not always equally good. In dry and hot years, it is vinous and sweet; but in cold and rainy years, art brings it to the truth, but cannot give it all the quality it could have. The avarice of those who grow them also very often makes this fruit despicable; their main aim is usually to make it large, because they think they can sell it better; and they don't realize that it can only be made large by watering, which takes away all its taste. The long Carmelite melon, like the round one, originally comes from Saumur; it was brought to the King's kitchen garden, from where it passed to the Carmelites, who took particular care in cultivating it. Its flesh is more or less red, full of water, very sweet, with a strong taste; but it is sometimes a little soft and mushy. Don't let it ripen too much: it's good in June if it's been brought forward; the skin yellows a little as it ripens. Some degenerate and ripen a little, and some are even slightly ribbed; but this difference is only external and does not change their quality; they are no less good, very thin-ribbed and very full. The round melon, apart from its shape, which is round and small, has all the same qualities, and ripens at the same time. The white melon has a smooth, whitish skin and a slightly oval shape; it is a good size, and ripens at the same time as the two mentioned above; it is preferred by many connoisseurs: I can assure you that I have eaten some that left nothing to be desired. The Saint Nicolas has an elongated shape, a slightly greenish color, evenly marked ribs, mediocre size, very thin ribs, firm, red flesh, full of water, sweet, vinous, and superior in finesse to those we have mentioned; it ranks first among the species we cultivate: it ripens in July. There is a very similar species, called the Avignon melon: some claim that it is the same under another name; others maintain that it is different: I would rather be of the former opinion than of the latter; for, having compared the two, I have seen only a slight difference in shape, which is a little more pointed in the Avignon melon than in the Saint-Nicolas. The Langeais melon bears the name of the 14 countries where it is best known, if it did not originate there. It has a slightly elongated shape, regularly marked with ribs, is a coarse green at birth, and golden yellow as it ripens; its size varies from year to year, sometimes being very small, and in the past being almost as big as market gardeners. It is also prone to embroidery, more or less, and sometimes not at all. Its flesh is red and firm, its taste sweet and vinous; it has a lot of water and is very full. A considerable quantity used to come from Langeais to Paris every year, but since we've succeeded in raising them well enough here, this little trade no longer exists. However, we must admit that it is better here than it is here, because it grows naturally in its own soil. Now, any fruit that comes without artifice is always superior in quality to that which is forced; it ripens in June and July. The white-seeded melon is a very early species of Carmelite melon; its shape is oval, of medium size; its skin is green and smooth, which changes very little as it ripens; its flesh is full of sweet water, but not sufficiently spicy; it is very delicate to raise: these two defects prevent it from being as widely cultivated as the others: it ripens in June: its seed is white, and gave it its name. CHAPTER LI. 201 The red-seeded melon is round, of medium size; its flesh is firm, red, and has a sweet, vinous taste: it ripens in July: its seed is a golden yellow, almost red. The Morin melon is a market garden variety, round and slightly flattened, with a sort of crown around the eye; its flesh is firm and red, with a sweet, vinous taste. Its flesh is firm and red, its taste sweet and vinous. It is very full, embroiders perfectly, and is usually a fortnight ahead of the maraîcher. It differs from ordinary market gardeners in that the little background that can be seen between the embroidery is a coarse black-green. The Florence melon, whose proper name is cantalupi, comes in four varieties: green, black, orange and white; all four are equally marked, very full and very early; they differ from one another in the following respects. The green one is small, a little elongated, with a few warts; the skin is green, with little embroidery; the flesh is red and sweet: it yellows a little as it ripens on the sunny side: its defect is that it splits. When it ripens perfectly, it's an excellent melon; but in this climate it doesn't always succeed, being good in mid-June. The black melon is a darker L4 green, round and small, with similar warts; it also splits, but does not change color as it ripens; in addition, it has the same qualities as the previous melon. It is the most esteemed of the four, being the sweetest and windiest; in Italy, it is called il melone degli santi, the melon of the saints: it ripens at the same time. The orange melon is elongated without warts, turns yellow as it ripens, embroiders a little and does not split; but its taste is a little less intense than that of the two preceding varieties: it ripens a fortnight later. The white variety is round and ribbed like the others, but larger: its flesh is white, full of water and very sweet; its rib is rather thick, so it has less flesh: it ripens in July. It should be noted that these four species of cantalupi require much more nourishment than the others, as their ribs are thicker and more spongy: the bed should be filled with six to eight inches of potting soil; you can even mix a third of prepared soil with the potting soil, as is done in Holland; the fruit is fatter and better nourished. The gros sucrin de Tours is the size of a market-gardener, extraordinarily embroidered and unevenly round, with imperceptibly marked ribs; its flesh is firm and red, with a sweet, spicy taste, full of water; it turns yellow slightly as it ripens, and is usually good only in August: it is an excellent melon. The small sucrin is at most half the size of the previous one; it is round and flattened; its skin is green: it changes little as it ripens, it is very good and full, with a spicy taste; but its smallness greatly diminishes its merit: it ripens the same way in August. From these details, we can see that the small species surpass the large market melon in all good qualities: water, finesse, sugar, everything is there; they are also earlier, and in the late season they do not lose their good qualities like the large species, which, although it ripens, has no taste after the first days of September. They also have the double advantage of stopping facilemént, and of being generally all good in ordinary years. The previous year (1749), despite all the bad weather that accompanied it, a private individual in Versailles found cantaloupe that were good on the fifteenth of June, and which were sent to the King. However, our market gardeners apply themselves only to the first species, because of its size, and the others are only seen in private homes where they are grown for themselves, which is a great loss to the public. 16 I'm not talking about the little early carmelite, which does very well in what it is, and which ripens first of all; but which, because of its smallness, is not worth the expense of cultivation, being no bigger than a calvil apple, and being a little sweet besides. I've heard a lot of praise for a species from Italy called the crimson melon, because its flesh is crimson red. A foreign lord, curious about this crop, also assured me that it was very successful in Germany, where he grew it himself every year: from which one might conclude that it should be even more successful here; however, it is not known here, and whatever research I have made, I have only been able to obtain imperfect information about it: the curious, within reach of its natural country, will be able to find out more about it. Some people like to grow a particular kind of watermelon, known here as the melon d'eau, and in Provence as the pasteque; it comes round, extraordinarily large, weighing up to thirty pounds and more. The flesh is crimson red, the seed black, and the skin green and smooth; but it is a waste to grow it, as it is not edible here; it is barely edible in its own country for those who have not accustomed their taste to it from youth. Its main merit is that it has a lot of refreshing water; but it's a bland water that neither flatters nor awakens; I testify to this with many people who have often tasted it as I have, both in Italy and Provence. There is another species that is very similar, but smaller, and which differs on the outside only by a string of yellow spots, arranged in the form of ribs that all end at the head of the fruit, whose seed is red, and which is known as the American melon; but it is only used for jam, and is excellent for this purpose. It can be cultivated for this purpose: it succeeds very well, and yields a lot: last year I collected twenty-one on four feet, all well conditioned. Its leaf is extremely indented, similar to that of the watermelon; it stops late, and it's only at the end of its branches that the fruit knots; but it grows visibly as soon as it's stopped: it requires a lot of space and little pruning. I could also name a species that is very excellent in the kingdoms of Naples and Sicily, which is known here as the green Morée melon or the Candie melon, and in Italy as the winter melon. The flesh is white, similar to that of the cucumber, very elongated; the skin smooth, pale green; and it has the advantage of keeping all winter hanging on the floor in a dry, airy place. In Naples, they are tied around the windows of outdoor houses: the temperature of this climate allows this, as it almost never freezes. This melon is full of water and so sweet that its excessive sweetness displeases some people; but this small defect does not prevent it from being a pleasure in the ungrateful season in which it is grown. I've seen it succeed a few times here, when the year has been favorable; but often it has neither taste nor quality. We can always risk the event of a few feet. For those who don't have it, it's time to give them an understanding of melon cultivation, by which I mean cultivation such as nature commonly demands, and not that for which art should be exhausted. It would undoubtedly be pleasant for individuals to be able in some way to have this fruit two months earlier than nature allows; and there is no doubt that by means of layers, it would be possible to serve it at the beginning of May, whereas it is not commonly eaten until July, and only abounds in August and September: I don't even doubt that in Spain, the kingdom of Naples and Sicily, Barbary, America and other regions close to the line, we could, by helping nature in its months of rest, have some throughout the year; we can at least presume this by the example of England, where, although the climate is very different, I mean much less clean, some curious people manage to have some, admittedly devoid of taste, but two months earlier than us: an example that has just been successfully put into practice, by a French Lord * who, with the greatest talents and all the knowledge that makes famous characters, recommendable and dear to the State, has always been a lover of plants, and has never disdained their cultivation. But however rare and satisfying this curiosity must have been here, it could only suit people to whom opulence allows everything; it would not be possible to find many who would devote themselves to it: one needs greenhouses glazed and built at great expense, an understanding man who always watches over them, which one can hardly find; an even and continuous fire, and much care besides, which * M. le Maréchal de Bellisle, having recently found himself in the vicinity of London, noticed this little marvel of nature, and set about imitating it as soon as he got to France. On his return home to Bizy, he actually carried it out, having brought with him an English gardener experienced in this cultivation, who, finding new facilities in our climate, succeeded in the first year, and presented him with some at the beginning of May. The plants are still, to tell the truth, only a fruit for the eyes, and too bad to be eaten. The method which produces melons a little later, but which are good, and which only require costs that everyone can afford, is therefore always to be preferred, and this is the one I'm going to develop. The bed of small melons is prepared at the beginning of January; whatever the quantity of seed you wish to sow, a length of nine feet in the bed is more than sufficient, as each bell can easily contain twenty-five to thirty seeds; on this length you can place twenty or more bells, which would make up five to six hundred feet; this is much more than is necessary for the first seed, even for those who trade in them. Individuals who are not in this situation can fill in as many bells as they see fit, and can occupy the remainder with lai tues or cucumbers. The bed should be no more than 2 and a half feet, or 3 wide, to make it easier to heat up, and three and a half feet high before being filled with compost. The dose of potting soil should not exceed three inches, for the reason I'll explain shortly: of the remainder, it is trained as explained in the chapter on layers. If it is greasy or too coarse, the plants are liable to melt: the same disadvantage results if it has already been used to blanch celery or chicory, as a small amount of hair remains, absorbing the heat and giving off a hint of corruption: if it is too old, it does not have enough virtue to raise the fruit to perfection, and it is often because of this defect that the plant melts halfway through. To do this properly, use only two-year-old compost, which has not produced anything during the second year, and which has been stirred from time to time; in this way, it will have acquired new salts during the resting period: nothing is easier than to set aside a small supply every year for the following year. Market gardeners generally don't start sowing large melons until the end of February, which saves them a month of rigorous care. Their main reason for not hurrying them any further is to save on their manures; for they make two seasons for them, as they put it, i.e., they serve them twice, being first used to raise turnips and lettuces, most of which only come up in March and April, and then for melons; They then turn over the old layers, and mixing the old manure with a third of the new, they make up new ones, on which they replant their melons, which are good in this time to put in place. This shows that it's not the risk of sowing them earlier that holds them back, but only the expense and care involved; for the large species they cultivate is less delicate than the smaller ones: If the weather is set for frost, put an inch of pigeon droppings in the middle of the bed, i.e. the thickness of manure, and cover it with old straw mats or warm manure after it has been sown; this helps it to warm up, and keeps it warmer for longer. When it's ready to sow, the most common practice is to sow the seed under a bell in holes made with a finger, two inches apart. Two or three seeds are placed in each hole, without covering them, or at least very lightly; but when you have sowing frames, which is much more advantageous, as I explained in the chapter on Layers, you sow it in the open .field, and when the soil is covered with it, so that each grain is an inch apart, we press the tip of our finger on each grain to press it in, and then pass our hand over the whole surface of the soil, to cover it lightly; but as there are few people provided with these frames, I must here mainly have in view the practice of bells, which is the most general, and match the principles to this method. It is to be hoped that the day we sow will be a fine one: in which case you can leave the bed uncovered until sunset ; but, as you can't delay, whatever the weather, when it's at its warmest, if you're threatened by snow or ice, or if the frost bites hard, you must cover as soon as you've sown, and bind the bells, i.e. swaddle them with litter or regain, so that they are completely covered, and double the covers during the night, as the weather requires: Fifteen days later, sow a few more bells on another end of the bed, and continue from fifteen to fifteen days until the end of March, to make up for the lack of the first ones when they die, and to have a succession of them until September. It is therefore important to ensure this succession of fruits, to prolong a pleasure that would certainly be too short-lived if we stuck only to the first melons. Your first well-cared-for seedling will sprout six to seven days after it has been placed in the ground, whatever the severity of the weather; for it obeys the warmth of the soil in which it grows; and when the seedling is a few days older, it needs to be put into the ground, i.e. a little bit of soil is brought to the base with the finger, and lightly pressed. The most difficult part of growing melons is preserving the plant: too much or too little heat melts it; too little or too much air has the same effect, and great care must be taken to maintain this degree of heat. To get it to breathe properly, you have to take the moments, so to speak, on the fly. During heavy frosts, the sun usually comes out; you need to take advantage of this, and uncover the bells' bottoms from around ten in the morning, until three or four in the afternoon; but the bottom must always be kept low. Sometimes, too, it's cold and sunless, and that's the worst kind of weather you can have; but you have to choose a few hours during the day to uncover the bells a little, otherwise the plant will turn yellow and melt under the covers. But if the fog is thick, or if it's snowing or raining, leave everything covered for as long as it lasts: the plants are in great danger, but there's no remedy. Manure vapors, as much as the deprivation of air, ruin plants in this situation: they attach themselves to the stems and leaves, and this humidity melts them: they fall again on these young plants in large drops, from the glass where they collect, and rust them. This last disadvantage can be overcome by placing pieces of woollen cloth under each bell, supported by three small sticks between the plants and the glass; the vapour attaches itself to them, and you change them from time to time to make them dry alternately; but I confess that this is a great subjection: when you have frames, the operation is much easier and safer, as I said in the first chapter. When a few fine, mild, quiet days arrive, you need to take advantage of them as much as you can to give the plant a bit of air, since it is stretching out in its shirt and becoming tender; one hour after sunrise, remove all or most of the bedding between the bells; and two hours later, the bell is given a finger's breadth of daylight, on the opposite side to the wind; it is lowered one hour before sunset, and the blankets are put back on, thicker or thinner, according to any change that may have occurred in the weather. The most important thing to keep the plant in vigor is to maintain the warmth of the bed by means of the stoves: every day, you stick your hand into the middle, and as soon as you realize it's cold, you warm it up; a few days later, when the heat of this first stove has considerably diminished, you reshuffle them, and always wet them as you change or reshuffle them, if the manures are dry. Finally, in all respects, follow what I said in the chapter on layers, which I won't repeat here: you should also follow what I've said about blankets; and don't neglect to have straw mats for the ease of shaking off snow when it falls, and to prevent rain from penetrating the layers. They are also very useful to protect them from the sleet of March, which is deadly for plants; nothing cools a bed more: you must be careful to prevent it when the weather seems ready, and remove the mats as soon as the sleet has passed. When the severe cold is over, you should always give the bells a little air during most of the day (a finger's breadth is enough), but when the weather begins to warm up, you should leave the same amount of air night and day. However, it must be said that until the end of April, it is almost always advisable to lower the bells at night. The method I have just described is used by all gardeners and market gardeners in general. I have found myself obliged to place it first, as the most authoritative; but on the example of some very well-meaning individuals, I have tried another which I have recognized as better; you are free to experiment with it. Sow the seed, as I said, on a piece of bedding; don't wait until all the fire has passed, as this seed needs a lot of heat to rise quickly and reliably: as long as you can sustain the heat, albeit with difficulty, by sticking your hand into the soil, that's enough. After four or five days, the seed lifts, and the same care as explained above is applied. A fortnight later, the seedling is strong enough to be transplanted onto another layer, which must be prepared, and it is transplanted much lighter, i.e., one bell should make three; but the layer must not be so hot as for the first seed. We leave it in this state for three weeks; it strengthens, and we transplant it again on another layer, where it should be more at ease: seven to eight feet is enough under each bell. We leave it on this third layer until it is strong enough to be replanted; fifteen days will perfect it, provided the weather is mild. With this new method, I've found that the plant stretches less, roots better, remains stockier, is less prone to melting and makes more progress; The reason for this is that it benefits from the warmth and sap of three new layers, which the one remaining on the same layer does not enjoy equally, even though it has been warmed exactly, so that the seedling you planted at the beginning of January is usually ready to be replanted at the beginning of March. If you follow ordinary practice, you will hardly be able to plant the seedling before the end of March; and it goes without saying that you will have kept the January seedlings, as the February ones are only good in 217. the beginning of April. Finally, in one of these two months you do your planting, and for this purpose you prepare your beds, which must be four and a half feet wide, and which are reduced to four because of the borders; two feet high is enough in this season, (I mean after having been walked on.) The rule is to put only one foot under each bell, and to form two rows in a chessboard: you raise them with a little clod, if you can; and transplanted right away, they tire little. They should not be wetted, as the vapour from the bed throws enough moisture into the soil; but the bells should be kept lowered and covered with a little bedding, until they are well established; and if it is a little hot, they should be covered with straw mats. If the plant is strong, the first pruning should be carried out as soon as it is well established, i.e. seven or eight days later, but not at the same time as planting, as some gardeners do; this often results in a kind of canker forming on the pruning, caused by the vapor from the manure, which spreads to the whole stem, causing it to perish. The best thing to do in this respect, when the plants are too far under the last layer in which they were transplanted, and there are no layers ready for replanting, is to stop them at two Tome II. K eyes, eight days before planting. This will seem surprising to those who have never practiced it, and I myself was not convinced of the good effect it produces until after I had tried it; for it really seems that this small quantity of compost is not capable of nourishing the fruit. One might also suspect that the root would heat up and heal in the manure it is forced to pierce, but experience destroys all arguments against this use. This plaute, as I'll soon say, fears nothing so much as water; and the more the soil abounds, the more moisture it retains, which commuuicates with the foot; the less there is consequently, the less it retains, because the air and the sun have a lot of six, and the water, for the same reason has rather, pierced this mediocre, thickness from where it runs off into the paths: this is the first reason. The second reason is that the plant, having less nourishment, stops growing fruit more quickly, and doesn't grow wood with the vigor that a fatter bottom would give it, which is the usual reason why fruit often sinks as it knots; moreover, we save a lot of work, which the pruning of this quantity of mutilated wood requires: as long as we have the principles of vegetation, we feel the need to make it bear fruit. But, you may ask, between too much humidity, which is admittedly contrary, and too little, is the middle ground sufficiently maintained? In hot weather, can this plant draw enough juice from such a weak substance? My answer is that it can, based on my own experience and that of several market gardeners; and to make the assurance I am giving clearer, it should be borne in mind that a vapor is constantly rising from the manure, which the glass, either cloche or frame, attracts, and which sufficiently moistens the soil; and that, in addition, when there is a great need, this is made up for by light watering done at the right time, in the way I will explain below. I'll add one more reason, which is that the heat of the stoves works much better and more quickly on a small quantity of potting soil, than on a larger one: besides, the root of this plant doesn't prick, and only runs on the surface. The result of this practice is 1° that the plant is less prone to melting due to humidity; 2° that the fruit stops earlier and more reliably; 3° that it ripens more quickly; 4° that it has more taste, because it is molas nourished with water. Experimentation will show the truth of what I'm saying. K 2 It could still be objected that piec, when replanted a little hard, does not find a home in this small quantity of compost. I must say, however, that since the root is so close to the manure, particular care is needed during the first few days, to protect it from the excessive heat of the bed, which sometimes starts a second fire after it has been planted, when the weather warms up: You need to probe it with your finger several times during the day, and especially at high noon; and as soon as you notice that the heat is increasing, you need to lard it, and give the bells a little air: these risks only last a few days, after which you can see the plants benefiting visibly, as the sun makes itself felt.
