As soon as it has recovered, we perform the first operation, which is to prune its upright at the second leaf, and to cut off its ears, as I said above; but if the stem is not sufficiently formed, we leave it to benefit until it is in a condition to suffer it. A modern author, who has written about this fruit in the context of other subjects, condemns this cutting off of the ears, on the general principle that nature does nothing useless, and that we must not give it laws or do it violence; but, starting from the same principle, it would therefore be wrong to prune trees or vines; it would be wrong to cut the shoots of several roots, in order to profit from them; it would be wrong to pinch peas and beans, melons and cucumbers: how many other operations would be wrong? Practices recognized as good by experience cannot be destroyed by simple reasoning, however specious it may be. Let's return to our subject. When the stems begin to grow arms, they must be pinched at two eyes; and as they grow new branches, they must be cut back again and again with a small, sharp knife. Finally, we must make it a rule to leave only two arms on each stem, and a third, called the false arm, because it grows out of the stump between the good arms: everything that grows more around the stem must be cut off. We always keep one of the false arms, because we know from experience that they often bear fruit more often than the good ones. A small number of gardeners always prune with one eye, and do well, they say: for my part, I neither condemn nor approve this K method, which I have never tried, and which is rarely used. False flowers begin to appear after the second pruning: they should be carefully removed, as should the tendrils that grow on the arms. You should also cut off the gourmands which usually grow straight from the middle of the plant; they ruin the good branches: you can easily distinguish them by their size and liveliness. If the branches are so arranged that they cannot be brought back, then some cut leaf must be used to cover them. There's a bad kind of leaf called hard leaf, which comes out of the arms, and consumes more sap than four good ones; it should be cut close to the stem: you can tell by its darker color than the others, by its thickness, and by its more pointed shape. After the first or second pruning, some hairy branches grow, with pipes five or six inches long without eyes; these must be cut off, as this type of branch rarely or never produces fruit. Genuinely good fruiting wood must be short, by which I mean that it must have its eyes close together; and the closer it is to the foot, the bigger and better the fruit, and the better it holds. Any branch with flat wood is also bad by nature, so it must be removed. During the course of these various operations, the stalks gain strength and the branches multiply; they must be thinned out to avoid confusion, and food must be provided for those most likely to bear fruit: These forks are usually pieces of barrel stave wood, sharpened at one end and enthrited at the other: they are driven into the soil more or less deeply, depending on whether you want to give the plants more or less air, and the edge' of the bell rests on the notch which retains it and even temys conpre the strong winds. But, for greater safety, these forks should always be placed on the opposite side of the wind, and the bells lowered as much as possible during its violence; however, as long as the weather is serene, they should be placed on the side of the rising sun, and all branches should be enclosed under the bell on the K4 1 opposite side, until the fruit is stopped; but some should be allowed to escape at the other edge where the fork is. When pruning, the branches should be distributed so that none stretches out over the paths; they should be thrown back on the sides and in the middle: from time to time, both hands should be pressed a little firmly around the foot, to push it back into the soil. When you start to see some fruit stopping, if it's outside the bell, bend the branch so that it's covered; and if it stops so far from the foot that you can't bring it back without risking forcing the branch, in that case take another bell and cover it; for it is extremely important, for the goodness of the fruit, that neither rainwater nor sprinkling water ever falls on it until it is perfectly ripe; the Provençals themselves observe this, despite the heat of their climate and the dryness of their soil, as I have noticed in the vicinity of Marseille. For this purpose, they raise a small inverted earthen pot, supported on three forks five or six inches above each fruit. They do even more; persuaded by experience that any water is not only contrary to the fruit's goodness, but that it even harms the plant, their method of watering them is quite different from ours: their melons, as I have already said, are in the ground, and are planted in the middle of their boards, which are domed; they put only one row of them, and give only three feet of width to the boards. From this we understand that the two sides are hollow, and form two small ditches into which they let water run when necessary; but this is only at the end, and when the leaves are very wilted; this water filters between two soils, and substains the roots without drowning the foot, as watering cans do, which often causes them to perish. The perfectly curious add to this: when the weather is rainy, or when they foresee a storm, they remove the pots I mentioned from over the forks, and cover the fruit entirely with them, until the fine weather returns: However, they have observed that, despite this precaution, as soon as the year turns rainy, their melons lose a great deal of their good qualities: incontestable proof that authorizes more and more the use of frames, where they never receive more water than they are willing to give. It's also to protect them from sunburn, which is frequent and dangerous in their climate, and at the same time to prevent them from ripening too quickly, which would make them dry and unhealthy; in this way, they prevent the sun from beating down on the melons, as the portion of its rays that revolves around them is enough to ripen them. But in this climate, we hardly need this precautton; for, as much as it is important, when the fruit begins to set, to keep it covered with its leaves so that it benefits better, as much it is necessary, when it approaches its size, to uncover it so that it receives through the bell or the frames the immediate rays of the sun: each one must regulate himself, in this respect, according to his climate. Vegetable growers generally take none of these precautions, even though they know them and are convinced of their good effect: But most do not want to expose their bells to unforeseen hail, and other small daily accrdens; they are much less concerned about the goodness than the beauty of the fruit, which is why they should not be imitated: however, there are only too many individuals who do this; but I have seen them repent more than once. For example, in 1746, there were clouds in May and June, which ruined most of the fruit. Some of my acquaintances promptly used their bells, and put the fruit back under cover; but in an attempt to repair one evil, they created a greater one. The fruit, which had become accustomed to the fullness of the air, found itself deprived of this abundance, so it hung up, hardly professed any more, and ripened badly and later. When you have done so much as remove the eloches, you must never put them back; the most suitable expedient on these occasions is to always keep a small trellis over the beds, ready to receive the doormats, when you have to fear cos kinds of bad weather. As the fruit appears, whether in the first, second or third sap, the branch should always be pruned to one eye above; but if there are several, after a while only one of the first sap should be left, and the strongest chosen; quinno days or three weeks later, you choose a second one from the second sap, and do away with all the rest; it's even much better to leave only the first sap, especially in cold, rainy years. Some market gardeners, greedy for profit, are not so reserved, and sometimes leave R 6 three or four on each plant; but experience shows that the more a plant is loaded, the less quality and size the fruit has: the person who breeds for pleasure must prefer to have one good one to two mediocre ones. However, although I don't want to give a decisive reason for this, we also know from experience that, under stretchers, one plant can produce three, and even four well-conditioned ones: I've seen as many as seven; the reason for this is that heat being evenly distributed throughout the body of the bed, and the temperature of the air being the same throughout the space between the surface of the bed and the glass, the fruit finds greater resources to feed itself; let's add to this that the branches not being violated, as under cloches, where they must be kept tight, nature acts at the same time with greater freedom and prodigality. When this fruit has reached a certain size, many individuals are in the habit of placing it on a piece of tile, with the idea that the humidity communicated by the soil is detrimental to its goodness: this is true in the last season, I mean for all those that have not ripened by the end of August; and this precaution is then very useful, because the soil cools down, as the nights become long and cold : Experience proves that potting soil in some way helps to nourish the fruit and make it swell, because it helps its parts to expand; moreover, it ripens much more as a reflection of the soil's own heat: the proof is clear, since the side of the bed is always riper than the side of the soil. But there is a way to prevent this evil, when it occurs, without falling into other inconveniences: Take small baskets, such as those used for peaches and apricots sold in public markets, which are eight to ten inches long and four to five inches wide, with a one-inch rim; fill them with straw, and place them under the melons; these baskets stop the moth, which cannot pierce them, and the fruit benefits equally from the reflection of the heat that passes through. The timing of all these operations cannot be fixed; they depend on the season and on how far the plants have advanced as a result of the gardener's care; but they are self-evident. Pruning is also determined by need. Nothing is more damaging to the plant and the fruit, as I have already said, than the confusion of branches; they need to be unloaded exactly: in the ordinary course, they need to be worked on at least once a week; and when they are vigorous, every four days? but a lot of trouble is saved when, from birth, you pinch the branches exactly without letting them spread too much, and the fruiting branches show up just as well. When pruning, market gardeners usually cut the wood they are pruning about an inch from the mother branch, to avoid the mess of cankers that often form where the cut is made, and sometimes spread from branch to branch to the perishing plant. This method has its disadvantages, in that it dissipates the spirits by these kinds of slang, which are ugly moreover: the one I follow, and which seems preferable to me, in that the wound covers better, and a canker never forms, is to throw on it a little dry loam, pulverized between the fingers, or to apply a small piece of leaf from the plant, which stops the flow of sap to it, and which dries while the callus is forming; both are equally successful: However, I prefer the use of potting soil, in that the operation is done more quickly, and it remains firmly applied to the wound; instead of the leaf sometimes being disturbed by the shaking of other nearby branches, which are worked on one after the other. Moreover, this precaution is only really important for branches that are cut close to the base; for all the same branches, the risk would be too great, and there is no need. Although the season is advancing, you must not lose sight of the fact that you must always maintain the heat of your layers in a degree of heat that is more or less equal; because it is sometimes appropriate, until the month of May, to reheat them: but in this respect, you must operate in relation to the changes in weather that occur. For example, you may have put in a new stove at the right time, and then a few days of unforeseen chaleturs occur; this stove, as a result of the event, overheats your bed, and threatens the plant; this must be remedied diligently, either by removing it altogether or in part, or by providing plenty of air under the bells, and using suction cups. Finally, the need must determine the remedy, and from time to time the finger must be thrust into the layer to probe its heat, in order to regulate it more reliably. On other, quite opposite occasions, we are sometimes reassured by a few fine days which seem to herald the end of bad weather, and which make us neglect all sorts of precautions; but then cold days and equally cold rains occur: the bed becomes mortified, (in gardening terms) and the plant runs to its doom. You can't be too quick, on these occasions, to revive it with hot sumier stoves, mixed with pigeon droppings; and it's a good idea to make little stoves in them, as I explained in the chapter on Beds; at the same time, keep the plant well covered, until the bed has regained its heat. When the month of May arrives, it is quite usual to see the heat begin; and if it is such that the plants need some light watering, they must be wetted, but with great care and restraint, as I have quite insinuated, and never water during the strong sun, whose bells could suffer as much as the plant. The English are in the habit of only ever wetting the paths, for the same reasons as I said above, and some people also practice this in France: I think everyone would do well to follow this method; but, for the water to be able to communicate itself to the bed, the paths must be at least level with the surface. In addition, the best time to water is from 5 p.m. until dark, or in the morning from daybreak until 7 a.m., if you haven't been able to do it in the evening. It's also very important not to water with water freshly drawn from a well, or from some other reservoir; it must have been left to cool in the sun for a day, more or less, in barrels placed at noon, if you don't have some basin next to the melonnière. The quality of the water also contributes infinitely to the quality of the fruit. Hard, raw water is extremely detrimental to this, and the fruit that is fed on it is never good: The latter are much inferior to the former, and there is only one voice to agree on the cause; to which we can add that the market gardeners of the Faubourg Saint-Antoine wet them much less than those of the other Faubourg, commonly using only one waterway for twelve feet, while the latter use two or three. The layer, which receives neither rain nor dew, and which is uniformly hit by the sun in all its parts through the reflection of the glass, deteriorates much more than an ordinary layer, and consequently needs to be helped. But once the fruit has reached a certain size, it must no longer receive the impressions of water, and neither must the stalk, for the same reasons I have set out above; and to protect them from the damage they would suffer, here is my method. I have a dozen earthen pots, which I cover with preds and fruits, and I wet over them; a jug makes my rule for four feet: these four watered, I lift my pots and put them back on the next four, as for the rest. The operation is neither long nor difficult, and I protect my fruit from the bad effects of water. Some people throw pigeon droppings into the barrels in which they cool the water, and claim that this greatly advances the fruit. I have never wanted to do this test, because apart from the repugnance I have always felt, others have assured me that the effect was not such, and that moreover this droppings could impart a bad quality to the fruit. There are often fruits which become counterfeit by a whim of nature, so that the afflicted part falls as if in paralysis, hardens and hardly profits any more; which makes the fruit not only counterfeit, but also bitter and bad while ripening. If this is noticed early on, it can be remedied by making several cross-shaped scars with the tip of a knife on the curved part, which relaxes it and facilitates its extension: this operation quite often restores them; but if the curve is considerable, the shortest way is to remove these kinds of fruit and wait for better conditioned ones to come along. There are also fruits with a disposition to be bad without resource, they are called hard melons; they can be recognized by their shape which is almost round, and of a large black green. This kind of fruit is never worth anything, even though it is profitable and grows; it must be plucked: the good fruit must be drawn and elongated as it stops, of a light, plain green. If the fruit of the second sap doesn't do well, you have recourse to that of the third, which arrives on Midsummer's Day. If both fail, we fall back on the last, which comes at Magdeleine; but quite often the fruit of this sap does not ripen well. When the first fruits stop ripening, we must take care to cover them completely with a leaf; and if the leaves holding the base are too far apart, we cover them with one of the leaves we cut off when pruning. Violent thunderstorms sometimes occur in May and June, which are dangerous for the fruit and bells that the wind blows away, and which suffer even more when accompanied by hail: it is prudent to always be on guard, and not to move your straw mats too far away, so as to be able to throw them promptly over the bells when the weather threatens.

Here are a few special considerations that I should have placed higher up, but it is better to give them out of place than to keep them quiet. The first is to beware of sunburn, which is quite common in the first days of heat that follow the cold of March and April; its bright rays are a warning: you should then throw some litter on the bottom of the bells, keeping a little air on top: the same precaution should be taken in February, when, after having kept the beds open for a few days due to the harshness of the weather, they are uncovered on a sunny day: the plants, tenderized under the blanket, are soon surprised, if you don't keep a close eye on them. The second is to visit the roots of languishing plants, and when you find them chancie (which is the usual cause), you need to push the plant deeper into the soil: it sprouts roots from the collar, which restore it. The third is to be attentive to the changes in weather that often occur in April and May: during this period, warm rains bring new heat to the layers, however old they may be, and such that all the plants would melt, if care were not taken to give them air in proportion to their needs; you must therefore behave according to their degree of heat, and probe them often so as not to be surprised. Fourth: if you have neglected to sow the last seedlings in March, in order to have fruit in September, and you find yourself pruning your melons in May, you can plant the branches you are pruning to unload them, place them under a bell, not too far forward, and cover them for a few days, as if they were rooted plants: it will perish, but it will survive; that's what I've experienced several times. Fifth: once you've planted as many layers as you intended, if you have any left over, there's little risk of putting it in the ground under any good shelter; the fruit comes later, but is sometimes better than that of the layers, when the summers are hot; I've eaten perfect ones in some years up to the end of September, and last year (1749) I had quite good ones up to All Saints' Day: It's true that the year was very favorable; I also admit that in other years the trouble we took was pure waste, but it doesn't cost much to always try to bring them out. They should be covered with cloches, and cared for in the same way as diapers: when planting them, put in a handful of potting soil, and nothing more; little water, for the same reasons I have already stressed; and in this same position, a pipe covered with straw under the fruit is very necessary, because the soil always returns more freshness than the potting soil. Sixth: in the month of May, as I've already mentioned in passing, clouds often fall, which others call "nielles", and which attach themselves to leaves, stems and fruit, which contract spots in the places where they are trapped by this bad weather, and which ruin them. These clouds are caused by a few dews or cold showers in the morning, interspersed with a few rays of sunshine and a pale sun. As soon as you notice it, if you haven't been able to prevent it by covering your beds with straw mats, you should sprinkle them lightly all over; this water often washes away and corrupts this kind of venom; but if it has bitten so hard that it cannot prevent its bad effect, you must immediately cut off the branches and leaves attacked by it, as well as any fruit that is stained; for however small the stain may appear at first, it makes very rapid progress, and corrupts it completely: or if it stops, it makes the flesh so bitter that it cannot be eaten. Seventh: as the fruit nears maturity, and heavy rains fall, the best part of the fruit often melts and bursts, taking away all its external merit and even its goodness. As soon as the damage begins, and there is reason to fear for the future, the stem should be torn halfway through the wood with the fingernails, two inches from the fruit; this prevents the damage by preventing the sap from entering so abundantly: it is also necessary, as much as possible, to turn the head of the fruit towards the north, because this is the part where it usually cracks; and, as it is the sun which contributes much to making it crack after the rains, it is often preserved by removing the aspect of this star. Eighth: if you want to bring the fruit forward when it's almost at its full size, and has been fully embroidered, remove half the old stoves, and replace what you remove with hot manure; Eight days later, you can cut it, as this new manure has two effects that hasten the fruit's ripening: first, it burns the roots that were filtering through the old stoves, thus weaning it off some of its nourishment; second, it increases the heat of the plant, which helps it ripen earlier. The result of this operation is sometimes that the violence done to it deprives it of some of its quality, especially when very hot weather is pressing on it; but if the weather is a little cool, it is good although premature: besides, it would not be prudent to risk everything, we only risk a layer. Ninth: horseflies and moths, as well as rats and field mice, often wreak havoc in diapers. See Chapter 1 for remedies. When at last, after so much care, one has reached the point of enjoying one's work, that is, of picking the fruit, all that remains is to do it at the right time, and this is another little study: sometimes its maturity is announced by a change in color, that is, it goes from green to yellow; I don't mean a perfect yellow, which is the sign of a bad melon, but a yellowish or russet green: sometimes, too, there is very little change on the outside, especially in the case of the smaller varieties, which need to be picked greener than the larger ones, and which complete their ripening when kept for a few days. The sense of smell, which many people consult, is not always a reliable judge either; for it is more common for a melon to be overripe when it gives off a lot of odour, than for it to be overripe. Some others make up their minds on a few small cracks in the embroidery, similar to fingernail punctures, from which they 'augur well; but all these signs are equivocal, and even vary according to the years and climates: those who are not in the habit of handling them, like our market gardeners, who judge them quite reliably by eye, must try to cone tre the right point by testing. The most certain sign that I couldas, quer, but of which not all melens ger Tome II. is when the tail becomes encircled, that is, when it seems to want to detach from the fruit. The signs by which a melon can be judged are when the stem is large and short, in proportion to the species, when it looks well nourished, when it is heavy in the hand, firm under the finger, and precipitated by the sun; it should also be noted that a freshly cut melon can never have the degree of goodness that it acquires when kept for two or three days. Care must be taken to reserve the seed of the most beautiful fruits, and to take only that of the upper part, that is to say, that which the sun has struck, which is always more August than that below, and to take it only from the fruits of the first or second sap: this seed, which is cleaned as much as possible of its silt, must be placed in the shade to dry and improve, by stirring it from time to time; and when it is quite dry, it is locked in some place where mice cannot get at it: it is good for seven to eight years and more. It is said that the older it is, the better the quality of its fruit; however, many market gardeners sow it in the first year, when they have no other variety. Here's an important observation in this respect. Many people are accustomed to having the fruit they eat cooled in fresh water, or even with ice, and finding it good, they reserve precisely the seed; but it has been observed that this foreign and supernatural freshness alters the seed, and changes the species. I do not undertake to explain the reason for this: in many things of this nature, we must surrender to experience; for the obscurity of the cause does not destroy the effect: it is only a question in this, to go as far as possible, of removing the seed from the fruit as it is collected, which is easy. When you have to send melons a little way, you should take care to pick them a little green, as the road will finish ripening them; and you should cut their tails a little long, leaving even a few leaves. CHAPTER LII. Description of the MELONGEN; its different species, properties, cultivation, etc. It is called Meringeane in Provence, Viédase in Languedoc, and Obergine in some other provinces. L 2 It is very common in all hot countries; but in this climate, it is grown only as a curiosity, and this is by default, as it usually succeeds very well: the Provençals nevertheless claim that it tastes better in their region; I leave them with this opinion; but according to my taste, there is little or no difference when summers are a little hot. There are four species; two with red fruit, one elongated and the other round; and two with yellow fruit, one round and the other long. I'll describe these four species separately, starting with the long red one, which is the most common. This plant has many long, whitish roots, packed with fibers; its leaf is water-green, covered on the surface with a kind of white powder like flour, elongated, up to seven to eight inches long, three to four wide, wrinkled all around without any serration, attached to long, thick tails; Its stem is thick and branchy, rising to two or three feet, and covered with leaves throughout its branches; its flowers emerge in large numbers from the body of the branches in clusters of 3 or 4, borne on a small pedicel. The flower is purplish, formed in a single piece, but cut in such a way that it represents six petals of equal size, pointed and wrinkled; its base is supported by a calyx, in the center of which rises a pistil, accompanied in its circuit by six species of yellow needles or stamens, which all meet at the tip in the shape of a keel; This pistil is then transformed into a full fruit, roughly shaped like a cucumber, but shorter, the color of a belle-de-nuit, sometimes striped with white and green, rounded at the tip, smooth and glossy, a little curved, with no odor; its flesh is white and soft, quite watery, with seeds arranged in two rows; The fruit's tail and calyx, which expands as it grows and envelops it to about two inches, are bristling with prickly red spines; the seed is small, round and flat, shaped like a small kidney, and whitish or russet in color. The second species is similar in all respects to the first, except for the shape of the fruit, which is round and slightly flattened, and the size of the finger. The third species differs in its fruit, which is round, a little egg-shaped and yellow in color, and in its leaf, which is much more indented; its stem is also less elevated and less dense; in all other respects, it is the same. The fourth species differs greatly from L 3 plus; its stem is very low, though roughly shaped like the others; its leaf is whiter green; it is also more indented, and its veins are lined with rough, prickly spines, both above and below; its flower is smaller, and its fruit of mediocre size, rather elongated. The fruit is greenish at birth, whitens as it matures and turns yellow as it ripens. The taste is more or less the same in all four species, although the yellow ones are more highly esteemed; they are widely consumed in southern countries: this does not, however, determine the excellence of this fruit; each nation has its own tastes, which often do not cross the border. This fruit is of a nature that should be neither overly praised nor overly despised; it is delicate, but tastes a little wild and bland at the same time. The best way to eat it in its natural state is to cut it in half lengthwise, remove the spongy substance where the seed is attached, when it is of a certain strength, and cook it on the grill over a low heat, gradually soaking it in oil or fresh butter, with a little pepper and salt, to which fine herbs are sometimes added: some use an anchovy that melts with the oil. This accompaniment goes a long way to correcting the blandness of the fruit, to which habit completes the taste. Some others cook it in the oven between two dishes, or in a tourtière, prepared in the same way as above; this preparation makes it less likely to smell of smoke. Here, it is more commonly eaten fried in a paste like artichokes, and in my case, it seems much better; but there is one preparation you need to know: as much as possible, the fruit should be chosen a little young; it should be cut lengthwise into thin slices, without removing anything; it should then be placed on a dish proportionate to the quantity, and sprinkled with coarse salt; it should be left in this state for five to six hours; The salt melts, and at the same time carries away the vicious juice of the fruit, from which a black liquor flows; we drain them, and squeeze them again in the hand, to remove the rest of the watery liquor from the pulp; after which we throw them into the batter, and from there into the frying pan. They are still very good and very delicate when cut into pieces, and mixed with meats in stews, and after having soaked for two or three hours in fresh water, and having been peeled beforehand; but they should only be thrown into the stew half an hour before removing it; if they remain there any longer, they will turn to mush. They can also be preserved for winter by drying them, and here's how. You cut them into slices, the thickness L4 of a finger, after removing the skin: you strip them well of their seeds, and throw them for a moment in boiling water; you take them out, and lay them in the sun on racks; after which you put them in a lukewarm oven. They are then placed in a lukewarm oven, where they are repeatedly baked until they are completely dry: they are then dried and, when you want to use them, they are cooked in lukewarm water. The plant and fruit, applied externally, are often used medicinally for inflammations, cancers, burns, hemorrhoids and to soothe pain. Cultivation of this plant is very simple in hot climates; the seed is sown in the open ground in good exposure, and the plant is then transplanted into well-prepared soil, with no care other than frequent watering: but in our climate, it should be sown early on a bed, and then replanted in a checkerboard pattern, eighteen inches apart on a soft bed, or at least in well-smoked and well-exposed soil, with care taken to wet it often in dry spells: moreover, it is not very delicate to raise; but at the first frosts in autumn, it perishes; that's why you need to bring it forward if you want to enjoy it safely. When summers are a little warm, it produces as much here as in southern countries, and the seed itself ripens quite well; but in tender years, neither the fruit nor the seed acquires its perfection, and it is always safer to take the seed from Provence or Languedoc. It should be noted that this fruit is only good when freshly picked; I have sometimes tried to keep it for several days, and I have found that it loses a lot of flavor: I even once brought some from Marseille to Lyon; it no longer had any flavor. CHAPTER LIII. Description of MUSTARD; its different species, properties, cultivation, etc. MUSTARD, otherwise known as Senevé, is an annual plant. There are five main species: garden mustard, rabe mustard, arugula mustard, Spanish mustard and Siberian mustard. c Root mustard is woody, brittle, white and full of fibers; its leaves are similar to those of the L5 rabe, but smaller and rougher; its stem grows to a height of four to five feet, round, hairy, divided into several branches with small, yellow, 4-petaled flowers arranged crosswise; When these flowers have fallen, they are followed by rather short, angular and pointed siliques, filled with small, almost round, reddish or blackish seeds, rather like those of turnips, with a pungent, biting taste. Ache mustard has a simple, short, finger-sized, woody, white root, with many very long fibers; its leaves are similar to those of ache, laciniate, especially the lower ones, with rough, pungent hairs; its flowers are similar to those of the preceding species, but attached to longer pedicels, with a pleasant odor; the flowers are followed by hairy, pointed siliques, filled with very pungent, almost round, white seeds. Arugula mustard differs from the two preceding varieties in that the plant is weaker, the leaf cut like that of arugula, and the seed reddish; it is not cultivated in gardens, and grows naturally in stony, damp, maritime places. Spanish mustard is quite similar to the first species, except that its flower is the color of sulfur, and its siliques are smaller. Its seed is relatively so small that its shape is barely distinguishable. In botany, there are three other species of Spanish mustard, which differ in their foliage; but, as they have no interesting qualities, I'll pass over them in silence. Siberian mustard has the advantage over all the others that its leaves, though large and thick, are very good in salads. The first two are used only for the seed, which is also used in food and medicine. When prepared for the first use, this seed awakens the appetite and facilitates digestion: it is used with most meats, fish and vegetables. Pigs in particular require this accompaniment, however they are eaten; black pudding, sausages, andouilles and feet would be unbearable without mustard. It is also eaten with boiled beef; mixed with oil and a few fine herbs, it makes an appetizing seasoning for cold poultry, which we call remoulade: it also pleases in salads with celery. It is also used in salads with celery. L6́ with scorched onion to make a sauce much used under various roasted meats, known as sauce-à-Robert. Finally, although its pungent taste displeases some people, it can be said to please most, and to be useful and almost indispensable, with the health advantage of cooking food and having no harmful qualities. There are several ways of preparing mustard. Some, after crushing the seed, bind it with half-thickened sweet wine, to the required consistency: others dilute it with vinegar, and some add a little flour; it is in this last way that it is most commonly prepared in Paris, and for this reason it is called white mustard. The newer it is made, the better it is: however, it can keep well for quite a long time, if kept in a well-covered vessel. It comes to Paris from various provinces, particularly Dijon and Gien, which are more highly regarded; I don't know if this preference is well-founded. There is another way to prepare it, which extends its life to a year or more: here it is. Take two ounces of powdered mustard, and half an ounce of powdered common cinnamon; make a mass with flour, and a sufficient quantity of vinegar and honey, of which you will make small balls which you will leave to dry in the sun, or in an oven when the bread has been removed; and when you want to use it, you will soak one or more of these small balls with white wine and vinegar : you'll always have a mustard that's pleasant to the taste, good for the stomach, easy to transport, and particularly convenient for people who travel, either by land or sea. This seed is no less useful in medicine: it is a powerful sternutatory and a most effective machicatory: a dragmeg of this seed is enclosed in a cloth, after having been slightly crushed, and is chewed by patients threatened with apoplexy or paralysis; this remedy makes them spit abundantly; it also relieves those whose head is heavy and laden with pituitary. This seed is useful in soporific and lethargic affections; it is also good for people prone to hysterical and hypochondriacal vapors; it is used successfully in pale-colors, scurvy and indigestion; this plant is aperitive, stomachic, antiscorbutic and hysterical. Mustard, as prepared to enhance the taste of meats, is applied to the noses of people of both sexes prone to vapors, relieving them in their attacks; it also awakens the lethargic. The following poultice is a good resolver for sciatic gout, rheumatism and squirrelly tumors. Fry leeks with strong vinegar, after chopping them finely; and when they are cooked, sprinkle them with crushed mustard seed, and apply them to the ailment. Some people make a poultice from pigeon droppings, mustard and turpentine, and apply it to the areas where gout is felt; but wait until the inflammation has subsided. Such a poultice would be very capable of bringing back scabs, the suppuration of which, once removed, would have given rise to a deposit on the chest or some other part. The oil extracted by expression from mustard seed is effective against paralysis, and for resolving cold humors. There's nothing unusual about growing this plant: the seed is sown in March; and, as it's very fine, it must be sown very lightly: if you have beds, it rises earlier and more reliably; if you don't, it's sown at the foot of a well-exposed wall, in loose soil, or in a tub filled with compost: When it is strong enough to be planted, it is transplanted and cared for as usual. The seed is harvested at the end of August or in September, and left to dry out before being stored: it keeps well for sowing for two years. CHAPTER LIV. Description of the turnip; its different species, properties, cultivation, etc. The turnip is the root of an annual plant of the same name. This root is of different shape, size and color, depending on the species; it is fleshy and soft, exhaling a pleasant odor; its leaf is a little elongated, deeply cut, rough and hairy, of a coarse green; it lies on the ground: its stem, which rises to two or three feet, is branchy and smooth, more or less large, depending on the species; flowers are born in abundance at the ends of its branches. This flower is yellow, sometimes white, with four leaves arranged crosswise; the calyx grows a pistil which becomes a silique when the flower has passed; this silique is composed of two panels resting on the edges of a partition, which divides this fruit into two chambers, filled with a few round, brown seeds. This fruit is very useful at the table; it is eaten fatty and lean, in many different ways: it is used in soups; it is used with meats of all kinds, but particularly with mutton and duck: it is prepared with white sauce and mustard; it is fried into a paste in certain countries: it is susceptible to all sorts of accommodations; and it is a healthy food, although it is accused of being a little windy. The turnip also has great medicinal properties; its decoction is very useful in broths for the chest: mixed with sugar, it forms a highly-prized syrup for soothing inveterate coughs and asthma. The way to make this syrup is to cut the turnips into wheels, rake them, fill an earthenware pot with them, cover it and seal it with paste: then put it in the oven, after removing the bread; leave it there for twelve or fifteen hours, and separate the juice at the bottom of the pot; on four ounces of this juice, throw an ounce of candy sugar which is left to melt, and take a spoonful on its own, or mixed with a glass of herbal tea: this syrup is sovereign for colds, as well as for softening the acridity of the chest. The seed of this plant is an aperitif: two large pieces are crushed and infused in a glass of white wine. The decoction of turnip also has the virtue of curing chilblains, when the feet or hands are washed often and warmly; others apply the cooked fruit as a poultice to the ailment. There are many species of turnip, of which I will reduce six, which are the best for our climate; I say for our climate, because there are some that are far superior, but which are only suitable for the vein of earth that produces them, such as the turnips of Ferneuse, Saulieu in Burgundy, Saint- Jôme, Gatinois, etc. We bring them in every year from France, France and the United States. Every year, turnips are brought to Paris from all these places; but they cannot be bred there, I mean to have them as good: the species degenerates, and all art cannot prevent it. Of all the plants, it is the one that is least accustomed to changes in terrain, and which grows best against all reason in places where the others only languish. The six species I have chosen are the small Berlin turnip, the Vaugirard turnip, the long white common turnip, the round turnip, the gray turnip, and the Meaux turnip. The Berlin turnip is very small, longer than round, and white; it is the earliest and best; it is not widely known; but those who have tried it prefer it to the others with justice: its taste is very spicy, and it is perfectly tender. The Vaugirard turnip is of mediocre size, a little elongated, dirty white, turning gray at the top, of good taste, and tender: it is very common in Paris, and highly esteemed. - 1. Common turnips, both long and round, are the most commonly grown in Aubervilliers and other neighboring towns, which supply Paris with them: there is little difference in quality between them, but the round variety is larger; the skin is very white, the flesh is soft and tender, and the taste is quite good. The gray turnip has a gray skin, as its name suggests, and a fairly elongated shape: it has its supporters who prefer it to the white variety, because of its slightly spicy taste; but it is not usually so tender, and is more prone to worminess. The Meaux turnip is the most profitable because of its size and length, which is commonly eight to ten inches; its exterior color is yellowish-white, its flesh very white; it is tender and has a very pleasant flavor: it is highly prized, but it has been noted that the one brought from Meaux is better than the one grown near Paris. These six species are cultivated in the same way: they are sown in two stages, in March and August; but in many soils, they do not succeed in spring. The lightest soil is the best for them: in strong, damp soils, this vegetable is almost always worm-eaten and tasteless; it is also important that the soil has been well tilled, and is healthy when sown: too dry or too soaked, the seed does not distribute itself evenly, and does not rise so well; and as this seed is very small, care must be taken not to spread more than is necessary; the best way is to mix it with three times as much ash or sawdust, and to spread it as evenly as possible; It should not be sown too lightly; moreover, when it has emerged and reached a certain point of strength, the plant should be thinned out so that there is a distance of about six inches from one foot to the other; at the same time as this operation is carried out, weeds are weeded out, and this double method is very important: I know that many people dispense with it, especially in the countryside, where whole fields are sown with it; but I also know that it's no better, and our market gardeners around Paris, who know how useful it is, are very exact about it: there's no other precaution to take. This plant is more usually sown on land that has yielded wheat, than in gardens; however, when you have a garden of a certain size, it is pleasant to have it on hand. It has a cruel enemy in the form of the lily; this insect devours both its ears as soon as it is born, and there is no longer any resource: to ward it off, it is suggested that ash be sprinkled on it, or chimney soot in the morning dew; a vain expedient, which I tried out a long time ago, without any success. The shortest way, when the damage has reached a certain point, is to give the soil a new look, to sow another seed which often has the same fate as the first, in which case you have to give up. This inconvenience is common in many soils, especially in dry years, and those who are aware of it must be careful not to waste time and seed. I have observed, however, that by sowing it late, i.e. after mid-August, this insect, which is beginning to retreat, no longer tires it so much. When it succeeds, the turnips are usually good two months later, and should not be left in the ground any longer, as they become ropey, or the worms get into them, or the field mice eat them: they are pulled out by hand, or with a flail if they are too big, and are closed in the greenhouse until needed, after having twisted their tops: those from spring are kept for the summer, and those from August spend the whole winter, being put in the sand. In Aubervilliers, market gardeners sow them in their enclosures, which they leave in the ground all winter, and they don't spoil as they do in many other areas. In countries where they are planted in large quantities, a hole is made in the middle of the room, proportionate to the quantity, and they are placed in it; they are then covered with thatch, and they keep perfectly well, provided that the rainwater has some drainage, and that the weeping of the soil does not flood them. Lovers of this vegetable sow them in beds as early as February, which they enjoy at the beginning of May: in this case, the bed must be filled with eight to nine inches of compost, and its heat must be considerably dampened, because if it is too hot, the turnips fork and become covered with small roots, which is a major defect. In Paris, we don't know another species of turnip, which is very common, and NAVET. Its color is white, both inside and out; its shape is flat, and its circumference is sometimes as large as that of a hat; it has a sweet and pleasant flavor, which outweighs that of all other turnips; but it is perfectly good only in soup; in any other sauce, it is not worth the turnip; however, country people, who are not very delicate, use it in every way. I don't know why it has never found favor in this climate, where it would be perfectly successful, having seen it tested in my own home and in the homes of others. I've heard a lot of praise for another species of turnip found in Germany, whose flesh is yellow like a carrot, and very tender; and I've been told that it's never worm-eaten, because it's attached to the earth only by the tip of its root, and the rest is protruding: those who are curious about it can find out more about it in the vicinity of Darmstad, where I've been told I've seen it. To collect the seed of all turnip species, choose the quantity of the most beautiful fruits you consider appropriate, and put them in the ground in March, a foot apart: they soon sprout their stalk, and the seed is good in August: you pull out the stalks, or cut them in the morning with the dew so that they don't fall off; you leave them to dry for a few more days, after which you beat them and lock them up: they only keep well for two years. CHAPTER LV. Description of the ONION; its different species, properties, cultivation, etc. Its root, which is the fruit itself, is bulbous, composed of several tunics, fleshy inside and membranous outside, of different colors and shapes, depending on the species, lined at the bottom with white fibers, filled with a subtle and very harsh juice, which grips the eyes and makes them cry; Its leaves are a foot or so long, fistulous, cylindrical and pointed, with a pungent taste like the root. When it goes to seed in its second year, it sprouts a bare, straight stem, three to four feet high, swollen towards the middle, bearing a fist-sized head at the top, composed of lily-like flowers, each with six petals, six stamens, and a pistil which then changes into a rounded fruit, divided into three chambers, filled with rounded, angular seeds, black on the outside and white on the inside. Everyone knows its uses for life; and its properties are such that there is almost no dish where it does not enter at least the juice; and, although there are people who fear it, its taste being softened with meats or vegetables, is bearable to its greatest enemies; and constantly there is little good sauce without the mixture of the onion. It can be used in all meat juices, in soups, to flavour broths, in green salads, and in salads cooked over charcoal, alone or with beet, capers and gherkins. In many countries, it's so popular that it's eaten raw like an apple: there's no other vegetable so widely consumed. Its virtues for health are no less remarkable: it is, by nature, a pectoral and aperitif, and sovereign in many illnesses. During the last plague in Marseille, it cured plague victims. The way to use it is to give the patient the expressed juice of an onion from which the heart has been removed, for which a little theriac has been substituted, and which has then been baked in an oven or under a tureen; as soon as it has been taken, the patient is well covered, and if he is happy enough to sweat, he is surely cured: for double safety, a similar onion that has not been expressed is applied to the bubo. Crushed with honey and salt, it's still a sovereign remedy for rabid dog bites. A famous contemporary physician also asserts that an onion cut into rouelles and infused in half a pint of white wine, taken on the last three days of the moon, is a proven remedy for nephritis. Cooked under embers and eaten with oil and sugar, it also soothes coughs and relieves asthma. Crushed with a little salt and applied to a recent burn, it stops the pain and prevents the formation of bells: the first can be followed by a second, cooked under ashes and kneaded into an ointment; the burned area is then wrapped with a cloth. The heart of a small white onion, cooked under ashes and applied hot to a damaged tooth, often alleviates the pain. For migraines, two onions chopped Tome II. M and soaked in wine spirit, applied to the head, dissipate the pain. Crushed and mixed with fresh butter, it also soothes the pain of hemorrhoids. Its expressed juice, soaked in absorbent cotton and placed in the ear, stops jamming. It also removes stains from the face and, when mixed with chicken fat, dries out the heels of mules. Nephritic colic is surely cured by having the patient take half a pint of white wine, in which an onion cut into pieces has been infused for two hours, and you're lucky when the ailment can give this infusion time. Eaten raw or cooked, it is very good against rheumatic pains in the kidneys. "These are its main virtues, which have often been tried and tested; there are still others that I'm not mentioning, but that's enough to show the merit of this plant: However, according to some doctors, its excessive use in food is harmful, in that it provokes wind and headaches through the fumes it carries, and causes restlessness during sleep: they consider it particularly contrary to bilious temperaments; everyone should be aware of the effect it has on them, and use it accordingly. There are nine very distinct species, and I could count more if I were not convinced that those I have left out are degenerated from the others, or that the difference between them is due to the climate or the quality of the soil. These nine species are the round Red Onion, the Pale; the round White, of which there are two species, the Early and the Late; the Long, which is of two colors, red and white; the Spanish Onion in the same way, and the small one from Florence. The red onion is the most widely grown in Europe, because it is the largest and keeps the longest: Its shape is round, a little flattened; its flesh white, veined with red, and its outer color reddish-purple; it is also the strongest, another reason why its lovers prefer it to the next one; but it does not have the same credit in Paris, where nothing in the foodstuffs is so dominant; so it is used very little: besides its color, which turns purple when it is cooked, displeases the eyes; however it is preferred for the juice. Carpenters prefer to use it to prepare their wares. The pale variety is shaped in the same way, but more flattened: its exterior color varies; some are straw-colored, with a hint of M 2 lemon, while others are a pale red: the first is the most esteemed by those who know it, because it is sweeter; but the other has the advantage of keeping longer: each therefore has its merit and its supporters, and it is the species that is most consumed during the winter. The cultivation of these two species is the same, and they are both sown at the same time; commonly it is only at the end of February, in light soils, and at the end of March, in heavy soils: they can also be sown in August, and transplanted in October; they are more likely to come this way, but they also don't keep that long. The soil must be well amended, and the fatter the soil, the more successful this vegetable will be; in sandy soils, it will not grow: however, care must be taken not to fertilize the soil in the same year as sowing, as this would scald the plant; soils that have been used to grow cabbage the previous year are very suitable for it. The land must be ploughed well on All Saints' Day, and a second ploughing a month before sowing, as the seed does not require such freshly tilled soil. We broadcast it on a fine day, when the soil is healthy, and we spread more or less of it, because a good part of it often perishes in the ground: the ordinary rule is to put two ounces on each plank fifteen toises long by six wide, on which we can settle. We must try to sow it evenly, and in calm weather; the way we bury it must be regulated according to the soil, and according to what we have or don't have to cover it: in soft soil, when the boards are upright, and you have nothing to put on top of the seed, you must first walk on them with your feet together, then spread the seed, and lightly harrow afterwards with the fork, without any other way; but if you have potting soil, you pass the rake after the fork, taking care to pass it very lightly so as not to move the seed, and you spread the potting soil on top as evenly as you can; a finger's breadth is enough. In heavy soils, it's best to sow the seed first on the rough ploughing, as it is: you then walk on it, dragging your feet a little, and harrow afterwards; and, whether or not you're terracing, you mustn't pass the rake over it, as rain and watering beat the soil too much when it's even, and cause it to split. It's a good idea to use potting soil or small amounts of manure in this kind of soil, especially when it's greasy and clayey, as it sometimes screeches and hardens to the point where the onion can't expand and remains almost like a ciboule. Potting soil also has the advantage of keeping the soil fresh, so that one watering has more effect than two when there's nothing on it. Three weeks after sowing, it starts to emerge, and some time later it needs to be weeded: it's a good idea to wet it afterwards, as water reassures the soil; even before weeding, if the soil is a little dry, it needs to be wetted, so that weeds can be pulled out more easily: When it's a little strong, we thin it out so that there are three inches between one and the other; this is what makes it big and well-rounded. When it finally begins to turn, i.e. to round out, you should stop watering it, unless there is an extreme drought, because too much water makes it tender and prevents it from keeping: in some countries, you don't even need to water it at all, but you should consult your own land. When the fruit is fully turned, and about the right size, you have to pull down and twist the stalk with your hands, over the fruit, or press your foot on it: others roll a barrel over the whole board; all this is pretty much equal, provided that the sap is interrupted, and remains in the root, which is more profitable. The people of Aubervilliers don't take this approach, and their onions are no less beautiful for it; but their terrain gives them privileges, as I've said on other occasions. When at last the leaf begins to fall to the side in case it has not been pulled, or begins to yellow, supposing it has been; without waiting, for it preserves better to be pulled a little green, especially the white; and, as it is pulled, the leaf should be cut a good two inches above the fruit, rather more than less : when it is cut closer, the heart grows; and, as it doesn't come to maturity all at once, it must be pulled in two stages, so that it is all set at the point it should be, and left on the ground lying clear for eight to ten days, to finish ripening; But if you fear that it will be stolen during the night, bring it close to the house and lay it out in a courtyard or under a shed, so that it can breathe and dry well. Those who have this convenience of being able to put it under cover, do even better to put it there right away, than to leave it in the open air; because, when the season is rainy, M 4 the humidity does it great harm, and it doesn't keep; this is what I've experienced more than once. Fifteen days later, remove it from there, and take it to the attic, or other place where it is to be kept; and another fifteen days after it is locked up, peel it, i.e., remove any earth that may be on it, and any loose skins, as well as the roots; because this sort of useless garbage sometimes heats it up and spoils it; cleanliness also requires it: It must also be stirred every month, and those that are beginning to sprout or rot removed; this little way, which gives it air, keeps it dry. If you have neglected this precaution, and have been surprised by the frost, you should not think that it has been lost, for it will return to its original state, provided you do not touch it until it has thawed out. There's another way to grow red onions that may seem surprising, but it's no less safe, because I've tried it. In June, when you thin out the planting beds sown in March, you pick up the onion you've pulled up, which most often gets lost; you lay it out very lightly in a well-ventilated place, and forget about it; you can even leave it out in the open in the garden, in some flowerbed, until autumn, when you cover it; The foliage dries out, but the stalk is preserved, and the onion grows to the size of a filbert, even though it receives no nourishment from the earth and is exposed to the sun and the air all summer long. It can be used throughout the summer, but would not be good for winter. To get the same onion in May, there's a second way, which I've also used several times, and which is just as successful; it's to sow a more or less large bed in the usual season, and to sow it so thickly that it touches each other: you let it come up without thinning it or wetting it, however much it seems to need it; you just have to weed it: This onion, which is pressed, remains small to the size of a hazelnut; we pull it out when it is at its peak, and treat it as I explained above; we then replant it in February, and it is good in May; but we must be careful, as it grows its upright, I mean the M 5 pipe that carries the seed, to cut it off at the level of the last leaves. Onions are of such a delicate nature, that in some areas, without taking any of the precautions I've just mentioned, they grow perfectly: our market gardeners in S. Our market gardeners in S. Denis and Aubervilliers, who have perfect soil for vegetables, do no more than plow it and then sow it: their method is to throw into each acre a pound of red onion, two pounds of pale, one pound of white and one pound of leek, all mixed together. They pull out the white onion first, which is the first to ripen, then the red, then the pale: the leek remains until Saint Martin's Day, when they dig small trenches, where they are kept all winter. The fertility of their soil is to be commended; but as there are few like them, we must not model ourselves on them; each must follow what experience has determined to be good in the terrain where he finds himself. There are years when most of the onion seed perishes, and when this happens you have to turn to your neighbors to make sure you're not left out in the cold: so you take what you can pick up from them when they thin out their beds, in May or June, and replant it; but you have to be careful in this respect: quite often this replanted onion succeeds well, and quite often it remains as a spring onion: everyone must know his own terrain; and those whose land, like mine, does not suffer from replanted onions, must not waste costs. There is a kind of capriciousness in soils that would be very difficult to explain; I know of some where this vegetable only does well when replanted, and many others where it can only succeed from seed; we must follow nature's indications, without always wanting to ask her for the reason for her operations. I've said all I can about red and pale onions; now I'll move on to white. There are two types, one of which is earlier than the other; they are distinguished by the leaf, the late variety having a much larger leaf than the other; otherwise, they are perfectly alike. These two species commonly come a little smaller than the red; both are cultivated in the same way, but are not sown at the same time. The early species is sown in July and August, up to mid-September, on light soil like our marshes, and replanted in October, three inches apart. It survives the winter very well, especially if covered with a little bedding or dry leaves during severe frosts and snowy weather, and starts to grow as soon as the fine weather returns: it is replanted in March, if it has lost some during the winter, and is good again in May. If necessary, weed it, and give it plenty of water; as it is not intended to be kept, we should only try to bring it forward and make it grow: this species supplies all summer, but when winter approaches, it is very prone to sprouting or rotting. So as not to lose anything, all those that start to grow should be planted on the coast in November and December: they stand up well to bad weather, and give their seed rather than the one that is replanted after winter; they supply themselves better, i.e., they grow more mounts. The late species can be sown in February and March, and the fruit can be stored over winter; for the rest, it must be cultivated and harvested with the same precautions as the red. These two species are the most prized here, especially as the fruit is sweeter; it is also the only one used from May to autumn, and is still eaten in winter. It is particularly appreciated as a large filbert, for garnishing soups and various kinds of stews: it is easy to obtain by sowing it very thickly; and, although it does not produce so much per bushel, it is not of lesser value, because it always sells for double or triple the price. If you sow it in July or August, make sure you don't water it again as soon as it's emerged; too much moisture causes the worm to grow and die. It should also be noted that the first crop sown in July never turns out as well as the one sown the following month; the only advantage is that, although it is less well formed, it is in a better condition to be used than the other. In addition, depending on the soil, you need to decide whether to sow it earlier or later; in light soils, you need to be in less of a hurry than in heavy soils. I said that the tai dive species would keep better over winter than the hâtive; but I must explain that it is as much as the latter was sown before winter; because, sown in March, it goes almost as far as the tardive. One, for example, is a fortnight or three weeks ahead of the other: this is a species originally grown in Grand Caire; another is harder, and more resistant to winter humidity, snow and frost. Another has the particular merit, when replanted for seed, of yielding extraordinarily well, so that one onion grows up to ten or twelve mounds, forming as many heads, while others grow only one or two. Another has the defect of coming double or twin, which makes it a poor seller. It is only in the long run, I confess, and after a particular study, that one can know these different qualities; each one, according to the idea which I give, and relatively to his views, will try to get the species which will be appropriate to him, by addressing directly the maraïchers, who make a particular traffic of this vegetable, and who are informed by their experiment, of all these differences. The long onion is not well known in the Paris area, but it is well known in Provence; it is almost wrist-sized and up to eight inches long, equal in size at both ends and in the middle. There are red and white varieties, the latter being the mildest and most esteemed; both are essentially good only in salads, or eaten with salt, according to Provençal taste. They are sown in spring, and cultivated like the others; but there are few soils suitable for them: they are kept all winter. The Catalan onion is good only raw, being very sweet: it is extraordinarily large, and spiky, both at the root and at the heart: there are white and red; it does not keep long; it is also cultivated like the others. The Florence onion is a small white onion about the size of a hazelnut, extremely soft and tender, which is eaten green throughout the summer in salads with lettuce: it is split in two with part of its leaf. It is the most weepy of all, and has the advantage of leaving almost no taste in the mouth. It is sown at different times to ensure a constant supply, and is sown very thickly; it requires nothing in particular for cultivation, other than frequent wetting. Onions of all kinds have one major enemy, which is the nightingale worm, and another small white worm common in light soils and dry years. These two insects suck and gnaw at the base of the plant, and sometimes kill entire planks; I know of no remedy. The mole also wreaks havoc in the early stages of the season, and must be kept on sentry duty to destroy it. It is also prone to clouding, an ailment that can neither be preserved nor cured. You must always sow more than less, and in different places; this is the only precaution you can take to avoid running out. Many gardeners are in the bad habit of mixing a little lettuce, turnip or carrot seed into their onion seed; it's easy to see that this does harm to the onion, because these overabundant plants consume the salts in the soil, and by their spread suffocate the plant in its youth; it's therefore a practice that needs to be reformed. Whether or not an onion comes into its own depends to a large extent on the quality of the seeds, which are often poorly ripened and not full, or too old, which is indistinguishable; and this is what you need to be very careful about: the safest way is to collect the seeds yourself. It is collected in August and September, from onions planted before or after winter, as they can be planted in both seasons, as I said above. The best place to plant them is on the south or east coast, six inches apart and about two inches deep in the ground. The custom of many gardeners is to place them flush with the ground; but, either because their method is worthless in itself, or because the difference in situations or soils contributes to it, I have tested both ways in the same row: the one that was flush with the ground was bare, and the other is perfectly fine; I therefore stick to my experience, and I advise everyone to do the same, to know more surely what to stick to. However, it is important to distinguish the season in which it is planted; onions planted before winter need to be buried less than those planted in spring, due to the humidity that causes them to rot when they are too far underground; I would also add, in this respect, that it is very dangerous to plant them before winter in damp soils; it is better to wait until the end of February. The onion planted in this way begins to grow its stem in May! By the end of June, when it is almost at its full height, and its head has formed, it must be supported, either by stilts to which several heads are tied together, or by running battens; for without this support, the hollow, weak stem is easily blown over by the winds: those who cannot find any wood of their own should at least tie five or six heads together with straw or rushes. Care must be taken not to bind them at the top until they have reached their full height, because if this is done earlier, the stem, which is stopped and cannot rise, will burst in the middle and perish. They should be weeded and watered occasionally in very hot weather, especially when planted along a wall: there is no other precaution to take until the seed has reached maturity, which is announced by showing the middle of the head; then it should be cut off, leaving a foot of stem to bind them together in bundles, and hang them on the floor or against a wall, head up, so that the seed does not fall off as it dries. But first, leave them to dry in the sun for a few days, spread out on a sheet; the seed that comes off naturally is the best, and it's a good idea to set it aside: as for the rest, if you're not in a hurry, leave it as it is until you need it; because the seed keeps for up to four years, thus remaining in its stuffing; and winnowed in the field, it hardly makes it through the second year. It's a good idea to always have a year's supply in advance, as it often fails due to the effect of the clouds that attach themselves to the stem, causing it to perish. It's better to sow in the second year than the first; it lifts more easily, and the fruit turns out better: when it's so new, there's a lot left over as chives. Description of the sorrel; its different species, properties, cultivation, etc. The sorrel, known in some provinces as Vinette or Surelle, is one of the best-known and most useful vegetable garden plants. Four different species are grown: the large, the long, the round and the perennial yellow. The first, called the grande espèce or virgin sorrel, has an extraordinarily large leaf, up to eighteen inches long and five or six wide. Its color is meadow green, and its root yellow: it is little used in cooking, as it is hard and has little juice, making it more curious than useful; it is, however, agreed that it is sweeter than the others. This species bears no seeds, although it does grow a few mounds: it multiplies from old plants, which are divided into several. Long sorrel, which is the most widely cultivated, comes in two varieties, one with a much larger, blonder leaf than the other, known as Belleville sorrel; otherwise, they are alike in shape, with a long, elongated leaf, preceded by a long, slender, pointed, notched tail, with ears at the base, of meadow green, edged with red when it begins to grow in spring: its root is fibrous, long, yellowish and bitter. When it goes to seed in the second and subsequent years, it sprouts a three-foot-long, fluted, branching stem: it bears a number of petalless flowers, composed of several stamens topped with yellowish peaks, rising from a calyx composed of six leaves; the pistil that emerges from the middle then changes into a triangular, blackish, shiny seed, enclosed in a reddish, leafy, three-sided capsule. There is a third species, similar in shape and size to Belleville, but much blonder, and beginning to spread widely. The round sorrel is shaped quite differently from the preceding ones; its root is small, and runs between two lands; its leaf varies, sometimes being almost round, sometimes pointed and eared, sometimes earless; its color is sea-green, with a sour taste; its stem is low and creeping; but as far as the flower and seed are concerned, it resembles the long sorrel. The yellow perennial has a fairly large leaf, more round than long, and very blond. There is also a wild species that grows in meadows, which could be used in place of the others, although it is drier, harder and more sour. I know of another which is found in the gardens of the curious, and which grows as a shrub; its leaf is quite similar to that of the poplar, smooth, glossy, and plain on the edges, of a light green, which has more or less the taste of the other sorrels; but it is drier, and its most real merit consists in its rarity. The four main species I have described have more or less the same virtues and qualities: however, only the two long sorrels and the perennial yellow one are commonly used for living. They are used in soups, mixed with pear and other herbs: they make a stuffing that can be eaten under œuss, fricandeaux, and various fish: they are also used to color purées, and for many other purposes. Its sour taste awakens the appetite and is generally appreciated, especially as it has no unhealthy qualities. On the contrary, Elie is aperitive and refreshing, and its use is sovereign in bilious temperaments: it quenches thirst and stimulates the appetite: its leaf moderates blood fermentation, and relieves scorbutic patients, mixed in their broths with watercress and spoon grass or cochlearia; this remedy has often cured them radically, and the peoples of the North, who are very prone to scurvy, use it with great success. Stuffed with eggs, it is equally beneficial. Applied as a poultice with sourdough, after cooking under hot ashes in a cabbage leaf, it advances the suppuration of tumors, and suppurates eye humors. Soaked in vinegar and eaten in the morning on an empty stomach, it is a preservative against all kinds of contagious diseases: it also cures fickle fire by applying it to the diseased part after having thoroughly bathed it. Its decoction is excellent against the bite of rabid dogs; it must be drunk several times a day, and the wound wrapped in the leaf washed until healed: the same decoction is effective against jaundice, at a dose of one pint a day. Its root gives the water in which it has been infused a red color, which serves to deceive sick people who want to drink wine: it is also used in most apozemas and refreshing herbal teas: it helps blood circulation when it is thickened or slowed down. The pulverized seed, drunk with water and wine, stops dysentery: it even enters into the composition of many other remedies: and, in all respects, it is a most estimable plant, and one of which a vegetable garden should never be lacking. This leaf also has a number of special virtues: it removes ink stains from linen; it removes rust from copper and iron, and polishes them: used with sand, it perfectly cleans glass, and colors new parquet floors. When your teeth are irritated, chew a few leaves and the irritation passes. There's nothing unusual about growing this plant; it comes in all kinds of soil, and can be sown on the ground from March to August: however, it is very prone to perish from spring frosts, or to be eaten by loaches, when sown too early, especially in heavy soils; the safest thing is to wait until the beginning of May. It can also be sown in beds or in borders around quarries; but it lasts longer in borders, being more at ease and more aerated; it is even better to transplant it eight or ten inches apart than to sow it: it comes out more beautiful. Wherever it is planted, the soil must be well-prepared, loose and healthy; for its seed is so fine that, if there are any clods or stones, it will only rise in places. In the border as well as in the bed, we make small rays, slightly dug, and we spread the seed on them as evenly as we can, and very clear, which we cover with half an inch of earth at the most; we then put back on top a little compost, if we have some, or well broken dung : If the weather is dry, it must be wetted every day until it is well established. Some time later, we thin it out, if we judge it appropriate, and it becomes more beautiful. It is good to cut six weeks after sowing, and the more often it is cut, the more tender the leaf: but care must be taken to cut it close to the ground; it is better to pull it up when you have the time. Finally, it produces without interruption until the frosts: at that time, i.e. in December, it should be cut flush with the ground, and the beds should be completely covered with compost or henhouse dung; it comes out much more beautiful the following spring: for this reason, it is a good idea to have it in beds as well as in borders. When it starts to grow in February, it's a good idea to cover it with burnt grass; and if it is prone to being eaten by partridges, as it is in the capitaineries around Paris, where the game cannot be touched, it is necessary to spread a quantity of vine shoots over it, or to stretch a net, which can be made as long and wide as you like, and which rests on hoops pricked into the ground at both ends, from height to height, and tied together in the middle by a running batten: This is the expedient I resorted to, finding myself so overwhelmed by this game that it was impossible for me to see any sorrel or spinach leaves at home until April. The leaf is finally ready to be picked in February, a little earlier or later, depending on the year, so it must be plucked with its tail, not cut off; it provides until May, when its stem begins to rise; and if you don't care to collect the seed, you cut it off as many times as the plant throws out: it sprouts new leaves, which you use as the first ones: but as it has much more strength in this season than in the first, you need a much smaller quantity for the uses to which it is put. N much smaller quantity for the uses to which it is put. This plant can be kept for ten to twelve years, but care must be taken to cut off its head when it begins to age; it sprouts new suckers which rejuvenate it: care must also be taken to destroy the weeds that intertwine in its roots, particularly couch grass and tern. The seed of this species is harvested in July: it is cut when the calyx has turned from green to reddish-brown, and laid out on a sheet in the sun for a few days, to improve: others pull it by hand all around the stem without cutting it, and in this way it takes longer to collect; but it dries more easily, and takes up less space.
