It keeps well for two years if winnowed immediately, and for four years if left in its wadding. Birds are very fond of it when it's standing, so you have to defend it as best you can. When you buy this seed, you are often deceived, because it has been picked up in meadows, and this type of sorrel degenerates, for the most part, into patience. There is no mark to distinguish it, so you have to be on your guard. It remains for me to talk about the other two species. The yellow perennial, which is not subject to climbing like the previous one, is not subject to any constraint, and provides a useful supply in this vein of time when the old seedling is climbing, and the new one is not yet in cut; it is moreover less acidic and less green, and therefore pleases many people more; this is why it is advantageous to always have some beds of it. It multiplies by small shoots which are detached from the old plants, and planted a foot apart; the clump grows from year to year, and can be kept for ten years. Our market gardeners don't like it, because it takes longer to regrow than the other variety, once it has been cut; but when you have a certain quantity, you don't notice this defect, which is nothing for people who are not obliged to live as they do on the multiplied produce of their land. Rondo sorrel, which is where I end, can multiply from seed like long sorrel, but it is usually from its offshoots that old plants are detached: it is planted and cultivated in the same way; it also lasts up to ten or twelve years. Some people esteem it more highly than other varieties, but the fact that so little is eaten of it seems to rule it out: I attribute this to the fact that it has little juice, although it is good, N 2 as it is known to provide much less than the long variety. When we want to warm this plant, I mean the long one, to enjoy it during the winter, we have to lay out boards only two feet wide, at the sides of which we dig trenches twelve or fifteen inches wide and two feet deep, which we fill with warm manure, and change every two weeks, from mid-November to the end of January. In times of frost and snow, we keep the beds covered with dry manure, which we remove as soon as the weather softens or the sun comes out. It can also be replanted on beds after All Saints' Day, producing even faster and more abundantly. A bed, more or less, is set aside for this purpose, three feet high and filled with eight to ten inches of potting soil, on which parsley and other plants with strong roots can be transplanted at the same time. Some people are content to cover it with straw mats, supported on a trellis which they load with dry litter, in proportion to the severity of the weather; others put it under a bell; the latter is the safest way, both to advance it and to preserve it: in either case, you need to give it air as much as you can, otherwise the leaf grows white and has no taste. Sorrel is preserved for winter, mixed with other herbs, and is very useful in times of snow and frost. All the necessary herbs are picked at the end of August or September: sorrel, which should make up a third or at most half the quantity; pear, lettuce, spinach, purslane, parsley and chervil, in equal quantities, with a little spring onion, if you don't hate the taste. All these herbs are peeled and washed in several waters: they are left to drain and dry for a while, after which they are chopped coarsely: they are cooked without water in a pot which is filled to the brim, pressing them as hard as possible, taking care to make only a very small sill underneath so that they don't burn: from time to time, we stir them; and when they are sufficiently cooked, we put them in pots which we fill to within an inch, and which we immediately transport to the place where they are to remain; and when they are in place, we pour over them a thickness of about a finger of melted butter, or olive oil; we leave them in this state until they are needed; they keep perfectly well throughout the winter. However, it should be noted that, when they are covered with butter, they fan and toast easily as soon as the jar is opened: they should be eaten immediately; and for this reason, oil should be preferred, as it always comes to the surface, and keeps them in the same state for as long as desired. CHAPTER LVII. Description of the parsnip; its various species, properties, cultivation, etc. Parsnip, otherwise known as Pastenade, is a plant whose entire merit lies in its root. There are three species: the Long, the Round and the Siamese parsnip. The long parsnip has a very long root, almost equal in size throughout its length, white inside and out, uneven and rough on the outside, usually topped with a few filaments or small roots, giving off a strong, sweet smell; its leaves are deeply cut, toothed on the edges, and preceded by a very long tail where they hold; round, slightly fluted, light green and glossy, with no down: the parts of which they are composed are placed two by two, ten to twelve in number, opposite each other, and ending in an odd one; its stem is very thick, deeply grooved and branched, reddening a little on one side: At the end of the branches is a very large parasol, made up of small pink flowers with five equal yellow petals, surmounted by a few stamens, whose calyx turns into an oval, flattened cinnamon-colored fruit that lightens at the edges. The second species, called the round parsnip or panais à la royale, is equally shaped in leaves, stems, flowers and fruit, but the root is larger and shorter, resembling a round turnip. The third species, called Siam parsnip or parsnip bastard, differs from the first only in its root, which holds the middle ground between the other two, being neither as long as the first nor as short as the second, but much more swollen on the head side than in the rest of its length: its flesh is also a little more yellowish. Cultivation of this plant is no different from that of the carrot. It is also sown in two seasons: what I have said for the one will serve for the other, and repetition would not be more instructive. All three species are very good; however, the Siam parsnip is more highly esteemed, as its skin is thinner and its flesh more tender and mellow: in addition, the N4 PANAIS. terrain must play a large part in the choice, as I observed for the carrot. It is also used for soups and juices; but it is not usual to eat it fricasseed or stewed with meats; its sweet taste pleases some, but not most. Beware that there is a sort of nerve in the heart of the root, which must be removed by cutting it, as it is unpleasant for those who eat it. It is of little use in medicine; however, its seed, which is carminative and diuretic, is used in the same dose as aniseed, along with all the other minor hot seeds, which are used to dissipate winds and vapors, to soothe colic and to stop the running of the stomach: but, although it is recognized as good, the seed of the wild parsnip is preferred. This root has no fear of frost, so only as much as can be consumed during the rigors of winter should be kept in the greenhouse. In March, however, the quantity needed to produce seed should be removed, and immediately replanted like carrots, a foot or eighteen inches apart. Then, in July, when the stems have reached about their full height, it's a good idea to support them with stilts, otherwise the wind will blow them over and ruin them. The seed ripens at the end of August, and is only good during the first year, that is, for the two seeds of March and September. CHAPTER LVIII. Description of PATIENCE; its different species, properties, cultivation, etc. There are eight species of Patience, which I will reduce to three that are the only ones cultivated and the most useful: these are the common Patience, otherwise known as Parelle; the red Patience, also known as Sang-dragon; and the garden Patience, known under the two names of Mountain Rapontic or Monk's Rhubarb. All three are perennial. The first species has a straight, long, thick, yellow, bitter-tasting root; its leaf is roughly similar to that of sorrel, from which it derives its original origin; for we still notice every day that it often degenerates into patience in sandy soils: this leaf is nevertheless longer, firmer, narrower N5 and more pointed, with a bitter and very sour taste; its stem is gnarled, and rises to four or five feet; its flowers are placed along the branches and in rings; They are small, petalless, and composed of six short green stamens with straight white tips, which emerge from a seven-leaf calyx, as in sorrel; the pistil then changes into a triangular seed, enveloped in a membranous capsule composed of three large calyx leaves. This plant is cultivated in gardens, to mix with sorrel and other herbs, but few people use it: it is mainly intended for health purposes. Its root is an aperitif and laxative: it is used for dropsy, pallor, jaundice and other diseases caused by obstruction: it is also used externally for scabs and gravel. Red patience is quite similar to the previous one in some respects; but the leaf is shorter, and crossed by a number of red veins, from which, when broken, a blood-red juice emerges, giving it its name. It is used in food, like the previous one, mixed with other herbs, and is very useful when you need to keep your stomach a little loose. It is also sometimes prescribed in emollient and refreshing broths. Its seed strengthens, tightens and soothes pain: a dram or a dram and a half is taken in any suitable liquor, to stop excessive discharge from the womb, and belly flow accompanied by trenches. A learned author claims to have cured a number of people suffering from the latter disease, by macerating half a drag of this seed in hot wine, which is passed over before drinking. Garden patience, or rapontic, has a long, thick, multi-fibered root, brown on the outside and very yellow on the inside; its stem rises to a man's height; it is fluted, reddish, and divided upwards into many branches; Its leaves are a foot to a foot and a half long, broad, pointed, firm and smooth, dark green, sometimes folded over at the edges, but plain and without crenation, borne on long reddish tails; its flowers are petalless, with stamens similar to those of sorrel, placed all around the branches. The flowers are followed by angular seeds enveloped in membranous follicles. This plant has almost the same principles as bastard rhubarb, whose virtues it also shares. It fortifies the stomach and aids digestion; it slightly purges Nó the bile, is highly astringent, and lifts obstructions of the liver, either by dissolving thick humors or by restoring the tone of slackened fibers; it is also sometimes very usefully employed, in powder or decoction, for flux of the stomach and obstruction of the viscera. The dose is half a dram in powder form, or one and a half drams in infusion. This plant grows naturally on mountains, and is also widely cultivated in gardens. Its cultivation, like that of the preceding plants, requires nothing special. All three are sown in March, with the same care as sorrel. The seed is harvested in August, when the stems are dry and the seed begins to detach. It keeps well for two or three years. CHAPTER LIX. Description of the PERCEPIERRE; its different species, properties, cultivation, etc. PERCEPIERRE is called by various names: Passepierre, Bacille, Criste marine, Fenouil marin, or Herbe de Saint-Pierre. There are two species, both perennial; the large ex the small. The first has a large, long, woody, whitish root, with a few fibers; its stems are from fifteen to eighteen inches long, paddle-shaped, thick, woody, and usually fall to the ground; its leaves are cut, small, narrow, firm, fleshy, subdivided three by three, watery green, with the odor of ache, and a salty taste; its flowers are born in umbels, made up of a large number of leaves.After the flower has passed, the calyx changes into a fruit filled with seeds that are joined two by two, flat, striped on the back, white, fragrant and pungent-tasting. The second species, which is more commonly cultivated than the larger one, grows to no more than a foot, and is much broader; its root is quite similar to the preceding one; and its leaves are likewise narrow, fleshy, subdivided three by three, but a little wider, green-brown in color, with a salty taste; its flowers are arranged like the other, and yellowish in color: it grows, like the previous one, by the sea in rocks that it seems to pierce, from which it gets its name of percepierre; but with care, it also succeeds in gardens. Its life uses are limited to winter salads, where it is mixed with anchovies, beet, capers, gherkins and so on. Pickled in vinegar, it stimulates the appetite and flatters the taste. It is candied in the same way as gherkins, with which it is usually mixed. Its medicinal properties are scarcely more considerable; however, it is one of the aperitive plants, and is used to cure obstructions of the viscera, for gravel, to alleviate kidney and bladder stones, to stimulate urine and women's months, and for jaundice. In the absence of the candied variety, take a decoction of the leaf, root and seed. It is propagated from seed, which is sown on a bed in March, or in pots filled with potting soil, exposed to full sun; when it has reached a certain strength, it is transplanted to the foot of walls facing south or east. It must be raised with care, as it is very delicate in this climate: the open air and cold are harmful to it; it is therefore a good idea to cover it with some foliage during frosts. Its leaves are cut off at the end of summer, when you want to make use of them. There is no garden, however small, where parsley does not have its place, marked by its continual usefulness in a household. There are six types, namely Common, Big Leaf, Big Root, Macedonian, Curly and Mixed parsley. Common parsley has a long, white root, fairly straight, with a few fibers; the leaves rising from its heart are numerous, extremely indented and toothed, and composed of three or four pairs of small thresholds, arranged on a rib ending in an odd number; The rib is small, rounded on one side and fluted on the other; the stem is thick, fluted and branched; it reaches three or four feet high: the tops of all its branches end in a bouquet of umbrella-shaped flowers with five unequal petals, russet in color, borne on a small calyx that changes PERSIL. into a small, round, gray seed with a very strong odor. Parsley with large leaves is similar in all respects to the preceding variety, except that its leaves are much larger. Parsley with a large root is also similar to the first, except that the rib of its leaves is larger, and its root is also three times as large, as is the seed. There are two species, one with an early green leaf, the other a coarse green. Macedonian parsley has a leaf that is more regularly cut, more hairy and more serrated, and its segments are more rounded and pressed together; its color is also less dark, and the rib is covered with a white down. The tops of all its branches end in bunches of whitish, five-petaled flowers, arranged in a parasol shape, in the same way as common parsley, but larger: its seed is also different, being elongated, swollen at one end and pointed at the other, rounded on one side and convex on the other, ash-colored, and with a strong, odorless flavor. Curly parsley has the same leaf shape as its name suggests, and thus resembles curly cress; moreover, it is shaped like the common variety: same stem, same flower and same seed. The variegated variety imitates the common variety in all respects, except for the color of its leaves, which are mottled with white veins tending to yellow. Of these six species, common parsley is the most commonly cultivated, although they all have more or less the same virtues and properties. It is widely used in cooking to enhance the flavour of meats, fish, eggs and most vegetables: this is its only life-giving property, as it has no inherent appeal as a substitute for food; its root is also used in many stews, and gives a very good flavour to soup. This plant has many health benefits: a decoction of the leaf and root, taken as a drink, provokes vomiting and clears the passages that lead to the heart; the chopped leaf, mixed with breadcrumbs and a little water and placed in a pan over a fire, cures scabs and resolves hardness of the udder; the same remedy causes women in childbirth to lose milk. Its crushed leaf, mixed with a little brandy, is sovereign for cuts and all kinds of wounds, as well as for bruises; it also removes the stench of breath, or at least stops the bad effect for a while. Its root is used in herbal teas and aperitif broths, and its decoction is very useful for smallpox and malignant fevers. Its seed is one of the four major hot seeds, and is also used in the composition of several remedies. This plant is very easy to grow: the seed is sown in March or April, either in borders around quarries, or in beds two inches deep, with five rows in each five-foot bed. Our market gardeners sow it broadcast to get more profit out of it, but it's cleaner to lay it out as I say, and at the same time gives the ease of crimping the plant in its youth, besides being more convenient to cut in this situation. It sprouts after three weeks: weed it, wet it, crimp it until it has taken the upper hand, and then leave it to grow at will, without further care. Any kind of soil is almost equally suited to it, provided it is a little loose, and it requires no expenditure of manure; but if you are abundantly supplied with compost or fine manure, it is not useless to put some on top of the seed, especially in soils prone to cracking. When you want to make use of the root, you need to thin out the plant a lot after it has gained a certain strength, so that it can grow; and you pull up part of it on the eve of frost, which you take to the greenhouse to find it if you need it. Care should be taken in September to cut it flush with the ground, so that it can regrow new leaves in the autumn; they are softer and greener: but in this case you must be prepared to cover it with plenty of litter in times of frost and snow, otherwise it will perish; and if you are not in a position to do this, it is much better not to cut it: its old leaves serve as a shelter for the heart and preserve it. Those who want to heat it on a bed to have it all winter, must take care to pull it out before the ground is sealed by frosts. Replanting is very simple: you put eight to ten feet of it under each bell, after having shortened the root a little, and cut all the foliage down to the heart: it picks up easily and soon sprouts new leaves. Air the plants as much as the weather allows, or at least uncover the bottom of the bells for a few hours. After three weeks, the plant can be cut, and it always grows again as long as you leave it, provided you keep the bed warm; but after February, the plant in the ground starts to produce, and no longer needs to be forced; it only needs eight to ten inches of potting soil. This plant doesn't come up in the first year, and the more you cut off its leaves, the more it grows back; but in the second year it makes its stem: however, when you cut it off in May, the stem grows suckers that last for another year: this means that it doesn't give any seed; but when you leave it free, it starts to come up in May: it flowers in June, and its seed is ripe in August. The stems are cut off at the base, and left for a few days exposed to the sun, spread out on a cloth: this precaution is necessary, otherwise more of the seed will detach and be lost; it is then winnowed and enclosed: it keeps well for four or five years. Parsley leaves are dried in October, to make up for the green ones in winter, and give the same taste to the stews in which they are used. The best way to dry them is to throw them into boiling water for a while, after they have been thoroughly washed and peeled, lay them out in the sun on racks, and then put them in a warm oven; after which they are locked in a dry place, wrapped in paper bags or boxes: others dry them in the shade, tied in bundles and hung from the floor. Now it's time to talk about the other species. Large-leaf parsley has all the virtues and properties of the common variety, and grows in the same way; but it has a defect that has caused it to be abandoned, which is that it aborts in the heart after it has sprouted its first leaves. For this reason, our market gardeners don't grow it. Parsley with a large root is not sufficiently well known in France, and it is inappropriately neglected. The Germans are justly fond of it, and it is the species to which they are most attached: its leaf has no particular merit, but its root, which is about the size of a carrot, and is therefore of great benefit, has the double advantage of being softer and sweeter. It is used in all soups, and gives a perfect taste to the broth: it is also mixed into many stews, where it has a very useful place. There are two species; the dark green leaf is the most cultivated, because it is more resistant to frost than the other, which is prone to yellowing and perishing. Macedonian parsley is not used in green like the others; its taste is different too. It is only used in salads, and even then only after it has been blanched. To make it white, we cut off all the leaves on All Saints' Day, and cover it with a thick layer of dry straw, which we increase in proportion to the weather, so that frosts cannot reach it: it grows new, tender yellow leaves under this covering, which we cut a month or six weeks later. Its taste is not to everyone's liking: we don't even see it in public markets any more. It is sown and raised like any other; it is not very delicate and can be grown in any kind of soil. Curly parsley has the same taste as the others, and can be cultivated just the same; but it has the disadvantage of being very tender in frost, and of perishing easily, which means that it is little cultivated. The variegated variety is more or less the same in taste, but it is very delicate to raise, and yields little: these two reasons prevent it from being grown. There are also two species of parsley that grow naturally, known as Wild Parsley and Marsh Parsley, which are not used for living, but are used for many purposes in pharmacy. As they are not allowed in gardens, and the others are sufficient for our purposes, I will not say more about them. Description of PIMPRENELLE; its different species, properties, cultivation, etc. Pimprenelle is only a salad ingredient, but it is aperitive and refreshing; it purifies the blood. Its leaves are used in broths and in aperitif and vulnerary decoctions; it stimulates sweating and urinates; it also stops haemorrhages: infused in common water for some time, and drunk on an empty stomach, it relieves those prone to gravel; and the same drink is very good when you feel overheated. The distilled juice of its leaves is used in the composition of several balms and syrups, and some ointments: it is a very healthy plant. It grows quite commonly in meadows, and is no less good than the garden variety, which differs only in cultivation. There are two species, called the small and the large, which differ only in their leaves; those of the first being round, and those of the second more elongated, both throw many leaves, which fall to the ground half a foot around the heart: these leaves are toothed, and arranged two by two along the rib from which they sprout; their color is quite dark: the heart does not rise, unless it goes to seed; for then it throws a weak and rowy stem, which rises to about two feet; and at the end of the

A kind of elongated bud forms on the twigs, flowering imperceptibly and giving eight or ten seeds; this is one of the first to ripen. Picked in June, it is reddish, fairly large, four-sided, gravelly and pointed at the ends. It keeps well for three years, when picked at the right time. Cultivation of this plant is very simple; it is sown in March in rows, or broadcast; it is weeded as needed, and watered a little in hot weather: the more it is cut, the more it grows back; and the younger the leaves, the more tender and tasty they are; it is always the small ones in the heart that should be used for salads. It is very prone to having its root cut by cockchafer worms; I have said enough elsewhere about what to do to protect yourself from these insects. CHAPTER CHAPTER LXII. Description of POIREAV; its different species, properties, cultivation, etc. THIS plant, which some call leek and others porreau, forms an upright, united and compact body, which is composed of leaves pressed and stuck together, and turned round to the size of a finger; it rises to nearly a foot and a half; and retains the same consistency at 6 or 8 inches from the ground; it then develops, and throws out leaves whose tips fold back against the ground, which are long, narrow and gutter-shaped, bluish-green, smooth and fairly thick, bearing a strong and unpleasant odor: All that is in the ground is white and tender, and this is the best; the rest is green; however, it is used like the foot as long as it has consistency. Its root is nothing more than a group of white, highly multiplied filaments, much like those of the onion. It is mainly used in soups, and is one of the most commonly used plants. It is also mixed into pea purées and stews, but it is not usually used in any particular preparation. O 13 no special preparation. It would not even be healthy to make too much use of it, as it is certain to engender ventosities and bad humors: it is also accused of producing other extraordinary effects during the night; but I say this only according to a few authors who are not the law, and without any other certainty than their testimony. Its medicinal properties are far superior to its uses in life; it is aperitive, resolutive and bechic: it is one of the most sovereign remedies for pleurisy. Cook one or two handfuls of the white in a cabbage leaf under the ashes, and apply to the side; or fry in a pan with good vinegar, crushed or lightly boiled, and apply to the tumors of the articles, it dissipates them. Broths made with leeks and turnips are good for extinguishing voices and chest weaknesses; they also make a favorable syrup for lung disease. The seed is as aperitive as the plant itself; for the same ailments, a large piece is crushed and infused in a glass of wine. The leaves, cooked and applied to swollen hemorrhoids, relax them and remove the inflammation. I have read that a handful of its seeds placed in a barrel of wine prevents it from souring, and corrects sourness when it does occur: but I have not experienced this. There are two types of leek, long and short, which differ only in length. The long leek, however, is the most cultivated, because it provides more by its length; but the short one also has the merit of being more resistant to frosts; and for the end of winter, it should always be preferred; it also has the advantage that the worm doesn't attach itself to it so much. The long one should occupy the stage until the frosts. In all likelihood, these two species originate from the wild leek, commonly found in vineyards, and have been perfected through cultivation. The seed is sown in March in loose, well-prepared soil: The seed is sown in the month of March in loose, well-prepared soil: after sowing, it is harrowed and top-dressed, but first walked on to ensure that the soil is healthy; it is wetted to help the seed germinate, and continued as needed; the seedling is weeded exactly, and replanted around Midsummer, when it is the size of a quill or a little larger; but if you want to bring it forward, it can be planted in the first days of June. The beds are set as wide as you like, with the rows six inches apart, and the plants only four inches apart: O2 first wet the bed before pulling it out, for ease of pulling; then cut off half the leaf and all the root, as close to the heel as you can: after this little preparation, we plant it with a planter that we push in, six inches apart, and in each hole we sink a leek, without pressing the earth against the foot in any way: we water it generously immediately afterwards, and the water that runs down the holes carries away as much earth as is needed for the moment: we continue for two days at a time, as this is one of the plants that requires the most water: It soon picks up again, and so easily, that three days later you can see the leaf lengthening; some time later you crimp it, and trim its leaves two or three times during the summer; this little method contributes greatly to making the plant grow larger: it can be eaten until All Saints' Day; but if you want to eat it earlier, it's just as good; and our market gardeners, who have the art of bringing it forward, bring quite large quantities to the market as early as July. In some provinces, it is eaten green in its earliest youth, without having been replanted, and is preferred to the white variety: everyone can be satisfied in this respect, as the taste is the same at all times. As Christmas approaches, we pull up the large species, which is very prone to perish on the vine, and bury it close by in a small trench, which we cover with litter during the frosts; but we leave the short one in place, because it resists bad weather, and keeps well until it goes to seed, i.e. until May: However, we take care to dig up a certain quantity and bury it in the greenhouse, to supply it during the frosts, if we don't have any of the larger species enclosed. After winter, you mark the quantity you want to let go to seed, and destroy the surplus if you have too much. In May, it begins to grow its stem, which rises to four or five feet, and bears at its tip a kind of very large tuft, covered all around with purplish bell-shaped flowers, followed by a triangular cup, which encloses the seed in three separate compartments: This seed is black in color, shaped much like the onion, but larger; it is cut when the hulls begin to open, and placed on a sheet to ripen: the one that comes off naturally is the best; and to draw out the other, it is crumpled with the hands, or if you like, placed in a wicker manna, and rubbed against the edges; it is then winnowed and enclosed. It's good for 2 years; but if you keep it in its shell attached to a floor, as some people do for onions, it keeps for three or four years. As this plant is extremely hard on the soil, it requires a good manuring afterwards, to be able to produce something else; it is also very prone in certain years to be eaten by a small white worm, which engenders itself in the heart: which cannot be remedied. The plant is replanted as it dies, and care is taken to ensure that a little plant remains. The beetle worm also sometimes destroys some: the progress of the disease can be prevented by looking for it at the foot of the plant, as I have said for many others. CHAPTER LXIII. Description of PCIRÉE; its different species, properties, cultivation, etc. THIS plant, called Bette in several provinces, is an annual: two species are grown, the white and the red. The white pear has a cylindrical, woody, white, long, thick root; its thresholds are large, broad, smooth, succulent; sometimes yellowish-green, sometimes true green, nitrous in flavor, odorless, with a thick, broad, white rib; its stem is fluted and branched, rising to 3 or 4 feet: its flowers arise from the leaf axils on long spikes; they are small, composed of several stamens topped with yellowish tops, and emerge from a five-leafed, greenish calyx, which then changes into an unevenly round, gravelly, ashy or russet seed the size of a pea. Red pear differs from the previous variety in that its leaf is smaller, reddish-green and the rib is bright red. It is of no use for life, and is only used medicinally when the former is lacking. The white variety is used in food, where it is mixed with sorrel to soften it, either for soups or stuffings; it is mild and very healthy. Its health properties are much more considerable: its leaf is emollient, soothing, laxative, desiccative and abstersive; it is used in ordinary decoctions and in enemas; its particular juice, well strained and purified, given in a clyster, is singular for emptying fecal matter that resists other laxative clysters; the leaf applied externally to the skin, when it has been removed by bladders or O4 caustics, facilitates the draining of the humor: it can also be applied to a tumor to attract the matter, and is a great help in bringing it to an end: it is also used against inflamed hemorrhoids; and if it doesn't make them suppurate, it at least dissipates the inflammation, and makes them withdraw; it's a good idea to steam them beforehand with the expressed juice. Sucking the juice through the nose, mixed with a little salt-free pot broth, unclogs the ducts and dissolves the pituita that has thickened there. A piece of the rib or root chopped and crushed in the same way, and introduced into the nostrils, has the same effect. The juice of the root is a good sternutatory, and when used with its pomace in the nostrils to relieve migraine or headache, it brings great relief by draining the serosities. The root is also used to loosen children's stomachs; to do this, a piece must be introduced into the foundation, after having been sprinkled with salt. This plant is very easy to grow, and any soil is good for it, as long as it is prepared with a good ploughing. It is sown in March in light soils, and in April in heavy soils. It can be sown broadcast, as our market gardeners do, or in rays eight inches apart, as is more common in private gardens: the latter is more convenient for crimping and cutting. Six weeks after it has been sown, you can start using it; and in this season, when the root is still weak, you cut it flush with the ground: it sprouts new leaves, and the more often it is cut, the softer and smoother the leaf becomes; it becomes hard and dry when it is old. It can be sown every month until August, and this last sowing is the one that yields the most and the most abundantly the following spring; but it is also more delicate to overwinter; it must be covered if you don't want to run the risk of losing it. The first plant, sown in March, is stronger and more resilient, but yields later; this one should be cut to the ground in September, so that it can regrow new leaves in autumn. After the frosts, the one that has been covered is uncovered, and given a little shaking to start it growing; when it is ready to be picked, the leaves are blown off at ground level, without the use of any instrument; the heart grows successively for two or three months, and when it begins to curl up to make its amount, it is abandoned; the new seedling then replaces the old one. 5 Pear for chard is a particular species of which there are two types, one extremely blond, the other darker in color, called demi-verte. The first has a wider, softer rib, but it has difficulty surviving the winter; the second is a little less perfect, but it is much more resistant to frost: the blond should therefore only be sown in spring, to be enjoyed in summer and autumn, after which time it is destroyed, with only a few plants left for seed, which is covered as best as possible during the winter: the other should be sown at the end of June, and replanted in mid-August, in borders or beds, eighteen inches apart in all directions, after cutting off half its leaves and the root tip. Both require frequent watering during hot spells, which is what makes the rib tender; during frosts, it must be covered exactly with dry litter, and in spring it is uncovered little by little in the same way as Partichaut: its carding is good by mid-May. Our market gardeners cut off the entire stalk, because custom forces them to do so, and because it would not be saleable in detached leaves: it might also be thought that it is the need they have for space in this circumstance of the weather, which gave rise to this custom; but private individuals who are quite happy to extend its duration, should be content to burst the widest leaves, pulling them to one side; those that remain around the heart strengthen, and are good successively for a month or six weeks. When it finally begins to rise, mark the plants you wish to keep for seed, and destroy the rest. 2 Many people, and our market gardeners in particular, plant these chard plants between their artichokes; I do not approve of this method, for the reasons I gave in the chapter on artichokes. Many people particularly like it with Gruyère or Parma cheese, grated on top: many others don't like it at all; it's true that it's a bit bland, and even in some areas it smells only of earth. This plant, both the simple pear and the two species of chard, multiplies only from seed, which is collected in September from overwintered plants; as they spread out and rise very high, they must be tied to stilts to support them, otherwise the wind will blow them over: they are cut when the seed is partly ripe, i.e. when it turns from green to an ashen or russet colorâO6 POIS. They are then left in the sun for a few days, and immediately locked up. It keeps well for 8 or 10 years. CHAPTER LXIV. Description of the pea; its different species, cultivation, etc. Although peas are widely known and cultivated almost as much as wheat, in order to follow my plan of giving an exact description of all vegetable plants, I will describe this one. The pea in general, of all species, is conformed in more or less the same way; its stem is single, smooth, hollow and weak, rising more or less, according to the species; its leaves are of a small bluish or celadon green, placed from distance to distance along its stem: the leaves are of a small bluish or celadon green, placed at intervals along the stem: they first form two kinds of ears attached to the stem, and from the middle emerges a rib which throws out four or six oval leaves, placed regularly at its sides, and ending in several tendrils which serve to cling to the others to support itself, when it doesn't have the help of oars: from the armpits of these same ears, higher or lower, depending on the species, emerges the flower whose calyx is a toothed cup, which grows the pistil; this flower is composed of four unequal florets, whose color is white or red, depending on the species, marked with a purpurine spot : each floret is usually composed of two equal flowers, sometimes only one, and along the length of the stem there may be as many as six or eight: these flowers are followed by the pod which contains the pea, which is more or less elongated, depending on the species. If this vegetable were as beneficial as it is pleasant to eat, I think most people would want to eat it all year round; but it is rightly criticized for being windy and indigestible: this does not prevent people from being extremely keen on them, especially in the new market, where they are sold in Paris for as much as 100 and 150 pounds a litron, a measure that contains about three half-setiers, or three quarters of a pint. Peas in general are of little use in medicine, apart from chickpeas and lupins, which I'll discuss in more detail; however, their flour is substituted for that of lupins and vetches when necessary; it is equally resolutive and emollient; its decoction is also laxative and soothing. Some claim that peas of all kinds soothe coughs, and one author maintains that they are useful for epileptics. For those who suffer from gravel, they must be avoided at all costs. There are an infinite number of species, of which we know only a few here; but we can make do with the ones we have: besides, nature likes to discover new ones from time to time, or, if you like, to produce new ones, such as the Baron, the Dominé and the Michaux, named after the farmers who discovered them today. These three species are the earliest to ripen, followed by the Lorrain and the Suisse. I will reduce the other most commonly sown peas to the small number of the best that do well in our vicinity, and that can suffice for all seasons; except for those who have other species more suited to their climate and terrain, to cultivate them. I therefore chose the common pea, the Normand, the white and green quarrels, the culpoir, the English green pea, the longuecosse and the pea without parchment. These eight species are all perfectly good, each in its own season. I'll say a word about their cultivation in general, before moving on to their specific cultivation. This grain, though rustic in appearance, must not be planted indiscriminately in all types of soil; it requires a certain combination for sowing; some species require light soil, another wants it to be a little oily, and another is better suited to soil that holds the middle; but all agree in wanting to occupy the same land only from time to time: well-meaning farmers make it a rule to sow their land with this vegetable only once every six years, and this is their method. One year they bring in wheat, and the next year beans; the third they sow in rye or March, and the fourth in vetch or broad beans; the fifth in oats or barley, and the sixth in peas; so that every year, out of six acres, they have one in peas, for which they choose the species appropriate to its vein; And when they have to turn over a meadow, uproot a vineyard or destroy a wood, that place is always reserved for peas, because they don't do any better than in this kind of new land; because manure, which helps other vegetables to bear fruit when the soil is tired, is harmful to them, far from being advantageous; these peas are carried away in wood, and don't bear fruit. This vegetable, more voracious than any other of the earth's natural salts, cannot tolerate any borrowed salts, such as those found in manure. What I have just said should serve as a rule, from the most to the least, for those who grow it in their gardens, to distribute the land they have, so that each year they have a place sufficiently rested to sow this vegetable: If they are careful, they will recognize, as I have often found, that one pole of soil used at the right time will yield more than four used at the wrong time; it even happens sometimes, when you persist in putting it in the same place for several years in a row, that it turns yellow as soon as it sprouts, and yields nothing at all. In small gardens, it is usual to grow a few early peas; but in large plots of land with large houses, it is very common to sow whole quarters of them to supply kitchen needs all year round, and their cultivation is then the same as in the fields; The same species are also used, which is why it's a good idea for me to outline the practice of our farmers with regard to each species in particular, and to begin with everything that relates to their cultivation in general, following the plan I've started with. Peas can be sown in two ways: with a plow or a hoe. The first is not suitable for a garden, so the hoe is the best, because it loosens the soil better. Two people are needed for this operation; one opens the gauge from one end of the square to the other, and the other, who follows, spreads the seed as the square is made; once the gauge is finished, the second is taken up, and the same earth that was lifted is used to cover the first: it is seeded in the same way, and as long as there is land, the same procedure is continued. But, as in a garden we want all the plants to have a certain air of arrangement, which at the same time facilitates the small operations they require, such as crimping them, watering them as needed, picking the fruit, etc., it is advisable to leave an inter valle for the paths, after four rows of seed have been sown. Many gardeners do not know how to handle the hoe; this tool is not known beyond the outskirts of Paris; in this case, four-foot planks must be drawn up, and four rays equally spaced from foot to foot and two or three inches deep, either with the hoe or the corner of the rake; and after spreading the seed and walking it, it is covered with the rake; the soil must have been freshly ploughed beforehand. Eight or ten days later, the seed sprouts, and when the plants are five or six inches high, they should be crimped and shod, making sure that the weather is fine, so that the weeds die right away. If you want to row them, go back two days later, when the weeds are burnt, and plug the oars, making sure to lay them down inside, two by two rows, one on top of the other, so that they don't obstruct the pathways: at the same time, the fruit that follows the oar is picked up more easily on the pathway side. I'll move on to the description of the species, and start with the Michaux pea, which gardeners take great pride in presenting first to their masters, or which is bred mainly by those who make peas an object of their small trade, and who sell them dearly in the primeur, as I've already noted. This pea is white, round, plain, fairly large, very tender, and sweet when eaten green, but of mediocre value, especially as there is usually only one pod in each bunch, and they are stopped at the first blossom, so as to have the fruit instead: it is almost the only one that is grown today for early harvest, although it competes with Baron and Dominé for earliness. Soft soil suits it better than any other, and even the most arid sands will support it, provided the spring is a little tender, or it is helped by watering. In free and black soils, it also grows well, and more reliably, but later. In cold, damp soils, it only languishes, most often rotting during the winter; even when it does escape, it comes so late that it gives no pleasure, and is not worth the care it costs; so the shortest way is to get rid of it when the soil is ungrateful. Here's how to grow it, which requires a great deal of detail. It is sown in mid-November in free soils, and at the beginning of December in light soils, so that it can rise before the frosts; and as it always perishes during the winter, it is a good idea to sow it a little thickly: it should be placed on the south or east coasts, where it is sheltered from bad winds, and within reach of all the sun's rays. Some people sow it in clumps of seven or eight, a foot apart; others sow it in rays two or three inches deep, and form two or three rows in the bed, depending on how wide it is, because you always need to leave two feet free along the walls, to be able to carry out the operations required for espaliers. The latter has always seemed to me the best, because 1. the peas are not piled up on top of each other, so they feed better; 2. the field mice, crows and pigeons, which are very avid eaters, destroy them less in this arrangement, because once they have smelled a clump, they eat every last one. Nor do they pull them so easily from a comb, especially if care has been taken to walk them after sowing them, as should be done; this binds them, so to speak, to the soil; but care must be taken not to sow them, especially in fat soil, until it is healthy; for in damp weather, the operation of walking them would be worthless. After they have been sown and marketed, they are immediately covered, and a little pigeon droppings is thrown on top, if available; this gives the soil a little warmth, which prevents them from melting so easily: after they have risen, they are topped up again with an inch of coarse compost, or horse dung. In the Paris area, there is a type of compost even more favorable than pigeon droppings, either for preserving them or for advancing them: it is commonly called gadoue (sludge); it comes from the roads where the city's sludge and refuse are transported: these materials, matured for a few years, are reduced to compost, and contain wonderful salts for all kinds of plants. It should be removed in the summer, when the weather is dry and it is, so to speak, dusty. Put an inch of it on the bottom of the shelves before sowing peas, and you'll soon realize what a great effect it has. You could make a supply of it anywhere, by collecting the sludge from the streets or nearby roads, and leaving it to ripen in some hole: there is no manure that has as much virtue as this material. Those who have a lot of walls, and who can place a single row of peas at the foot of the walls, without harming the trees, which are often bare at the bottom, enjoy the fruit eight days longer than those who have peas only two feet away, and this eight-day difference certainly makes a big difference to pleasure or profit. Peas sown at the times and in the manner I have just described, are raised, and even having three or four leaves require great care to be defended from the inclemency of the sky: each one, according to his industry and convenience, must preserve them, either with straw mats supported on a kind of trellis that we make at the height of the peas, or with large litter supported in the same way by several poles tied together, so that its weight does not crush these young plants, especially when it is loaded with snow: between the rays, we interweave a little short straw to keep them straight: when the frost becomes stronger, we increase the covers in proportion. Sometimes, for lack of convenience, you don't cover them at all, but they still escape when winters are neither long nor harsh; but it's always risky to leave them to chance. Care must be taken to uncover them a little when there is a fine day of sunshine, provided that the frost has not attacked them under the blankets, in which case the sun would lose them; and as soon as the weather is milder, they must be uncovered completely, always leaving the blankets beside them, so that they can be put back on quickly if necessary: when they remain too long suffocated under the blankets, without air or sun, they turn yellow and melt. Those who have no walls to shelter them, raise slopes of land facing south, and make little straw shelters on the ridge, four feet high: they are no worse off in this situation; but as much as possible, choose light soil, as it warms up much more easily in spring, and the plants are less likely to melt than in greasy, damp bottoms. Once you're past the hard frosts, which usually last until the end of February, at which time the sun begins to gain strength, you remove the blankets, crimp the peas and shoe them; this helps the soil to warm up, and you row them as soon as they are seven to eight inches old: They then begin to show their flower, which is called a loquette; for this kind of pea only grows to eighteen inches or two feet, all the more so as care must be taken to stop them at the third flower; and this operation, which is the last, brings them forward a long way: when only two, or even one, is left, the fruit is still rather ripe. It is often difficult to deprive oneself of half of one's expectations, so everyone can do as he or she sees fit. These pinched tips of the stalk can be used to good effect in soup, giving it a pea-like flavour. After this operation, the pods are visibly fuller, and can be found in April or May, depending on the weather. Care should have been taken to weed them where necessary, and to pull out those that have degenerated; it's easy to identify them, in that the flower has not shown itself as in the others, and the plants are more vigorous. The heat of March sometimes dries out the soil, especially coastal soil, to the point where the plants need to be helped by a little watering; in this case, they should be wetted according to their needs, in the morning with dew, because during the main part of the day, wetting is of no use to them; the fresher they are kept, but without excess, the bigger and more tender they become. It sometimes happens that they perish, despite all the precautions you've taken to preserve them: here's what you can do in such a case to make up for lost time, as far as possible. As soon as you notice them, sow new ones in thick rays on a new layer; they grow quickly and profitably; and when they are four to five inches old, pull them out and replant them in good shelters, reasonably spaced; they regrow very easily, and you gain nearly a month by this advance. Those who have a great eagerness for this vegetable, and who are in a position to make certain expenses, can give themselves the pleasure of enjoying them a month earlier than those who have them in the ground, despite shelter and care, and they can also keep them until Christmas; here's how. Sow them in the first days of November, in baskets 7 to 8 inches high and 10 to 12 inches in diameter, filled with earth and potting soil mixed together, with an inch of manure on top; twenty to twenty-five seeds in each basket are more than enough; We leave them in the open air, sheltered by a wall, until the hard frost; by then they have grown five or six leaves, if autumn is a little fine; we then move them to a greenhouse that is not too hot, and to which we can give air whenever the weather permits: As soon as the weather gets milder, we put them outside, but not too far apart, so as to be quick to bring them in when the frost starts again, and until mid-February we continue to keep them in sight so as not to be surprised: we then change their situation, and put them on warm beds, which we must have prepared for this purpose. These layers must be buried 2 feet deep, and are, strictly speaking, deaf layers; in the middle of the thickness of the manures, we put five to six inches of tan, and two or three inches on the surface, which we then cover with potting soil in such quantity that the baskets are filled all around without exceeding: these baskets are placed on the surface of the manures, and arranged in a chessboard Tome II. I in three rows, so that they are six inches apart from each other: it is important to let the great fire pass through the layers, before putting them in place. They bloom promptly as soon as they have felt this air of warmth; but as they still have to fear the frosts that occur in March, here are the precautions you need to take to guarantee them. Take circles of large barrels; support the two ends, and make them enter the ground on the two edges of the layers which are level with the ground; space them from three to three feet along the whole length, and to maintain them take running laths, with which you will bind them: three rows are enough; and for greater solidity, drive a few stilts into the middle of the layers and tie them to the circles with good wicker; over this trellis, if necessary, throw down straw mats made with string, which envelop the whole circuit, and double them if one is not enough; you will also close the two ends of the layers, so that frost cannot enter. If trained and cared for in this way, they will bear fruit as early as the first days of April, provided the month of March is fine; but whatever the weather, they will always be three weeks ahead of the coastal ones, and once the fruit has been harvested, your layers, which, thanks to the tannin, remain warm for three months, can still be used to grow whatever you see fit. It is understood that you must stop them at the second or third flower, as I said for the others, and that you must empty the baskets when the fruit is picked, dry them, and lock them up, to serve again when you want to use them. As for peas that you want to raise for the late season, sow them at the end of August or in the first days of September, in the same baskets and in the same way as I said. You arrange them along some well-exposed wall, and take care to wet them after they are sown, which you continue to do from two to two days, unless it rains; a few days later they rise, and when they are six to seven inches old you row them; you let them enjoy as long as the weather is fine: they are in full flower a month later, and the pod follows closely. Corn is often subject to frosts in this season, which could ruin it, so bring it close to the house and be ready to lock it up as soon as the weather threatens; and as these kinds of frosts don't last long, put it in the air as soon as it's over, and keep taking it out or bringing it in whenever the weather changes. The fruit starts to taste good at All Saints' Day, and you can pick them from one or the other until Christmas, just as good and just as tender as the spring ones, provided they are always well moistened: I've tried this two or three times. It should be noted that only the Michaux pea succeeds in this season. This quality of pea is not only the best for both spring and autumn, but I find it equally good for all months of the year, and I use it in preference to the others: they are tender and sweet in all seasons, provided they are wetted exactly with morning dew, and eaten as soon as they are shelled, (this circumstance makes a lot:) and you don't languish to enjoy them; in six weeks from the day you sow them, when it's the right season, they give their fruit. The last ones to be sown in the ground are sown in mid-August, and bear fruit in October: if there are no heavy frosts in that month, they can be kept, or, to put it better, they succeed one another until after Saint-Martin's Day, depending on the climate; in any case, sowing can be brought forward or delayed by a few days. In spite of my particular affection for this species of pea, I have no wish to subjugate anyone's taste to my own; each person, according to his or her terrain, will choose the ones that are most successful. It is much better to sow alternately a bed of peas and one of some other plant that is not shaded, such as chervil, parsley, pear and others. When you want to keep a pea bed for seed, you must take care to uproot the plants that degenerate; you can tell by their wood, which is thicker, and their flowers, which are higher; sometimes they don't flower at all. I shouldn't omit to say that whatever the species, they must always be rowed if you want to make a real profit from them, otherwise the field mice and pigeons that prey on them ruin most of them; the free sparrows, which you can't defend them from, already do them enough harm. You don't have to wait until the grain is completely dry to harvest them; as soon as the pods turn yellow, even though the peas are still green, you can pull them out; and they finish ripening exposed to the sun in a courtyard, where birds and other animals don't get in so easily: when they are dry, you beat them and winnow them right away; because rats and mice destroy them in attics, when you lock them up with the cossat. They are good for sowing for two years; in the third year, only part of them come up, and sometimes none at all; but when you are lucky enough to have places free of these insects, and they can stay in their cossat, they keep for four or five years, provided you don't beat them until you want to sow them. To preserve the free species, you need to do what I've said elsewhere, change every year or two with someone a little away from you. With regard to peas that are left to dry to be eaten dry, they should be exposed to the sun for a few days after they have been winnowed, in order to improve their quality and keep them in better condition. One thing should also be kept in mind when cooking them, and that is to use water that is suitable for them, as the more they boil, the harder they become, and the quality of the pea is often blamed for a defect that comes solely from the water. I'll move on to describe the other species. The Dominé pea is the earliest after the Michaux pea, to which it succeeds; and although there is no difference between them other than the fact that one comes eight days earlier than the other, this is always a lot for people who are eager for it, and especially for those who trade in it; However, one does not give way to the other, and for those who are not in a hurry to enjoy it, it is much better value than Michaux, its pods being regularly doubled and fuller; it also rises higher, and requires proportionate oars. It is grown in the same way, and has the advantage of withstanding bad weather better than Michaux, particularly in slightly damp soils: it should be sown light, especially after winter; the grain is white, fairly round and of a good size; it can be sown at any time, but spring is its most favorable season. The Baron pea follows the Dominé very closely, but is very inferior; the pod is extremely small, the grain the same, without sugar or finesse; it was known before the two preceding peas, and its credit was established on its earliness; but since these peas appeared, it has lost a lot, and with 4 justice: however, as it provides a lot, and the flower is not prone to run, it still has some supporters; perhaps also that in other climates it would be better than here. The Lorrain pea is the successor to the previous three, and supplies more abundantly: it is large and fairly sweet, but only succeeds in spring; in the other seasons, the nile attaches itself to it, and the flower perishes: light soil suits it better than any other. It can be eaten dry or green, but is neither as tender nor as soft as many others; its color is white, its shape round and fairly plain. The Swiss pea, otherwise known as the large early pod, is a close successor to the previous varieties, and is more resistant to bad weather. It is grown equally in gardens and in the countryside, and is sown as early as December: Its shape is round and plain, its color a small greenish-yellow and of good size; it fruits a lot, and is not prone to run; its pod is long and full, its grain very tender when green, but when dry it is not used; it is sown until Midsummer, and is very good for the late season, not being prone to nililil like some others: it is one of the most profitable species; it requires good soil. The common pea is about the same in terms of earliness and yield: it is sown like the previous one in December, and bears its fruit within a few days of the other, i.e. at the end of May or the beginning of June, depending on the soil and the year: it is round in circumference, a little flattened on the ridges, which is the effect of the hindrance in which it finds itself in the pod, which is so full that the seeds can barely fit inside: it is of medium size: its color is russet: it must be picked under the flower, i.e., the flower, although dried out, must still hold on to the tail, and it is very tender when picked at this time; but if you wait until the flower has detached, it hardens; it is sown until the end of March, and the last ones sown are good for Midsummer: We don't sow it later, even though it will also come, because the following month the white quarry arrives, and excludes all the others; it requires good soil. The white quarry is the one which, generally speaking, is desired by everyone, and which almost alone occupies the scene as long as it lasts: it is rightly warned in its favor; it is tender and soft, better nourished, fatter, and of a sweeter taste: its color is white as its name announces, its shape uneven and more quarried than round, either green or dry; it is a little late to bring in, rises very high, and does not provide much; these are its faults. We start to sow it at the end of March, and this first sowing is good in July: we continue until the end of May, and we don't go any further; because beyond that it yields almost nothing: it has little taste in the dry, and we must only let it dry for the seed: it requires mediocre soil; in good soil, it only grows wood. This is the species chosen for green drying, and for eating during winter and Lent: it is very delicate and very good, prepared as follows. Select the most tender grains; put them freshly shelled in a boiler with boiling water; after they have made a broth, remove them and put them in fresh water; then lay them out in the air on a white cloth for two or three days, turning them over from time to time; Then put them in a lukewarm oven, clearly laid out on racks, with paper underneath, until they are completely dry. Finally, put them in paper bags and lock them in a dry place. When you want to use them, fry them in lukewarm water, or put a piece of butter married to the flour, and leave them to soak for twenty-four hours; then cook them in the same water, and season them with butter or cream, as you please. Cul noir comes in two varieties, one round and the other quarré, both with a black germ, from which they take their name; the quarré is more highly prized. The former is russet in color; the latter is green, and is used during Lent for purées: it provides a lot, and picked green, it is tender and good. It should not be sown until the end of April, or the beginning of June; sooner or later, the fogs attach themselves to it and make it cloudy: only what is needed for sowing should be kept from the first species, as it is not good to eat dry; it requires good soil. Green quarry differs from white quarry only in its outer color, which is green: it is not so delicate or soft when green, but as a reward it is very useful when dry for purées: it must be very dry before being enclosed, because if it retains any moisture, it will go off and take on a bad taste: it does not provide much, which is its defect like the other. It should be sown at the same time, and placed in slightly worn soil, for the same reason that it grows too much in wood when the soil is too good: some soils make it so hard that it cannot be cooked, particularly in strong soils; care must be taken to place it properly, or to detach it from the soil, if the soil is contrary to it. The Normandy pea is square-shaped, whitish-green and very large: it is tender and soft, eaten green; and dry, it is equally good, and yields more puree than any other species, having an extremely thin skin: it does not fruit much, which is its defect. It is sown from the end of March to Midsummer's Day; later the blossom sinks, and nothing comes of it: the first sown yield much more than the second sown; it requires good soil. The long pod is round, white, plain and clear; its size is mediocre: it fruits a lot and quickly; its pod, which is very long and very full, contains twelve to fourteen grains; it is of all the species that yields the most, and is the most successful for the late season; it does not nuile or curl. It is sown from mid-April to the beginning of July; mediocre soil is more favorable to it than better. For these last seven species, it should be noted that you don't need as much seed for some as for others. The Swiss pea, the common pea, the Norman pea and the cultivated pea want to be sown a little thickly, because they are only one crop; farmers use seven to eight bushels per acre; with regard to the white and green peas, and the long pea pod, they only use five to six bushels, because they fork a lot, and would choke if they were pressed harder. The English green pea was recently brought to us from the country whose name it bears, and is known to only a few people; it is very large, plain, a little oval, of a whitish green, and perfectly good, either green or dry, for purées: it grows quite high, supplies from the stem to the tip, makes its pod large and full, with no flower missing. I sowed it last year for the first time in three or four seasons, and it has always done well: I said elsewhere that my soil was quite strong; I don't know if it would do as well in poorer soil. The pea without parchment, which in some provinces is called the gourmand pea, has very valuable qualities, which make it very popular, except in the vicinity of Paris: I don't know why it is so little appreciated in this capital, where it is hardly to be found in the public markets. It is eaten with the pod like green beans: its taste is sweet and fine, and it is more profitable than any other. There are many species, which I will reduce to 5, as the others seem to me to be degenerated from this one. The most common has a white flower, rises from 5 to 6 feet, and produces a pod of mediocre size: it is the most widely cultivated, the others not being well known: it is quite fertile in grain, provided it is sown light and well branched: the pea is white, uneven, and of mediocre size. The second species has a red flower and grows to seven or eight feet; it therefore requires long, strong oars to withstand the shocks of the wind: it fruits a lot, and gives pods once as big as the previous one, which are very tender and very sweet; the pea is partly reddish and greenish, speckled with purple spots, round, large and smooth. The third has a white flower, rises to the same height and supplies a lot of the same produce as the previous species, but the pod is less wide and more curly; the pea is large and white, fairly smooth and fairly round. The fourth species has a white flower, rises only three or four feet, and produces surprising pods; I have collected some that are eighteen lines wide by four to five inches long, tender and sweet as can be; they do not fruit as much as the previous two; However, their beauty and goodness compensate for this defect, in addition to the fact that they are a fortnight earlier than their predecessors; it should also be taken into account that they are held in a mediocre elevation, which costs neither so many oars, nor so much subjection to defend them from the strong winds: All these advantages make it well worth preferring to any other, when you can get the real thing, which is very hard to find; you have to sow it light like the others; the pea is white, large, smooth and round. The fifth species is the dwarf pea without parchment, which forms a small, rounded, low tuft: its pod is very small, and its taste similar to the others; it is not very heavy, which makes it less useful than curious. These 5 species are cultivated a little differently than the other peas: they are sown in March and April, until the end of May; later on, they are prone to mildew: they are crimped like the others when they are well up, and they are often wet; this is what tenderizes them: They necessarily need to be branched, and when you want to get the most beautiful and earliest fruit, you have to pinch them at the third or fourth flower: three rows in a four-foot plank are enough; the thicker they get, the less they fruit: for the same reason, don't put two planks in a row. This species of pea is extremely sensitive to thunder and lightning, which can ruin it if they occur at flowering time; this is the main reason why it should be sown every two weeks, from the beginning of March to the end of May, because if the first seeds perish, the others, which are more or less advanced, make up for those ruined by the storm. They are eaten fat and lean, with the fillets removed and cooked in boiling water to make them more tender. They are also eaten as green beans, when they are of a reasonable size, and are preferred by connoisseurs over all others: they are tender, soft and have a distinguished taste; they are also very good dry, and cook perfectly; but it is the white kind that should be taken. We reserve as much as we need for seed, and leave them for a long time to dry, because their husk is fat and thick, and only dries out with time and good weather; because if it rains heavily when they are at this point, this husk, which retains water like a sponge, is prone to rot and cause the grain to rot; That's why it's always a good idea to have some from one year to the next, and to tear out any plants that degenerate, which I've found to be particularly the case with the white species. When they come to good, we pull them out, and if we have the time, we seed them right away: I say we shell them, because when they are beaten, half of them remain in the pods, which cannot be detached, the grain being stuck to the pod; this has led me to take the decision in my particular case to tear off the pods as they dry, and I don't even wait until they are completely dry, as I am beset by birds that devour them; they finish drying in the sun, where I expose them for as long as they need. If you can close them safely without shelling them, so that rats can't damage them, they keep much longer. I have experimented this year with drying them green, picked tender, in the same way as beans, the recipe for which can be found in the chapter on this vegetable, and I have found them to be better; it will be very easy to put them to the test. In addition to the fourteen species of peas I have just mentioned, there are a few more of a fancy nature, of which I shall say a word, and a few others about which I feel I must disabuse those who are prejudiced in their favor on the basis of their former reputation; such is, for example, the Charantonneau, of which we were eager before we knew those who now occupy the scene with greater justification: It's a small, hard pea, without sugar or finesse, and much less hasty than the first three species I've named; such is also the small dwarf pea with bouquet, which is not as abandoned as the Charantonneau, but which is hardly better: all its merit is that it takes up little space, and can be stored anywhere; a poor merit, when it is not accompanied by any other quality. The large bouquet pea is of the same order; its singularity is all that recommends it: it rises to six feet, and carries at its summit a group of flowers and pods in a row gathered one on top of the other, which give a very mediocre grain in taste, and it is only by dint of small poles that it can be held upright, a pain it certainly does not deserve. I include in the same class the crown pea, the horn pea, the gray pea, the grosset and others. There is, however, one in the provinces near the Rhône, called Quarantain, which is said to be good and early; I don't know it; it's up to those who grow it to consider whether it wouldn't be even more advantageous for them to sacrifice it to Michaux; for one must always strive for the best. Among the fancy peas (with respect to us), I give first place to the chickpea, which is highly esteemed in Languedoc, as well as in Italy; it is pointed on one side and sunken on the other, resembling the head of a ram: It has a peculiar taste, but one that pleases its enthusiasts; it is very hard and difficult to digest: for this reason, no one pays any attention to it in Paris, where it comes just as well and just as good as in warmer countries; I have tried it several times. I will say, however, for the satisfaction of those who like its taste, but fear its defects even more, that soaking it for twenty-four hours in lukewarm water, with a small bag of ash, before cooking it, makes it lose much of its hardness: you can also, as is the practice in Languedoc, put it in a bowl, pour in a spoonful of olive oil, and rub the grains together; then put it in lukewarm water, with a little salt; leave it there from evening to the next day; then cook it in half-boiling water; this little preparation tenderizes it a lot: We must be careful not to use well water, which is too raw and hardens it; any other water is good. There are two types, one white and large, with a white flower; the other reddish and smaller, with a red flower: the latter is better for pharmacy, being more aperitive; the other is better for life, and is almost exclusively consumed, both in Italy and in our southern provinces: there is a third species which is black; but I don't know its particular qualities. The plant that produces the first two species is quite different from common peas: it only grows to about eighteen inches, and throws out a number of branches that spread out around the base: its leaves are water-green, arranged in twos like those of sain-foin, but shorter, serrated at the edges, and ending in an odd number: From the axils of each leaf, or at least from the largest part, a small flower emerges, with a hairy calyx, divided into six pointed parts, whose pistil turns into a bladder-like, swollen fruit, about an inch long, ending in a small thread that contains one or two seeds. Some people eat them not only when they are dry, but also when they are green, and not only cooked, but raw; however, they are difficult to digest, and in the latter case are only suitable for country people, whose stomachs are accustomed to digesting everything: They are usually only eaten dry in oil, so they are left to dry well and then plucked, beaten and winnowed without further precaution; they keep well for sowing for two or three years, but are best eaten in the first year. In hot countries, it is sometimes used in place of coffee; its taste is somewhat similar, but it is more bitter, and therefore requires more sugar: when it is mixed in equal portions, it is difficult to notice, and in the public cafés of Italy, it is very common to use it. It is sown in March or April in clumps like beans, eighteen inches apart, and five or six seeds are placed together; it is crimped exactly when young, and requires no other method: in Italy it is sown with a plough, but the climate is different. The decoction of this pea is good for suppressing urine, stone and nephritic colic; it is also used for jaundice, to kill worms, to bring milk to nursing mothers, restore menstruation and facilitate childbirth: its flour, used as a poultice, is also suitable for resolving tumors of the udder and testicles; it also cleanses gravel and fickle fire. The pea of every month has a particular merit, which is to flower for four to five months in a row, and to always give fruit successively after the flowers; this is what gives it entry to some gardens: but if you value it rightly, the fruit is worth nothing. The Lupin pea, which is where I end, is generally only cultivated for medicinal purposes, and is only successful in southern countries; however, the Greeks used to eat it in ancient times, as Galen and Pliny assure us with remarkable circumstances, adding that this grain has the virtue of appeasing both hunger and thirst; but they took the precaution of macerating it in hot water, to remove its bitterness and make it pleasant to the taste: This preparation also removes a harmful quality that some other authors have attributed to it, assuming it to be true; but the proof is not well established: even today, in some parts of Italy, the lower classes still eat it, cooked simply in water with salt or vinegar. The root is single, woody, with several fibers; the stem is 12 to 18 inches high, moderately thick, straight, cylindrical, a little hairy, hollow and full of marrow; the leaves are alternate, borne on long tails, composed.s most often of seven oblong, narrow, dark-green segmens, plain at the edges, hairy underneath, and topped with a silvery-white down, fan-shaped by their arrangement; the edges of these segmens approach and narrow at sunset, and incline towards the tail; the flowers are arranged in a spike at the top of the stems; They are leguminous, white, borne on short pedicels; a pistil emerges from the calyx, which turns into a thick, broad, flattened pod, about three inches long, straight, pulpy, yellowish, a little hairy on the outside, containing four or five fairly large, flattened seeds, reddish on the outside and yellow on the inside, and very bitter. The second species, of which I have never been able to see the plant, although I have sown it several times, is, as I have been told, identical in all respects to the previous one, except for the color of the flower and the seed. The flower is a ruddy blue, and the seed a dark purple. This species is almost unknown.
