The first is very commonly used in medicine; its flour, which is one of the four resolving flours, is used in all emollient poultices; its decoction is aperitive and specific for liver diseases and visceral obstructions; it stimulates months and urine, and kills worms: Its flour produces the same effect, mixed with honey and vinegar; the same decoction is detersive, curing scabies, scabs and ulcers; it also cleanses and decleanses the skin perfectly: boiled with vinegar and applied as a poultice to tumors and scabies, it gradually dissipates them, especially in their birth: the grain is also marvelous for fattening oxen; it is also used in some countries to make powder to powder. Lupin is sown in March in warm countries, where it is easy to raise, and its cultivation is no different from that of other grains; but here, where the climate is too cold, it can only be raised by first sowing it on a bed, from where it is replanted in the warmest possible location: some grow it in pots. CHAPTER LXV. Description of LONG PEPPER; its different species, properties, cultivation, etc. Three species are cultivated, differing only in the shape of the fruit they produce: one is elongated, slightly curved, and cone-shaped; the other is short and pointed at the tip, and the third is round: their similarities are perfect in all other respects, so I need only describe one of the three. It grows a branching, green, gnarled stem, formed into a shrub that rises to fifteen or eighteen inches; its leaves are oblong, pointed and smooth, of a beautiful glossy green; The flowers emerge in small bunches from the leaf axils, in the form of white stars, borne on a small calyx which changes into a fruit of different shape, depending on the species, which is green at first, and becomes a bright, shiny red, approaching coral as it ripens; it encloses the seed, which is round and flat, of medium size and russet in color. It is used very little in France for living, and is cultivated more for garden decoration and eye candy than for utility; however, it is used for many purposes: it is candied with sugar when green, and eaten at a dose of half an ounce, to fortify the stomach, aid digestion and dissipate the winds: yeast growers also use it to give more strength to vinegar; it is mixed with candied gherkins. Some people use it in their food instead of ordinary pepper, and find it tastier. But in foreign countries, in India, Spain, Italy and even Flanders, it is widely consumed; some eat it preserved in salt and vinegar; others, who have become accustomed to it from youth, eat it raw when it is green: I admit that this may seem surprising to those who are familiar with its pungent taste; but the fact is no less certain, and I have witnessed it a thousand times. This plant grows very easily in this climate, as well as in hot countries, and its cultivation is very simple; it is sown in beds in March, and replanted in May, either in beds a foot apart, or in pots; it requires good soil, a little moist, and a slightly shady situation; too much sun is detrimental to it: it is moistened as needed, and the fruit is cut as desired, green or dry: the seed keeps well for several years. different species, properties, cultivation, etc. THIS plant, although of little use to the table, nevertheless has good enough qualities to be considered one of the best in the garden. Its leaf is small, snowshoe-shaped, thick and fleshy, full of water, odorless, dark green or yellowish, depending on the species, and covered underneath with a snowy white down; its stem is of a very similar consistency, and is used in the same way as the leaf. It grows straight and fairly high, when forced to rise by others; but when placed at ease, it does not rise much, and its branches, which form in fairly large numbers, crawl on the ground. The leaves are arranged two by two, opposite each other, and from their axils come the second branches, which divide into several small twigs, at the end of which form a group of small yellowish flowers, followed by small pointed shells containing the seed. Q2 PURSLANE. When the plant is young and tender, it is used in salads; when stronger, it is used in soups, mixed with other herbs; it is also prepared with cream, and is a familiar dish in many countries, almost unheard of in Paris; but for this to happen, the chops must be the size of two feathers, plain and straight, with no lateral branches: at the same size, they are pickled in vinegar in the same way as gherkins, alone or mixed with pickles, and used in winter salads. Its medicinal properties are much more remarkable: distilled water or the juice of its leaves is used in hot fevers, at a dose of three or four ounces, to calm the impetuosity of the blood and spirits: the water in particular, taken at the same dose, is one of the most effective remedies for haemorrhage and blood loss in women; it is also good for killing worms in children, and in this latter respect the juice of distilled water has the same effect. The plant is also good for scurvy and spitting blood; its chewed leaf cures mouth ulcers and quenches thirst, as well as the pain of irritated teeth: it is also used in many other remedies, but as they are less tried and tested, I will pass over them in silence. Its excessive use in food would, however, be dangerous for those with weak stomachs, who need it more to heal than to refresh: this is what we must take care of. There are three species of purslane, Green, Golden and Wild, which grows in confusion in most gardens, and is a great nuisance to plants of all kinds, without being able to be destroyed: if the other two were lacking, it would be just as useful as a remedy; but for life, it is too dry and hard. There is a fourth, little-known species, formed into a shrub with a hard stem, and which is very delicate to raise and preserve; but as it is only useful for the pleasure of the curious, I will say no more about it. Its root is small and fibrous; it grows several weak, creeping stems, like wild purslane, which do not extend very far; its leaves are narrow and long, thick and fleshy, more or less like percepia, and arranged in a star shape around its leaves, eight or ten in number: the stems are round, smooth and reddish on the sunny side; the tops end in a group of small, petalless flowers, like those of wild purslane, which turn into small, green, elongated shells, wrapped 23 in a thick, white stuffing; they enclose the seed, which is black and even smaller than that of ordinary purslane: its taste is bitter; it would not be suitable, therefore, for the use of life; but it could have other properties that I do not know: it is sown on the bed like the other species, and it comes easily enough; but it must be enclosed during the winter. The first two can be propagated from seed, but are cultivated differently: the golden variety is delicate, and can only tolerate the open air when the heat begins; the green variety, on the contrary, is hard: it is therefore sown on a bed throughout the winter, and is hardly ever sown in the open air. The first seed is sown under a bell in January; however, as the cold can be very harmful, it is common to wait until the hard frosts have passed, i.e., around mid-February: it is buried with small lettuces; the same amount of soil is enough for them: the seed is sown very thickly, without burying it; if you wish, it is enough to beat it a little with the hand, and it rises very well and very quickly. When the plant has risen, care must be taken to keep it well covered, as the slightest hint of freshness will melt it; it does however need to see the sun, and whenever it appears, you must not fail to uncover the bells a little. We cut it as soon as it has two slightly formed leaves, and at this point it is more red than green; but it is such a pleasure, that it is on all new lettuces; we always sow a little from fortnight to fortnight, so as to have a succession until we can risk the other species, which, depending on the year, is sown in the middle or at the end of April, and under cloche as well, because in the open ground you have to wait until mid-May. There are no other precautions to be taken for these first seeds on a bed; but for those sown in the open ground in May, they should be placed along a well-exposed wall, in the loosest soil available; and if they are heavy by nature, they should be loosened with plenty of compost, or sand if there is none: after ploughing, harrow it well with a pitchfork, rake it over, then sow; and as the seed is extremely small, and always escapes more than you want, mix it with sawdust or ash, to control it better. After sowing, lightly beat the soil with the back of a wooden spade, and then spread an inch of loam over it as evenly as you can; lightly wet the spot the next day, and continue every day, until it has risen, unless it rains: if there is no loam, harrow the Q 4 POURPIER. If you don't have potting soil, you have to harrow the soil with a fork after sowing, and then beat it with a spade; but unless you keep it very wet at all times, it can hardly break through the crust; and if you don't have watering cans that sprinkle water in a fine shower, the sprinklings beat the soil so hard that the seed suffocates rather than emerges. In the months of June and July, when new seeds are sown, the coast is no longer so necessary; it can be sown in beds wherever you like, as it does very well everywhere, subject to the same precautions explained above. One important thing to remember is to wet it every day, and only at midday, when it is starting to take shape: watering in the evening or morning takes away its golden color and turns it green. Only a few should be sown at a time, as it is only good in salads when it is young and tender: of course, it must be weeded if necessary. To collect the seed, we replant it from the first seeds we've sown a foot apart: we water it as soon as we've planted it, and continue for two days at a time until the seed has formed; its maturity is revealed by the first hulls, which begin to peel off, revealing the seed; we must then carefully pull out the plants, and have a sheet ready to receive them: You leave them on for a few days to allow the seed to ripen, stirring them from time to time, and then rubbing them with your hands to loosen and enclose them. It's black, round and as fine as grains of sand; it keeps well for eight to ten years. Others put it in a barrel, well pressed with all its stalks, and leave it there until all its stalks are consumed with rot; the seed does not suffer, and is gathered at the bottom of the barrel, from where it is removed; and after washing it, it is dried and locked up. The reason for this practice is that the seed feeds better, and there is none left in the capsules, as it is air-dried: each person will follow whichever of these two methods is more convenient for him or her, because in either case, the seed lifts equally. CHAPTER LXVII. Description of RAIPONCE; its different Species, Properties, Calture, etc. Pumice grows naturally in meadows and hedges. There are four 25 species, all of which have their Latin name, but three of which are known only in botany, and are not used in food: I shall pass over them in silence, and speak only of the one with which we are familiar. Its root is fleshy, long and white, both inside and out, the size of a small turnip, with a mild flavor and no odor; it forms a group of small, oblong, tender-green leaves, similar to lamb's lettuce and arranged in the same way; its stem is branchy and slender, rising from twelve to eighteen inches; its branches are terminated by blue, bell-shaped flowers, in a single piece, cut into a five-rayed star; the calyx, from the middle of which rises a forked thread, becomes a fruit divided into three seed-filled chambers: this seed is one of the finest that can be produced, with a rounded, slightly elongated shape and a yellowish color. In spring, the root and leaf are eaten together, alone or mixed with lamb's lettuce: it is healthy, and many people are eager to eat it; its taste is a little like hazelnut. It is of little use in medicine: however, it is agreed that it is refreshing, and that its decoction is useful in the onset of throat inflammations. This plant comes without any cultivation, as I have said; nevertheless, it is much better nourished and more tender when grown in gardens; but it must be placed in fresh, soft, slightly shaded soil: it is sown in June; and as its seed is extraordinarily small, it must be mixed with sawdust and sown very lightly: one full dice is enough for a large bed; it requires more earth than any other plant, and a little burial. The best way to sow it is to pass the fine rake over the soil, after it has been well tilled, and to spread the seed over it as evenly as possible, without stirring it with a fork; it is then covered with no more than half an inch of potting soil, which is spread lightly with the same rake; it should be moistened often until it is well established, and then weeded: during droughts, it should also be watered regularly. Good raiponces come to us from Meaux en Brie, where the soil is more favorable to this plant than that around Paris; and it's also from there that good seed comes to us. There is no need to take any precautions to preserve it during the winter; it is left in the ground until needed, and we begin to enjoy it from the beginning of Lent until May, after which time it grows its stem, and is no longer good: the seed ripens in the month of July, and what is destined for seed should be left in the ground; for it should be noted that this little root recovers with difficulty when replanted, unless it is pulled out and replanted immediately, without having been washed. CHAPTER LXVIII. Description of RAVE and RADIS; their different species, properties, cultivation, etc. AS turnip and radish are two plants that are quite similar in both taste and form, I will treat them together in this article. Radish, more properly known as Raifort in most provinces, is the root or turnip of a plant that bears the same name; this turnip is more or less long and large, depending on the species, plain, straight, and mostly red on the outside; its flesh is white, firm, brittle, watery and pungent to the taste, with no odor; its leaf is elongated, deeply cut, coarse green, rough and hairy : from the center of its leaves, which are more or less numerous, depending on the species, emerges a stem about the size of a finger, hollow, fluted, reddish and branched, rising to about three feet; the ends of its branches are covered throughout with flowers placed alternately; Its flowers are arranged with four white or reddish petals, supported by an elongated calyx, in the middle of which rises a pistil that changes into a short, swollen, round and pointed silique, which contains five or six round seeds, the size of a grain of hare's lead, which are green at first, and become grooved as they ripen. There are three distinct species of turnip, not to mention several degenerate ones: the smallest is called the hâtive, or four-leaf turnip; the second is called the commune, and the other the grosse. They all resemble each other in foliage, stem, flower and seed, but differ in turnip shape, color and taste, and each has its own season. The early variety is very small, a beautiful red, very tender and sweet, with few leaves. The common variety is twice as big, also red, but a little less tender; it grows a lot of leaves before turning into turnips. The grosse is partly white and partly red, about the size of a thumb, and has a more pungent taste than the preceding varieties. The hâtive is the most esteemed, though the least profitable, because it comes first, and we manage to make it come against all the insults of the bad season. The common variety has the advantage of being large, and of succeeding perfectly in the open ground, either in spring or autumn; it is true, however, that there are areas where it cannot be grown in spring, unless the soil is extremely earthy. The large one has neither the delicacy nor the sweetness of the other two; but in addition to being more profitable, it grows quite well in the warmer months, an advantage that the others do not have. This plant, which can only be eaten raw with salt, has both good and bad qualities, which makes it loved by some and hated by others. Its good qualities are to be pleasant to the taste, to awaken the appetite, and to provide abundantly almost all year round, provided that all due care is taken. Its bad qualities are to be difficult to digest, and contrary to weak stomachs: it also has the defect of giving reports during all the day when one ate some; which is unbearable with many people. However, it is a vegetable which, generally speaking, everyone is eager to eat, especially in spring, and which is consumed immensely, both in Paris and in the provinces; it is also one of the main crops of our market gardeners during winter and spring. It has a number of medicinal virtues, which I must mention: when cooked, it is used for the same purposes as turnips; it is particularly good for kidney and bladder diseases caused by mucus or gravel: its juice is extracted, and three or four ounces are taken in the morning with half an ounce of honey, which is continued for three or four days. Its distilled water is also used up to four ounces in aperitif potions, but should not be given to those suffering from stone, as it carries too much urine salt into the bladder. The root, crushed and applied to the soles of the feet, is good for malignant fevers and dropsy. Some claim that frequent use of this plant is harmful to the eyes; those who perceive this bad effect should refrain from using it. I'll now move on to its cultivation (by which I mean early cultivation). To get it to the degree of perfection required, i.e., to be tender, soft and brittle, even, straight and very red, there is only the layer that can make it so, and this is also where our market gardeners turn all their attention: They prepare their first beds at All Saints' Day, for those which are to start the year in the following January; they give these first beds only two feet in height, and fill them with eight to nine inches of compost; they let them lose almost all their heat before sowing, so that it is only lukewarm; for the turnip comes covered with little filaments when it is hotter than I say, and this is a great defect. For the distribution of the seed, here is their method: some, after having marked the plaoe of their bells on the soil, make holes with their finger all over the place, two inches apart in all directions, and throw three seeds into each hole, where they run a little soil with the doïgt to simply cover them; if more escape, they pull them out after they have risen, and shoe at the same time those that must remain : others, to have it done better, broadcast it, stir up the soil to bury it; and when it's up, they pull out what's left over. The first way is the best; the turnip comes longer and straighter. For the rest, they leave the layers in the air until all the seeds have risen; then they put the bells on only during the nights and bad days, always leaving the plant uncovered as much as the weather allows. If there are frosts, they cover the bells with litter: when you have straw mats, safety is even greater; they are particularly necessary in times of rain, which makes them rust, of course they are placed so that the water runs off into the paths. These first-seeded turnips are usually good in January, provided they have been well cared for and properly warmed, and that the season has not been excessively harsh and contrary. In December, they sow them for the second time, to succeed the first ones; and these are the most difficult to raise all year round, as they only have bad days to go: however, by dint of care they manage to keep them; but they require a stronger layer, and much more covering and warming than the first ones: they also need clean weather, extreme rigor or too much mildness being equally contrary to them. We noted (in 1746) that January and the beginning of February had passed without any cold, so the turnip crop ran out of leaves and did not produce any turnips; most market gardeners were obliged to turn over their beds without having made any profit; but, as these cases do not happen often, this second sowing is always done in December, and the turnip crop is usually good in February or at the beginning of March. What I have just said forms an apparent contrast with what happened in this last winter (1748), when we quite commonly saw raves in the months of January and February, which were as mild as the most beautiful spring, except for two days of frost; But if we had taken a good look at the weather, we would have observed that the sun came out much more frequently in the latter period than in the former, which I mentioned earlier, and there is no doubt that it was the influence of this star that produced this effect, which was the opposite of the other. You can't be too careful, therefore, to take advantage of the slightest ray of sunlight to give this plant the benefit it deserves, and if you can't, you must at least give it as much air as you can; for if it remains suffocated under the covers, it will either wither away without doing any navét, or it will perish, which amounts to the same thing. The third sowing is done in January, and must be covered with bells as soon as it is put in the ground, to conserve heat; the layers must be even stronger than in the previous month, and cared for in the same way. Sow for the fourth time at the beginning of February, and then dispense with the bells, as the season becomes more favorable: we also reduce the volume of the bed, and sow it in full; we space the seed by two or three inches, as I explained above; and, if we want to give an air of cleanliness to the bed, after it is well erected, we stretch a string from end to end that we rub with lime, and this lime, applied to the soil in the distances we judge appropriate, forms straight lines to regulate the holes we make. The holes are made with the finger, as I said, or with a small planter of the same size, cut diametrically, so that the three grains thrown in, as they fall to the bottom, can move apart: we then cover the bed with straw mats, or large litter, until the seed begins to rise; then we remove the cover, and build a small trellis on both edges of the bed, six inches inside and four inches high, on which we place straw mats during the nights and days of bad weather: we load them again with dry litter, if the cold is severe, filling the sides with a good thickness of litter. When, in spite of all these precautions, frost has penetrated and the cowslip is attacked, you must be careful not to uncover it in the sun, which would destroy it: you must let it thaw little by little under the cover, simply removing the bedding that forms the sides, so that the air can pass through: but if the weather is mild without the sun shining through, you must put everything in the air. The rabe from this seed is usually good at the end of March, or the beginning of April, and is the best to eat; for those that have preceded, having suffered much, do not have nearly the same tenderness, nor the same taste; and those that follow begin to become too strong. However, we continue to sow them in March, and they are good in May; but after this month, we no longer sow them on the bed: we mix them with other seeds, when the soil is suitable, and we sow them in this way until the beginning of May, to eat them in June; and we use the common species, which is more beautiful and more profitable. However, those who like them so much that they can't be bored with them can continue to sow them, but as they would be too prickly in full sun, they should be placed in the shade, along a north-facing wall, where a foot of compost will have been added to replace the same amount of soil that has been removed, as the soil makes them hard and cordate in this season. If you don't have a suitably arranged wall, you can make a shelter with straw mats five to six feet high, and sow them behind, watering them exactly every day, and taking care to fill the place with compost. Until September, only people who are determined not to do without them grow these first two species of turnip; but you can sow a little of the larger variety, which doesn't harden so much, provided it is watered daily. Once September arrives, we start sowing them again in the open ground, where they are better than on the bed; and it's always the common species that is to be preferred: we sow them very lightly, they are redder and better; we then mix them into the spinach and lamb's lettuce seeds, into chicory, etc.; wherever we put them, they go well. We continue in October, but these require more attention, as the frosts that start in this month, coming upon them, cause them to perish. They should be sown in a bed under some shelter exposed to the sun, and covered with straw mats or pleyons during bad weather, and especially at night: it is even safer to sow them on old layers, or on ados of potting soil, where they are less likely to rust than in the ground, and are always sweeter to eat; they are also not so exposed to being eaten by insects. If they are well cared for, they provide a succession of crops until Christmas; but they lose much of their taste in the latter season, and if the slightest frost surprises them, they have none at all. The year is over there, and we start again in November, as we said before. To collect the seed, in March or April we replant a sheet, more or less, of the first ones that have been raised on the bed, and we choose the reddest and plainest; we space them a good foot apart, and we water them right away, which must be continued until they are well established: many people leave them free to spare themselves any care; but very often the winds and rainstorms break them and lay them on the ground, where the seed rots for the most part: the more this seed is aired and struck by the sun, the better it is. Birds wage a cruel war on it as it approaches maturity: you have to defend yourself as best you can. When, in August, most of the siliques are yellow, we pull out the stalks and leave them exposed to the sun for a few more days, after which we bind them in bundles and tie them to the floor in some dry place where mice cannot get at them; for this seed needs to remain in its siliques as long as possible: it feeds there and keeps much better. As a rule, I experimented with threshing and winnowing as soon as I had harvested it, and sowing it the following spring on the same bed where I had sown others that I had just threshed: the production was quite different: the new threshing made the turnip bigger, more even and more colorful. This seed keeps well for ten years or more, if left in its siliques. The radish I have yet to discuss is similar in many respects to the turnip, but differs in several others: its leaf is broader, more inverted on the ground, and less cut; its turnip is quite different, and its flesh firmer. not to mention a few others degenerated from these: these eight species are the small white and round radish of every month; the white and long; the gray and long; the long summer black; of the large species, the large winter black; the winter white; of Italy. The small radish of every month is white and round, ending in a slender tail, the size of a small walnut: it is very early, and is sown on the bed, as is the early turnip: it is raised in the same way; so what I have said for the one will serve for the other. It is tender and delicate, sweet and watery; many people prefer it to turnip, which has a more decided taste: it can also be sown on the ground at the end of February, but it doesn't come off as delicate there, unless the soil is top-dressed and always kept very cool. The small, long white radish is quite similar in taste to the previous one, but is less tender and a little more pungent; it is also less early: its size is like that of a finger, and its skin and flesh are a beautiful white: it can be sown on the ground and on the bed, and has the advantage of coming in well all year round, provided it is kept moist in the warmer months. The small gray radish is the same size as the previous one, but has less water, and therefore a stronger taste: its skin is gray, its flesh white, and its shape not very long: it is sown on the ground, like the previous one, throughout the summer, with the same care. The small black summer radish is still of the same size, but its shape is more elongated and its flesh harder and drier: it has a hazelnut taste which outweighs all the others, and which gives it preference over them when it is known: the difficulty is to find the real seed, which is very rare; its skin is completely black. The large radish is very long, about the size of a small egg; its skin is very white, as is its flesh: it is tender and full of water, quite sweet, but not very spicy in taste, and prone to glassiness: it also ropes if not thoroughly wetted, and does not succeed in summer; but in autumn it is very good, and the most profitable for its size: it requires very light soil. The large black radish, otherwise known as the Strasbourg radish, is quite different from the others; its turnip is black, as big as the wrist, and elongated in proportion: it keeps well throughout the winter, if sown in June, and watered frequently: if sown earlier, it rises: its flesh is hard and dry, and its taste extraordinarily pungent; moreover, it has Tome II. R the defect of being very indigestible; however, it has its supporters, and it pleases those who are unable to grow the other species of turnips and radishes during the winter, and find this one in their greenhouse throughout that season; this is the most real merit I know of it: it must be pulled up before the frosts, and buried in sand, or placed close by in a trench, and covered during the frosts. The large white radish differs from the previous one only in its dirty-white outer color and sweeter taste: in all other respects, the resemblance is perfect. The Italian radish, which is the Ravaneli of the Italians, so tasted and so cherished in their country, has truly superior qualities: it does not succeed as perfectly here; however, when you have Italian seed, it comes good enough to erase all the others: its length is commonly from six to eight inches, its size a good inch, and its external color a brilliant white, with some red shading on the side of the head; some are also quite white: its flesh is transparent, extremely soft, tender and brittle, with a lot of water: it requires very loose, well-soiled soil, and lots of watering: we sow it from spring to autumn, with a lot of water.

be sure to place it in the shade in the warmer months; it's a species everyone should be eager to grow. All these species multiply from seed like turnip, and are harvested in the same way: they are redder and smaller; that's the only difference: they can be kept for the same length of time, with the same precautions, which I won't repeat. For the two large winter radishes, the seed is better on the over-wintered fruit, which is replanted in March, than on the seed that could be sown in spring: it feeds better and ripens more surely. With regard to the others, we replant the fruits that come first, as I explained for turnip. It is used medicinally for the same purposes as sage. CHAPTER LXIX. Description of ROSEMARY; its different species, properties, cultivation, etc. Rosemary is a perennial shrub, fragrant and aromatic, which grows naturally in warm countries and is also cultivated in gardens. There are four species, but I know of only two, and whatever research I've done, I've been unable to find the other two, which M. Tournefort calls rosmarinus spontaneus latiore folio, and rosmarinus almeriensis flore majore purpurascente. The two cultivated species are common and variegated rosemary. The common rosemary grows to about three feet, and produces a large number of branches; its root is woody and covered with many sibers; its leaf is a large, slightly glossy green, white underneath, thick, fluted in the middle, long and very narrow, similar to that of lavender, with a strong, pleasant odor and a bitter, pungent taste: its stem is woody, very branchy, and its young branches are covered with a small white down: the flowers are born in small bunches from the leaf axils. The flower is bluish, shaped like a pipe, cut into two lips at the top, the upper of which is raised, indented and usually inverted: the lower lip is cut into three parts, the middle of which is hollowed out; the stamens are hooked; the calyx is a horn toothed with two or three points, at the bottom of which is a pistil that fits into the hole at the bottom of the flower: this pistil is composed of 4 embryos, which then become as many almost round, brown seeds, enclosed in the capsule that served as the flower's calyx. The second species of Rosemary differs from the first in that the stem is taller and fuller, and the leaf is smaller and accidentally yellowish; it also flowers earlier, and this year (1749) was in full bloom as early as January. In all other respects, it bears the same resemblance to the first, and has the same properties: both retain their leaves throughout the winter. This plant is of no use for food, but it has very great properties in medicine, especially in hot countries, where the heat of the sun makes it more spirituous: it is hot, desiccating, incisive, astringent, and essentially cephalic: it is mainly used for apoplexy, epilepsy, vertigo, paralysis and other head ailments: it clears the eyes, strengthens the memory, and revives the heart; it also corrects the stench of breath, and lifts obstructions of the liver and spleen; it is also good for jaundice and for white flowers, taken as an infusion. Simply macerate the leaves and flowers overnight in common water, R, several glasses of which are taken during the day: leaves boiled in wine fortify nerves and joints. Aromatic wine, so commonly used by surgeons as a fomentation to dissipate swelling in wounds, is made from this leaf, mixed with that of Thim and Sage: the leaves, infused like tea over a period of time, are useful in treating écrouelles. It is also claimed that vindind in which the leaves and flowers have been boiled, mixed with a little honey, and taken as a drink before going to bed, is an excellent remedy for asthmatics. Four or five drops of essence of this plant, taken in a suitable liqueur, will often cure third-grade fevers. A leading author also asserts that eau de vie distilled from wine in which rosemary leaves and flowers have been macerated cures scabies, cancers and fistulas that resist other remedies. The well-known eau de la reine de Hongrie is obtained by distilling the flowers of this plant, then digesting them in eau-de-vie, and if the young leaves are added, it is even stronger: it is used daily for fainting, dizziness and vertigo, as well as for hysterical and hypochondriacal vapors: two or three large spoonfuls are taken internally in a glass of water, and rubbed externally on the temples, nose and other nervous and muscular parts, weakened or afflicted by the pains of rheumatism: it is also used successfully for contusions, wounds and cold humours, toothache and even gangrene. Although this plant requires a warm climate, it is easy to grow everywhere, with the difference that in cold countries it must be locked up during the winter, or at least stuffed; but in this one, unless the winters are very harsh, these precautions are unnecessary; it withstands the rigors of bad weather, and needs no special care once rooted. It can be propagated from seed, but usually from cuttings or from rooted plants: in the first case, it must be bedded, or at least moistened often when placed in the ground, and kept covered for some time in the heat of the sun: usually, several cuttings are placed in a pot, kept in the shade until they begin to grow, and then separated for a while: this plant will remain in the same place for a very long time. R 4 AC 2 ===== CHAPTER LXX. Description of the ROQUETTE; its different species, properties, 56 cultivation, etc. There are two species of arugula, the cultivated and the wild, both perennials. The first is grown in gardens: its root is white and slender; its stem rises to eighteen inches or two feet, a little hairy; its leaves are white, long, narrow, deeply cut on both sides, tender, smooth and acrid; Its flowers are born at the top of four-petaled cross-shaped stems, pale yellow with blackish stripes, enclosed in a hairy calyx, from which emerges a pistil that changes into a long silique, borne on a short pedicel, and divided by a partition into two chambers that are filled with yellow, small, round and slightly elongated seeds. The smell and taste of this plant are equally strong; however, it has its enthusiasts, particularly in Italy, where no one expects to eat a good salad without it. This is the only use to which it is put for life, and from which we derive many health benefits; for it stimulates the appetite, aids digestion, and dissolves slimy matter in the stomach. It is good in disgust and indigestion: eaten alone, with oil and vinegar, it excites the urine and slightly irritates the parts destined for the secretion and evacuation of the seed; this is why it is used in compositions intended to awaken love; but as it is very hot by nature, it is not suitable for hot and bilious temperaments, nor for those with a burning stomach. Its seed is used in winter in place of the leaves, and produces the same effects; it ranks first among the anti-scorbutic seeds, and is included in all the medicines suitable for scurvy; it is prescribed in a dose of one large, crushed and infused in a glass of distilled cochlearia water; and in its place, the leaf is also used, which is eaten raw, or the juice extracted from it, which is drunk mixed with wine: the leaf is also boiled in the broths and apozems prescribed for this disease, taking care to boil them only lightly, as they lose their flavor and virtue if left too long in the fire. Its seed is also an excellentR 3 slow preservative against apoplexy, paralysis and soporific affections; chew fifteen to twenty seeds every morning on an empty stomach, alone or mixed with cumin seeds; its use is very favorable, especially for the elderly; and various authors claim to have seen surprising effects. It also has the virtue of making people salivate a lot, and is therefore beneficial to those suffering from pituitary problems. A decoction of its leaves, taken with sugar, is also good for curing children's coughs. Wild arugula has a slightly different shape to the preceding plant; its root is white, thick and long; its stem is very branchy, hollow, fluted and hairy; its leaves are cut like those of the dandelion, dark green, smooth, with a burning taste; its flowers are yellow, arranged like those of the preceding plant; and the flowers are followed by long, angular siliques, filled with seeds similar to those of wild mustard. The whole plant gives off a strong odor, which pleases some and displeases others; its flavor is also pungent, burning and bitter at the end. This plant grows naturally in uncultivated and arid places, and particularly in old walls: it has all the same qualities as the previous one, for medicine, it even surpasses it; but as food, it irritates the taste more because of its pungency; however, country people also eat it in salads, especially in hot countries. It is also eaten finely chopped on buttered toast, like cran. The first species is sown in gardens in March, and requires no special care: any soil is suitable. The seed is harvested in July and August, and keeps well for two years. "========= 1 CHAPTER LXXI. Description of RUE; its different species, properties, cullure, etc. Rue is a species of aromatic plant, which is perennial; there are three species, domestic, wild and small-leaved. The domestic rue has a woody, yellow root with several fibers; it grows like a shrub, and when well cultivated, can reach five to six feet; its stems are as thick as a finger, woody, twiggy, and covered with a whitish bark: its leaves are divided into several parts, small, oblong, fleshy, a little fatty, smooth, sea-green in color, arranged in pairs, on a rib ending in an odd one; its flowers are born at the tops of the branches, R 6 small, composed of four petals, pale yellow in color; The flowers are followed by fruits, usually composed of four capsules, assembled against a stone, and each capsule contains several seeds, most often in the shape of a small rooster's kidney, which are angular and black in color. The whole plant has a very unpleasant odor, and a bitter, acrid taste. The wild rue differs from the previous one in that its stem is lower, and its leaves are divided into longer, narrower parts, with a darker green color, a stronger odor and a more pungent taste: in all other respects, the same resemblance. The rue à feuille menue (small-leaved rue) has a rather large, white, woody root; its leaves turn over on the ground, and are divided very small, pale green in color, with a very strong odor and a pungent taste; between the leaves, two or three stems divided into twigs sometimes arise, which end in bunches of flowers similar to those of the preceding species, but smaller, pale yellow in color; they are followed by fruits, composed, like the others, of four capsules containing small, black, acrid seeds. These last two species grow naturally in Languedoc and Provence, in stony, uncultivated places. We'll confine ourselves here to the plant's nutritional qualities; but in Italy, its young shoots are eaten with pleasure in salads, and even without any seasoning: there, it doesn't have the pungency and bad odor it has here, and this is undoubtedly due to the climate. We'll confine ourselves here to its health-giving properties, the first of which is the most commonly used: it is hysterical, cephalic, stomachic, vermifuge, carminative, anti-scorbutic, cordial and vulnerary. One or two pinches of its fresh leaves, infused in a glass of white hemp, or a dragme of its powder, when dried, is very suitable for restoring the course of the months and soothing hysterical vapors. For the same illnesses, boil a small handful of its leaves with as much hyssop in wine, and drink a hot glass. Preserving the leaves and flowers dissipates indigestion, and the oil extracted from them kills worms. Soak a cotton ball in it and put it in children's navels; if this oil is not available, its expressed juice has the same effect: a few spoonfuls can even be drunk on an empty stomach, mixed with couch grass water. The decoction of its leaves, taken as an enema or applied as a poultice to the stomach, is sovereign for colic: the olive oil in which the leaves and seeds of this plant have been infused is still a powerful remedy for the same ailment; and the essential oil extracted from it is one of the best known for hysterical passion. The leaf is very suitable for treating écrouelles; children afflicted with this disease are made to eat three or four of them on an empty stomach with bread, over a period of time, and this simple remedy has often cured them. If they are too reluctant, they can be made to swallow three ounces of the juice purified from these leaves. Two spoonfuls of the same juice, mixed with the same amount of good wine, are still very good for protecting against bad air, in contagious diseases: the dose can even be increased to a glass, and one is taken in the morning, and another four hours after dinner. In Italy, a vinegar is made from it, which serves as a preservative in these kinds of cases, and especially during the bad weather that reigns every year on the Remne side, from Midsummer to All Saints' Day. It is made by infusing the leaves of this plant in the strongest vinegar, adding a little burnet, betony, a few cloves of garlic, walnuts, juniper berries and a little camphor; the dose is one spoonful in the morning, and as much in the afternoon. The leaf is also very useful for epileptics, being crumpled and introduced into the nostrils at the time of the attack. A decoction of the leaves is also an excellent gargle for the gums of scorbutic patients, and for those with smallpox: this gargle resolves the seeds around the throat; it can also be used to baste around the eyes. Three or four ounces are swallowed, and the pomace is applied to the bite. There is also experience that these leaves, applied to both pulses, prevent drunkenness. Here's another particular virtue of this plant. By boiling a few shirts in a decoction of its leaves and natural water, no pus is ever produced; a useful remedy for those who, by the nature of their flesh, are prone to this vermin. So many virtues indicate that a garden should never be without a few feet of rue, to find it when needed; it is a plant, moreover, that gives no care, and which, once planted, remains abandoned to itself. It has no fear of bad weather, can be kept for many years in the same place, and thrives in any kind of soil, as long as it is slightly exposed to the sun, which enhances its virtues: it can be propagated from seed, cuttings or rooted plants. The third species is sometimes prone to perish in the open ground, when winters are harsh and long: but as the other two suffice us well, and are foolproof, we can dispense with any expense for this one. "CHAPTER LXXII. Description of SALSIFIS from SPAIN; its properties, cultivation, etc. THE merit of Spanish salsify, otherwise known as Scorsonnera, lies in its root, which is one of the best and healthiest. This root is black on the outside, white on the inside, about the size of a little finger, almost equal at both ends, and very straight when the soil is very loose: but in many soils it becomes forked. The leaf is very narrow, up to a third of its length; it then widens precipitously, and ends in a very elongated point; so that its fullness, which is about an inch, is placed at two-thirds of its length, which is eight to nine inches: its edges are trimmed with small, sawtooth-shaped spikes, unevenly placed, and its color is a meadow green; its stem is round and loose, slightly fluted, soft and branchy, trimmed with a few leaves from interval to interval, and about three feet high: its flowers are placed at the end of its small branches, arranged like a daisy, yellow in color: the petals that compose it emerge from a kind of elongated bud, which grows as the flower passes, and which contains the seed, which, coming to ripen, breaks the calyx, and spreads out in the shape of a tassel, the size of a billiard ball, having a clip at the end of each of its seeds, which are long, small and white. Its root is used as a lean food, from All Saints' Day to Easter; but it's mainly used during Lent: it's seasoned with white sauce, or fried with a paste like artichokes. Good cooks accommodate it in many other ways, and give it different shapes; it is also used to make entremets en gras, which are highly esteemed. It has the same health-giving properties. Its main quality is to be cordial and sudorific: it is used in all the teas made for diseases suspected of malignancy, and mainly for smallpox. It is good against the bite of snakes and other venomous beasts; it is also used to provoke women's months: its leaves also have their virtues, and are used in the composition of several distilled waters for chest pains. This plant therefore deserves its place in a vegetable garden. The seed is sown at the end of April in dry soils, and in mid-May in heavy soils. When it is sown earlier, it is prone to rot; the soil must be warmed up a little before it germinates and sprouts: it usually remains for a fortnight or three weeks before coming out. It can also be sown in August. The soil must be perfectly loose and well-prepared, with no clods or stones; because the better it is worked, the more the root shoots forward and comes up straight and even, which is its main merit. In strong soils, it is prone to becoming forked, glassy, and consequently bad, unless you lighten it with a lot of loam, which you mix well with the soil. A very deep ploughing should be done first, in November, and a second one before sowing; and when it can be placed in newly broken ground, it does even better. If this is not possible, the soil should have been smoked the previous year, and occupied by plants that have not crumbled it too much, such as cabbages: furrowing the soil before sowing should be avoided, as all the roots would be counterfeit. The seed is sown in a border or bed, in rays seven to eight inches apart; after the seed is sown, or even before spreading it in the rays, the soil must be left to tan for a few hours; it is then sown, and lightly treaded; it is then covered, and the rake is passed over to unite the soil: the next day, when the soil is a little tanned, wet it well, and continue for two days at a time, if the weather is dry, until it has risen well: Our market gardeners in Aubervilliers, who sow whole fields of it, don't take so many precautions, but they owe the advantage of being able to do without it to the constitution of their soil. This soil, whose parts are supple and loose, almost never seals or cracks; nor does it dry out as quickly as many others, and the roots of all kinds of plants emerge from it with unusual ease: it may also have particular salts and nitrates, which make this root benefit in one year, as much as it does in two in most other soils; this means that it is lifted at the end of the year, and that everywhere else it is left in place for two years, in order to grow to a reasonable size: we must therefore not take them as models in this respect. Six weeks after this root has sprouted, it should be thinned out, leaving a good two inches between each plant: weed it at the same time and wet it afterwards, to reassure the soil and fill in the gaps: after that, we don't water it any more. This plant, different in this respect from all other roots, bears its seed the same year it is sown, without the root receiving any alteration. From the end of June, it begins to rise, and is ripe in July. Care must be taken to remove it as it blooms, as the slightest wind blows it away, and birds, which are very fond of it, shake it off and eat it. When it is completely picked, all the stems must be cut off flush to the ground, and the plant must be thoroughly wetted; it will soon green up again: there is no other way to grow it. If you want to eat it during the winter, you can pull out what you want at the end of November, and bury it in the greenhouse or in a trench, as I've explained for other plants. But if you're saving it for Lent, you only pull it out as and when you eat it; and it's always better fresh from the ground. What I have just said is as much as one would like to eat in the first year; but as this is not usual in private gardens, it is left in place without any care, until one wants to enjoy it, at the end of the second or third year: one is content simply to crimp it at the beginning of spring, when it begins to grow its new leaves which make it recognizable; for during the winter all its foliage perishes. Shortly afterwards, it sprouts its stem, which grows and provides much more than that of the first year; its seed also ripens earlier, and is good to pick at the beginning of July: it should be preferred to that of the first year; and the reason for this is that the stalk, which has more strength, feeds it better. It keeps well for two years. In areas close to woods where birds are common, it is very difficult to save it at this time of year, when neither the seeds nor any other seeds are yet ripe; and we are often obliged to keep it, if the scarecrows are not enough; for, without even waiting for it to ripen, they pierce the husk of the bud, and eat it green. To avoid, at least in part, their damage, when you don't have the convenience of keeping it, you can do what I do: cut off all the buds that are beginning to yellow, without waiting for them to open, and lay them on a sheet in the sun; soon after they develop, and the seed is equally good. This seed is also subject to another scourge, which is nail, which attaches itself to the stems and causes them to perish. There is no remedy for this: you need to reserve more than less, so as not to be deprived. This plant's enemy is the beetle worm, which cuts its roots. As soon as you see a plant with a wilting leaf, look for it quickly, otherwise it will ruin entire rays. The mole also sometimes wreaks havoc: watch out and catch it. CHAPTER LXXIII. Description of common salsify; its properties, cultivation, etc. Common salsify, otherwise known as white salsify, differs from Spanish salsify, which I have just described, in that its root is white both inside and out, and is also larger and less prone to forking. Its leaf is very narrow and smooth, shaped like a gutter, twelve to fifteen inches long, of a whitish water-green: its stem is branched, fairly large, three or four feet high; its flower is a violet-red, sun-shaped: it is placed at the end of its stems, and comes out of an elongated bud in the same way as the Spanish salsify; but the bud and flower are much larger. After the flower has passed, the bud opens, and the seed spreads out in the same way, in the shape of a tassel, each seed being accompanied by its aigrette. The seed is twice as large as that of black salsify, a little longer too, and gray or ashen in color. The root, although less effective than the first species, has the same uses for suspicious diseases. This root, although less spicy in taste than the other, is nevertheless very useful during Lent, and makes a very healthy food; it is eaten prepared in the same way. I won't repeat what I've said about it. It has the advantage over blackcurrants of occupying the land for only one year: it is sown in April or May, depending on the soil, and is ready for harvest as early as All Saints' Day; but it is commonly reserved for Lent. It is grown in the same way as the other variety, and requires the same quality of soil. It is quite resistant to frosts and bad weather, so you don't need to pull it up to use it during Lent: but if you want to use it during the winter, you need to pull up the quantity you think you'll need around Christmas, and put it in a greenhouse in fresh sand, or bury it in a trench dug in the garden, which you take care to cover well during frosts. Leave as much as you want to keep for seed, and if you're worried about frosts damaging it, although there's little risk of that, you can cover the spot with dry sills: manure is too precious to use for this purpose. Its foliage dries out entirely during the winter; but as soon as spring arrives, the leaf grows back, and we immediately give it a little hoeing; soon afterwards it makes its stem, and its seed is ripe in July. The same precautions need to be taken as for the other plant, to be able to preserve it: the birds devour part of it, and the wind carries away the rest, if you are not clever enough to pick it up as it opens out, to make its tassel: there is, however, a way I use to defend against the wind'; it's to cut the flower buds above the seed with scissors, when they start to yellow. It's easy to see how much space the seed occupies in the bud, so as not to damage it. This operation provides a second benefit, which is to pick up the seed clean, because the chisel takes away the burrs that catch the wind, and you don't have to go to the trouble of cleaning it, which is a hassle. After picking it up, leave it exposed to the sun on a sheet for a day or two, winnow it and lock it up: it's only good for sowing in the first year; in the second, some of it still rises, but you don't need to make a sure foundation. Volume II. S CHAPTER LXXIV. Description of the SARRIETTE; its different species, properties, cultivation, etc. CETTE plante n'est pas d'une grande elle sert quelquefois, et qu'elle a d'autres propriétés, il faut lui donner rang avec les autres: l'on appelle indifféremment Sarriette, Sadrée et Savorée. There are four species, but only one is cultivated usefully; the second is known to us only by relation; and the other two, which are not very widespread, have no merit other than to please the curious. The first species, which we cultivate, forms a rounded, regularly branched tuft, about a foot wide and of the same height; its root is simple, small and woody; its wood is hard, and resembles dry wood; its leaves are narrow and oblong, holding the middle ground between those of thyme and hyssop; its flowers emerge from leaf axils in places; they are mouth-shaped, perfectly similar to those of thyme, white in color, tending to purpurin. When they have passed, they are succeeded by small, almost round, slate-colored seeds enclosed in capsules that served as calyxes for the flowers. Its main cooking advantage is that it enhances the taste of marsh beans, with which it goes very well. To achieve this, sow a little on a bed, then replant it at the foot of a well-exposed wall, so that it comes up at the same time as the first beans: it is also sometimes used in certain sauces that call for strong herbs. The Germans put it in their candied cabbage, which they call Saurkraudt, and claim that it helps to preserve it longer. It is much more useful in medicine, and so good that it is called sauce aux pauvres gens (sauce for the poor); it restores and strengthens the stomach: its decoction, syringed into the ear, is very good in soporous affections, to awaken the sick: it is also useful as a gargle for loosening the uvula, and for inflammation of the tonsils. The powder of its dried leaves, drunk in a glass of wine, relieves chest pains. Some authors extend its virtues much further, but I'll confine myself to those that have proved most effective. S 2 Its cultivation is very simple; it grows easily in any soil, and so easily that it reproduces itself every year, without any care, in places where it has been grown once: it can be used in any degree of strength until it goes to seed; and to collect it, it is left to ripen to the point where it begins to detach from the first capsules; at this point, the whole feet are torn off, and tied into bundles, which are hung on a floor: it does not need to be exposed to the sun; the air is enough to perfect it. It keeps well for four or five years, when left in its capsules. Those who need this plant during the winter should pull out as much as they see fit, before it flowers, and dry it in the shade, attached to a floor that is not extremely exposed to flies. The second species, called Sariette de Candie, is known to us, as I said earlier, only by reputation, and I have never been able to see it. According to M. Chomel, who gives a short description, its scent is much more pleasant than the one I mentioned, and an essential oil with great virtues is extracted from it. He compares its properties to those of thyme, and says no more, but that's enough to make us regret not knowing about it. It seems to me, however, that being common in Candia, whose remoteness is not very considerable, those curious about botany should not remain privẽs of it. The other two, which I saw without having cultivated them, are both perennials, and form a fairly tall shrub: one has an oblong leaf much like that of tarragon, and bears at its apices clusters of bluish-white flowers, borne on a group of small calyxes piled one on top of the other, followed by a small, elongated, russet-colored seed, by which it is produced: it can also be multiplied by its offshoots. It can also be propagated by its offspring, but must be raised in pots and sown in the greenhouse when frost approaches. The other has a small, elongated leaf, and grows small white flowers all along the branches, which bloom imperceptibly; and as it is very delicate, it must be locked up early in a warm greenhouse: it keeps its leaf there all winter. I know of no useful qualities for these two species, and the authors have said nothing about them. - THE SAUGE. CHAPTER LXXV. Description of Sage; its different species, properties, cultivation, etc. Sage is a perennial and aromatic plant; I know of fourteen different species, and I know that there are a few other less remarkable ones, which I will pass over in silence. These fourteen species are the purplish-flowered grande saugrande, the white-flowered d'Espagne, the lavender-leaved d'Espagne, the variegated yellow, the curly, the berry-bearing, the broad-leaved petite du Levant, the large du Levant, the yellow-flowered d'Afrique, the purple-flowered d'Afrique, and the mountain sage. The large sage, otherwise known as the large species, and in botany as Spacellus Theophrasti, has a woody root with many fibers; Its leaf is oval-shaped, moderately pointed, thick and chagrined, water-green, covered with a small white down, borne on a reddish stalk, with a strong, aromatic odor and flavor; it grows several branching, woody stems, hairy and reddish at the tips, rising to about three feet: Its flowers are born at the end of the stems, and are borne on a kind of spike; they are ash-blue in color, and form a pipe cut into two lips at the top, the upper one arched, and the lower one divided into three parts. From the front of this flower rise the stamens, attached together and arranged in such a way that they do not misrepresent the hioid bone: the calyx is a cone that pushes out from the bottom a pistil composed of four embryos; this pistil fits into a hole at the bottom of the flower, and these embryos subsequently become so many almost round seeds, enclosed in a capsule that served as the flower's calyx. Sage franche, which botanists call Calvia minor aurita and not aurita, is similar in many respects to the preceding variety, but it has more leaves and does not grow so high. Its wood is also redder and more woody, and its leaf more elongated and narrower, sometimes having ears at its base; its grain and color are quite similar, as is its flower. Provence sage, called Salvia folio tenuiore, has the root and wood of its stems equally woody; but its leaves are much smaller, narrower, 4 1 and in greater quantity, being as if piled on top of each other at the end of the branches: its flower is arranged like the others, but bright blue. The large purpurine sage differs from the first only in that its flower is reddish, tending to purple, and its odor is stronger. White-flowered Spanish sage differs from the above in that its leaves are long and narrow, yellowish-green; its stems are also green, sparsely hairy, and its flower white. It grows to about two feet tall, and produces only a few, rather thin branches. Spanish sage, with a lavender leaf, is called by this name because its leaves are long and narrow, a little toothed, whiter than green, and strongly resembling the lavender leaf in all respects. Its stems are also more naked and whitish than the other species. The variegated yellow, otherwise known as omelette, has a leaf mottled with green and yellow, and is a little obtuse; in all other respects, it bears the same resemblance to the others. Frisée has a broad, almost round leaf, toothed and curled at the edges, carried on a long tail, pale water-green, soft on the finger like marshmallow, without any marked grain, and with a less penetrating odor than the others: it only grows to a foot or eighteen inches; its flower is blue, similar to the others. Botanists call it Salvia baccifera for this reason: its leaves are heart-shaped, tender green, yellowish and velvety; its stems are also regularly green, and its scent quite sweet: it forms a very tall shrub, and bears small pea-sized fruits, in which the seed is enclosed. Levant sage, with its broad leaves, is totally different from all the others in that its euilla is winged and arranged like that of the rosebush; but the impaire ending it is considerably larger than the segmens on the sides; it is also very hairy, toothed at the edges and meadow-green; it does not grow upwards, and its stems crawl on the ground; its flower is blue and very large. Levant sage differs from the first species only in its leaf, which is larger. African sage, with its yellow flower, grows like a shrub; its leaf is rounded, smooth at the edges, velvety and sea-green; its wood is ash-colored, and its flower golden yellow. African sage, with violet flowers, also grows as a shrub: its leaves are small, almost round, a little toothed, and whitish water-green; its wood is hazelnut-colored, and its flower violet. Mountain sage, otherwise known as wild sage, has a large, broad, soft leaf, with a garlic-like odor; its flower is yellowish, and grows to three feet or more; but its stems die every year; it must be cut flush with the ground in late autumn, and grows again in spring: it grows naturally in mountainous woods, around bushes and hedges. Some claim that all these species of sage are merely varieties of the first species, which grows naturally in Provence, and that the differences we notice there have gradually arisen from changes in terrain and climate, as well as from cultivation. I cannot accept this opinion; for these species differ too greatly from one another to be regarded as mere varieties: there is an extreme disproportion in size and grain, and even in smell: one grows higher, the other lower; one has a blue flower, the other purple or yellow; the latter bears fruit, etc. All these characteristics must be recognized in the plant. All these characteristics must be recognized as species differences, or they must no longer be admitted in any other plant; for it would then follow that there would only be one species of cabbage, one species of lettuce, etc., because their tastes and other properties are always the same, from the most to the least. I agree that changes of soil and climate diminish or increase a plant's vigor, as well as its virtues; but they don't cut the leaf, if it wasn't cut in the first place; they don't change the color of the flower from white to yellow. Let's take all these species of sage to the rocks of Provence, where they are said to have originated, and I'm convinced that they will always retain the same differences there as they do here. In a word, one came to us from Spain, the other from the Levant, the other from Africa, and so on. Why pretend that these regions, which are so far apart from one another, drew the species they have communicated to us from the same stem, and that these species did not originate naturally in their own bosom? Everyone will conclude as they see fit. The fourteen species I have described all have the same virtues, from the most to the least, and some can be used in the absence of others; however, Spanish sage is reputed by botanists to be the best; little Provence sage comes S6 after it, and frank sage after that; jumping sage has particular qualities, which I will discuss in a separate article. The leaves and flowers of this plant are very useful in aromatic decoctions and fomentations, for strengthening nerves, firming flesh, softening tumors and dissipating the swelling of wounds; It is also good for scurvy, especially if the gums are well bathed with half its juice, and as much cochlearia juice mixed with similar salse and balauste; it makes white flowers pass; but fat women should not be given it, because it promotes menstruation. An infusion of the leaves is taken internally for dizziness, vapors, drowsiness and other affections of the brain, which threaten apoplexy, paralysis and so on. At the same time, it revives the movement of liquors and blood circulation. Eight or ten leaves are infused in half a cup of boiling water, and drunk every morning on an empty stomach with a little sugar, for several days. The same infusion is very useful for suppressing menstruation and urination, indigestion and stomach weakness, wind, colic and spitting blood, killing worms and clearing the lungs of asthmatics; the effect is even more certain when the leaf is smoked like tobacco. The infusion of this leaf in wine is also sovereign for toothache, if two large quantities of good tobacco are added. Water distilled from sage, which is poured over wounds, mundializes them; drawn through the nose, it stops haemorrhaging, fortifies the brain and limbs, and by washing the mouth with it, tightens the gums. Finally, this plant has so many virtues that it is considered by many to be universal and suitable for all ailments; this is why an ancient saying goes: Cur moritur homo cui salvia crescit in horto? and this is why it is called Salvia, from Salvus, healthy. However, although salvia is beneficial in all countries, it is unanimously considered to have more virtues when grown in hot climates, such as Provence. It is said to attract the snakes and toads that intertwine in its branches, and that they spread their venom on the leaves, from which bad effects can ensue, in addition to stinging those who approach them; but the remedy for this inconvenience is to mix in a few feet of rue to keep them away. Wild sage, which, according to M. Tournefort, is a species of Germandrée, has the merit more than the others of removing jaundice and tierce fever, by taking its juice or an insusion in wine; and for dropsy, an infusion of this leaf is given daily in white wine, of which a glass is taken every four hours, with great effect. This plant could be propagated from seed, but as it is easier to propagate from cuttings or rooted plants, and is more enjoyable, we leave it at that for the species that can overwinter in the ground; an old separate plant provides a dozen more or less, which are replanted in spring or autumn, it's all the same; and as the most common use is to form borders around vegetable garden quarters, either outside or inside, a string is stretched out, and a small trench seven to eight inches deep by the same width is opened, and buried close to the end of the stems, i.e. to within four inches, treading the soil with the foot after the trench is filled in. It goes without saying that the roots must be shortened beforehand. No other preparation is required, and there is no lack of it, provided the plants have roots. This plant is by no means delicate; it fears neither frost, nor drought, nor humidity; however, it has more virtue in dry soils than in watery ones; so, as much as possible, it should be placed according to taste. It can be kept in the same place for as long as you like, but as it grows and spreads too much after three years, and makes an ugly effect on the eyes, it should be destroyed at this age, and renewed. When planted from cuttings, a little more care is needed, and it should be planted in spring to give it time to root during the summer. The most important thing is to lay the sprigs down, press them down firmly with your foot after they have been covered with soil, and water often until they are well established; but the safest way is to root them on the bed, and after six weeks, you can remove them with a little clod and place them wherever you like. It should be noted that the two African salges, the curly one and the berry-bearing one, do not overwinter in the open ground; they must be placed in pots, and locked up in the greenhouse when frost approaches; the following spring, they are buried with the pots in a well-exposed spot, as they require a lot of heat. THYM. CHAPTER LXXVI. Description of THYME; its different species, properties, cultivation, etc. THYME is an aromatic perennial plant of which there are five main species, not to mention several kinds of wild thyme known as Serpolet. These five species are common garden thyme with small leaves, variegated thyme, Crete thyme, broad-leaved thyme and lemon thyme. Common thyme has a woody root with many fibers; it sprouts a number of small twigs, rising to eight or ten inches, and forming a full, rounded tuft; these twigs are covered throughout with small, oblong, narrow leaves, ash-colored, with a strong, aromatic odor and a pungent taste: The flowers are small, purplish, shaped like a maw and cut into two lips at the top. The flower is followed by a capsule containing four almost round seeds. Thyme panaché differs from the previous variety only in the plume of its leaves, which are partly white and partly ashy; consequently, it is more of a variety than a difference in species. Cretan thyme grows as a shrub to twelve or fifteen inches in height, and produces many large, woody branches with small, opposite, narrow, whitish, pungent-tasting leaves; its flowers are arranged in the same way as the others. Broad-leaved thyme grows lower than its predecessors; its branches fall to the ground in great numbers; its leaves are oblong and much larger, of an obscure green color, with a less penetrating odor. It is very similar to the others, either in root, flower or seed. Lemon thyme differs from the others only in its odor, which is similar to that of lemon, from which it takes its name. These five species have more or less the same virtues; however, Crete thyme would be preferred if it were more common; but as it is very difficult to grow in this climate, we stick to the others, and particularly to the first species, which can be found in every garden. It is used in food with other fine herbs, to enhance the flavor of meat and fish, especially in short broths; it has no other use. In medicine, it is used in many cases; it is resolving, desiccating, attenuating, incisive and discussing; it enters into the aromatic and cephalic decoctions and infusions, so commonly used in fomentation, to basin nervous and muscular parts that are weakened or too swollen. Its powder, mixed with honey and barley flour, relieves sciatic gout when applied to the affected part as a poultice. It fortifies the brain and alleviates pituitary. Its main use is in tartarous affections of the lungs, such as asthma and obstinate coughs: it is also used for windy colic, to stimulate appetite and aid digestion. It is also used externally for cold humors, eye contusions, gout and paralysis. This plant multiplies from seed, cuttings and rooted plants, but more commonly in the latter way than in the other two: it is replanted in autumn or spring, in the same way as I said for sage and lavender. The first, fourth and fifth species are winter-hardy, and require no special care; but the second and third need the greenhouse, and must therefore be raised in pots. The one in gardens is renewed every three years; it forms very clean borders around vegetable garden quarters; and at flowering time, it gives off a very pleasant odor; it is then that we cut what we deem appropriate, for the various uses to which we wish to put it, as the flower greatly increases its virtue; and as it is the sap's forte, all its parts are more spirituous. CHAPTER LXXVII. Description of TOPINAMBOUR; its uses, cultivation, etc. HERE is the worst of all vegetables, in the general opinion; yet the people, who are the most numerous part of humanity, feed on it: I must therefore place this vegetable with the others. Its root is an assembly of a multitude of small roots and fibers, between which bulbs are attached: Its stem is stalked and three to four feet high, about the size of a thumb, fluted, hollow and hairy; its leaves are large, oblong and pointed, a little hairy; each branch ends in a yellow, radiate, sun-shaped flower, all the petals of which are supported by a ball-shaped calyx, which encloses the seed; this seed is small and brown in color. The fruit is egg-sized, sometimes two or three times larger, baroque in shape, with spikes and knots around the circumference, white inside and reddish outside. This fruit, which is the whole point of the plant, is eaten with white sauce, after being cooked in water; others fricassee it in butter with onion: its taste is quite similar to artichoke or salsify, but it is soft and mushy: it keeps all winter until Easter. It is sometimes used medicinally to stop stomach upsets, being by nature detersive, astringent and pectoral: it is eaten cooked, or a decoction is drunk. This plant originated in America, in the land of the Topinambous, from which it takes its name; it is also called Potato. It multiplies from seed, or from its own fruit, which is replanted in March, in the same way as the truffle. Any loose soil is good for it, and it requires no special care: the fruit is pulled up when frost approaches, and placed in a heap in the greenhouse; others run through the winter event, and leave it in the ground until Lent. CHAPTER LXXVIII. Description of TRIQUE-MADAME; its properties, cultivation, etc. LA trique-madame, which others call Trippe-Madame, is a species of small houseleek that grows naturally on old walls, but which cultivation has perfected. Its stem is small, fibrous and blackish; it grows several fat, fleshy, tender, creeping stems, covered with many small, oblong, thick, fat, pointed, bluish-green or reddish leaves, filled with juice: its flowers, which are born at the top of the stems, are small, with several leaves arranged in a pink, and yellow in color; the flowers are followed by a small fruit containing five seeds. The use of this plant is limited to salads, where it is mixed with other supplies; it has its supporters, but not the greatest number. In medicine, it is sometimes substituted for houseleek, as it is humectant and refreshing; good, in particular, for haemorrhoids. It multiplies from seed, cuttings and offshoots; each foot makes many arms, which, when separated and replanted, form as many new feet; it is almost equally successful from cuttings, provided they are watered often and kept out of the sun for a few days. It is planted a foot apart, and watered often; this is what makes it tender. CHAPTER LXXIX. Description of the TRUFFLE; its different species, properties, cultivation, etc. Here is a plant of which no author has spoken, and probably it is out of contempt for it that it has been excluded from the class of vegetable plants; for it is too long known and too widespread for it to have escaped their knowledge; yet there is injustice in omitting a fruit that serves as food for a large proportion of men of all nations. I don't want to elevate it more than it deserves, because I know all its faults, which I'll talk about; but I feel it should have a place with the others, since it serves a useful purpose, and has its enthusiasts. In most of our provinces, it is not only the lower classes and country folk who make a living from it, but even the most affluent people in the cities; and I can also say, from the knowledge I have of it, that many people love it out of passion. Whether this is a well-placed affection, or a depravity of taste, I put aside; it has its supporters, that's enough for me. There are two species of truffle, differing from one another only in external color, one being red, the other white tending to yellow: the latter is preferred, having less pungency than the former.
