The fruit is born between two earths, and is held to the roots by a kind of pedicel numbering twenty or thirty, some larger, others smaller: this fruit is elongated, rounded at both ends, uneven, with a sort of sunken eyes all around, which are as many sprouts of the plant, three to four inches long, about eighteen lines in diameter: it is covered with a film that lifts easily when cooked: its flesh is white and firm, a little watery, with no odor. The plant grows several branches at a time, which are hard and woody, almost triangular, partly green and partly reddish in color, lined with thresholds and small twigs throughout; these leaves are arranged in the same way as those of the rose, and of similar size, dull green, hairy at the tops of the stems: from the leaf axils emerge a few bunches of flowers borne on a fairly long tail: these flowers are one-piece, star-shaped, flax-gray in color, with a few yellow stamens in the center, whose tips meet and form a sort of keel; they are borne on an embryo at the bottom of the calyx, which turns into a round fruit, the size of a small walnut, which is green at first, turning yellow as it ripens. This fruit is fleshy and contains a number of small seeds, by which the plant can multiply if necessary, but they are not used. This fruit can be seasoned in all sorts of ways: sliced thinly and fried in butter or oil, with a light dusting of flour; cooked in water, with the skin removed, sliced and fried in butter with onion: Others cook it in wine, but the best way is to chop it after cooking, and make a paste with breadcrumbs, a few egg yolks and fine herbs, from which we make dumplings, which are browned in butter in the pan. Common people eat it simply cooked in ashes, with a little salt; and in the mountains, it's made into bread. Finally, it is consumed in considerable quantities, particularly in the provinces near the Rhône; and, in addition to being used as human food, it is also used to fatten animals. I will admit, however, that it is a bland, insipid food, and very hard on the stomach; but it has a certain taste that pleases its lovers: what can one object against it? And when one is accustomed to a thing, how much of its defects does it not lose? One thing is certain: this fruit is nourishing, and by force of habit, it doesn't bother those who have been accustomed to it from youth. What's more, it's very economical for the lower classes: these advantages may well outweigh its faults. It is not unknown in Paris; but it is true that it is abandoned to the common people, and that people of a certain order put it below them to see it appear on their table: I do not want to inspire in them a taste for it, which I do not have myself; but one should not condemn those to whom it pleases and to whom it is profitable. I don't think it has any properties for Tome II. However, a few years ago, it was devised as a powder to be used as a substitute for ordinary powder in times of high grain prices. At first, it met with some success, and the Ministry helped the enterprise with its protection; but when it was put to use, it was found to be too heavy, and not suitable for hair, which caused it to fail, and it is no longer used. This plant is sown in March; it requires loose, fat soil, ploughed deeply: some people make holes with the planter, and throw the seed in: others make rays with the hoe, and spread it in, covering it with three or four inches of soil; this last method is the best. Besides, this seed is nothing other than the fruit itself, which is cut into six, eight or ten pieces, depending on its size; as long as there is an eye in each piece, no more is needed. You can also sow small, hazelnut-sized truffles, which you set aside every year when you dig them up: you space them twelve or fifteen inches apart; when they've risen to a certain height, you crimp them; you don't need any other cultivation. Some, however, cut the tops halfway off when they are at about their height, to make better use of the stalks; others knock them to the ground, and throw a spoonful of earth on top; but most do nothing about it, and I have found that they grow very well without any of these precautions. Around All Saints' Day, we pull out the stalks and detach the fruit, if the soil is not too well sealed; a fork is better for this than any sharp tool: we let the fruit dry out a little and then lock it up, noting that we don't want a greenhouse that's too hot, which would make it sprout, nor a cellar that's too damp, which would make it rot, nor any place where frost could penetrate; once it's in the right place, it keeps until after Easter. I'll say a word in passing about another species of truffle that grows naturally in the ponds of some provinces, particularly in Bourbonnois and Burgundy: it goes by many other names, truffle or water truffle, macer, cornouelle, water chestnut, echarbot, etc. This fruit is produced by an aquatic plant that grows in ponds. This fruit is produced by an aquatic plant; its shape is horny, walnut-sized, its rind brown, and its kernel white; its taste is sweet, approaching that of the chestnut. It is prepared for eating in various ways: some cook it in ash like chestnuts, others in boiling water; it is made into bread and a kind of porridge in Limosin, where children are very fond of it; some eat it raw like hazelnuts; and all the ancient and modern authors who have spoken of it, going back as far as Dioscorides and Theophrastus, have praised it as a refreshing dish and a useful food: It is even said to have properties for certain illnesses: when cooked and applied as a poultice to inflammations, it resolves them; its juice is also very good for eye diseases; and its decoction with honey as a gargle is very suitable for cleaning ulcerated gums. A PARIS Chez DELALAIN fils, Libraire, quai des Augustins, Ne. 38. An XI, 1802. EEI Google IERUET Google A misplaced wisdom is a real follyWanting to bring men back to one's taste, however reasonable it may be, is of all enterprises the least sensible. Each century has its own mores, which must not be contradicted. It would be useless to try to introduce even wiser ones; the shortest way is to follow one's own, without exhausting oneself in reasoning to try to bend others to it. A philosopher by temperament, and perhaps by reflection, will I go and extol to the worldly the charms of retirement, incomprehensible to those whose hearts are not made for it? Gardening is my attraction; for many years I have made it the amusement of my leisure, and the most solid occupation of my life. Is this a reason to flaunt the merits of this exercise? It seems to me that I would do as well as anyone else in painting the golden age, when cultivating the land was the noblest of arts, as it still is the most useful. I could recall men to their natural vocation, and adorn this discourse with touching descriptions, which would hardly make more of an impression on most of my readers, than the sight of a beautiful landscape placed in the cabinet of a rich man, does on these men of the century, accustomed to the pomp and noise of cities; but that is neither my mission, nor my object. I'm not trying to inspire a taste for gardening; I'm writing for those who already have it, and this little work, intended for their instruction, should offer nothing strange to the goal I propose. My birth and the course of affairs in which I was engaged from my youth, should, it seems to me, have left me forever ignorant of all that belongs to Gardening; but who can answer for events? From a hectic and constrained life, I passed almost all of a sudden to a free and quiet life; and the circumstances which detached me from all kinds of business, made me leave the city at the same time. I retired to a country house just outside Paris, which, having belonged to a Minister of State before me, had all the advantages one could imagine. Its happy situation, the good quality of the bottom, its extent and the convenience of the water, attracted me more and more. Finally, I resolved to settle there, and to do so usefully, I made a regular occupation of it. The cultivation of my land was the object to which my taste turned. At first, like many others, I was handed over to an ignorant gardener in bad faith, and I allowed myself to be led by his advice in the first operations I tried, but I soon recognized that they were wrong. Consequently, on other occasions, I wanted to go to the council; but the diversity of feelings and the variety of practices embarrassed me more often than they decided me. I visited all the houses whose gardens seemed to me to be well maintained; I read all the authors who had written most on the subject; in a word, I left no stone unturned in my quest for knowledge: but all this study only served to make me realize that without practice and experience, one acquires only very superficial knowledge. I therefore decided to carry out, by myself, all the operations that were within my reach; and, with the exception of the hard work, I can assure you that I put my hand to everything, i.e., I sowed, planted, pruned, palmed, grafted, etc. I wanted to know everything there was to know. I wanted to know everything inside out, and by doing this myself, I was more likely to notice any defects in the work. My mind worked even harder than my hand; I sought the good and the best; and experience, which I took as my guide, served either to assure me or to disabuse me. It was through this long application that I acquired the knowledge I share with the public. The success of my plantations introduced me to many honored people, who had the same passion, and whom curiosity attracted to me. Finally, a person of the highest esteem asked me one day for some instruction on the cultivation of peach trees, whose fruit she particularly liked, so I made it my duty to oblige her, and wrote down all my views. This little treatise, which pleased her very much, then passed from her hands into those of several curious people, who found it to their taste, and I was urged to publish it. This is the work I am giving today, with no other interest than that of being useful, and of sparing an amateur the mistakes to which inexperience can lead him. If the success of this piece fulfills my intention, I shall successively give some on the cultivation of other fruits, and on all the other parts of gardening. Although several authors have already written on this subject, I think I can say that it has never been more than sketched out, and that none of them has made a sufficient study of it to be able to serve as a guide; however, I rightly exclude M. de la Quintinie, who has given very judicious rules, based on experience and good reasoning: but he has not given his subjects an order that puts the reader at his ease, and he has not said all that the subject requires. I flatter myself that one will find in the manner in which I treat more clearness and exactitude. Perhaps the curious and the true connoisseurs will find it a little too detailed; but they must consider that they form a small number in society, and that the greater number are so little or so poorly instructed, that they need to be led, as it were, by the hand; it is essentially them that I have had in view, and my object will be fulfilled, if I succeed in being useful to them. FROM FISHING. CHAPTER ONE. Fishing in general. PEACH is one of the most excellent fruits of Europe; but in our climate, it is also the one that requires the most care, and consequently the most intelligence, to be cultivated usefully. We owe the abundance we enjoy in Paris and the surrounding area when the years are favorable to the large number of seedlings everyone has made of it; for the way they are usually managed means that we don't get out of a hundred trees what we could get out of twenty if they were managed with a certain art, and that of all the fruit trees that can be planted in a garden, there is none whose yield is so uncertain. It is true, on the other hand, that there is none that so pleasantly lines walls, that offers fruit so bright to the eye and more pleasing to the taste, and that yields so quickly. All these advantages are seductive: so people who are in a position to obtain this satisfaction, should not stop at difficulties or expense. As for those who plant for profit, they must detach themselves from the prejudice that peach tree espaliers are more profitable than other fruits. It is said that M. Girardot, a former King's Musketeer, earned up to thirty thousand pounds in income from a very small area of land he owned in Bagnolet. This example appealed to many, but it's important to distinguish the times. In the past, the cultivation of this fruit was known only to a few people; today, everyone is involved, plantations have multiplied extraordinarily, and this fruit, which has become more common, has decreased in value in proportion to its abundance. Another example is the village of Montreuil, which is said to produce nothing but peaches and a few red fruits, and which, on this simple income, pays the King fifty thousand pounds in taxes every year, to which must be added the rent for the land, which is commonly rented at 200, 250 and up to 300 pounds per acre. The conclusion is that the cultivation of peaches must in itself be very profitable. I don't dispute the Montreuil fact, but there are several things to consider. 1°. The product of these peaches is shared between four thousand inhabitants, who are dedicated to this crop, and who are, as it were, cradled in it. 2°. The location and quality of the soil are highly favorable to this species of tree, which takes five or six years to form and covers its wall from top to bottom, a feature rarely seen elsewhere. 3e. The layout of the espaliers is different from that of ordinary gardens; all the soil is cut by walls, twenty or twenty-five feet apart: these walls, forming shelter after shelter, defend the fruit from bad winds, and preserve it while it perishes elsewhere. 4°. The care that the inhabitants of Montreuil take to make the most of their fruits is inconceivable: they cultivate them with their own hands, and care for them with uncommon intelligence. They watch them night and day; they carry and sell them themselves, in the markets, with a particular art. And what private individual would want to buy them for the same price? I'm talking here about those who plant for their own benefit, and aim only for abundance; It is mainly for the latter that I am writing, and in order to do so with some order, I will begin by examining the different species of peaches, and mark those to be preferred to the others. CHAPTER II. Of the different kinds of Peaches, and the choice to be made. Sentiments vary widely on the different species of peaches. M. de la Quintinie has claimed to know thirty-two, which he has named, without the Brugnons and Pavies; others put the 3 number at forty or fifty. These, or I am very much mistaken, often use the same for two, especially as most have up to two and three names. As far as I'm concerned, I don't have anything specific to say in this regard; I've never even tried very hard to unravel this exact difference. It's a question of knowing the best; and we lose nothing by not knowing all those that are inferior to them. I declare, therefore, that I know of only about fifteen to which we should stop; the others are either very mediocre, or very bad. Others, though good, don't charge, and aren't worth the space they occupy; others are too prone to gum or ant; a few others, finally, are not fashionable, though of fairly good quality, because their elongated or biscornu shape doesn't appeal. But when it doesn't cost more to have the perfect than the defective, we must stick to the former: good exhibitions are too precious not to use them for the best we can get. Provided we have an uninterrupted succession of good peaches, from the sin of July to mid-October, after which time the fruit ripens only imperfectly, we need ask for nothing more. Finally, I would not like to enter into the fantasy of certain people who want to have everything known, good or bad, especially with regard to a fruit that costs so much care, and occupies an expensive place. The fifteen species I have chosen follow each other without interruption; and they are, without question, the most beautiful in color, the best made, the biggest, the best, and the most expensive, as everyone knows them. What I say about them is enough, without stopping to praise each one in particular. If you think it's a good idea to leave it at that, I'd advise you to distribute them as follows. Assuming you have room for 50 trees, you would plant 2. Petite Mignone. 6. Grosse Mignone. 5. Chevreuse. 4. Purple. 2. Madelaine rouge. 5. Persique. 4. Galande. 3. Tetons of Venus. 3. Italian peaches. 3. Early violets. 5. Beurdin. 4. Admirable. 3. Belgarde. 3. Royal. 2. Nivette. 2. Pavie de Pompone. If you have more or less space, you will make a proportionate distribution; and if you only have a very-small garden, where you only have room for 6 feet of trees, I advise you to choose in preference to the others, 1. Grosse Mignone. 1. Pourprée. 1. Early Violet. 1. Admirable. 1. galande. 1. Nivette. I give a place to the violet, which many people esteem only mediocrely, especially since not all soils are suitable for giving it taste and size; but when it comes in its perfection, I put it above all other peaches, and I have many supporters of my taste. V3 O I still put on the range the Pavie de Pompone, which many persoones disregard, and which I don't think much of myself, as far as taste is concerned; but I consider it for three reasons. The first is that its monstrous size and beautiful coloring perfectly decorate a table; the second is that it comes when all the last peaches are finished; and the third is that it is eaten all year long preserved in vinegar like gherkins, and that it surpasses in quality anything that is usually preserved in this way. It has been much sought-after for this reason for some years now, but there is a different way of pickling it from that used for gherkins. We also have a small dwarf peach tree raised in Orléans, which amuses the curious, but is only good for the pleasure of those who want it. It is cultivated in earthenware vases or small crates, where it is sufficiently nourished with the help of watering, and it hardly extends beyond the circumference of a clove plant; the fruit knots there very well, and it sometimes yields up to twenty or twenty-five rather large peaches: The fruit and the stem are served together on a table, and this makes a very pretty effect, especially in the eyes of those who don't know it; but the fruit is insipid, and it is even rare for it to ripen in this climate; perhaps it would be better in southern countries. Those who live in warmer climates than ours may add or subtract from the selection I have made above, whatever their climate may suggest. For example, I make no mention here of all the pavies, which I value highly in Italy and Provence. Nor do I take several kinds of late peaches, which ripen here only halfway, and which have no taste: perhaps they would be very good in these hot countries; it is up to each curious person to examine what is appropriate to his climate: but we must not envy anyone in this respect, because there is no country where this fruit has as many good qualities as it has here. I've eaten them in all the southern countries around us, right down to the depths of Italy and Sicily: our fine peaches don't do well there; the sun hits them too hard. It's a good idea to plant immediately, in the order I've just described, the fifteen species of peach and the pavia I've mentioned. I've never seen anyone do this, and I think it's important, because you don't need to run all your espaliers from one end to the other when you want to pick your fruit. When peaches of the same season are gathered together, you find everything under your hand. What's more, if you need to defend your fruit, either from people or beasts, you only have to watch over one spot. If it's necessary, in times of drought, to give water to trees whose fruit is maturing, you don't have to go so far, and you don't have to beat the ground so much along your espaliers: (I'm assuming, in saying this, that there are contr'espaliers). Finally, as it costs no more to plant them in this order, I strongly urge you to practice it. 4 On the right choice of trees, and how to raise them in nurseries. Iss environs de Paris sont remplis de pépinières; ainsi, on n'est pas embarrassé de trouver des arbres, quand les gelées de l'hiver n'ont pas fait périr les greffages, comme il est arrivé eu eu 1740 et 1742. Vitry, Fontenay-aux-Roses, le Pré-saint- Gervais, are particularly devoted to raising them; but as Vitry has the widest choice, it's the best place to get them. I've sometimes grown them in Orléans, and they've also worked well for me. The essential point is that a tree should not remain exposed to the ravages of time after being uprooted, and that its roots should be kept fresh by the help of moss and good wrapping. They must also be in the condition they should be in: the place where they were born makes no difference, as long as there is no significant difference between the land where they were grown and the land where they are put back, which is something to be careful of. They sell for 5 sous for low stems, 10 sous for half-stems and 15 sous for stems, when the year's grafts have been successful. Those who pay more are always fooled, either by the merchant's vainglorious reputation, or by the person hired to buy them. Nothing could be further from the truth than to imagine that by paying three times as much for them, as some people have the talent to sell them, you'll get better quality, or that you'll be more sure of the cash. With regard to the first point, one has eyes to see them; and with regard to the second, I admit that one can be deceived. In this profession, as in all states of life, there are people of bad faith; but there is this difference from that to the others, which is that they gain little from deceit. I will agree, however, that as there are species whose grafts are very difficult to reproduce, such as the Mignone and the Galande, certain merchants avoid giving as many as they can, and often substitute other, more common species; but this does not mean that they are all unfaithful: in this profession, as in all others, there are people who are jealous of their reputation, and who understand that their own interests depend on it. It's only a question of getting to know them, and to do that you have to get information: the advice I could give in this respect would only be of monientaneous help, since everything changes from one day to the next; so everyone will take the measures they think best. I will only say that it is customary for people of good taste to have a catalog of the plants in their nurseries and grafts, in which they list them as follows: Telle pièce de terre tenant ici, et aboutis sunt là, greffe en pécher, contient tant de rangs d'nne telle espèce, tant de telle autre, etc. You take a look at this catalog, which you have no reason to suspect is false, and by following the rows, you 4 choose what suits you best. If your dealer doesn't have a catalog, don't trust him, and go to another. These precautions are dispensed with for all other pome fruits whose species can be identified by their wood and leaf: but with the exception of four or five species of peaches, namely, grosse and petite Mignones, Madeleines, Violettes and some Pavies, all the others are so similar in wood and leaf that the merchant himself would not recognize them if he did not observe the order I have just described. For all these reasons, it is very important to make your choice yourself, when you are capable of judging it, or to be sure of the person you hire to do it: for, if you rely on your gardener, the bait of a penny per tree, which they have tyrannically subjugated the merchants to give them as a right, makes them docile to whatever they want; and this is the case where you are badly served, because in favor of this bait they pass on everything they have that is defective. With regard to those who live in the provinces, and who, by taste or necessity, are obliged to draw their trees from Paris, Orleans or other places, I wish them a true friend who is willing to make this choice for himself, with the attentions and precautions of which I will speak, and which are exactly in accordance with those I recommend in Chapter XIX, for the transport of trees. 1°. From mid-October onwards, you must go to the nurseries, as the first to arrive always take the best. Peach trees are good to lift at this time, even if the leaf has not yet fallen; and if your land is not ready to plant them, have them buried in a trench, one next to the other, and not in bundles, as those who like their work done quickly do. 2e. Make sure they have not been rebooted: (I'll explain this term for those who don't understand it). Peach trees are not like other trees, which can only be replanted after two, three or four years of grafting; they must be replanted after their first growth, i.e. at the end of the year; and as it often happens that merchants can't sell them all within the year, they cut back at the end of March or in April, what remains to the thickness of half an inch above the graft, from which it immediately grows one or more new shoots; but these kinds of trees usually do not do well, and we mustn't take any: they're easy to spot, and all you have to do is watch out for them. 3e. Make sure your trees are healthy, that the gum has not attacked them, and that they are strong enough. The biggest ones are not the best, because they usually don't have good eyes at the bottom of the stem, below the size they should be pruned; and the thinnest ones, whose thinness is a sign of weakness, don't promise to ever be very vigorous. Avoid some and

the others, and choose the medium-sized ones, in which you'll find well-fed eyes next to the fat, because that's what's important. Everything I've just said applies particularly to low stems. With regard to stems and half-stems, or the same disadvantages that must be avoided, you must take care that the stems are straight, united, clear, without moss, and that they have a good body, i.e., a good inch in diameter, or three inches in circumference at the bottom; for if they are weaker, count on your tree always being puny: it will truly take up like another, and live; but it will not do in four years what a good one will do in two, and will last much less, besides that the fruits will never be well nourished. Take care, moreover, that the graft has grown straight, and that it does not make what is called the trumpet; for the result is sometimes that, despite all your attention to dividing the branches of your tree equally, the side where the graft leans always prevails over the other, which makes your tree defective. When pulling them out, make sure the digging is done at a good distance from the base, so as not to offend the roots, and reject those that are pulled out incorrectly, or whose roots are damaged. "As soon as they are uprooted, have them put in bundles with labels to distinguish the species, and lots of straw around them, which you will take care to have carried to the nursery, so that the bindings and the pack animals that will be transporting them don't skin them: likewise have the roots wrapped in straw, to protect them from the sun and the sun. These precautions, though very important, are often overlooked. In this case, I advise them to find a good merchant, and to give him their commission in good faith, on condition that you receive only what meets all the conditions I have just prescribed. This merchant, who will spare what he is obliged to give to a third party, and who will want to acquire your practice, will have an interest in giving only good, and will find his advantage in doing so by doing yours. Peach trees can be grafted onto three types of subject: the peach pit itself, almond trees and plum trees. Few of the first type are grafted, however, as they are too prone to gum; they are commonly found only on the other two. It's the quality of your soil that should decide whether you choose one or the other. If your soil is light and sandy, you should choose almond trees, because their roots, which prick or pivot, in gardening terms, seek their nourishment further underground than plum trees, and therefore defend themselves better in dry weather. If your soil has body, and if the bottom is tuff or clay, take it from the plum tree, because its root is content with little soil, and only seeks to run on the supersiccia where it is sufficiently nourished. In addition, you should follow what has been practised before you in the place where you will find yourself; for there is scarcely a countryside where this fruit has not been cultivated, and the kind of subject in which it has been kept, after the experiments made, must naturally be the best. I make this observation here, because despite the general rule, which is the one I've just established, it sometimes happens that one of the two produces less gum than the other, especially in soils that hold the middle ground between the two extremes. You can also graft on apricot trees, and the grafting succeeds quite well; but this is only done when you have too many apricot trees, or when you want to graft on a peach of which you are curious, and whose fruit you want to see quickly. You can also, if necessary, graft onto the peach tree itself; and this is a very good practice, when you see a few bad species in a young plant. Grafting is done in escutcheon in September on the young wood of the year, or of two years at most; and to do it with more rule and safety, it is necessary to cut down in March at twelve or fifteen inches of ground the trees which you want to graft; they push back soon after of the young wood, which you take care to disbud, so that you leave only four good branches well spaced, which take all the force, and on which you apply your escutcheons at ease at the end of the summer. They usually do very well, as long as the gum doesn't set in; and that's why you have to make sure there's no sap left in the trees when you graft. CHAPTER IV. The location and exposure required for peach trees, and how to prepare the soil. THE tender peach, generally speaking, does not succeed in full wind in any country. In our climate, the red spring winds, cold rains and white frosts scramble the blossom as well as the leaf, and it is very rare for any fruit to set. In hot countries, the sun's great ardor alters the fruit after it has been tied, and it does not hold on to the tail, so that it falls off in the slightest wind that beats it, and very few are left to ripen; even those that do escape are unwise, and there is neither the water nor the finesse that a more temperate air gives them. The same is not true of certain common species, such as those found near Fontainebleau and in all our southern provinces, which, being harder by nature and of very mediocre coarseness, are quite resistant to the ravages of time, and grow in vineyards and gardens without any cultivation: However, compared to the peaches we grow here, they are almost nothing more than peaches; they are undesirable: consequently, I distinguish from this class the Pavies, which are called Presses, Auberges or Mirlicotons in some provinces: this kind of fruit succeeds perfectly in the open air in all hot countries, and its taste is much stronger there than it is here; but this is its greatest merit, in addition to the hard flesh and little water. However, for want of anything better, we do very well to make do with it, and to grow it abundantly: cultivation is simple and easy, and requires no instruction. Back to our tender peaches. Experience has shown that they can only succeed in espaliers; and in this climate, there are only two suitable exposures: south and east. I know, however, that many private individuals plant them in the sunset position; they do succeed in a few places, but this is rare. I had planted two espaliers of one hundred and fifty toises each at this exposure, which I had the patience to cultivate for 8 years, without getting anything out of them. Tired of such ungrateful cultivation, I cut off their heads a few years ago; I grafted them all into Reine-Claude plums, which, in four years, have almost covered my wall, and give me perfectly beautiful fruit. I would advise anyone in the same situation to do the same, if their trees are suitable for grafting, and others never to plant any at this exposure, at least in this climate, and in cold soils like mine; for, I repeat, there are some situations where this exposure is fairly good for them, especially when the gardens are well sheltered, and I would add that in southern countries, it may even be better for them than the other two. It's up to each individual to know what his or her climate requires. There is one particular care to be taken in this respect, and that is never to plant this species of fruit at the foot of walls that back onto land, and which serve, as it were, as terrace walls; for, although the trees flower, the humidity that these walls communicate almost always ruins the flower, which sinks instead of knotting. And it's also quite common for these walls to be full of ants, which destroy the trees, especially as you can't maintain the plaster, which is soon loosened by humidity. Finally, the place of your trees having been decided, in relation to what I've just said, all that remains to be done is to put them in the ground; and this requires no less attention than the rest. I suppose you are making a new espalier: you must have begun by having your land broken up in fine weather, six feet from your wall, and three feet or at least two and a half feet down, if the clay or tuff has not been found closer to the surface, at which caz OF CULTIVATION you must stop where you find it; for the agsured loss of trees is when you want to pierce the tuff. and substitute good soil. Experience has convinced me of this, and the reason is that rainwater, having stayed in this sort of tuff or clay box that you form, rots all the roots after a few years, and the more you plant, the more die, because the soil itself becomes putrescent and has no more virtue; so you must give up putting any more in. However, don't be discouraged when you come across this kind of matter; as long as you have a foot and a half or two feet of good soil, your plum plants will do just fine, especially if it's new soil, or soil that has been occupied by other kinds of fruit; for I would observe that if your land has long been occupied by peach trees, the salts suitable for the production of this fruit are exhausted, and it is very rare for a new plantation to succeed. Here's the remedy. If you have new soil in the vicinity, have some brought in to replace the old; in other words, put in two and a half feet of prosoondeur, five or six feet wide; if you don't have any, or if you have to look for it far away, and the expense of transport is too great, have used manure brought in, and mix it well with a fork with your old soil, as you break it up, so that there is an equal quality from the bottom to the surface. Then, when you plant the trees, take care to keep the manure away from the root zone, where it could heat up and shrivel. I've tried both methods several times, and they worked, at least for a while, because this precaution keeps the trees in good shape for five or six years; but it's not equally certain that they'll continue to do well: often, at the end of this term, the roots, being obliged to work in the neighbouring soil, which they find sealed and worn, make useless efforts, and the tree only languishes. Use the same expedient when you want to replant old espaliers. The best way to get the most out of your land is to change species, i.e. to put stone fruit where there used to be stone fruit, and stone fruit where there used to be stone fruit. But as there are often few good exposures and you want to take advantage of them for fruit that necessarily requires them, stick to what I've just said. CHAPTER V. How to plant trees. Once you have prepared the ground, have your trees dressed, i.e. the roots shortened and cooled, so that the cut is on the ground, and have all the hair cut off; then have a hole made in proportion to their roots, and have loose earth thrown all around, which you will push in between the roots with your fingers; when they are fully covered, you lead the foot, i.e., you press the foot mediocrely all around, to reassure the soil, and take care that the graft is flush with the surface of your land. If you can't avoid either of these extremes, in the first case, you'll always plant them as I've just said, and in the second, you'll dispense with lead; But in both cases, you'll need to build a water channel, to better bind the soil, and thus get your trees working more quickly. Before doing so, be sure to wet the trees, cut them down to six or eight inches above the graft, and turn the cut against the wall. Hold the tree steady with one hand, while you cut it down with the other, so that the effort of the cutter doesn't disturb the foot. I'm assuming here that the soil is greasy and well soaked, because if it's healthy, all you have to do is press your feet down on the root, so that the tip of the shoe rests lightly on the buttress. The same precautions apply to stems and half-stems if they are planted between two plants. But as the graft is at the tip, there's no harm in burying them a little more or a little less. The main thing is to ensure that the heads are all on the same line, or, to put it another way, at the same distance from the coping on your wall. Now it's a question of setting the distances in which they should be planted. If your walls are only six, seven or eight feet high, I advise you to plant only low stems of fifteen in fifteen feet, especially if it's new soil; because in seven or eight years, your well-mannered trees will cover your wall with trellises. If it's old soil, and your walls are nine feet high, you can plant half-stems four feet apart, provided your low stems do well, you prune the underside of your half-stems from year to year, and after about six years, when your low stems have reached the middle of your wall's height, you remove your half-stems altogether, to give the others a chance to grow. In the latter case, to avoid losing anything, you can do what I've done, which is to graft them in March, into plums or apricots, below the old graft, if it's on a plum tree; and the following autumn, you pull them out and give them another place: this makes mid-wind trees, of very good quality, and which defend themselves better from the winds than the others, as they have more body. I have grafted many in this way, all of which have succeeded perfectly; and out of eighty that I replanted in one year, only one failed. Lift them and replant them with the precautions I recommend in Chapter XVI. If you like it better, instead of half-stems, you can plant a marcotte of grapes, either Muscat or Chasselas, which will give you fruit for a few years, and which you will pull up in the same way as the half-stems I have just mentioned, as soon as the lower stems need the space. But make sure that there is always at least a foot and a half between the winter pruning of your low stems and the lowest branches, either of the half-stems, or of the base of your stock; for, in order for the latter to rise easily, they must not be bothered by the shade of the others, and moreover you need space to trellis the new shoots. If your walls are ten to twelve feet or more high, then you must necessarily plant six-foot stems in between your low stems, otherwise you run the risk of not seeing your walls covered for a long time, if ever; there is even enough space for both to survive together without harming each other. For the sake of your trees, I now ask only two things: one, that your walls be in good condition; the other, that they be covered with trellises. For your walls to be in good condition, they must be well plastered on the tree side, and in good lime and sand mortar on the outside, if you want to spare the expense of plaster. In countries where there is none, and where mortar is all that is needed, it must be extremely fatty, i.e. two-fifths good freshly slaked lime on three-fifths water-free sand. If your mortar is thin, frosts will cause it to flake, and rains will soak it and make it run: and insects of all kinds, especially leeches and mice, settle in your walls on all sides, ruining your fruit as it ripens. Happy are those who can build them in brick, as they are in a large part of Italy! Once these walls are built, they can't be touched up again, and insects can't penetrate them; so our plasters and mortars only last a short time, and every ten to twelve years we have to redo the coping and sometimes the plaster. Secondly, your walls need to be lined with trellises. This requires a great deal of detail, because outside the Paris area they are rarely used, and only a small number of people even know how useful they are. Moreover, the expense is a monster, and those who would like to do it often don't know how to go about it in countries far from Paris. To make up for trellises, various expedients are used, all of which are rather poor: some use large iron wires, of which they make five or six runs along their walls, and which they stop from distance to distance with large nails; some even form meshes with this iron wire, as is done with wood. Both methods are equally damaging to the young branches of our peach trees: The wind, which blows them against the iron, causes them to bark, and the gum which soon follows, kills most of them; moreover, these strands of wire are too far apart for the branches to space themselves properly and attach themselves firmly; it is even impossible to secure certain large branches, which sometimes have to be moved from one place to another, and raised or lowered as necessary: I'm omitting other reasons which I could add. The rods that some people use are no more solid: a gust of wind sometimes blows down an entire tree, the branches break on one side, crumple on the other, and the fruit falls off; moreover, arranging all these rods is time-consuming work: You have to drive in pegs or nails to stop them; this spoils the plaster on your wall, and then forms a series of little holes, which are a haven for insects; moreover, you have to renew them often: it's a miserable expedient, all things considered. Some others, when building their walls, insert sheep bones into the masonry, about six inches apart, and protruding three or four times, on which they attach their branches; but, if I am allowed to judge by my eyes, it makes a hideous sight; moreover, one is often obliged to pile several branches on top of each other, and this becomes a very irregular trellis. The nail-pierced loquettes used 24 ** M ul 43 R 4 U 121 D"- 4 R 4 DU PECHER. 25 used by the people of Montreuil and the surrounding area are less dangerous in all respects, and I admit that you can spread out and place your branches as you like, with enough solidity and cleanliness; perhaps they even have some reason to claim that the fruit that is stuck against the wall ripens a little more, because of the reflection of the heat, than when there is a trellis in between. But what work is it not to have to prick three or four hundred nails on the expanse of a tree that is in its strength, and to have as many pieces of cloth to turn around the branches to embrace them? The people who are the most experienced at it, through the observations and comparisons I have made, could not do in less than two and a half hours what is done on trellis in an hour at most. This difference in time is considerable; moreover, the cost of nails and brackets, many of which have to be renewed every year, is a considerable expense. What's more, what is practiced in Montreuil would not be practicable elsewhere. These people have plaster on their doorstep; it costs them only four pounds a muid, while everywhere else it costs eight, ten, twelve, and even fifteen pounds; moreover, their land is full of small stones that they excavate themselves, and which they mix with plaster from the demolition of their old walls. These are the materials they use, and the cost is very low. When these walls are filled with joints and plastered with a good inch of plaster, they are easy to nail wherever they are spiked. X that they are driven in; but in ordinary walls, which are built of hard stone and mortar, often even built of earth, or at the very most re-created in plaster with an open stump, the nails can only encounter the stone, on which they cannot bite, or the mortar, which does not hold them securely. This method of trellising is therefore impractical, and the only good one I know of is trellising, without which I maintain that a tree can never be raised properly. Experience will convince us better than anything I could say: it is by the very operation that we recognize how useful it is, both for giving it the shape we want, and keeping it always full and without confusion, and for dispatching the work in the trellising season, and for preserving the fruit. CHAPTER VI. Description of Trellising, and how to do it. The UTILITY of trellising is not disputed by anyone; but the expense, it is said, is considerable, and one sometimes finds oneself unable to do it. I pity those for whom this expense is an object capable of depriving them of it, and I announce to them that whatever expedient they use to make up for the trellis, they will never see their espaliers regularly furnished, nor their fruit generally well conditioned. Let them restrict themselves to a lesser quantity, twenty toises in good condition will give them 27 more pleasure and more prosit than the ill-understood triple: I have no other advice to give them. But to be useful to everyone, I'm going to give some ways of saving money. It's not by the hands of a garden decorator that we need to pass here: the job of these kinds of people is to make money on the worker; nor by those of certain famous trellis makers who charge a lot for their reputation: for our trellis is not like a cradle where all we're looking for is ornament, and which therefore requires the hand of a skilled man. Trellising is the simplest of all operations: if you're smart enough, you can learn to do it in twenty-four hours, like someone who's been doing it all his life; and around Paris, there are twenty people for every one you can use. A bundle of ready-to-use trellis, which Paris merchants commonly sell for forty cents or more, can be bought for twenty-five or thirty cents at the wood sales where it is made, or from country merchants who have it made: the bundle contains twenty-five mounts, called strands, nine feet long, and when the strands are shorter, the number is proportionately greater; for there must always be two hundred and twenty-five effective feet in the bundle: this is the rule. But, both because of the assemblies that absorb six inches, and for the greater solidity of the work, there is a double advantage in taking all strands of nine feet, X 2 as much as merchants are willing; on which they are not always well treatable. In this respect, care must be taken to ensure that the wood is not greasy, because then it is brittle, and much is lost in dressing. To be of good quality, it must be made from young poles, in which case it is white, straight and knotless, or at least not very. A bundle of trellis is about 2 toises quarrées of work, counting the mesh of eight inches by seven, as it should be; so that half a bundle per toise enters, which, at the price marked below, is worth 15 sous, ci. o l. 15s. There are four hooks per toise, which sell for 5 to 6 sous a dozen, for this... . . . . . . . ........ Iron sil comes in, which costs to s. a pound, for... ... ............ The custom of each toise is commonly paid............... And to paint the trellis with oil, two coats in wood color... 2 2 6 10 Total.....1 1. 14s. Those who wish to have it painted green, must count on triple the expense; but this no longer concerns the utility of the trees, which is our object. It seems to me that this expense should not be so excessive as to be unattainable, since the account I have just given shows that for twenty-five pistols a wall one hundred toises long and nine feet high can be lined. I will now describe these trellises for those who have never seen them, so that they can have them made wherever they are to be found. The wood, as I've just said, should be young chestnut poles, ten to twelve years old; failing that, oak heartwood can be used, which I value no less; but the former is easier to work, and is quite commonly found everywhere. We choose the longest, straightest poles in the coppice, which have not been eaten away by wild cattle, which can damage them badly in some places. Any size is good, as they can be split in two or four if necessary. Each strand, planed and quarried, should be one inch or fourteen lines wide, and nine or ten lines thick; and it is up to the workman who has his wood in front of him, to take care of the thickness of his pole before putting the instrument on it, so that it has the thickness I have just mentioned. He shapes it with the plane, helped by an easel that stops it at one end, while he works on the other. However careful you are in choosing straight poles, it almost always happens that there is still some curved part in the length: but this defect is corrected by a second operaX5 tion. When you're ready to put your wood to work - and this is called dressing the trellis, i.e. straightening it - you hold your wood with your left hand, resting it on a log, and under the curved parts you make a half-wood slanting notch with the sickle; Then, by pressing the hand gently at some distance from the notch, while the strand is held a little above by a sort of iron hinge driven into the end of the trestle, the wood is made to make a little cry which straightens it; and as many curved parts there are, as many notches must be made, until it is straight, and the eye is satisfied. Now that the wood has been prepared, it's time to use it, and here's how. The first thing to do is to calculate how many running laths you'll need to line your wall, according to the mesh you want to make. I assume nine by eight inches, including the wood thicknesses. On this foot, for example, you'll find that a nine-foot wall needs twelve; because nine multiplied by twelve makes one hundred and eight, which is the same height as nine feet reduced to inches. These divisions are marked on the wall, with black chalk or charcoal, approximately from eight toises to eight toises. When all the divisions are marked, two iron pegs are driven into the wall, from one division to the other, on the lines where the hooks are to be set; and a rope is stretched from one peg to the other, to regulate the position of the hooks. To make this clearer, I first assume that three rows of hooks are needed to stop the trellis, the first at the top penultimate stitch, the second at the bottom penultimate dernic̀re, and the third in the middle of these two stitches, i.e., at the sixth or seventh. This arrangement made, we seal the hooks in a chessboard, from three to three feet, with plaster and a few pieces of pipe. Mortar in its present state would be of no value, unless you give it time to dry thoroughly before starting work. When you're in a hurry and don't have any plaster, you can use large hardwood dowels, which you force into the stone joints; and in these dowels you prick the hooks, which in this case should be four to five inches long, instead of the plaster hooks, which should be six to seven inches long, and split at the end into a swallowtail, so that they are better retained in the wall. But both must be angled out by about an inch, to retain the laths. This explanation is only for those who know nothing about it. The hooks must protrude one inch above the wall level, to receive the battens that fit into them, and which are fastened to them with a strand of iron silk, for greater solidity; they must also be flat, and one and a half lines thick by four or five lines wide. As for those intended for wood, they should be round or square, roughly like a large nail, but with the elbow flattened. Once the hooks have been placed along the entire length of X4 your wall, you first lay your three rows of laths on top (I use the terms laths, strands and trellises interchangeably, which are all synonymous here). We must not omit a third operation that the laths require, which is to sharpen them at the ends, i.e., to reduce the thickness of the wood to six inches from the end, so that it comes to nothing (this term is dedicated to what it expresses): we understand that this circumstance is necessary to couple the laths, which by this means are more easily bound together with two meshes of wire. With these three rows of laths in place, the hardest part of the job is done. All that remains to be done is to divide the spaces between the upright strands, which, in the above supposition, should be eight by eight inches, including the thickness of the wood. This division is marked like the first with black chalk, on the lowest and highest of these three rows of laths, taking care to throw a plumb line from distance to distance, so that they are all plumb; and it's especially important to do this at the beginning. To make it easier to pass the superfluous running battens behind the mounts, first lay only one mound, about six feet by six feet; stop the nine running battens on it with lé fil de fer, which remain to be placed of the twelve that should be there, as I said. When they are all in place, the rest of the mounts are put in place, and the whole thing is tied together with the wire. I don't think anyone in any country is unaware of how this is done. I'll just say that you have to tighten the ties as much as you can, with the pliers in your right hand, while with your left you hold the wire, which you always leave in a ball while you use it; then, with a little turn of your hand, you break it between the pliers themselves, when you've made three or four turns back and forth. It should be noted that the finer the wire, the shorter it runs, i.e. the more work it does and the better it does, provided it can withstand the work. To do this, it must be burned, and burned properly, because when it is burned too much or too little, it breaks under the pliers. The time it should remain in the fire is about a quarter of an hour. To return to the trellis, I must not forget that, whether the walls are level or sloping, the mounts must always be plumb, and the running battens placed as I have indicated above. When the wall is on a slope, take care to set the hooks so that the lines of the chalk line are closer together, so that the battens follow the slope of the wall. If it happens that a wall is higher in one part than in another, so that twelve battens are needed at one end, and ten are sufficient at the other, the mesh size must not be changed; and the irregularity of the running battens must fall to the bottom of the wall, and not under the coping. X5 Once the trellis is in place, all that remains is to paint it. Now, as painting is done everywhere, I will only observe in this respect that it is necessary to take a good weather, and not to spare in the color the litarge or the spirit of turpentine, so that it dries promptly; because, when it comes to rain before it is dry, the rain makes it run. If you follow this description exactly, you can, with a little intelligence, obtain the satisfaction of decorating your walls with trellises in any country; otherwise, I repeat, you can never expect real satisfaction from your espaliers, and all the less so, as there are few gardeners in a position to make up by their industry for the lack of conveniences. CHAPTER VII. Pruning. Peach trees are commonly pruned when they are in bloom; some even wait until they have deflowered, and the fruit is knotted, based on the fact that they are more sure of what they leave; but this last usage is subject to many inconveniences. When you start pruning so late, assuming your trees are in full bloom, how many of them don't fall off, no matter how skilful you are? If, on the other hand, they are more advanced, what harm are you doing to them? The sap that would have fed the bottom of your branches, if they had been stopped early, has all gone to the ends; it's only in these ends that the fruit has stopped, and to keep it, you are obliged to leave your branches excessively long, which ruins your trees, or you have to abandon the fruit to keep the tree: a cruel alternative for amateurs. In my first assumption, that the tree is only in full bloom, what constraint is there to trellis it after pruning? What damage would you do to the flowers, and how much more time would you spend on pruning than you would if you had done it sooner? What's more, the eyes that start to grow at the same time as the flowers bloom, partly hide your work, and you can no longer get your pruning close to the eye; so that as many pruned branches produce as many snags to remove for the following year. Your ties, moreover, can be neither so clean nor so well secured, because of the embarrassment caused by the multitude of these flowers. You can no longer see the defects in the wood, if any, nor the old snags that need to be brought together: in short, you work haphazardly. I experienced all these inconveniences the first few years I wanted to work by myself, following the general method. But having subsequently tried the effect of pruning a few trees, the fear I had, according to popular opinion, that they would get too far ahead, and that the spring frosts would ruin the blossom, soon vanished. These trees, which I pruned at the end of January and February X6, were no further advanced than the others I pruned in April: neither the flower nor the wood had suffered. The following year, I continued to prune a greater number with the same success; and in the third year, I pruned them all in the same months, and have continued to do so for many years with complete satisfaction. I therefore advise everyone to do the same: despite the public prejudice that may arise against this novelty, you will soon see that the work is done better, faster and more rapidly. We can see clearly at least in everything we do, and we can distinguish very well in these months between the good flowers and the bad ones; for the dry weather has begun to make the good ones swell, unless the winter has been very long and very hard, in which case, we must postpone it a little later. For it is important, as I have just observed, to be able to distinguish the good buds from the bad ones, and for this purpose, the sap must have acted on the good ones; I mean, they must have swollen to a certain extent. It will be argued that winter has not yet passed in January or February; that such and such a bud may be good then, and perish in the following months; and that we then have no resource left to skirt a few branches in favor of fruit. I agree with this fact: but I would reply that if bad winds blight the blossom in March and April, trees that are not pruned are no more exempt than others. What's more, as four times as many buds are left as could be left if everything went well, I have three quarters to lose through the uncertain accidents I am confronted with before making an actual loss. So I don't run much of a risk: but if it were even more real, the advantages I find in the method I've adopted would make me overlook it. And what's the point of having an abundance of knotting fruit, when you have to sacrifice everything that passes the tree's reach in May or June? Last but not least, my fruit, once tied, is much more profitable than that of trees that are cut in full bloom; and the reason is obvious. The sap has dissipated much less, because it doesn't have to nourish the quantity of branches that are pruned so late, and is carried much more abundantly in the tree's nourishing fibers. I've experienced all of the above, and since I've been following this practice, I've never had any problems. I have always had an abundance of fruit, except in the year 1741, which was generally so fatal to all fruit. Such a complete experience should reassure against all the reasoning of people attached to their prejudices, and warned against all the roads they do not know. I must not omit a circumstance that is more important than you might think: for the sake of the work, and to avoid breaking branches, you should never start pruning a tree until it is completely untrimmed, and well cleaned of all the old rushes and wicker, and the dry leaves that often gather between the wall and the trellis. Apart from the fact that cleanliness is detrimental, it destroys the retreats of many insects that hide there and damage your fruit, especially purple peaches, which limas and limacons are so fond of. This brings me to the description of pruning. The peach trees you have to prune are young or old, vigorous or languid: it's the state of your subject that must regulate your operation; the work it has done tells you what you have to do: in two words, you have to treat it according to its strength and need. I'll start with this year's new plants. If they have only grown weak branches, you will reduce them to two or four well placed on the sides, and equal in strength, which you will prune to five or six inches. If there is a small one with fruit in the middle, you can leave it; but if it is of the same strength as those on the sides, remove it; for, as the middle always fills out enough, you should only be concerned with the sides, etc. It's on the bend you give your tree in the first and second years, that everything depends for the following ones. If your trees have grown two strong, well-placed branches, one on the right and the other on the left, keep them and prune them back to eight or ten inches; at the same time, you can leave a few small twigs here and there for fruit. The method of many gardeners, who regard these kinds of branches as leeches to which they have sworn to do no harm, is to remove them without mercy, and to fall back on the small ones: as for me, experience has taught me not to be always so severe. It has happened to me, when pruning certain stumpy trees, to cut off as many of these branches, which starve the others, as were present, and to be seven or eight years without being able to give them any shape, and without drawing any fruit from them. These same trees then went from one end to the other, and fell into languor. This phenomenon, I confess, has always been incomprehensible to me: but the fact is certain, and the cause is one of the secrets of vegetation, which I do not undertake to investigate. However, it may be conjectured that the roots, which operate and help each other with the branches, being stopped in their action by the repeated violence done to them, tire, so to speak, of acting uselessly, and that the sap, having withdrawn from the branches, is lost in some way in the roots, which likewise remain without action, as soon as the head no longer acts. I have found, on the contrary, that by following the inclination of these kinds of trees, and by pruning the strong branches with a certain amount of care, they soon form a beautiful tree, which after two or three years grows no more. When, in the second and third years, they continued to grow, I pruned not only on the large branches, but also on the small ones, born from those in the same year, to give more diversion to the sap. By doing this, my trees moderated themselves, and from these branches, which were vicious in their origin, a number of good ones sprang up, which quickly covered my wall, and yielded abundantly. The only disadvantage I've sometimes seen resulting from this practice, and which you have to watch out for, is that as the sap is always carried to the ends, the tree doesn't become bare at the bottom: but when you are careful to pinch off the new shoots in May, and to take a lot of pressure off the head of the trellis, you prevent this disadvantage, and the bottom is maintained full. If there is only one of these strong branches on your young trees, this is another case: you must remove it wherever it is; if there are two, one on the right or left, and the other in the middle, you must also cut them off, as the weak side will perish, and your tree will never take shape; you must also then throw your pruning onto the small branches, giving no more load on one side than on the other. I would observe in passing that you would never see these kinds of badly placed branches, if in the month of May you took care to do what I advise in the following chapter; for by unloading the strongest side, the sap would be rejected from the other; but as there are few people who want to take the trouble, it is a question of giving a means of repairing the defect of this operation, when you have missed it. That's how you should behave for the first year. Let's move on to the second through to the sixth. I've explained what to do with trees that get carried away by wood, and that need to be loaded to moderate their fire and force them to set fruit. As for those that grow only moderately, treat them in proportion, and in all things, keep the middle short, and don't load one side more than the other. It very often happens that one side is stronger than the other; you must not let yourself be seduced by the lure of the fruit it promises. Withdraw everything that exceeds the strength of the other, and don't tolerate any branch that is stronger on one side than on the other. As a fundamental rule, the whole economy of your tree should be based on two or four good branches of equal strength, which should be like the mothers of all the others: it's on these that you should take particular care to space them evenly, and allow them as much scope as they can bear. You can give them up to twelve or sixteen inches in height, when they are of reasonable strength. As for the medium-sized buds, leave them in proportion to the vigor of the tree, and prune them from six to eight inches, depending on the place they occupy, and whether the flowering buds are more or less distant from the foot of the branch; for they are often very far from it, especially in certain species; and, as the fruit is our main object, we can sometimes, in its favor, deviate a little from the rules, and prune longer. In this respect, it should be noted that good flowering buds are only those that are double, and have a wood eye between them: those that are single, albeit with a wood eye, flower well, but do not knot, or at least rarely: however, try not to let yourself be tempted too much by the fruit: too much load would first throw confusion into your trees, and then ruin the middle and bottom. With regard to small branches, both those called rags, and the twigs so respected by most gardeners for their fruit, I eliminate them as long as I have better ones, I don't mean big ones, but medium ones, it being unquestionable that a branch with a certain body feeds its fruit better than a weak one, and that it also stops on it, despite the common prejudice that small ones hold it better, as those who want to pay attention will remain convinced. It is therefore only in the absence of the former that I retain the latter: there are, however, exceptions to be made in this respect. 1°. If your tree is extraordinarily vigorous, and needs to be fatigued by the load in order to stop it, you should leave a good portion of these twigs. 2°. If your middle branches don't have flower buds below the right size, and you're curious to have some fruit, you should also leave some of the shortest and most fruit-bearing branches. Besides, I don't understand certain short branches under the name of twigs, which it would be more appropriate to call bunches, since they are usually only one or two inches long. These are precious, and usually yield very fine fruit: I am therefore careful not to detach myself from them. The use I sometimes make of these small branches, which I condemn, is to cut them off at the first eye when they are placed in a spot where I foresee a gap forming. If one of these branches is pruned in this way, it may produce a better one the following year; and if it is still useless to me, even though it is good, I always prune it back to one eye until it is needed: these kinds of branches are resources that it is important to save in order to maintain a tree that is always full, and to make up for branches that have worn out in bearing fruit; this should be done equally on medium-sized branches as on the small ones I am dealing with here. In the case of trees that grow only weakly, it is necessary to examine whether the evil is in the root; in this case, it must be uprooted: but if the root is good, it must be preserved, and smoked in the way I indicate in Chapter XVIII. Manure often restores vigor to these kinds of trees; but as long as they languish, they should be pruned very short and on the best branches. This is my method for managing peach trees during their first few years; to which I'll add, so as not to omit anything, that great care must be taken to trellis them properly after pruning. .1e. As the inclination of trees is always to grow upwards, it is essential to keep the lower part of the tree trimmed, as I have observed, and this is achieved in two ways: firstly, by the effect of good pruning, which I have discussed at some length; secondly, by taking care to bend or constrain the branches on the sides, when training them, and to space them evenly. At the same time, care must be taken to ensure that they don't take on any bad bends; and, if the damage has already been done, this defect must be corrected by removing branches that are piled on top of each other, and those that have been crossed at the wrong time, or that are curved. These are straightened with the help of wickerwork and chopsticks, and skill alone must guide these small operations, which I won't go into here. 2°. Another point to bear in mind when trellising branches is to make sure that the eye at the end of the branch is always turned so that the branch that is to grow from it naturally takes its course over the trellis, and not behind it: this can be seen without a great deal of study; and in this case, with a small rod supported and attached to the trellis if necessary, you can easily make the branches that seek the wall return to the front. To these observations, I would add that wicker should not be tightened too much, because it often happens that being too tight, it cuts the branch when it grows; you should also avoid passing your wicker over the branch's eyes when tying it: a little skill prevents these little faults, which are of no great consequence, but which nevertheless have their disadvantages. As there are often pruned branches that cannot reach the trellis, we use small sticks that are interwoven into the trellis, where we pass a piece of wicker at the end, in the form of a noose, which is attached to the trellis by the other end, and which secures the branch: this is all the more necessary, as when fruit comes to these branches, and they are not supported, it often carries the branch away. These last observations apply to peach trees of all ages. Now back to pruning. I've indicated everything that's appropriate for first-age trees: let's move on to second- and third-age trees. I call second-age peach trees those that are in their full strength; third-age trees are those that are a little on the decline: these cannot be too sparingly cared for; they must be pruned short and only on the best branches: the small ones must not be kept at all, because on old subjects, they give only etic fruit. When, by chance, a vigorous branch emerges from one of these old trees and is capable of renewing the tree, it should be treated with this in mind, and carefully preserved to replace the old branches that are gradually being destroyed; but if it emerges from an old branch, it should be removed. I have nothing more to say about these old trees, except that they should only be cared for when, despite their old age, they are still bearing good fruit; for as soon as this condition is lacking, they must be uprooted. As for the trees of the second age, which are in all their strength, and which load abundantly, as they are those which make our wealth, they deserve the most particular attention. Most gardeners who work without principles, without reasoning, and without concern for the future, manage them in such a way that they are soon ruined. This is why it is generally said that peach trees only have a life span of twelve to fifteen years. I've got a few that are over forty years old, and they're still going strong. They were not under my care for the first twenty years, but they have had the advantage of being well looked after, and since they have been in my hands, I have looked after them so well that they are still bearing good fruit. I have quite a few others that I planted 15 to 18 years ago, which are undoubtedly of great beauty, and which I still only regard as children; so that if I could count on thirty years of life, and if I could always manage them myself, I would be sure that they would still survive all that time. To convince yourself of this, all you have to do is visit the Montreuil plantations: you'll see trees of the age I'm talking about in the homes of certain individuals who have no doubt taken better care of them than is usually the case in this very place, where trees are forced to produce, without bothering about their duration, because the tenants of the land are in a hurry to enjoy it. I have seen trees up to today's toises in length, equally healthy and vigorous throughout, and which I have judged to be fifty years old. So let's get rid of the prejudice that this tree doesn't last: it's bad behavior that makes it perish when it doesn't live about as long as another fruit tree. However, in saying this, I'm assuming that it's in a good soil, and that no accidents happen to it: for, despite good conduct, if the soil is worthless, if it's infested with gum, if the bug or the ant attacks it violently, it must perish. I have the advantage, along with many others, of being free from all these scourges: they should not therefore be considered as inseparably attached to the species, and consequently all care should be given to keep it alive. Pruning is the operation that contributes most to their longevity. This is the art of pruning, which may appear simple, but which is not without its difficulties, given the choice of branches, the work the tree has done, the position of flowering buds, the type of fruit, and many other circumstances on which a few rules can be based. I'm not going to talk about certain cases that can only be decided in relation to the object in question, and which can only be taught by practice. Everyone has his own method, and prunes according to his own ideas. Some prune short on all branches, others lengthen the branches they intend to bear fruit, and leave spurs to give them wood the following year. Mine is quite different, and it's only after much study and experience that I stick to it as the best. I assume that the tree I'm dealing with has been managed in its youth more or less according to my principles, and that it is regularly well stocked; for, if it is defective in any part, it must be treated according to its needs. Starting from my assumption, and finding my tree in good condition, after it has been stripped, I like to make a search for the worn branches, which it is easy to see from their thinness, and the bad sprays they have made, I cut off the worn branch as far as the large branch from which it comes, unless in its extent it has grown some good branch, on which I gully it, if there is nothing in the vicinity to fill the place. I then move on to the current year's branches, and remove any large ones that have grown. By large branches, I mean all those exceeding the average size; I also remove all the small ones, unless one is needed to fill a gap, or to serve as a resource; in which case I trim it to about the thickness of an ecu, as I indicated above. I always exclude the small ones I've mentioned. Once this first retrenchment has been made, I'm left with branches that are equal in strength: I can then see my work clearly, and at last I only have one reform to make in terms of quantity, and here is my rule for that. I leave only one of all those that have grown on the branch I pruned the previous year, and it's the lowest one I leave, because it's always good, thanks to the precautions I took at the time of the pruning. Those who have not done so, will choose the best of the lowest. After this second reform, I move on to the third, which is pruning these branches. I then examine whether my tree was heavily loaded the previous year, and what species it is; depending on these two cases, I shorten or lengthen my pruning. If my tree has loaded a lot, I spare it; and if it's, for example, a Madelaine or a Violet, as these trees are more vigorous than the others, I give them more load: but if my tree is of any other species, and has not been tired of the load, I lengthen my pruning to eight inches, if the place allows, but if I find myself constricted, and if I have nothing below to replace what is exhausted, I keep my pruning short, and give it only three or four inches. It is common to find, by the different arrangement of the places, that half of my branches are lined, and that the other is kept short. In this way, I keep my tree full, and I don't tire it. I said that I never leave more than one branch of all those that came on the branch pruned the previous year. However, there are cases (such as that of a neighboring branch that the gum Tome II. Y will have caused it to perish) where, when I find two good branches equal in strength that have come from the bottom of this pruned branch, and I have a large space to fill, I prune both to five to six inches. If I don't have a definite need, and I'm only afraid that the space will become bare after my branch is exhausted, I give the above pruning to the higher one, and cut back the lower one on its first eye, in order to ensure a good branch for the following year. As far as the tips are concerned, I'm governed by the space I have. If my tree completely covers its wall, I prune so that there is always a good foot's distance between the end of my pruned branch and the edge of the coping, to be able to support the new branches, to which end I cut off everything that has borne fruit during the year, and replace it with new branches, which the underside must provide. M. de la Quintinie, in dealing with this subject, recommends always leaving three feet of distance: I confess that this seems outrageous to me, as walls are too precious to individuals to leave such a large space cu worthless. It may be objected that, by my method of pruning only one fruiting branch, and keeping none for wood, I expose myself to having no good wood left for subsequent years.
