People will say that these branches, which are exhausted with fruit, only grow thin branches between them, not very capable of bearing fruit; they perish in the end, and consequently your tree is thinning out on all sides. This objection is specious, because in the common order of pruning, most of the fruiting branches are ruined under the load, and are no longer capable of any function, either for wood or for fruit, so that we are obliged to cut off some of them at the end of the same year, and the others at the second or third; but they don't have quite the same fate with me, and don't perish until very late, as I'm going to demonstrate. When you leave two branches, one intended solely for fruit, the other for wood, you naturally feel that the sap is obliged to divide between the two, and that the woody branch, which has no fruit to nourish, benefits much more than the other: Moreover, some of my branches are kept short, as I have just observed, and I spare myself resources wherever I see the slightest danger of emptiness. So I don't run the risks that might frighten people; but above all I have experience, which will convince, as I have, all those who try my method. The method used in Montreuil is similar in some respects to mine, in that they leave few branches, and cut back all the small ones; but it differs in that they load their trees extraordinaiY 2 rement, especially in the first years; they lengthen their main branches up to two and two-and-a-half feet, and at the same time prune a good proportion of the small ones that have grown between their leaves. It's true that the quality of their soil in some way authorizes this practice; and besides, they don't bother about the duration of the tree, nor about its beautiful shape: their whole object is to gather quickly and abundantly; so that the bottom of their wall, after the first four years, is almost always bare: a defect that a curious person must not suffer. But if someone, following their example, wanted to pass over this wall to enjoy a quick harvest, he would certainly fail, unless he came across a vein of earth perfectly similar to theirs. This circumstance may occur, but it is so rare that of all those of my acquaintance who have attempted this practice, not one has succeeded. In a word, I can say, in general, that it is more admirable than imitable. CHAPTER VIII. De l'Ebourgeonnement. In peach tree cultivation, disbudding is the most important operation, and yet the most neglected. When I say most important, I'm excluding pruning, which is a necessity. The usefulness of disbudding lies in the fact that it facilitates all the other operations, and gives the fruit these three advantages: safety, beauty and goodness. M. de la Quintinie has given some very judicious rules on this subject, but he hasn't put his money where his mouth is, and he's even a little vague on the subject at times. I must, however, do him the justice to say that I have benefited greatly from his insights in the remarks I have made based on him, and that his observations have led me to make new ones, which will be of benefit to others after me. Disbudding has a large number of equally interesting objects, and I shall follow them in order. The word "disbudding" carries its meaning with it. Disbudding means removing buds. Nothing could be easier on the face of it; but to remove them properly and with choice requires a perfect understanding of the subject. The best time for disbudding is May, when the buds are sufficiently formed to fix our choice, and are still tender enough to be easily removed by thumb action alone, without the aid of any instrument. Disbudding is usually confused with a second operation, called pinching, and a third, called arresting, since all three are performed together. It's enough to have seen a peach tree once in your life, to know that in spring it's teeming with branches that necessarily need to be thinned out. Now, disbudding means making this retrenchment early on, which should be done at the first Y3 trellising, if it hasn't already been done; but there is an infinite difference in doing it earlier. All peach tree work, as I have already said, can be reduced to two objectives: the tree's beautiful appearance, and an honest yield of good fruit. With this in mind, it must be relieved of everything that is either useless or harmful: I call useless everything that is misplaced, though good; and harmful, everything that is bad in itself. It's these two vices that need to be cut out, and it's particularly on the pruned branches of the year that they are found. Now, as these branches, according to my method, are for the most part seven to eight inches long, and sometimes more, there are as many as eight and ten eyes in this expanse, which are as many branches. This quantity of branches would not feed equally well, and would necessarily form a confusion. I therefore reduce them, according to my place, to two or three, and I choose one or two of the lowest, opposite each other, with the one at the end, in case there is fruit attached, and there is none lower down. If he has tied only at the bottom, or if he has tied all over my brancha, where the number of eyes may be eight or ten; in either case, I cut my branch down to about half, and keep only three or four fruits as far apart as possible, of course I always leave a good branch of the new arrivals at the end; At the same time, I pinch off with my fingernail, to the thickness of two ecus, the surplus branches holding the fruit (which is called stopping), and if there are others without fruit next to them, I cut them off completely, always limiting myself to two or three. If my branch has not retained any fruit, I cut it back to the second of those it has grown, that is, I keep only the two lowest, unless the great vigor of the tree obliges me to leave more to consume and divide the sap. With regard to the branches I have pruned short, I keep only two of the new additions, the highest and the lowest on the opposite side; I remove the others which have no fruit on their side, or I pinch them, if there is fruit; and, when it would not have retained any, I always treat it more or less the same way; sometimes, however, I leave only one of these new branches, and that is the lowest. If a large branch arises, either from my pruned branches of the year, or from the body of my tree itself, I consult its need and vigor. If it is harmful to its neighbors, and useless to me, I cut it off; but if it is useful, either to fill a gap, or to prevent it, or to consume the sap that is too abundant, I pinch it off at four or five leaves; and as from each leaf a new branch comes out, I soon find four or five mediocre ones, Y 4 F from the number of which I choose at the first trellising the one that suits me best. As for the small ones that may emerge from the old wood, I remove them, unless they are needed to fill a gap, or I see them as a resource for the future. But I do keep those little bouquets I mentioned in the previous chapter, wherever they come from. You need to pay close attention to the base of your tree; often branches come out of it that are very useful for replacing others that are beginning to wear out: you need to keep them carefully, and if they grow very strong, pinch them off at five or six eyes. As for the greedy branches, which can be identified, as I said, by their green color, spotted with red spots, and by their size, you must carefully remove them wherever they are, unless they are extremely needed to repair a gap, and you have no other resource; but in this case, they must be pinched back and even re-pinched a second time in June. It often happens that two or three branches grow together from the same eye, especially at the end of your pruning: in this case, leave only one, and choose the one in the best position. When you come across twin peaches, if one is smaller than the other, as is usually the case, you must skillfully detach the smaller one, without shaking the other: but if they are of equal size, remove them, or leave them; it's all one, they'll never work out. These are just about all the attentions that disbudding requires. Now it's time to show how useful it can be. First of all, it's easy to understand that this timely pruning infinitely strengthens what's left, both the fruit and the branches. As they go from mediocre food to good, it's only natural that they should do better. What's more, the branch you cut off, which was poorly placed, and which you would have had to cut off at the first trellising, if you hadn't done so then, was often the one best favored by the sap, and would have made the well-placed branch suffer. You have another advantage in this retrenchment, which is that you detach these branches cleanly, instead of cutting them with the serpette, when you are waiting for the trellising, the confusion in which you are working means that you would not be able to approach your cut, and your branches become covered with snags which are unpleasant to the eye, and which take up a lot of your time when you have to bring them closer for winter pruning, or which make a very ugly effect if you neglect to remove them; In fact, it almost always happens that these snags, left on the trellis, immediately sprout two or three bad shoots that uselessly consume sap, cause confusion and harm the fruit. It also happens that, when cutting off all these superfluous and misplaced branches, the jarring effect of the force of your serpette, and the embarrassment of the worker who cannot see his work, cause part of the fruit to fall off, which must then be greatly regretted, because it has, in a way, passed all the risks. What can I say! these much-desired fruits would be, so to speak, in complete safety, if disbudding had not been neglected. But what happens then? The fruits, hidden, smothered and as if buried in this thick foliage, become tender, as their whiter-than-green color visibly indicates; and suddenly breathing the fresh air, combined with the scorching rays of the sun, which is new to them, they wither for the most part, and fall off: For it should be noted that as the sap is always carried to the extremities, and that moreover it is only these extremities, by which I mean the ends of the branches, that enjoy the benefit of the air and the sun, most of the branches that are suffocated at the bottom are stripped of their leaves, which dry up to a certain distance, and can no longer, therefore, cover the fruit, or cover it only imperfectly. All this does not happen when, on the one hand, these branches have been exposed to the air throughout, and, on the other, you have accustomed your fruit from youth to the small insults of the air and the rays of the sun; they harden in this situation, and once they have reached the size of a walnut, it is very rare for them to fall off, when there are not more of them than the tree can feed. The same operation I'm talking about also has another benefit for the fruit: insects, especially snails, don't try to eat it when it's a little uncovered, as they do when it's smothered under the tenderizing leaves. It's also easy to get some of the fruit off the ground when it's caught between the wicker and the trellis, or between the trellis and the wall. In the first case, all you have to do is let go of the ties, and in the second, pull them out; but if you wait until the trellis is up, the fruit, which has by then reached more than half its size, is counterfeited without remedy. With regard to the branches, it was easy for you to remove those that slipped in at the wrong time from behind the trellis; instead, if you had waited until the trellising, these branches, which are sometimes very important, would have become hard and strong, and broken off when you tried to remove them. This is a good opportunity to talk about Cloaca, but as not everyone understands this term, I'll explain it first. This disease, very common in this climate, is the result of a bad wind, which causes the tree's leaves to curl up. They thicken and become yellow, red and scabby, which is unpleasant to look at, and very harmful to the fruit, in that they uselessly absorb all the sap at the expense of the fruit. When your trees are affected by this blister, Y6 you must not only remove all the bad leaves, but also cut off the branches that are infected with it, which form a sort of hideous tuft. This operation allows the sap to grow new branches underneath, which are also good for the following year. Note, however, that if your tree is completely infected, as sometimes happens, and there are no healthy leaves left to cover the fruit, you must leave some of the bad ones behind, to shelter it until good ones grow again. If you neglect this operation, all these infected leaves consume the sap, and prevent it from seeking other outlets; they then dry out and fall: your fruit, which is then slender, is uncovered; the sun catches it, it withers, and it falls to such an extent that sometimes no watch remains on the tree. The ant and the aphid sometimes cause the same disorder to leaves and branches; in this case, we must use the expedient I have just given; but these insects usually insist on poisoning all the eyes of the branches, and the sap cannot take over: we will see in Chapter XII the remedy that can be applied. If the gum has attacked a branch, you cut it back an inch below the damage, and prevent it from perishing altogether: by cutting it off, it immediately regrows one or two branches below it, and the damage is then of little consequence. The final advantage of disbudding is that the time spent on this operation is amply repaid when it comes to trellising, since there is almost nothing left to cut, and no need to reflect on your work: Each branch that has been left indicates to the worker the place he should give it; it goes there, so to speak, of its own accord, and all he has to do is attach it; which is such a great advance, that I have more than once found that it took no longer to trellis three disbudded trees, than one that had not been disbudded. However, even if this operation has been carried out with the utmost precision, it is still necessary to check the espaliers every eight days, either to remove from under the trellis any branches that slip back in, or to destroy more and more snails and other insects (and it's always after some rain, or after the morning dew, that you find them at work) or to observe the ravages of gum, and remedy them, which is a matter of a few hours, which are very usefully employed. When you have all the time in the world, it's even better to divide everything I've just said about this operation into two stages, and here's how I do it: at the end of April, I start by removing all the misplaced buds, i.e. those on the front and back of my branches, and at the end of May, when the fruit has stopped, I do the rest: everyone can do as much as their leisure allows. The rule I've laid down for doing this disbudding properly does, however, require some distinctions in relation to the different ages of the trees. For those planted this year, I give them a first disbudding at the end of April; in other words, I remove all the buds on the front and back of the stem, leaving only those on the sides; and when one side grows much more than the other, I unload that side to release the sap on the other, and at the end of the following May, I do a second review; and when I find a branch much stronger than the others, I cut it off or pinch it. I do more or less the same thing for the first three or four years, with the difference that if the tree is vigorous, I unload much less at disbudding, the big braches that I left at the first pruning, than the badly placed shoots, keeping everything that comes on the sides, as much as I find room to store it. As for old trees, I remove not only all that is badly placed, but also all that is weak to a certain point, to strengthen the rest. I limit myself to a small number of the best branches, and leave only a small quantity of fruit. I also wait to disbud those after all the others, because their growth is later. I have nothing special to say about trees that are in their full strength: you will find at the beginning of this Chapter, everything that needs to be observed with regard to them. CHAPTER IX. The first trellising. Peach trees are first trellised in June, a little earlier or a little later, depending on how far advanced the year is. Trellising means tying new branches neatly and in order to the trellis. If you have not neglected disbudding, this is the simplest and easiest of all operations: as I have already said, there is almost nothing left to cut. No more reasoning about the choice of branches, no more cutting back on fruit, or very little; no more subjection of any kind: all you have to do is tie up what you find. The whole science consists in spacing the branches well, spreading them out, and making them take the turn they require, to form a pleasing fullness throughout the tree. As for the rest, care must be taken to keep the fruit covered by the leaves as much as possible, so that it can grow bigger and better. You must also be careful never to cross the branches without extreme necessity. The defect of emptiness is the biggest one in the peach tree: this reason alone may excuse it, when it cannot be filled otherwise; for a small evil is to be preferred to a great one. One or two of the small branches born between the leaves of the current year's branches should be kept as far as possible, and it is the lowest ones that should be chosen; for it is often more appropriate the following year to prune on these small branches than on the mother, which its excessive strength, or lack of flower buds, forces to be cut back; and when the tree gets too carried away from the top, the strongest branches should then be cut back on these small ones, to fortify the rest. Small green marsh rush is the best for trellising: Marseille rush, though soaked in hot water, is too hard, and bruises young branches easily; in addition to not being as flexible as the other, it doesn't bind so securely, so you don't get the job done as quickly, and it also spoils the pruning shears badly. The straw or the large horbe that some people use, makes a rather ugly effect, and stops the branches badly. That's all I have to say about this operation, assuming the trees are disbudded: if they weren't, what wouldn't I have to say? With a great deal of time, trouble and precaution, you have to do everything that should have been done a month earlier, with the greatest of ease. It is necessary, in a way, to pierce the chaos, and to make oneself light in the darkness, to arbitrate the retrenchments that must be made, both on the wood and on the fruit. I cannot think of such a work without fear: so I refer the reader to the previous chapter; he will judge what he must do by what has not been done. He must always work on the same principles. The same causes, the same objects, the same disadvantages: it is up to the person who finds himself in the predicament I have described, to come out of it as unscathed as possible. CHAPTER X. The second Palissage. As the sap acts without discontinuity from February to September, we find ourselves obliged, a month or six weeks after the first trellising, to start the same work again; but it is much easier then. If the trees have been disbudded, only a few new branches are formed, because the removed branches have been broken off at birth, and the sap has not been able to penetrate the same places, and has all been carried to the retained branches, or to the fruit. If they have not been disbudded, it is quite common for false shoots to sprout from the feet of branches that have been snipped at the first trellising, and these should be broken off with the thumb, if they are soft enough not to need an instrument. If in any other place some have come up that are useless or misplaced, as they almost always are, in this case, they are removed in the same way: but observe to detach with the thumb, only that which bursts easily; for, if the wood has begun to eudurcate, you would damage the mother branch; and the gum that couldroit̀ occur afterwards, would cause it to perish: use a serpette then, and bring it as close as you can. If one of the branches that was trellised when the first trellis was put up, having found itself next to a greedy branch that has been cut back, has itself become greedy by the abundance of sap that has flowed to it, as often happens with vigorous trees, this branch must be removed, or at least cut back to the lowest of those it has produced. The remainder of this operation consists in giving an extra link to all the branches that have grown up since the first trellising, and cutting back those that exceed it to roughly the level of the coping. Some espaliers are so vigorous that they sometimes need to be trellised a third time in September: if necessary, this should be done in the same way as above. CHAPTER XI. How to uncover the fruit, and when to pick it. As much as it has been necessary to keep fruits covered by their leaves until they are ripe, to protect them from the sun's excessive heat and to give them more nourishment, they also need the sun's view to perfect their taste and give them the beautiful color that is their main ornament. However, it would be dangerous to expose them too soon to the sun's burning rays: they need to be accustomed to it gradually, that is, in three stages. We start to uncover them when the fruit begins to turn, i.e. when it whitens a little; we first remove a few leaves on the west or north side, depending on the exposure of the espalier: three or four days later, we remove a few more on the opposite side; and the same number of days later, we remove those opposite; finally, we put it completely out in the open, so that it doesn't lose any of the sun's rays. Soon after, it takes on color, it ripens; and we pick it when we see that the color is yellowing where the sun hasn't struck, i.e., on the side of the wall: If you're in the habit of picking it, you can tell for sure by looking at it; if you're not, you should observe that it comes off without violence: all you have to do is kiss it whole, pulling it slightly towards you. If the fruit has reached maturity, it remains in the hand, and you must be careful not to press your thumb against it to test it, as some people do, as this bruises the fruit: But if you're picking it to be sold, or to be transported some distance away, you need to pick it firmer, i.e., so that it resists your hand a little when you detach it. An important thing to observe when 1 uncovering the fruit in the manner I have just described, is not to tear off the leaves, but to tear them with the fingernail close to the tail; when they are torn off violently, this evades and spoils the eye of the branch where they are held, and prevents the flower bud from forming for the following year. CHAPTER XII. The various insects that damage the wood and fruit of the peach tree, and the remedies available. First of all, there is a species of green caterpillar which eats the flower bud before it has bloomed: when this damage is noticed, you must look for them; you will surely find them behind some branch, and crush them. Lories, field mice, rats, mice, mezcrines, all these animals with more or less the same coat and face, are the greatest scourges of the peach tree. They sometimes ruin the espalier, so that the master doesn't have the pleasure of tasting a single peach before them. If they stuck to the ones they start, the pain would be more bearable; but they try them all one after the other on the sunny side, as they ripen, and that's as much lost. There are two ways of destroying these kinds of insects: the first is to use dobbies and four ciphers, which should be placed on the walls from distance to distance, and especially around the trees where the fruit is ripening, with the proviso that they should be visited every day, and the bait changed from time to time; but, for the best results, it would be better not to wait until the fruit is ripe, as they are much less eager to use the bait when they have plenty of fruit than when they find nothing to eat. The second means, which does not exclude the first, is to have all the holes in your walls, both inside and out, plugged up to the letter; because when you take away all their retreat, they don't multiply on your premises; they go and find a home elsewhere; and if the species isn't destroyed altogether, it at least thins out considerably: you can still put arsenic kneaded with flour or minced meat along the chaperones; but at the same time you have to beware of the accidents that can result. How easy it would be to find remedies for the ant! This little insect ruins the peach trees it attacks, and we can't guarantee that they won't be affected: we can reduce the quantity; there are various means that we can use: But I don't think it's possible to destroy them completely, and if it's true that they could be made to give up their place by the antipathic virtue of some drug, as some people claim, I maintain, from my own experience, that the evil would only change object; for in that case, they would be thrown back further onto another tree: this is what I've sometimes seen happen quite naturally, without having been able to identify the cause. You must therefore avoid them as much as possible, and be content to clear them up as much as you can to reduce the damage. Of all the methods I've used, the one I've found most successful is to place a freshly killed ox foot at the foot of each afflicted tree, with the skin half removed, and place a half-full tub of water next to it; This foot is then skilfully lifted and thrown into the water, where it drowns all the insects; it is immediately removed and placed back in its original position, where the same effect follows a few hours later; so that the operation can be repeated five or six times a day, destroying many of them; and the foot is changed after a few days, when it is seen to be dried out by the sun and no longer has any virtue. I would observe, however, that this expedient is only effective when the weather is a little warm; for, having tried it in the spring, the ant has not attached itself to it. There is a distinction to be made with regard to what I have just said, which is that it is not possible to guarantee peach trees against this wretched insect: strictly speaking, this should only apply to low stems; as for high and medium stems, it can be avoided, and here's how. First, if the stem is a little flexible, you shake it several times to make the insects fall off, until you can no longer see them. You then need to have the patience to remove all the leaves that are poisoned with their spawn, and the aphid that is inseparable from it: once this is done, you stop it about a foot away from the wall, with a kind of wooden fork, which holds it firm in the position you want; and above this fork, you make a small basin of soft wax all around the stem of the tree, which you then evaporate. By this expedient, the ant, which doesn't like to swim, withdraws at the sight of water, and the tree recovers. There's one more thing to bear in mind in this respect, and that's to pass two or three laths behind your tree, to secure the branches that the wind and the load of the fruit would ruin; it goes without saying that these branches must be tied to the laths as well as possible. Another expedient is used instead of the soft wax basin. Take cotton or oak twine soaked in oil, and tie it around the tree stem: aspic oil is the best; olive oil is good, but neither is always an effective barrier against this insect, although it often keeps it at bay. From time to time, you need to refresh this filasse with oil. The ant is also the scourge of orange trees; and, to defend against it in Italy, where most of the walls are covered with this species of fruit, more suited to the climate than any other, here is the practice observed, and which I noticed in the garden of a curious man in Naples. His trees were planted a good foot from the wall, each with a small stem about a foot and a half long. On this same alignment were buried ten by ten feet, oak poles, four inches square, and the height of the wall, to receive a trellis in the fashion of the country, which, though very different from ours, had the mềme destination. Trees were placed between two of them, and trellised underneath in the same way we erect our contr'espaliers. At the foot of each post and each tree, there was oiled cotton, as I described above. The virtue of the remedy was demonstrated by the beauty of the trees, where the master assured me that the ant had never touched since the espalier was planted; and I saw others a few paces away, set against the wall, which were infected with it. Nothing would be easier than to raise our peach trees in the same way; but by guaranteeing ourselves against one evil, I can't answer that it wouldn't result in another, perhaps even worse. Our climate is very different from that of Italy; the cold winds that slip between the trees and the wall could do them great harm: moreover, fruit deprived of the reflection of the wall could ripen later, and have less taste. If someone finds himself excessively tired of this insect, he can try it out on a few ques toises of espaliers; in any case, the expense and risk are not great. Bugs often attract ants, so during the winter you must take care to rake with a wooden knife all the branches where their brood is found: this attention is absolutely necessary. Slugs and snails also like to eat our peach trees, and especially the violet, of which they are very fond; but if you take care to do a few inspections, either in the morning after the dew, or after some gentle rain, you'll manage to destroy them. Birds are just as curious to taste them, especially when some insect has damaged them; but they can be kept away with scarecrows; and when they don't work, a child can be walked along the espaliers to keep them away. Failing that, here's another method I've seen practised recently by some curious people, but which I haven't tried myself: it's to stretch six inches by six inches over the tree, in all its extent, of red spun wool such as we use for tapestry; we positively assure that this keeps them away, by I don't know what antipathy that it's not easy to understand; and this, it is said, preserves in the same way the grapes of the espaliers: the test is easy, and will cost little to those who will want to do it. It's not as easy to protect against earwigs and wasp flies as it is against other insects. These two enemies sometimes cause Tome II. Z of great damage to this fruit, especially to the small and large cukes, which ripen first. To defend against the former, there is no better remedy than to keep the walls well plastered to prevent them from retreating; and if, despite this, you are still bothered by them, you must put a few sheep's horns or pig's hooves behind the branches, into which this insect retreats: every morning you shake it, and you kill whatever is in it. As far as flies are concerned, we can destroy a few with half-full bottles of water beaten with honey, where they come to drown; but this is a small reduction in the evil, and several other similar expedients are no better. What I've always found really useful is to place a few peaches, already tainted, on the trellis, at a distance from each other, and leave them there, or some other sweet, anti-chewy fruit, such as plum, fig, pear, etc.; they stick to these as long as they can find life in them, and they don't attack the whole fruit. You can even destroy many of them, if you have the courage, several times in the heat of the day, to seize and crush them with your full hand, when you see a certain quantity picked up on these bad fruits, which is very extraordinary; and, to avoid being offended by their sting, you use a bad glove. CHAPTER XIII. Precautions to take during the summer heat. IN good soils, and especially in damp ones, provided your espaliers are well tended with ploughing, unless there is an extraordinary drought, there is nothing to fear, nor any precautions to take; but in arid and scorching sands, drought often causes trees to perish, or at least makes them languid, and the fruit they yield is of neither quality nor size. In this kind of land, I strongly advise all those who have the misfortune to be located there, to throw three lanes of water at the foot of each tree every two weeks, and a few hours later, with a rake, to give it a little polish. At the same time, throw a good load of large litter on top to maintain freshness. As for the stems, I recommend wrapping the body of the trees in long straw, tied with wicker; this will preserve your trees, and you'll see the good effect on your fruit. In southern countries, trees should be covered with straw mats during the hottest part of the day, otherwise the heat of the sun will burn the fruit and cause the tree to perish in a short time. 22 20 Blankets. The delicacy of our peach trees, or, to put it better, the inordinate fear we have of seeing them perish, based on the great affection we have for them, has led us to look for every means capable of defending them from the insults of time; but after having experienced all kinds like the others, I declare that I have recognized the abuse: there is, however, a good one which I will talk about; but it is so expensive, that it suits few people. There are those who, at flowering time, which is the critical time, cover their peach trees with pea cossats, a bad expedient; for, of two things, one is either clear or thick, i.e., if you put on little, it protects neither from frost nor from the sun's rays, which cause the greatest harm after frost; if you put on a lot, it can truly protect against frost, or at least against the sun; but how many inconveniences does this not cause? The risk period lasts at least six weeks: during this time, your tree, suffocated under the blanket, and yet docile to the movements of nature, which goes its own way, grows its branches, as well as its flowers and its fruit; but how does it grow? like chicory in a cellar, that is, the leaves and fruit are whiter than green; and when you come to discover it at the end of April, or the beginning of May, half of these tender young branches are gone,

The tender, delicate fruit cannot withstand the slightest cold air, nor the faintest ray of sunlight; it dries out and falls: this is usually the success of your work. Others use doormats, which are made in two ways: some with string or gut rope, others with trellis wood. The former are very bad, and I say this in full knowledge of the facts. 1°. They are applied too close to the tree, and the agitation of the wind ruins half the flowers and buds, not to mention what is destroyed every time they are removed and replaced. 2°. The fruit becomes too tender under these mats, and doesn't get enough air, so that, accustomed to this softness, the slightest breath of fresh air that it comes to feel (if you neglect to put them back on in good time) causes it to perish, which makes for great subjection. Even supposing that, with great accuracy, this expedient had a good effect, who can rely enough on the vigilance of his gardener, to be sure that, throughout the risky season, he will not once forget to cover and uncover your peach trees in good time? This consideration alone should disillusion everyone, for it has been shown that it only takes an hour too early or too late for the fruit to perish. Doormats, made from trellis and wire, are more useful, in that they don't touch the tree, and the blossom has air underneath; but they also have their drawbacks: first, they need to be the full height of your wall, and 23 they need to go from one end of your espalier to the other; for if you only put a few here and there, you're doing nothing; the slushy winds, which come through the sides, are as damaging as full frost. Now, to cover large walls from one end to the other in this way, you need a large quantity, and it's a real expense. A doormat nine feet high and four feet wide, which is the usual measure, costs almost twenty sols, all told, and hardly lasts more than two years. The daily wear and tear of carrying and returning them, the ravages of time, the damage done by mice while they are locked up, all this makes them wither away: Besides, how much work is it for a gardener to put up and take down twice a day, and sometimes more, this quantity of straw mats that are not easy to move when they are soaked with water or covered with snow? What a mess that makes at the foot of your trees! Besides, you can hardly use your flowerbeds to grow peas, early lettuces or other vegetables. What's more, you'll be subject to the same constraints as the others, when it comes to putting them in and taking them out. All things considered, the expedient is costly, and certainly does more harm than good; so I don't recommend it any more than the other. Heavy canvas curtains, or glass frames, are quite different, and I consider them of good use, since it takes little time to open or close them; this can be done without much trouble, and without any of the inconveniences to which doormats are prone. Indeed, the fruit has air, it is not crumpled, it is protected from all the ravages of time; and, if they are glass frames, ii ripens three weeks before the others; but these means are expensive: with canvas curtains, there is a risk of having them taken away overnight, if you don't have them guarded. As for the frames, how many fractures are caused by the wind, and sometimes by hail! What a job it is to assemble and dismantle them every year, along with the framework that depends on them! They are therefore only suitable for people who want to spare nothing to satisfy themselves, and who are in a position to make this expenditure. A few years ago, someone tried to persuade me that by having half-consumed manure carried from distance to distance along my walls, and set on fire at the approach of daylight, in times of frost, the smoke that came from it prevented the cold from biting so hard on the flower and on the fruit when it was knotted, and that it also blunted the first rays of the sun, which burns after the frost, so that it surely preserved the trees. I found something specious in the advice, and put it to the test; but it was at my expense, for lack of reasoning, for one must always operate on a few principles. Now, frost doesn't set when there's wind; everyone knows that: so it's only when there's no wind that there's a risk, and in that case, the smoke wouldn't have the effect you'd expect of it, which is to fall back like a riz 4 deau on your espaliers, it goes straight up, like a chimney, and is useless: that's precisely what happened to me. From all that I have just said, my reader will no doubt conclude that I am teaching him nothing that can defend his fruit from the insults of time. I agree that I am inadequate in this respect, but since my aim is only to disabuse him of the bad practices of which he may be the dupe, he must count my observations for something. In the absence of a sure expedient, since I don't know of any, I have nevertheless to propose a use whose success I experience every day; to have perfected it in some way. Mr Girardot, a former King's Musketeer, so well known for the beautiful plantations he planted in Bagnolet, and for the immense yield he obtained from them, is the inventor of this practice, which has since been used by many of the local inhabitants. All along his walls, below the copings, and from one toe to the next, he had pieces of wood projecting two feet or so: He had boards laid on them when the risk season arrived, claiming that spring frosts only fell perpendicularly, and that by covering his fruit from above, they were safe, not only against frosts, but also against cold rains, which are also harmful to fruit: this is what he practiced constantly, and others after him, proof of success. You can walk boldly in similar footsteps, and that's what I've done too, but differently. Instead of these pieces of wood, permanently embedded in the walls, which make an ugly sight in summer, I had small gallows made of light wood, the top of which slopes a little to help the water run off the roof they support; They are attached with wicker to the last mesh of the trellis, from six feet to six feet; and instead of planks, I have made, in imitation of the inhabitants of Montreuil, small doormats about two feet wide by twelve and a half feet long, linked by two battens. In February, I place my straw mats on these gallows, and secure them with wicker; they remain in this state until May, when I untie everything and bring it back to my greenhouse: only two days are needed for this operation, the costs are low, and this cover protects the fruit well, although it doesn't make it completely safe. That's all I can advise: for the rest, we have to rely on Providence, which takes care of all our needs. I have twice seen the fruit buds of pear, plum, apricot and cherry trees freeze for the most part, and the blossoms of peach trees suffer very little. The last expedient I have just described can only be used as long as there is a trellis on the espaliers; a new reason which should make us realize its advantage more and more. Z5 CHAPTER XV. Sin diseases. THE most considerable disease that the peach tree has to endure, and one that is without remedy, as it is so far without a determined name, is when all the branches of the tree, the leaves. It's a kind of contagious leprosy that spreads to everything around it; and if you don't take care, as soon as a tree is attacked by it, to have it uprooted, and to have the wall that contracts the disease coated with lime, so to speak, and that blackens as well as the tree, all the plants in your espalier will perish one after the other. I can't say where this contagion originates: the common opinion, that it's the bug, doesn't seem likely to me, or, if it's there, there's some other cause mixed in, either some bad fog that attaches itself to one place rather than another, or a corrupted wind, or some bad disposition in the body of the tree, or finally a sunburn after the fog. Whatever the cause, the ailment is certain; and, as it is absolutely without remedy, we must content ourselves with halting its progress by promptly sacrificing the patient. The second disease that most afflicts our sins is gum, for which there is no remedy. If it only damages a few branches, and the rest is worth preserving, try to maintain what is good; but if the ravage spreads to all parts of the tree, uproot it and replant another of a different species. Red and white Madalaines, and violets, suffer the most: they should be given up if they cannot be raised. The depredation of ants is also one of the greatest scourges of peach trees. I have said all I know about this in Chapters IX and XII: I will only add that, if they persist two or three years in a row on the same tree, as often happens, the shortest thing to do is to uproot it; for they do not leave it until they have destroyed it, and a new plant does not always have the same attraction for them, especially when it is a different species. If a tree is languishing, and you don't know the cause, have it dug up, and look at its roots; sometimes it's cockchafer worms that eat them, and this happens quite often to young trees: in that case, have them look for exactly where you see gnawed roots: sometimes the red ant attaches itself to them, and makes them wilt; the remedy is to destroy as many as you can. Rake the roots well, discard the infected soil and replace it with new soil. If the disease comes from a few rotten, spoiled roots, cut them to the quick, and have new soil brought in: with these attentions, your tree will recover. But before you do this, test whether some water might revive it, supposing it is in the height of summer that it is languishing. In the months of June and July, the peach trees are sometimes hit by a white cloud that makes them very tired, and also damages the fruit. There is no other remedy for this accident, which cannot be prevented, than to shorten the branches; others grow from the last eyes, which are sometimes healthy, and other times they inherit the contagion: there is no disadvantage in running the risks. The last disease common to all trees is old age: you can tell by the yellowish leaves, the thinness of the shoots and the smallness of the fruit. If you can rejuvenate your tree by means of a few good branches that it will have sprouted from the foot, you must cut it back on these young branches, or promptly give it a successor who must be at its side, if you practice what I advise in the following Chapter. If you cut it back on its large branches, as some people do, it is very unlikely to regrow new wood: the sap hardly penetrates its bark, which is the hardest of all trees. There is no point in smoking it or changing your land; it has had its day. CHAPTER XVI. Precautions to take when replanting Espaliers. E strongly advises all those who plant new trees to set aside a small reserve to meet their needs: gum, or some other unforeseen cause, sometimes kills a tree when it is least expected. There's nothing more unpleasant than to see a gap in an espalier: it's like a hole in a tapestry. You should prevent this by planting elsewhere, at any exposure, a dozen trees of the same fruit species as your plant, which you should label on pieces of slate attached to each tree, or to the trellis; and when you miss one, you use the same species to fill the gap. Whether they are four, six or up to eight years old, lift them carefully, they will pick up perfectly - I've made a thousand experiments. These attentions consist in making a small trench all around the tree you want to raise, about two and a half feet from its base, and three feet deep by twelve or fifteen inches wide: Once the trench has been made, you gradually uncover the roots, and to avoid offending them, you use a winegrower's tool called a forked hoe, resembling an iron hook; you gradually raise your soil to the right and to the left, until your roots are clear, and as you pull the tree, you feel that it comes easily to you: if any large root resists too much, you cut it off as far as you can. As soon as it's uprooted, you carry it to the place you want to give it: after refreshing the root tips and trimming it short, you sit it at the same height as the others, or rather six inches higher, because the soil you've just dug up sags accordingly: A few months later, you spread all these roots to the right and left, and while one man throws loose earth over them, a second man, who must be in the hole, insinuates this earth, with his hands, between the roots, so that they are not piled up one on top of the other; you finally fill in your hole, and throw over it three channels of water, which reassure and bind the earth to the roots. Once you've taken these precautions, you can be sure that the following spring it will be just like the others, and that you'll even get some fruit from it, but don't let it bear too much. Then, during the heat of the following summer, take care to give it a wetting from time to time, and a hoeing afterwards. For extra safety, you can also throw a load of large litter at the foot of the plant. This transplant should be done around Saint Martin's Day, and it's easy to see that it needs to be relieved of much of its wood. If you have not taken the precaution of making this small reserve body for your needs, and you are obliged to replace your dead trees with young ones from the nursery, plant them in this case in the way I have indicated in Chapter III. Everything I have just said should only be applied to young espaliers, as old trees should be treated quite differently. As for old trees, you need to get as much out of them as you can while they are still alive, and prepare successors for them as soon as you see that they are coming to an end. To do this, plant young trees in the middle of the old ones, taking care (as they grow) to prune all the branches of the old ones that could shade them and prevent them from growing. Then, when they start to appear after four or five years, you uproot all the old ones, and find yourself a new espalier. In Montreuil, this is how the trees follow on from one another without let-up, and are always perfectly formed. However, this does not mean that they must be equally successful everywhere, as the terrain in this village seems to have been created expressly for this fruit; but it must always be tested, especially as if they do not succeed in this way, one must not flatter oneself that by making a new plant, after having uprooted all the old ones and broken up the soil, they will succeed better. 1 CHAPTER XVIL Ploughing. It is common knowledge that weeds absorb salts from the soil and impoverish it. They cannot therefore be destroyed with too much care: this is the first reason why all trees are ploughed. These weeds are unpleasant to the eye, another reason for eradicating them: they attract many insects which then spread to trees and fruit; this is the third reason: they dry out the soil, the fourth reason; finally, they prevent light rain from penetrating easily and usefully; this is the fifth reason. To this we can add that all unshaped soil cracks during droughts, and the sun's heat, which penetrates through these cracks to the tree's roots, causes considerable damage. For all these reasons, we can't maintain our espalier trees too well with ploughing. First, in autumn, they need a good spade ploughing, a second in April; and during the summer, they need to be ploughed with the hoe or the rake, as often as they need, and always in good weather, so that the weeds dry out and die. Spade ploughing in this season is not suitable; it alters and fans the tree roots. 89 CHAPTER XVIII. Whether it's a good idea to smoke Espaliers. Sentiments are very divided on this subject, and each finds reasons that seem solid to support his opinion. M. de la Quintinie is formally opposed to any kind of manure; and after establishing, by specious reasoning and seductive comparisons, that it can do no good to trees, he apprehends that it can do them harm. I respect this great arbiter more than anyone else; but, whether he was susceptible to prejudice, as all men are, more or less, or whether the Versailles grounds, where he exercised his talents, had a particular and unique quality, which would be to need no foreign help, his feeling could not prevail against the experience of a thousand people who smoke, and who find it very good. I don't want us to rely on the feelings of a few individuals, since opinions are divided, and there are many people who pronounce lightly for or against, and rather on the basis of their prejudices, than on exact knowledge. But I believe that the practice of a whole country, whose inhabitants, from father to son, have for a long time made their sole occupation and their trade from their espaliers, should serve as a rule. It is the general practice of the inhabitants of Montreuil, Bagnolet and other neighboring towns to smoke their trees every three years at a brisk rate, i.e. those that are of a certain age and beginning to tire of the manure; young trees that are growing vigorously don't need it; the manure could even harm them, preventing them from bearing fruit. Those who lack manure notice the difference and, by their own admission, their fruit loses much of its quality and size. To argue against the experience gained over such a long period of time is to contradict the truth. But, you may say, their soil may need a help that others don't ask for? that's still trying to fool oneself. In their extensive terroir, they have veins of strong soil, others lighter, others stony, etc., some situated in amphitheaters, others on plains. Manure has the same effect everywhere, and it must be said that they recognize the need for it, for if the use of it were equivocal, they would not incur the expense, which is a real object. Some people who are stubborn on this point, and who are nonetheless obliged to agree on the good effect of manure, because it has been demonstrated to them (which has happened to me sometimes), retrench to say that it is true that manure gives vigor to the tree, and size to the fruit, but that it takes away the taste, just as it takes away the quality of the wine. All this reasoning is wrong, and I have experience to back me up. I have found, on the contrary, that fruits that have been smoked have more water and delicacy than others, and this because they are better nourished. As for the quality of sumier, as much as possible, horse, mule or donkey sumier should be used in strong soils, both because it warms the soil while imparting its salts, and because it makes it looser, i.e. softer and easier to cultivate. Cow's milk, on the other hand, is much better in dry, scorching soils, fattening them and keeping them cool. The best time to bury it is in November, because frost often arrives in the following month, and you're no longer in time. One thing to remember the following spring, when you give the espaliers their second ploughing, is to put the manure that the spade brings back to the surface of the ploughing back into the bottom of the gauge. I shouldn't omit to mention here that if the beds of your espaliers are only three or four feet wide, as too many of this type are, your trees benefit only slightly from the manure; because it doesn't act on the body of the large raciues, it only acts on their extremities, which we call the hair. As a result, it must be kept away from the base of trees, in proportion to their size: anything put at the base is pure waste, and can sometimes harm the tree, either by wilting the roots, if it bears on them, or by attracting various insects that attach themselves to them and cause them to perish. In addition to the disadvantages I have just mentioned, this manure forms a kind of floor at the foot of the tree, which consolidates and prevents rain from penetrating. It would therefore be much better to leave them without manure. Those who find themselves in the situation I've just described, i.e. whose beds are so narrow, should not hesitate to widen them to at least six feet, if they want their trees to benefit from both manure and ploughing. In some cases, a border of grapes that closes off the flower bed and that you don't want to destroy, holds you back; but in this case, all you have to do is leave a long wood at each foot, lay it down the following year at two or three feet in the alley, and in the autumn wean it off, and pull up the old plant; in this way, you lose nothing of the ratio, you find yourself with a renewed plant, and you remedy the defect in question. It remains for me to justify the use of manure, by the experience I've had with it for twenty years. I use it not only for my espaliers, but also for all kinds of other trees, both espaliers and bushes, with the exception of a few species, and trees on hardwoods, which grow quite well on their own. This practice has always worked so well for me, that I don't understand how anyone can dispute its usefulness. The fruit I harvest in the years I smoke increases by a third in size. What more perfect proof could one wish for? What's more, depending on the quality of the soil, and whether the trees are more or less full of fruit, you need to smoke more or less often. Some plants require fertilization every six years, while others need it every three, four or five years. CHAPTER XIX. Transporting trees to distant lands. The life of the peach tree, like that of all plants, lies in the moisture that is spread throughout all its parts: as long as this moisture exists, the tree is alive. It must therefore be maintained, and prevented from being consumed by the open air and the sun; for a tree that has been uprooted no longer has the same resources as a rooted tree, which pumps daily into the juices of the earth what it needs to repair the losses that its outer parts make through the action of the air and the sun. It has to subsist on its own, and consequently not allow anything to be lost. As soon as your trees are uprooted, shorten the roots a little, as well as the head; form bundles of twenty or twenty-five at the most, if they are dwarfs, and of twelve, if they are stems. Skilfully arrange their roots one between the other; spread straw between the bodies, so that they don't bruise when crumpled, and tighten the whole with a wicker; then take some fresh moss, and insinuate it between the roots, pressing it as hard as you can, until they are all covered; on top of that, put long straw, tied with rope, and wrap the body of the trees with a good thickness of straw, tightly tied with the same rope. For the final façosi, wrap the body of the roots with a mop, secured all around. Conditioned in this way, you can ship them to any country you like, and be sure that when they remain on the road from the end of October to March, they will not perish. I have sent some to Muscovy and deep into Italy, and they have been perfectly successful. There are still a few other precautions to take when they make a long journey by land, and when it's very hot and sunny, as sometimes happens in October and November; it's to recommend to the valet to throw a few jugs of water over the roots' bodies from time to time; and if, on the contrary, they find themselves on the road during severe frosts, to cover them with lots of straw, and even blankets, if possible. When you take them out to sea, you don't need to take any of these precautions, because they're sheltered from the sun and the frost. As for the dwarfs, you can condition them in another way, which is preferable to the one I've just mentioned; that's to bring them down to within a foot of the graft, and to store them all tightly together in hatter's mantles, which are known everywhere; it goes without saying that before doing so, you'll have lined the mantle with straw all around, and that you'll also line all the roots with moss, as you put them on top of each other. If, for added safety, you want to put another straw wrapping around the manna, you can rest easy about their fate. When your trees have happily arrived at their destination, we recommend that you soak the roots in water for two days before planting them. CHAPTER XX. How to raise and graft Peach trees. I have said that there is more embarrassment than economy in the convenience of finding as many as one wants from one moment to the next, and at a fair price; but as not everyone is within easy reach of Paris, and as these trees often arrive poorly conditioned when you are some distance away, whatever recommendations you may have made, and as it costs you money, time and uncertainty, it is a good idea to grow some yourself, to find them when you need them, and to be more sure of the species. I therefore advise all those who are far from nurseries to make their own, in proportion to their needs. The first thing to do is to prepare the soil, which should be broken up by about two feet, and always choose a good bed. It is then a good idea, as far as possible, to let it rest for a year before forming the nursery, and to give it a few hoeings during the summer. Peach trees can be grafted onto three types of tree, as mentioned in Chapter III: peach stones, almond trees and plum trees. The first is almost no longer used, although the body of these trees is more beautiful than that of the others, and the tree is also more vigorous, because it is too prone to gum: we must therefore stick to the other two species; and each, according to the quality of his land, will choose the one that suits him best. The almond tree is best suited to light, stony soils, and the plum tree to all other types of soil: I won't repeat the reasons I've given for this. As for the almond tree, here's how to raise it. In the bottom of this vessel (whatever it may be), you will put two inches of fresh, greasy sand, and a bed of almonds on top, an inch apart, with the point down; two more inches of sand on top of the almonds, and another bed of almonds on top, continuing in this way until your vessel is full: this is done in November. You leave them in this state all winter; and the following spring, you remove your almonds from the sand, and put them in the soil you've prepared eighteen or or twenty inches apart, and about four inches deep. These almonds, which have begun to germinate in the sand, soon sprout out of the earth. However, this practice is not widespread: many individuals only put the almonds in the ground in January, as they don't want them to get so far ahead of time : and, instead of locking them up, they leave them out in the open air, in tubs exposed to the sun, taking care to cover them during heavy frosts, should they occur; others put them in even later, and soak them for two days in hot water, before burying them, to get them ready to germinate; still others sow them in their garden, along a well-exposed bed, and cover them during frosts: But there is a real risk that field mice or crows will destroy many of them, if they are not kept free of these animals. In this respect, everyone has their own ideas, but climate and terrain must play an important role. The essential point of view must always be that the almonds germinate reliably, and that they are not too far advanced when you want to replant them in March; for however skilful you are, you break a large proportion of the sprouts, which are extremely tender, by removing them from the ground when they are too much lined up; and, even if you don't break them, they are very prone to perish after being replanted. When the last operation has been carried out, they should be planted with a straight line, Tome II. A a and make sure that from one row to the next, there is a distance of two and a half or three feet, for ease of passage, and to give the soil more freedom of movement during the summer; for great care must be taken to destroy the weeds that grow around them. In the first edition of this work, I said that the tender almonds of Provence were more successful than all the others, having indeed recognized that the peach graft was perfectly suited to them, and produced very fine sprays; but I have since realized that this species was more prone to gum than the others, and I therefore retract my first statement. At the end of August of the same year, the shoots are strong enough to receive the escutcheon, and you then graft them two inches from the ground, observing that the eyes of the branches you take are double and accompanied by two or three good leaves: single eyes, which are accompanied by only one leaf, never make good subjects. Everyone knows how to graft, so I won't say any more about it; I'll just recommend that you take care to loosen the twine a little in the following month, if you see that it's tightening the graft too much. I say spun yarn, but I must explain that it is much better to use spun wool, which is not prone to cutting the crest, like hemp and linen, because it lends itself more. In the following spring, as soon as the eye of the crest begins to open, let go of the ligature without removing it, and bend the subject downwards precisely above the graft: the eye soon lengthens and forms its shoot. By the following October, the peach tree is fully formed, and you can then uproot it and replant it in any location you wish; for it should not remain in the nursery any longer, by which I mean a second year; but it can stay there all winter until March. What I've just said only applies to dwarf trees, because when you want to grow stems and half-stems, you have to let the almond trees grow for three or four years, and help them to grow their stems straight by tying them to a few stilts; at the same time, from year to year, you have to cut off the lower branches; but you mustn't cut them off at first, i.e. at the top of the stem: The first time, they are cut a few inches apart, and the following year, they are cut back to flower; and the reason for this is that the sap must be allowed to emerge several times, so that the plant can root better; for the more roots are formed, the more the body grows and draws nourishment. Nor should the sap be allowed to dissipate too much, so that the master shoot can benefit and grow; as this is the object you are aiming for, you must therefore maintain a middle ground: when it has reached a reasonable size, you graft it at the same time, and in the same way as the dwarfs, at four, five or six feet from the ground, depending on whether the stem allows it, and whether you deem it suitable. A to 2 To form stems and half-stems, choose those most likely to grow upright, and use for dwarfing those that fork. For the first, we take offshoots or cuttings from some old damask plum stump, which is a common plum everywhere; so we're never at a loss for cuttings, and the cuttings that grow at the end of fruit-bearing trees are just as good as those from stumps that have been reseeded on purpose; we pull them out in November, and immediately replant them in the nursery, in the same way as almonds, observing the same distances. They are then cut back to five or six inches, and left for two years, i.e. until August of the second year, to be grafted in the same way as almond trees, and replanted at the end of the year. When it comes to raising stems and half-stems, follow the same procedure as for almond trees. I would like to warn you that for the violet peach and the chevreuse, you must take offshoots of a plum called Saint Julien joré; only this type of plum is suitable for these two species. I don't want to go into the reasons for this; it's enough that experience has convinced all those who are in the business of raising trees around Paris, From all that I have noted above, it is easy to conclude that those who want to have peach trees every year, must, from year to year, prepare a piece of land, and observe that the bottom that has once grown, can no longer be used for the same purpose until three or four years later; but it is very good for yielding grain or vegetables. To shorten the calculations for those who want to know how much land is needed for how many seedlings, by establishing, as I said, the distance between rows at three feet, and that between trees at eighteen inches; the perch of land, worth eighteen feet of king in all directions, contains seventy-two, which, multiplied by one hundred that make the arpent, gives for each seven thousand two hundred. There are provinces where the perch is twenty and twenty-two feet; this has nothing to do with our object: it is enough to know what an eighteen-foot perch should contain; it is then easy to know the increase in plants, in proportion to that given to the perch. CHAPTER XXI. Special method for a new plantation. In the course of this little work, I have explained everything that could be most useful for planting peach trees, but in this I have only followed ordinary practice. Since then, events have shown me (I mean since the first edition) the good effect of a practice I had conceived a long time ago, but which I had not yet tested enough to propose. My experience now entitles me to recommend it, and I dare to assure you that those who wish to A a 3 follow it, can count on deriving from their plantation all the satisfaction that the thing can give: but, before coming to the point, it is essential that I make a few observations relating to the object, to make the usefulness of the method in question clear. There is always something to fear when planting, whatever precautions you take. 1°. Lack of security in the species. 2°. The uncertainty of the subjects on which they have been grafted. 3°. The quality of the grafts used, and finally, the recovery of the trees: these four points need to be well understood. The safety of the species is self-explanatory, and consists in having them as free as one desires; it is on this that one must necessarily run the risks of the merchant's good faith, and of the order he has maintained in his plantations, which may have been disturbed in many ways. As I have already observed, there are few species that are known by wood and leaf, and consequently the most accurate precautions cannot establish anything as certain: this is the first disadvantage. The uncertainty of the subjects to which the grafts have been applied is a second point, the importance of which almost no-one is aware, and which is nevertheless a capital issue. I'll start by saying, with regard to the almond tree, that some merchants have no qualms about grafting on bitter almonds, because the growth of crests is stronger than on sweet almonds, and this is more favorable for their sales: but for the buyer, the effect is quite different; for the tree never fruits more than imperfectly, and is consumed as wood; the very fruit it yields is bitter and loses its size, which is an irreparable harm, and the most skilful merchant would not be able to avoid this inconvenience. Other merchants, through poor economy, look for the smallest almonds they can find, especially as this fruit is bought by the bushel, and the smaller it is, the more it fits into the measure, therefore costing them less for the seed; but the result is that the shoot they grow is much weaker than that of a well-fed almond, and the tree it forms afterwards feels this weakness all its life. When almonds are in short supply in the Paris area, nurserymen often use almonds from Provence, which, according to my experience, produce much more gum than the others in peach trees grafted onto them, as I observed in the previous chapter; and this is something that no one can discern when trees are raised in nurseries: this is what concerns the almond tree. The same disadvantages apply to the plum tree: there are an infinite number of types of plum, all of which produce cuttings equally capable of taking peach tree escutcheons, but which have a very different effect on the quality of the fruit they produce. It is important to know which cuttings are suitable for each species, and merchants often graft indiscriminately on all A to 5 types of cuttings, sometimes without even knowing them themselves; for they buy them right and left, and use them as they find them. The result is sometimes that the trees don't bear fruit, or that the fruit is poor, and this defect is often attributed to the soil, which has its source in the quality of the grafted subject. This is another pitfall that cannot be avoided, as there is no way of recognizing it when the trees are lifted: there are still other things to say about the age and quality of cuttings, which I'll mention shortly. The choice of branches for grafting is another important point for good tree production, and this is the third disadvantage I have to mention, where it is no easier to become acquainted. Merchants, whose sole aim is to produce trees that look good in order to sell them better, choose, as much as they can, the largest branches of the trees from which they draw them, because the crests are stronger, stick together more easily, and grow more vigorously : but these large branches are usually gourmands, whose escutcheons do produce beautiful shoots, which is their main merit; for the trees they form are exhausted of wood, and only produce fruit very late and in small quantities, because they always retain the vice of their origin. However, there is a considerable difference in the quality of the fruit, even when they are of the same species: the grosse mignonne, for example, when it is quite clean, can be recognized by its almost round shape, and by an insininity of small red dots on the side that the sun has hit, while the opposite side is a yellowish white: the false cutie, on the other hand, which is nevertheless the most widespread, is longer than round; these small dots do not occur, and it always retains a background of greenness on the side of the wall. This outward difference would be nothing, if the quality were the same; but it's far from it; it's not nearly so fine, nor so melting, nor so high in sugar; yet they're both cute in general terms: but how can you tell them apart in the nursery? and on what can you base your confidence that the nurseryman will have applied himself to seeking out the best in preference to the least? More often than not, he takes the branches as he has them, or as he finds them elsewhere, and doesn't believe himself obliged to take any more particular care. This is what sometimes causes the difference in quality in fruit that you find better or worse in your neighbor's nursery than in your own, without understanding the reason for it. It's much the same with all other kinds of peaches, and you can see how little you can be sure of what you're buying. Finally, who can promise that the trees planted will do well in the soil in which they are placed, in the ignorance in which we often find ourselves, if the soil in which they were raised was of more or less the same temperament? conA a 5 dition necessary for their perfect success; and this is something that almost no one pays attention to: yet we must be persuaded that when a tree is moved from a strong soil to a light one, or from a light one to a strong one, this change of situation usually exposes it to languish, and sometimes to perish, whatever precautions are taken when planting. Who knows, moreover, if it does not have some bad disposition in the foot, which the eyes do not perceive, or if its root has not suffered any alteration since it was raised? How often does it happen that cankers form on large roots that have to be shortened? It's all too common, in all tree plantations, to have a part of a tree missing; where does this come from, if not from unknown causes or unforeseen accidents, since everything we plant is judged to be good to look at? All these considerations should make you realize how many different risks are involved in planting fully-formed trees; and you will find yourself (I think) inclined to follow the advice I'm going to give to protect yourself, which is to graft yourself in place the species you want to have; and here's how you should proceed. First of all, you lay out the location of your espalier, as I explained in Chap. IV, and, depending on the quality of your soil, you decide whether to plant almonds or plums; in the same way, you regulate the distance between your trees, depending on the height of your walls, as I explained in Chap. V. Suppose, then, that you are set for the almond tree: in November, plant three almond trees in the set places, eight or nine inches apart from each other, and six inches from the wall; just push the tip down four inches, and plumb the earth over it with your foot; cover them during the heavy frosts with a little short manure, and uncover them as soon as they have gone: They'll sprout the following spring, and you'll care for them as I recommended in the previous chapter. At the end of August or in mid-September, when the sap has completely run its course, you will graft them into an escutcheon two inches from the ground, as explained; finally, in the following spring, the escutcheon will make its throw, and you will observe to pinch it at the fourth or fifth leaf, as soon as it is seven to eight inches long, the reason for which I will soon explain : but beforehand (I mean as soon as the eye begins to grow) you will cut your plums back into a slope very close to the graft, covering the wound with a bit of soft wax, or failing that, you will use loam. If your soil is suitable for plum trees, plant three plum cuttings of a suitable species in the same season as I said, in the set places and at the same distance as the almonds. In March, cut them back to two inches of soil, turning the cut towards the wall: this branch will strengthen, and in August or September, you'll graft it into a scion, provided it's strong enough; failing that, you'll postpone the operation until the following year; and in the following spring, that is, as soon as the hard frosts of winter have passed, you will cut your trees back into a slope, as mentioned above, and release the ligature; the escutcheon will sprout immediately, and you will pinch it at the same point and in the same way as I said for almond trees. If you don't graft the first year, remember to cut the new shoot back to half an inch in March, so that you can graft in August on the new shoot that will grow from it. Grafting is always more successful on this year's wood than on the old one, even though you graft equally on both. I recommend planting three almonds or plum tree cuttings, but this is only to ensure that at least one of the three comes to fruition, as only one should remain, and many things would have to happen if we did not have the good fortune to escape it. But this precaution is necessary, because, on the one hand, field mice and large birds, i.e. magpies and crows, are very greedy for almonds, and always destroy some of them; on the other hand, not all grafts succeed: but when you have both tiera to lose, you can put your mind at rest about the event. If, however, the site is infected to some extent with field mice, it would be better not to plant the almonds until March, after having germinated them in the sand, as I said in the previous chapter. At the same time as you are pinching the shoots of the escutcheons, as I explained above, you must reduce yourself to a single subject, if all three have come to fruition, by which I mean leaving only one that you choose as the best, and pulling out the rest; for they would harm each other if left to remain together any longer; and in pulling them out, you must avoid undermining the one you leave. When the plant left behind has grown three or four good branches, after the pinching operation which produces this effect, care must be taken to trellis and space them properly. But when your walls necessarily require stems or half-stems, this is the case where the operation makes you sigh a little; because you can only graft them in the second or third year; and I know how much these two or three years of waiting cost those who are in a hurry to enjoy; but it cannot be avoided. In this case, you have to train the plants differently, and train them relatively to the point of view you have of grafting them four, five or six feet above ground: To do this, as soon as your almond or plum trees have started to grow, reduce the shoots to a single one, choose the strongest and best-placed one, and tie it to a rod or stile so that it stands upright. So you let it grow as much as it wants for two or three years, until you judge the branch strong enough to be grafted; and it must therefore be at least the size of a good finger in the part that is to be budded.
