At this point, you graft it in the same way, and observe the same precautions as I have recommended for low stems; but there's one extra precaution to be taken, and that's to leave only two of the three that you first plant, assuming that all three have recovered well, and to remove the middle one to give the other two more room to grow; as they occupy the earth longer than the low-stems, their roots would get mixed up together if you left them all: It's enough to leave two of them, and you even need to use skill when you come to cut the second, after they have been grafted, so as not to tire the one that remains. For greater security that the grafts do not fail, i.e. that one fails and the other succeeds, you can put two grafts on each subject, four inches apart, opposite each other; and as soon as you see that the eyes are ready to grow, you fold the stem over to the better of the two, and the lower one should always be preferred. We must now explain the care we must take, both with regard to the subjects we choose for grafting, and with regard to the mềmes grafts. If it is almonds you are planting, you must choose the most beautiful you can find, the whitest, the sweetest species, and observe that they are new this year. You should also take care, when the germ starts to emerge from the ground (which is the most critical time), to defend it as best you can from destructive animals, especially field mice, which scrape the earth around it and break it open to find the almond underneath. You can destroy many of them with bells, which you bury flush with the ground at the foot of the walls, and fill halfway with water: as these animals come and go, they rush in and drown. If you don't have bells, use earthen pots; you can also put two pieces of tile to the right and left of the sprout, and leave them there until they are strong enough: this prevents them from scratching. If it's plum cuttings you're planting, you need to take current-year cuttings of good strength, i.e. the size of your little finger, with good roots and clear wood, and bury them about eight inches deep. As far as grafts are concerned, they should be taken from healthy, vigorous trees in full bearing, free from gum and cankers, and whose fruit you have seen.I say more: every amateur should cut the branches himself; for a gardener, to whom one relies, can make many equivocations for lack of a certain orderly mind; and how many there are who feel no interest in their master, and who would be capable of taking them at random, to save themselves a few pains or a few steps? There's nothing easier, in the fruit season, than to go for a walk in the gardens of Montreuil, or of some of its neighbors, and mark the trees whose fruit you like, by putting labels on them; You then come back when it's time to graft your seedlings, and make sure that the branches you choose are of average strength, with good double eyes; the biggest ones are usually gourmands, which should be avoided, as I said earlier; and the weakest branches don't have eyes capable of serving as a crest. If you are not in a position to carry out this operation yourself, and you absolutely must rely on someone else to do it for you, then take all the precautions that prudence suggests, so that the choice of branches is good, and the species carefully marked. For this purpose, you can give a substitute to your gardener, by the proverbial ruison that four eyes see better than two. In the case of those who are isolated in the provinces, and who have no supplies of their own in their vicinity, they should advise the person entrusted with their commission to arrange the branches they bring in neatly in boxes, sorted by bundle and number, and to prick their feet in small cucumbers to keep them fresh: with this precaution, they could remain there for a month without spoiling. A similar precaution when grafting is to keep the branches in a vessel with two inches of water, for as long as the grafting operation lasts, so that the air and sun do not dry them out. I'm not talking about the specific operation of grafting, which is now commonplace in every country; but I will not fail to point out that there is a skill involved in doing it well, which is not common to all those who take part in it: so, as far as possible, you should enlist the services of an intelligent workman, who is commonly found near Paris. In more distant countries, each person will do as he or she sees fit. I'd now like to talk about cuttings, i.e. plum species suitable for grafting peach trees. We have only three species which require a particular subject, the two violets, early and late, and the chevreuse; these, by well-recognized experience, want to be grafted onto the Saint-Julien, of which there are two species, the common and the joré: the first can be used for want of the second; but the latter is much more reliable, and should always be taken by preference. All other types of peach require damask, either large or small, which are known everywhere, but the former is preferable. The difference between the two is that the large damask has a changing gray wood, a little mealy, and the heart of the shoot is whitish; the small damask, on the other hand, has a large even brown wood, and its shoot is reddish: sometimes these species are bred from pits, and in this case, they are perfectly safe; but usually, cuttings are taken from the foot of old fruit-bearing trees, or from some old resprouted stump, and they are equally good, provided they are well rooted, and are not attached to old snags that are worthless for replanting. As far as possible, they should be taken from the current year's crop; however, they are still good after two years. If you don't have any at home or in your neighborhood, you can buy them at La Vallée, where they are sold regularly from All Saints' Day to March. It will undoubtedly be found that there is far more care and subjection involved in shaping an espalier plant, as I have just said, than in planting grafted trees: I agree; but at the same time, let's consider how many kinds of risks are involved in planting them in the latter way, and how mortifying it is to have cultivated a plant for several years, and to find oneself deceived at the moment of enjoyment; I mean to find fruits that are all different from what was expected, either in taste or in fruitfulness. There are all too many people, however, who experience this disgrace, which is cruel for an amateur, and their example can serve as a lesson. The method I propose removes all the disadvantages, and prevents all that can be feared; moreover, there is nothing difficult about its execution, and what's more, I suggest that you can enjoy the fruit more immediately than by planting fully grafted trees, if you only have to bud low stems; for it is well known that this replanted tree, in addition to the particular risks it runs, loses the firstic̀re year to take up and form new roots; but the same is not true of one that is grafted in place. In the same year that the crest grows, we begin to form its head by means of the pinch we make on the new shoot, and it is much easier to give it the shape it requires, because it is morally certain that its second branches, born from the first shoot, are all uniform in size, and that by this the sap is equally divided. Moreover, it is constant that a subject that remains where it was born always behaves better in all its ages than another that has been transplanted: the interruption of its action in the latter, and the change of soil, necessarily make impressions on it, which not only delay its growth, but disrupt its entire economy; for what happens? The stem, which suddenly finds itself deprived of the nourishing juices that give it life, falls into a kind of lethargy, and remains there until the foot has formed new roots that provide it with new help; and, in this state of suffering, although it does not perish, it is certain that its external parts, prey to the air and the sun, experience an alteration that they never repair sufficiently to return to their first state: from which it follows that vigor is never the same again, and that, in the order of nature, its duration must be less. I go further, and say that if we were to experiment with the different effects that result, we would find that, as a consequence of this first disorder, the fruits that come from it afterwards lose something of their quality. My opinion is based on the avowal of several nurserymen, who have often assured me that the fruit they sometimes gather by chance in their nurseries, from high-stemmed trees destined for replanting, is far superior in taste to anything that comes from gardens; and I even tasted some once, and was obliged to agree with them. I'd like to return to the subject of tree vigour and duration, and to justify my sentiment, I'd like to offer a few thoughts for public consideration. It has been noted a hundred times that a walnut tree left in place grows its stem and head much more quickly and more regularly than one that is transplanted: it is also known that a replanted oak never makes a fine tree, and that it must be produced from the acorn; it is also our experience that cherry trees and wild cherry trees, which come from a core, grow with an ease quite different from those transplanted in the countryside. I could move on from trees to garden plants, and I would point out a number that do much better in situ than when transplanted, such as (for example) lettuces, chicories, onions, cardoons, vegetables and roots of all kinds, etc. All this is too well demonstrated for me to go into detail here. All this is too well proven to be disputed. But the constitution is not the same (one might say) in all plants, nor in trees: I agree; but I say that order is always the same in vegetation, and that nature is always more pleased in her free and uninterrupted productions, than in those where she is disturbed. A few years ago, a private individual of my acquaintance destroyed ten to twelve acres of bad wood, and having noticed that, among the undergrowth with which it was filled, there were many wild apple and pear trees, which came naturally from the seeds, he had all the young trees that could be grafted reserved, and had them grafted the following spring, some in the form of splits, others in the form of crowns, according to their size: The grafts succeeded perfectly, and from the third year onwards, he harvested fruit of admirable goodness; but it must be said at the same time, that in destroying the other trees, he had them uprooted as far in advance as he could, and that he had a sort of general clearing done throughout the land, which contributed greatly to the profit of these grafted wildlings; in such a way that in ten years they produced larger heads than replanted trees would have produced in twenty-five, and this plant, formed by chance, became an orchard of excellent quality, which I saw with amazement. I have also observed something that comes to my mind in the way plantations are made in the cider-producing countries around Paris. Intelligent individuals come here every year to remove all the wild plants they can find in nurseries of suitable strength; they transplant them at home, and graft them after two years when they are well established. I have met them several times in the nurseries, and in discussions with them, they have all unanimously assured me that their species, grafted in this way onto trees taken from the ground, are much more successful in every respect than when the grafted trees are planted. I have confined myself to the facts, without wishing to go any further into the causes, and I believe that this little digression may suffice to justify at least that my opinion is not based on mere conjecture, and to make the usefulness of the practice I am advising more and more seusible.
